MFT 704 — Ethical and Legal Issues in Therapy

MSc Marriage and Family Therapy · Semester One · 1.71 Units · 14 teaching weeks · Prerequisite: none

In this book

Course leader's summary. This course trains judgement, not just compliance. You will learn the codes, the law, and — more importantly — how to reason when the codes and the law point in different directions, when the family's culture conflicts with the institution's policy, and when your own values are in the room.

How to use this book

Every week presents a standard, a case, and a decision. Ethics is not memorised; it is practised. The weekly incidents in this book are drawn from the ordinary texture of clinical life — the secret, the subpoena, the gift, the neighbour, the unpaid fee — because those are the situations that actually end careers.

1. Course description

Family therapy multiplies ethical complexity. In individual work there are two parties (you and the client). In family work there may be six, with conflicting interests, unequal power, and secrets that each person believes belongs to them. Add a school, a child protection agency, a court, an insurance company, and a faith community, and the question "who is my client?" becomes the central professional question of the course.

The course combines normative ethics (codes, duties, principles), law (the parts a clinician must know and the parts a clinician must refer), and professional formation (the kind of clinician you are becoming).

Week 1 — Moral philosophy for clinicians

You do not need to become a philosopher, but you need to know which argument you are making when you say "that would be wrong". Most ethical failure in clinical practice is not the product of a bad person; it is the product of a good person using one tradition of reasoning while believing they are using another, and therefore being unable to see the objection that the other tradition would have raised.

TraditionCentral questionTherapy example
Deontology (duty)What are my duties regardless of outcome?Keeping confidentiality even when disclosure would be convenient
ConsequentialismWhat produces the best overall outcome?Breaking confidentiality to prevent a serious harm
Virtue ethicsWhat would a person of good character do?Refusing a gift that would create obligation
Ethics of careWhat does this relationship require of me?Attending to the family's dependence on you
Justice / rightsAre power and resources fairly distributed?Whose voice is missing from this room?

The four principles (Beauchamp & Childress) remain the most portable framework: autonomy, beneficence, non-maleficence, justice. In family work they collide constantly — respect for the father's autonomy may conflict with the mother's safety, and a clinician who has not learned to name the collision will resolve it silently, in favour of whoever is more articulate in the room.

Why a family therapist needs philosophy more than an individual therapist does. In individual work the ethical questions are mostly about the boundary between two people: the client and the clinician. In family work the questions are about distribution: whose interests the clinician is serving, whose secret they are holding, whose account they believe, and whose voice the institution will hear. Those are questions of justice and care before they are questions of compliance, and the codes — which are written mostly for two-party work — do not answer them.

— A father telephones the clinic to ask what his wife said in her individual session. He is polite, he is paying the fee, and he is, in his own understanding, being a responsible head of household. The clinician has three duties in tension: confidentiality to the wife, honesty to the husband, and the welfare of a couple whose therapy depends on both of them returning. Which tradition she reaches for decides what she says in the next thirty seconds.

1.1 The lecture in ninety minutes

MinutesSegmentWhat the lecturer doesWhat students do
0–10Why this week existsThe difference between compliance and judgement, with the caseWrite the sentence they would say into the telephone
10–24The five traditionsEach tradition, its strength, its characteristic failureClassify six clinical utterances by tradition
24–36The four principlesAutonomy, beneficence, non-maleficence, justice, and how they collide in family workRank the four for three different cases
36–48Ethics of care and its critique of principle-based ethicsGilligan, Noddings, and the relational objectionName what a principles-only analysis would miss
48–60Moral distress and moral injuryThe difference between not knowing and not being ableIdentify one episode of each from placement
60–72The decision modelThe seven-step model that the course will use all termRun it on the case, in pairs
72–84Cultural and religious moral frameworksWhen the family's moral world and the code's are not the sameArgue one case from inside a framework not their own
84–90The assessmentHow the incident analyses are markedDraft the outline of their first incident analysis

The teaching decision that makes the week work. The lecture begins with the telephone call, before any theory, and every student writes the sentence they would say. The sentences are read aloud and sorted onto the board by tradition — and the class discovers that most of them are consequentialist ("it will damage the therapy if I tell him"), a few are deontological ("I cannot discuss her sessions"), and almost none are about care or justice. The sorting is the lesson: their instincts are already philosophical, and they have never inspected them.

The sentence the lecture must land. "You already have a moral theory. This week is about finding out which one, and whether you would defend it if a family asked you to."

1.2 Five traditions, and what each one cannot see

1. Deontology: duty regardless of outcome. The tradition that produced the codes. Its strength is predictability: a rule that holds when it is inconvenient is a rule that can be relied upon by a family who has never met you. Its characteristic failure is rigidity — the clinician who keeps a confidence that is killing someone because the rule says so, and who experiences the resulting harm as bad luck rather than as a decision. In family work, deontology's specific blindness is to distribution: a rule about confidentiality applied equally to a powerful father and a dependent grandmother produces an unequal result, because they have unequal capacity to use the information.

2. Consequentialism: the best overall outcome. Its strength is realism — it takes harm seriously and it permits the clinician to weigh a small breach against a large protection. Its characteristic failure is that nobody can calculate the consequences, and the calculation is usually done by the person who will benefit from the answer. In family work, its blindness is to the unrepresented: the person whose interests do not appear in the therapist's forecast is the person who gets sacrificed, and that person is usually the one with least power in the room.

3. Virtue ethics: what a person of good character would do. Its strength is formation — it asks what kind of clinician you are becoming, which is the question that survives when the code is silent. Its characteristic failure is vagueness and self-congratulation: "I would know" is not a method, and the practitioner who trusts their own character is the one least likely to notice its defects. In family work, virtue ethics supplies the questions the codes cannot answer — courage, patience, humility, temperance about one's own influence — and it needs a discipline to keep it honest, which is supervision.

4. Ethics of care: what this relationship requires of me. The tradition that family therapy needs most and uses least. Its central claims: that moral life is constituted by relationships rather than by isolated choosers; that responsiveness matters as much as rules; and that dependence is not a defect to be overcome but a condition of being human, which every client in your room is in. Its strength is that it describes family work accurately. Its characteristic failure is that it can justify almost anything in the name of the relationship, and it has no natural defence against a clinician who is more attached to a family than is good for the family.

5. Justice and rights: are power and resources fairly distributed? The tradition that asks who is not in the room and who is paying for the room. Its strength is that it names the questions the other four avoid: who has access to this service, whose language is spoken, whose account the institution will believe, and who carries the cost of the arrangement. Its characteristic failure is abstraction — a clinician can conduct a fine analysis of structural injustice and still be unjust to the person in front of them.

The five are not a menu. They are five different questions, and a competent clinician asks all five about a hard case and then has to decide, in the open, which one governs. The decision is the practice; the inspection of the decision is the profession.

1.2a The four principles, and how they collide in family work

The four principles are the most portable framework in the field, and the reason is that they are not a theory: they are four considerations that any defensible decision must have addressed. Their use is not to produce the answer but to make sure the answer has not ignored something.

Autonomy. The client's right to decide what happens to them, grounded in their capacity to do so. In individual work it is the dominant principle and it is generally straightforward. In family work it is immediately difficult, for three reasons. The first is multiplicity: there are several autonomous people in the room, and their autonomies conflict — the adolescent's autonomy over her own body against her parents' legal authority; the wife's autonomy over her own disclosure against the husband's claim to know. The second is capacity: a nine-year-old, an eighty-one-year-old with dementia, and a woman in an abusive relationship all have autonomy claims that are not identical to an adult's unconstrained choice, and "respecting autonomy" without distinguishing them is a way of abandoning the vulnerable. The third is context: in many of the households this book is written for, decisions are made by families rather than by individuals, and a clinician who insists on individual consent is not being respectful; they are being parochial.

Beneficence. The duty to act in the client's interest. It sounds unarguable and it is the principle most often used to justify paternalism, because "the client's interest" is defined by the clinician. In family work the principle has a specific structural problem: whose interest? A decision that benefits the marriage may harm the wife; a decision that protects the child may end the family's engagement.

Non-maleficence. First, do no harm. The most useful of the four in practice, because it operates as a check rather than a goal: before acting, ask what harm this could do, including the harms of the intervention itself. In ethics, the harms that are most often overlooked are the ones the clinician's own action causes: the disclosure that ends a therapy, the report that produces a violent reprisal, the note that is read in court five years later.

Justice. Fairness in the distribution of benefits, risks and resources. In family work it appears in three places that the other three principles do not reach: access (who can get to this clinic, at these hours, at this fee); voice (whose account is taken seriously, in a room and in a record); and consequence (who bears the cost of a decision — frequently not the person who made it).

The collision, worked. A seventeen-year-old discloses to the therapist that she is sexually active and does not want her parents told. The four principles give four different answers. Autonomy: she is nearly an adult and her confidence should be kept. Beneficence: telling the parents may protect her; not telling may leave her without support. Non-maleficence: telling may produce the beating she fears, which is a harm the clinician will have caused. Justice: her parents have legal responsibility and a legitimate interest; she has the greater exposure to the consequences. The principles do not resolve it. They ensure the clinician has seen all four, and then the clinician must decide and be able to say why.

The order of the principles is not fixed. Students frequently assume autonomy comes first because the codes list it first in some jurisdictions. It does not. In a case involving a child's safety, non-maleficence governs. In a case involving an adult's refusal of treatment, autonomy governs. Deciding which principle governs in this case is the act of judgement the course is teaching, and it cannot be reduced to a rule without producing the very rigidity deontology is criticised for.

1.2b Ethics of care: the tradition family therapy should own

Family therapy is, almost alone among the mental health professions, built on a relational ontology. It is therefore strange that its ethics are usually imported from a tradition built on isolated choosers. This section states the alternative, because it is the one that fits the work.

The relational claim. The ethics of care, developed by Gilligan, Noddings, Tronto and others, holds that moral life is constituted by relationships of dependence and response rather than by transactions between independent agents. Three consequences follow.

First, responsiveness is a moral skill. The question "what does this person need from me, here, now?" is not a softer version of "what is the rule?" It is a different and equally rigorous question, and it requires attention — the capacity to notice what is actually happening rather than what the category predicts. A clinician who has not developed attention will apply the right rule to the wrong situation.

Second, dependence is normal. Every client in your room is dependent on someone, and most are dependent on people who are also in the room. The tradition therefore refuses the picture of the autonomous chooser as the ethical ideal: a grandmother with dementia, a nine-year-old, a woman who cannot leave — none of them is a defective version of an independent adult. Each has claims that arise from their relationships.

Third, power is the central ethical fact. Care is not symmetrical. The person giving care has power, and the ethics of care requires that the power be used responsively rather than according to the carer's preferences. This is why the tradition is useful in family work: the therapist's power is enormous and largely invisible, and a principles-only analysis gives almost no account of it.

The critique, which the tradition must answer. Care ethics can justify almost anything in the name of the relationship. It has been used to keep women in caring roles, to excuse clinicians who will not end a treatment, and to defend a therapist's attachment to a family that has outgrown the therapy. Noddings's own answer is that caring must be received — the cared-for must experience it as care — which is a testable condition, and it is the version this course uses. "I did it because I cared" is not an ethical justification unless the person cared for can say that they were cared for.

The clinical use, in three questions. Whose need is being met by this decision — theirs or mine? Who in this family is dependent on whom, and is the dependence being used or served? And what would the person with least power in this room say if they were asked, in a language they had, with nobody watching?

1.3 The decision model: seven steps

The course uses one model all term, so that the incident analyses and the examination are comparable. It is deliberately pedestrian. Its value is not that it produces the right answer but that it makes the reasoning visible, which is what supervision, a court, and a professional body will all ask for.

Step 1 — State the ethical question, not the practical one. "What should I do about the husband's telephone call?" is practical. "Whose confidence am I holding, and to whom do I owe what?" is ethical. Most hard cases are hard because the ethical question has never been stated.

Step 2 — Establish the facts. What is actually known, by whom, and how. This step takes longer than students expect and prevents most errors: a great many ethical dilemmas dissolve when the facts are established (the husband already knew; the wife had said she would tell him; the child is not the father's).

Step 3 — Identify the parties and their interests. Every person affected, including the absent ones and the institution. Name the interests, not the positions. Then ask which party has least power to protect their own interest.

Step 4 — Apply the frameworks. All five traditions and all four principles, briefly, and record where they disagree. The disagreement is the case's real content.

Step 5 — Consult. The code, the law, the agency policy, a supervisor, and — where appropriate and possible — the client. Consultation is not a step for difficult cases; it is a step for any case in which the clinician notices they have a preference.

Step 6 — Decide, act, and record the reasoning. The decision, the reasons, the alternatives rejected and why. In a record, because the record is the only evidence that a decision was made rather than a drift.

Step 7 — Review. What happened; whether the prediction held; what would be done differently. The step that nobody does, and the only one that produces learning.

1.3a Moral distress, moral injury, and the difference that matters

Two terms that are used loosely in the literature and that name different things. A clinician who cannot tell them apart will misdiagnose their own state, and will look for the wrong remedy.

Moral distress is what happens when you know what the right action is and you are prevented from doing it. Its classic form is institutional: the clinician who knows a child should be referred and cannot get an appointment; who knows a family needs an interpreter and is not given one; who is required by an agency to keep a number of sessions that the work does not need. The distress is not confusion about the good. It is the experience of being unable to enact it.

The remedy for moral distress is structural, not psychological. It consists of changing the condition, or of naming it collectively, or of leaving. What does not work — and what is usually offered — is resilience training, which asks the clinician to feel better about an arrangement they are right to object to.

Moral injury is different and more serious. It names the lasting harm that follows acting against one's own moral commitments, or witnessing such an act and being unable to prevent it. The term comes from military psychiatry and it travels well into clinical settings, because clinicians do commit acts they believe to be wrong under constraint: a disclosure that produces a reprisal, a report that ends a family's engagement, a court report written in language the clinician knows to be misleading. The sequelae are not anxiety or burnout; they are shame, a changed sense of self, and a withdrawal from the aspects of the work that once mattered.

The distinction, in one line. Moral distress is "I could not do what I should". Moral injury is "I did what I should not, or I stood by while it was done".

What follows clinically. For distress: audit the condition, escalate, and use supervision to keep the judgement intact. For injury: supervision is necessary and insufficient; the remedy involves naming the act, restoring the moral account (which sometimes means an apology to the family), and in some cases a change of setting. A trainee who has been required to write a report they believe false needs a supervisor who will say that it was wrong, not one who explains the agency's constraints.

The course's position. A significant proportion of what is called burnout in this profession is moral distress that has been individualised. The remedy is not more self-care. It is a decision model that makes the reasoning visible, a supervisor who will disagree, and a professional identity that can survive being in the minority in a room.

1.4 The trolley, in a clinic

The trolley problem is a thought experiment about whether it is permissible to harm one person in order to save several. It is useful here not because clinicians face trolleys but because the structure of the problem — may I redirect a harm toward someone who did not choose it? — is the structure of half the hard cases in family work.

Variant 1 — The disclosure. A wife tells the therapist, in an individual session, that her husband has been physically violent twice. She does not want him told. The couple is in therapy together. The therapist can: say nothing and continue couple work (harm: the wife is unprotected and the therapy is built on a secret); disclose to the husband (harm: the wife loses her confidentiality and may be at greater risk); or stop the couple work and see the wife alone (harm: the couple loses the treatment). Redirection: every option pushes harm onto someone who did not choose it. The wife did not choose to lose her confidence; the husband did not choose to be discussed; the children did not choose any of it.

Variant 2 — The paternity. A father asks whether the child is his. The mother knows the answer. The therapist knows because the mother told her. Nothing the therapist does is neutral: silence is a lie by omission to the father; disclosure is a breach to the mother; ending the therapy is a harm to the child.

Variant 3 — The adolescent's risk. A fifteen-year-old discloses self-harm and asks the therapist not to tell her parents. Disclosing may end the therapeutic relationship; not disclosing may leave her alone with a risk. This is the variant that most resembles the trolley, because the clinician is choosing which of two identifiable people will bear the harm.

Why the thought experiment is worth the time. Because it trains three things. First, the recognition that someone will be harmed in every option, which dissolves the fantasy of the harm-free decision and with it a great deal of paralysis. Second, the discipline of naming who bears the harm, which is the justice question. Third, the habit of asking whether the harm is being redirected onto someone who did not choose it — and if it is, whether that person's consent could be obtained, which is frequently the move that converts an impossible case into a workable one.

— Take three ethical dilemmas from your own life, not clinical ones. For each, identify which tradition you used at the time, and which you used to justify it afterwards. Then answer one question: were they the same? Most students find that the tradition they acted on was care or virtue, and the tradition they justified with was consequence. Write down what that gap suggests about how you will account for your decisions in a clinical record.

1.5 Case: the telephone call from the father

The case from the lecture, worked through the seven steps. This is the model the incident analyses follow.

The facts. The Okonkwos — Ada, 34, Chuka, 39, Ebuka, 9 — have been in couple therapy for six weeks. In her third individual session, Ada disclosed that Chuka's night shifts have resumed and that she is "managing everything alone", and that she has begun to think about taking the children to her mother's in Lagos. She has not told Chuka. She asked the therapist not to tell him. On Thursday, Chuka telephones: "I want to know what she said on Tuesday."

Step 1 — The ethical question. Not "what do I say to Chuka?" but: I am holding a confidence from one member of a couple I am treating jointly; to whom do I owe what, and how do I honour the couple's treatment without deceiving either of them?

Step 2 — The facts. What does Chuka actually know? (He knows the sessions exist; he does not know what was said. He has noticed she is distant.) What has Ada been told about the confidentiality arrangement? (At the start of therapy the therapist said, in front of both, that individual disclosures would be handled by agreement — a sentence Ada may or may not have understood as a promise.) Is there a risk to anyone? (No violence; no risk to the children; a risk of family separation if the decision is taken unilaterally.) What is the therapist's contractual position? (The clinic's policy: no information from an individual session is disclosed without the individual's agreement, except in the specified exceptions.)

Step 3 — The parties and their interests. Ada: to have a place where she can think without consequences, and to be supported in a decision she has not made. Chuka: to know what is happening in his marriage, and not to be humiliated by a therapist who knows more than he does. The children: continuity and the marriage's stability. The therapist: to keep the treatment viable and to remain honest. The clinic: its policy. Least power: Ada, because she has already been described by her husband to the therapist as "difficult", and because she is the one whose confidence is at stake.

Step 4 — The frameworks. Deontology: the confidence holds; the clinic's rule is explicit. Consequence: refusing Chuka may end his engagement with the therapy, and his engagement is what has kept the family in the room. Virtue: what would a clinician of good character do — and would she be able to say it to both of them in the same room? Care: Ada needs a place to think; Chuka needs not to be excluded; the couple needs an arrangement that holds. Justice: the person with least power must not be the one who bears the cost of the therapist's convenience.

Step 5 — Consultation. The clinic's written policy; a supervisor, by telephone the same day; and Ada herself, before anything is said to Chuka.

Step 6 — The decision. The therapist does not disclose content. She says to Chuka: "I cannot tell you what your wife said, and I want to be straight with you about why: if either of you cannot use an individual session to think out loud, this therapy will not work. What I can do is ask her whether she is willing to talk to you about the thing that is on her mind, with me in the room." She then telephones Ada, tells her exactly what was said and what was not, and asks whether she will raise it in the joint session. Ada says she will, on one condition: that the therapist opens the session, so that she is not the one who starts it.

Step 7 — The review. At the next joint session, Ada raises the question of Lagos. Chuka is angry and then frightened. The disclosure does not end the therapy; the manner of it — preceded by a week in which Ada had chosen to make it — is what keeps the therapy alive.

What made the difference. Two things, and neither is a principle. The first is that the therapist consulted before answering, which is what converted a trap into a process. The second is that she told Ada exactly what she had said to Chuka, which is the practice — not the rule — that keeps a multiperson therapy honest. Neither is in any code. Both are what an experienced clinician does.

1.5a The four questions that stop a decision going wrong

Before the seven steps, four questions that an experienced clinician asks almost automatically, and that a trainee can be taught to ask deliberately. They take ninety seconds and they catch the majority of errors.

Question 1 — Who is not in this conversation? The absent person is usually the one who will bear the cost. A decision about a child made without the child; a decision about a marriage made without the husband; a decision about a placement made without the person who will be moved. Naming the absent party does not always mean convening them; it means accounting for them in the reasoning.

Question 2 — Whose interest does my preferred option serve? Asked honestly, this question is uncomfortable, which is why it is asked before the decision rather than after. The clinician who wants to disclose a secret is frequently protecting themselves from the discomfort of holding it. The clinician who wants to keep it is frequently protecting the therapy they have built.

Question 3 — What would I do if the person with least power were the one I had to explain this to? The test is a version of the publicity test: a decision that cannot be explained to the person most affected by it is a decision that needs re-examining. The explanation does not have to be acceptable to them; it has to be possible.

Question 4 — What would I write in the record? If the sentence that would have to go in the file reads badly, the decision is bad — not because records are read, but because writing forces the reasoning into plain language, and plain language is unforgiving of self-deception. This is the cheapest ethical instrument in the book and the one most likely to change a decision in the thirty seconds before it is taken.

1.6 Transcript: the call, annotated

Eleven turns, reconstructed from a supervision recording. The therapist is a trainee, four months into placement. The annotation names the tradition governing each move, which is the discipline the week is teaching.

T1 Chuka: I want to know what she said on Tuesday. I am her husband. I am paying for this. — Two claims, both true in his frame: a status claim and a contract claim. Neither is answered by silence, which he will read as confirmation that something is being hidden. T2 Therapist: You are her husband and you are paying, and both of those are reasons I should be straight with you rather than avoid you. — The move: concede the true parts before refusing anything. Deontological in form, care in tone. T3 Chuka: Then tell me. T4 Therapist: I am not going to tell you what she said. I want to say why, and I want you to hear that it is not because she has said something terrible about you. — The disclosure of the rule, and the pre-emptive denial of the catastrophic reading. Without the second sentence, the first produces exactly the fantasy the therapist is trying to prevent. T5 Chuka: Then she has said something. T6 Therapist: She has said something she is not ready to say to you, and I have said I will not say it for her. If I break that, neither of you can use a session to think. You would be sitting in that chair wondering what I am writing down. — The justification is consequentialist, and it is chosen deliberately: the deontological version ("it is the rule") is true and it lands as bureaucracy. The consequentialist version gives him a reason he can use. T7 Chuka: (long pause) So what do I do? — The question that tells the therapist the refusal has been accepted. Note that it took four turns, not one, and that a therapist who stopped talking after T4 would not have got here. T8 Therapist: Two things. I will ask her whether she is willing to bring it into the room with both of you, and I will tell you what she says either way. And in the meantime, I want to know what you have noticed at home, because you have noticed something or you would not be telephoning me. — The offer of a next step, and the redirection of his energy from a demand for information to an account of his own observation. This is the turn that keeps him in the therapy. T9 Chuka: She is packing. I saw a bag. — The fact the therapist did not have. The husband is not asking for gossip; he is frightened, and the therapist's rule has, until this moment, kept her from knowing why. T10 Therapist: (pause) Thank you. That is important, and I would not have known it if you had not telephoned. — The acknowledgement is accurate, and it is also the repair: his call has produced something of value rather than only a refusal. T11 Chuka: What do I do about the bag? T12 Therapist: Nothing yet, and not nothing forever. Give me until tomorrow to speak to her, and I will telephone you. If she will not talk to you by Friday, I will tell you that too, and we will decide then what the three of us do. — A time limit, a commitment, and a fallback. The deontological refusal has been turned into a process with dates, which is what makes it survivable for a frightened man.

What the transcript shows that the model does not. The seven-step model produces the decision; the transcript produces the sequence, and the sequence is where the skill lives. Three things in particular. First, the therapist never says "I understand your concern" — she demonstrates it by conceding the true parts. Second, she offers a reason rather than a rule, and the reason is chosen for the listener. Third, she ends with a date, which converts a refusal into a relationship.

1.6a Bad arguments, named

Students lose marks, and clinicians lose cases, by using one of six arguments that sound ethical and are not. Naming them makes them visible.

"It is my duty." Sometimes true. More often a rule is being used to end a conversation the clinician does not want to have. The test: can you state the duty, its source, and the harm it is preventing?

"It is for their own good." The paternalist's sentence. It may be correct; it is never sufficient. The test: who decided what their good is, and were they asked?

"Everyone does it." A statement about a norm, not about an obligation. The test: is the practice defensible or merely common?

"It will damage the therapy." Frequently true and frequently the clinician's own convenience in therapeutic clothing. The test: whose therapy, and what is the damage measured against?

"I had no choice." Almost never accurate. There is usually a choice with a cost, and the honest statement names the cost rather than denying the choice.

"I was following the policy." The defence that fails at every professional tribunal, because the professional's obligation is to exercise judgement about the policy, not to be its instrument.

The exercise. In the laboratory, students are given six anonymised sentences from real disciplinary findings and asked which of the six arguments each one is. The recognition is fast, and it transfers: once a clinician can hear the shape of the bad argument, they can hear it in their own mouth.

1.7 Laboratory: the ethics incident analysis

Aim. To produce a 600-word incident analysis that a supervisor, a professional body, or a court could read and follow. This is the format of the four assessed pieces of coursework.

Procedure. Students bring a real or witnessed incident (anonymised), or use one of the four briefs the trainer supplies. They write the analysis in the session, in fifty minutes, against the seven steps, and then exchange with a partner who marks it against the rubric. The last twenty minutes is the plenary, in which two analyses are read aloud and the class identifies which step each one skipped.

Marking rubric (20 marks).

Criterion0–12–34–5
The questionA practical questionEthical but vagueEthical, specific, and the practical question is explicitly set aside
The factsAssumedListed, but no account of what is unknownEstablished, with what is unknown named and how it would be established
The partiesClient and therapistAll present partiesAll parties including absent ones and the institution, with the least powerful identified
The frameworksOne tradition, unnamedTwo traditions, namedFour or five, applied briefly, with the disagreement between them stated
The decisionA preferenceA decision with reasonsA decision, the rejected alternatives, and the review

The distinction that carries most of the marks. The top band requires the student to state where the frameworks disagree. A paper that applies five frameworks and finds they all point the same way has either misapplied them or chosen a case that is not an ethical dilemma; and a paper that applies one framework and calls it the answer has written an opinion rather than an analysis.

The four briefs.

Brief A. A couple's sixteen-year-old daughter asks to see you individually, and says she does not want her parents to know. The parents are your clients and are paying.

Brief B. A colleague tells you, at lunch, that a family you both know is "probably abusive" and that he has not reported it because "there is no evidence".

Brief C. A family offers you a substantial gift at the end of therapy. You know the family cannot easily afford it.

Brief D. You are asked by a court to produce a report on custody, and you have been treating the mother for a year. The fee is three times your usual rate.

1.8 Six errors

Error 1 — Confusing the rule with the reason. "Because the code says so" is a true statement and, said to a frightened husband, it is heard as evasion. The code is the floor of the reasoning, not its content; a clinician who cannot give a reason a family can understand has not yet understood the rule.

Error 2 — Applying individual ethics to a multiperson system. Almost every professional code is written for two parties. Applied unchanged to a couple or a family, it produces a therapist who is scrupulously confidential with one member and quietly deceptive to the other, and who believes they have been ethical because they have followed the rule.

Error 3 — Calculating consequences in your own favour. Consequentialist reasoning is the most useful and the most corruptible. The test is whether the calculation was made before or after the therapist noticed what they wanted to do.

Error 4 — Virtue ethics as a substitute for method. "I would know" is not a decision procedure, and the clinician who most trusts their own character is least likely to notice its defects. Virtue needs supervision the way a knife needs a stone.

Error 5 — Care as an alibi. "I did it because I cared" has justified more boundary violations than cynicism ever has. The test is Noddings's: was it received as care by the person cared for?

Error 6 — Treating moral distress as a personal failing. The clinician who cannot get a child an appointment, and who concludes that they are not resilient, has misdiagnosed a structural problem as a character flaw. The remedy is escalation, not self-care.

1.9 Culture, faith and the moral framework

Three moral worlds in one room. The family's moral framework, the profession's, and the clinician's own are frequently three different systems, and the clinician's is the one least likely to be named. In the households this book is written for, the operative framework is often not any of the five Western traditions but a religious or communal one: duties are owed to a lineage rather than to an individual; the family's reputation is a moral good in its own right; elder authority is not negotiable by argument; and shame, rather than guilt, is the mechanism by which wrongdoing is registered.

What that changes about the four principles. Autonomy in the individualist sense is not the organising value in many of these households, and a clinician who treats its absence as a deficit will misread deference as incapacity and consultation as inefficiency. The workable translation is not to abandon autonomy but to ask the family's version of the question: who in this family has the standing to decide, and does the person most affected have a route to be heard? That question travels across frameworks, and it is answerable in any of them.

Shame, guilt and the ethics of disclosure. In a guilt-oriented framework, wrongdoing is registered by the individual's conscience and confession is restorative. In a shame-oriented framework, wrongdoing is registered by the community's judgement and disclosure may be catastrophic for the whole household. This is not a reason to keep a secret that must be told; it is a reason to plan how it is told, with whom, and in whose presence, which is a clinical skill and not a concession.

Faith as a moral authority in the room. A pastor, an imam, or an elder may hold the authority that the clinician's code does not. Where that is so, the code is not the only source of moral authority in the case and pretending otherwise produces a clinician who is right and useless. The moves that work are the ones from Week 13 of MFT 703: join the framework, add a mechanism, and hold one non-negotiable.

DEBATE — "A therapist should never act against a competent adult client's stated wish, whatever the consequences." Argue for: the client's authority over their own life is the foundation of the profession, paternalism has a long and cruel history, and a clinician who overrides once will override again. Argue against: competence is not a switch, adults are frequently pressed by people with more power than they have, and a clinician who watches a foreseeable death without acting has chosen an abstraction over a person. Then state your actual rule, and the case in which you would break it — because every clinician eventually meets that case, and the ones who have never thought about it are the ones who improvise badly.

1.10 Evidence note

What the research supports. The four-principles framework is the most widely used analytic structure in clinical ethics teaching, and studies of ethics education consistently find that structured decision models improve the consistency and documentation of ethical reasoning, though not necessarily the quality of the decisions. The finding that moral distress is prevalent in mental health work, and that it is associated with intention to leave rather than with clinical incompetence, is well replicated. The association between reflective supervision and improved ethical reasoning is modest but consistent.

What is weaker. Almost everything about outcomes. There is no body of evidence showing that clinicians trained in one tradition make better decisions than clinicians trained in another, and the field's outcome measures are self-report and vignette-based. The literature on moral injury in mental health professions is recent and largely qualitative, mostly borrowed from military and nursing research.

What is not supported. That codes prevent misconduct — the disciplinary literature consistently finds that violations are committed by clinicians who know the code. That ethics can be taught as compliance — knowledge of rules is necessary and has never been sufficient. And that ethical reasoning is a stable trait; it is a practice, and it degrades under workload and isolation, which is why consultation is a structural protection rather than a courtesy.

The matrix cell. Development is probabilistic and non-linear; the same input produces different outcomes depending on timing and context. A clinician with excellent ethical reasoning at nine in the morning, with a supervisor available, is a different decision-maker from the same clinician at seven in the evening with a waiting room full of people. The variables that most reliably predict ethical failure are not moral but situational: fatigue, isolation, and speed.

1.10a What the codes do not cover

A short section with a long consequence. The professional codes are necessary and they are not a complete account of the moral life of a clinician, and the gap is where most of the difficulty lives.

The codes address conduct, not character. They say what may not be done; they say almost nothing about what kind of clinician to be. The virtues that matter most in this work — patience with a family that returns for the eleventh time with the same problem, courage in a case conference where the consultant is wrong, humility in front of a grandmother who has raised six children — are not in any code and are not enforceable. They are formed by supervision, by example, and by the company a clinician keeps.

The codes are written for individuals. Confidentiality, consent and competence are all framed around a single client and a single clinician. Multiperson work, which is the whole of this course, is covered by adaptations rather than by the codes' own logic. The clinician who follows the code precisely in a family case may still be unjust, because the code has no category for the second client.

The codes are jurisdiction-bound. Every code is written against a legal background that differs by country and, in federal systems, by state. Statements about reporting obligations, minors' consent, and record retention that are correct in one jurisdiction are wrong in another. This book states principles and flags where the law must be checked locally, which is a permanent obligation rather than a limitation of this text.

The codes lag. Reproductive technology, digital records, online therapy, social media contact with clients, and cross-border tele-therapy are all areas in which practice has outrun guidance. A clinician working at the edge is making policy by their conduct, and the obligation that follows is to be able to defend the reasoning, not to be able to point at a rule that does not exist.

1.11 Key terms

Deontology — the ethics of duty; the right action follows from a rule or obligation regardless of consequence.

Consequentialism — the ethics of outcome; the right action is the one that produces the best overall result.

Virtue ethics — the ethics of character; the question is what a person of good character would do, and how such a person is formed.

Ethics of care — the ethics of relationship and responsiveness; moral life is constituted by dependence and response rather than by transactions between independent agents.

Justice — fairness in the distribution of benefits, risks and resources; in family work, the question of whose voice is heard and who bears the cost.

Autonomy — the client's right to decide what happens to them, grounded in their capacity to do so.

Beneficence — the duty to act in the client's interest.

Non-maleficence — the duty not to cause harm, including the harms of one's own intervention.

Moral distress — knowing the right action and being prevented from taking it.

Moral injury — lasting harm following an act against one's moral commitments, or witnessing one.

Paternalism — overriding a person's wishes for their own good.

Relational ethics — the position that obligations arise from relationships and their power asymmetries, not only from rules or outcomes.

1.12 Self-test

  1. Name the five traditions and state the characteristic failure of each.
  2. How do the four principles collide in a case involving a seventeen-year-old's confidentiality, and which one governs? Defend your answer.
  3. What is the difference between moral distress and moral injury, and why does the remedy differ?
  4. In the Okonkwo case, what two practices — neither of them in a code — kept the therapy alive?
  5. Why does the lecture insist that "because the code says so" is an inadequate answer to a family?
  6. What does the ethics of care add to a principles-based analysis, and what is its characteristic corruption?
  7. What is the test for whether an act of care was really care?
  8. Give the seven steps of the decision model, and say which one students most often skip.

Answers. (1) Deontology — rigidity; consequentialism — self-serving calculation and blindness to the unrepresented; virtue — vagueness and self-congratulation; care — it can justify anything in the name of the relationship; justice — abstraction, analysing structures while being unjust to the person present. (2) Autonomy supports her confidence; beneficence may support disclosure as protection; non-maleficence warns that disclosure may produce the beating she fears; justice notes that she bears the consequence and her parents hold legal authority. Which governs depends on the assessed risk: in the absence of danger, autonomy and non-maleficence together; where there is danger, non-maleficence governs and the disclosure is planned with her rather than about her. (3) Distress is being unable to do what you believe right, and is remedied structurally; injury is having acted against your commitments, and requires naming, repair and sometimes a change of setting. (4) Consulting before answering, and telling Ada exactly what had been said to Chuka. (5) Because the code is the floor of the reasoning, not its content; a family cannot use a rule they cannot understand, and a clinician who offers only the rule is heard as evasive and hides something in doing so. (6) It adds attention, dependence and power; its corruption is that it can justify almost anything as care. (7) Noddings's test: the cared-for must be able to say they were cared for. (8) State the ethical question; establish the facts; identify parties and interests; apply the frameworks; consult; decide, act and record; review — the review is the step most often skipped.

1.13 Reflective task

Write down the last time you did something you believed to be wrong because you were required to. Do not write about the ethics of the requirement; write about what you did, in one paragraph, in plain words. Then answer three questions: who else knew? what did you tell yourself at the time? and what would have had to be available for you to have acted differently? Most students find the answer to the third question is concrete — a supervisor, an hour, a policy, one other person who agreed — which is the argument for structures rather than for character.

1.14 Further reading

  • Beauchamp, T. and Childress, J. Principles of Biomedical Ethics — the four-principles framework, and its critics' best answers.
  • Gilligan, C. In a Different Voice — the origin of the care critique, best read with its reception.
  • Noddings, N. Caring — the relational account, and the reception test used above.
  • MacIntyre, A. After Virtue — the strongest case that rules without a tradition of practice are empty.
  • Jameton, A. on moral distress — the original nursing account, which transfers cleanly to this work.

Next: Week 2 — The codes and informed consent.

Week 2 — The codes and informed consent

What codes do and do not do. Codes set a floor, not a ceiling. They are written to be enforceable, which means they are often silent on the hardest questions. They also vary by jurisdiction and by professional body; know which one governs you.

The core obligations across the major codes:

  1. Competence and its limits
  2. Informed consent (including consent for recording and for supervision)
  3. Confidentiality and its limits
  4. Multiple relationships
  5. Sexual intimacy with clients and former clients (prohibited; time limits vary)
  6. Fees and financial arrangements
  7. Advertising and representation
  8. Research ethics
  9. Cultural competence and non-discrimination
  10. Reporting obligations and legal compliance

Informed consent in family therapy is a document and a conversation. It should cover: who the client is; who is seen and in what combination; the confidentiality policy including secrets; recording and supervision; fees and cancellation; the limits of confidentiality (risk, abuse, court order); and the complaint route. Review it aloud in the first session, in the family's language, and record that you did.

Why consent is the hinge of the course. Almost every other week depends on it. Confidentiality is only meaningful if the parties understood, at the start, who would be told what. A boundary can only be violated if it was known to exist. A record can only be defended if the client consented to what it contains. And the defence that succeeds at a professional tribunal is almost never "I did the right thing"; it is "the client understood, agreed, and I recorded that they did." Consent is the one document in this book that a clinician will be asked to produce years later, and it is the one most often written badly.

— A signed form is not consent. Consent is a process of understanding, and the signature is merely evidence that the process happened. A family that signed a document they could not read, in a language they do not speak, at the end of a first session in which they were upset, has not consented, and a tribunal will say so. The signature protects the clinic; the conversation protects the family; and the clinician needs both.

2.1 The lecture in ninety minutes

MinutesSegmentWhat the lecturer doesWhat students do
0–8The floor and the ceilingOne disciplinary finding, read aloud, and the code clauses it breachedPredict which clause was breached before being told
8–20The ten obligationsThe ten, and the two that generate most complaintsRank the ten by how often they appear in findings
20–32Comparing codesAAMFT, APA, IAMFC and a national code, side by side on three issuesIdentify where the codes disagree, and what a clinician does then
32–44Informed consent as a processThe eight components, and the difference between disclosure and consentDraft the eight headings for a couple-therapy consent
44–56Consent with people who cannot readThe four adaptations, and the witnessRewrite a consent paragraph at the level of a twelve-year-old reader
56–66Consent and minorsDevelopmental capacity, parental authority, and the mature minorDecide three ages, with reasons
66–76Recording, supervision and the limitsWhat families are rarely told, and why it mattersScript the sentence that explains supervision
76–86DocumentationWhat to write, where, and the sentence that saves a clinicianWrite the first-session consent note
86–90The assessmentHow the consent document is markedDraft their document's structure

The teaching decision that makes the week work. The lecture opens with a real disciplinary finding — a clinician suspended for something that sounds trivial — and the class predicts which clause was breached. They are usually wrong, and they are wrong in an instructive direction: they assume the violation was dramatic (sex, fraud, a breach of confidence) when it was almost always procedural (no documented consent, a record that did not match the account, a policy never explained). The exercise revalues documentation, which is the part of ethics that students find least interesting and that ends most careers.

The sentence the lecture must land. "You will not be struck off for having the wrong theory. You will be struck off for not having written down what you agreed."

2.2 The ten obligations, and the two that generate most complaints

1. Competence and its limits. The obligation to practise within one's training and to refer when a presentation exceeds it. The obligation is not to know everything; it is to know the boundary and to act on it. (Week 7.)

2. Informed consent. Everything in the rest of this week.

3. Confidentiality and its limits. The obligation to protect information and the obligation to disclose in specified circumstances. The second half is the part families do not hear. (Weeks 3, 4, 5.)

4. Multiple relationships. The obligation not to hold a second role that could impair judgement or exploit the relationship. (Week 6.)

5. Sexual intimacy with clients and former clients. Prohibited. Time limits on former clients vary by code, from two years to never, and the variation tells you something about how seriously the profession regards the boundary.

6. Fees and financial arrangements. The obligation to agree fees in advance, not to exploit financial dependency, and not to continue treatment for financial reasons when it is no longer indicated. (Week 12.)

7. Advertising and representation. The obligation not to overstate competence or outcomes. In an era of social media, this is the fastest-growing category of complaint, because a clinician's profile is advertising whether they intended it to be or not.

8. Research ethics. Consent, deception, anonymity, and the ethics of publishing case material. (Week 13.)

9. Cultural competence and non-discrimination. Now written into most codes, and the clause with the least agreement about what compliance looks like. Week 13 of MFT 703 is the substantive account; in this course the obligation is to notice when the clinician's cultural assumptions are producing a decision.

10. Reporting obligations and legal compliance. The obligation to know the local law on child protection, adult protection, and the reporting of serious harm. A clinician who does not know their jurisdiction's threshold has an obligation they cannot discharge.

The two that generate most complaints. The disciplinary literature is remarkably consistent, and the answer surprises students: not sexual misconduct, which is rarer and more dramatic, but failure to maintain adequate records and failure to obtain or document informed consent. The two are connected, because the record is where consent lives. A clinician with an immaculate record and a mediocre theory is safer, in professional terms, than a brilliant clinician who cannot show what was agreed.

Why that is not as cynical as it sounds. The record is not a bureaucratic artefact. It is the only evidence that a family was told something, understood it, and agreed; that a risk was assessed; that a decision was reasoned rather than drifted into. In the absence of that evidence, a tribunal has only the family's account and the clinician's memory, and it will prefer the family's. Documentation is the clinician's honesty made checkable.

2.2a Where the codes disagree, and what a clinician does then

Students often assume the codes say the same thing in different words. They do not, and the differences are instructive because they reveal that the profession has not settled the question.

Issue 1 — Secrets in couple work. The AAMFT code requires that therapists clarify at the outset which persons are clients and what relationship each has to the therapist, and that they obtain written consent to disclose information; it does not prescribe a secrets policy. The APA ethics code addresses multiple relationships and confidentiality but is written for individual treatment and gives no guidance on therapeutic secrets in couple work. National codes vary further, and many say nothing at all. The result is that a couple therapist's confidentiality policy is a professional judgement rather than a compliance requirement, which is why it must be written down and explained.

Issue 2 — Sexual relationships with former clients. Most codes prohibit them outright or impose a time limit that ranges from two years to five years to never. The disagreement is not trivial: it reflects a genuine dispute about whether the harm arises from the power asymmetry (which does not end when therapy does) or from the therapeutic relationship (which does). A clinician moving jurisdictions must check, because the rule that was correct at home may be a violation at the new post.

Issue 3 — Duty to warn or protect. In some jurisdictions the clinician has a legal duty to warn an identifiable third party of a serious threat; in others the duty is to protect, which may be discharged by other means; in many there is no statutory duty at all and the obligation is ethical only. A clinician who learns this from the code rather than from the statute will get it wrong.

Issue 4 — Minors' consent. The age at which a young person may consent to treatment without a parent varies from about twelve to eighteen and is frequently different for different treatments. The codes defer to law, which means the clinician must know the local statute and not the code.

What a clinician does when the codes disagree. Four moves, in order. First, establish which code governs you — by licence, by membership, and by employment contract, which may impose a stricter standard than either. Second, apply the stricter standard, because a tribunal will. Third, where the codes are silent, write your policy down and follow it consistently, because consistency is the only defence available when there is no rule. Fourth, ask the professional body in writing where the stakes are high, and keep the reply.

The professional point. The existence of disagreement is not a defect of the codes; it is information about which questions the profession has not resolved. Those are exactly the questions on which a clinician must think rather than look up, and they are the ones this course is about.

2.2b What a professional body actually does when a complaint arrives

A short account, because clinicians fear the process more than they understand it, and fear produces both over-documentation and concealment.

The route. A complaint is made, in writing, usually by the client or a relative. The registrar assesses whether it falls within the code's scope. If it does, it is put to the clinician for a written response, with a deadline. Many complaints end there, because the response shows that the matter is a disagreement rather than a breach. Those that continue go to a panel, which hears both parties and may dismiss, caution, impose conditions, suspend, or strike off.

What the panel asks, in practice. Not "was the outcome bad?" but three questions: what did the clinician do; what did they record; and what would a reasonable clinician in that position have done? The third question is answered by expert evidence and by the code, and the first two by the file.

What helps a clinician, in order of usefulness. A contemporaneous record of what was agreed. A record of the reasoning, including options rejected. Evidence of supervision or consultation on the matter. An apology offered early and without qualification. And a consistent account — the same story in the file, the response, and the hearing.

What harms a clinician, in order of seriousness. Altering or backdating a record, which converts a complaint into a finding of dishonesty and ends careers that would otherwise have survived. An account that changes between documents. Defensiveness in the response, which is read as an absence of insight. And criticising the complainant, which is heard by the panel as a failure to understand the harm.

The one sentence to remember. The panel is not asking whether you are a good person; it is asking whether a reasonable clinician could have done what you did, and whether your file shows that you thought about it. Almost every answer to that question was decided months earlier, in a note written or not written at the end of a session.

2.3 Informed consent: the eight components

Consent has eight components. A document that omits any of them is incomplete, and the omission is frequently the one that matters in the case that goes wrong.

1. Who the client is, and who is not. In family work this is the first and hardest component. Is the client the couple, the family, the individual who telephoned, or the child who was brought? The answer determines who holds the confidentiality and to whom the therapist owes a duty. The consent document should name the client explicitly, and the answer should be recorded in the family's words: "the client is the couple, and the children are seen as part of the family's treatment."

2. Who will be seen, in what combination, and on what terms. Couple work with occasional individual sessions is a different arrangement from individual work with occasional joint sessions, and the family must know which they are getting before they begin. The most common complaint in this area is not that individual sessions happened, but that nobody said they would.

3. The confidentiality policy, including the secrets policy. Stated in plain language, with the exceptions named rather than implied. (Weeks 3 to 5.)

4. Recording, observation and supervision. Families are routinely recorded, observed through a one-way mirror, or discussed in supervision, and routinely not told. This is one of the clearest and most commonly breached obligations in training clinics. The disclosure needs three parts: what happens, who sees it, and how it is stored and destroyed.

5. Fees, cancellation, insurance and what happens when the money stops. Including the awkward parts: the fee for a missed session, the position when a third party pays, and what the clinician will do if a family falls into arrears. (Week 12.)

6. The limits of confidentiality. Risk to self, risk to others, abuse of a child or vulnerable adult, court orders, and any statutory reporting duty. Named, in examples rather than in abstractions: "if I believed a child was being hurt, I would have to tell the authorities, and I would tell you first that I was doing it."

7. The complaints route. Who to contact, and what will happen. A family that knows the route is less likely to need it, and a clinician who discloses it looks less like someone with something to hide.

8. The right to withdraw. Including the right to withdraw from recording, from research, and from treatment, and what happens to the record afterwards. Consent is not a contract of attendance.

The test of the document. Hand it to someone who has never been in therapy and ask them what will happen to their information if they tell the therapist something dangerous. If they cannot answer, the document has failed, whatever it says.

2.3a Consent when the family cannot read the document

Most of the world's families do not read a clinic's consent form fluently, and a substantial minority cannot read it at all. A clinician who responds by reading the document aloud in English has produced a signature and not a consent.

The four adaptations, in order of importance.

1. Change the medium before you change the words. Consent is a conversation and it can be witnessed. Sit with the family, explain the eight components in their language, in plain speech, and ask each of them to say back what they understood. Then record, in the file: "consent explained orally in Yoruba to both parents and to the paternal aunt; each repeated the confidentiality limits and the supervision arrangement in their own words; all agreed. Written summary provided; both parents marked; consent form signed by the father as head of household and by the mother." That record is stronger evidence of consent than any signed form.

2. Use a witness who is not a family member. A colleague, an interpreter, or a member of the clinic staff who can attest that the explanation happened. The witness is not a formality: in a dispute two years later, a witness is a person who can be asked what was said.

3. Reduce the document, do not translate it. A translation of a dense document remains dense. The effective version is one page, in short sentences, with the eight components as headings and the exceptions in bold. Where literacy is limited, a page of icons or a simple diagram of the four confidentiality limits is more useful than prose, and is used in several services.

4. Give them something to take away and to show someone else. The consent conversation usually needs to be repeated at home, with a relative who was not present and whose opinion the family trusts. A one-page summary in the family's language, with the clinic's telephone number, is the only part of the process that continues after the family leaves, and it is the part that most determines whether they return.

The error to avoid. Do not treat limited literacy as limited capacity. A woman who cannot read a form can understand confidentiality perfectly well, and will hold the clinician to it more precisely than a reader would. The obligation is to make the understanding possible, not to test the reading.

2.3b Consent and the young person

Consent with children and adolescents is not a single question but three: who consents, who assents, and who is told.

The legal position, which varies and must be checked. In most jurisdictions a parent or guardian consents for a child below a stated age; above that age, and in some jurisdictions for specific treatments such as contraception or mental health care, the young person may consent themselves. Many jurisdictions recognise a "mature minor" whose capacity is assessed rather than assumed. Both parents may need to consent where they share custody, and a clinician who accepts one parent's consent without checking may find the treatment challenged later.

Assent, which is not the same as consent. A child who is old enough to understand what therapy is should be asked whether they agree to it, in language they have, and their refusal should be taken seriously even where the law permits the parent to override it. The clinical reason is not legal: a nine-year-old who has been brought to a therapist without being asked will cooperate in the first session and withdraw by the third, and the family will describe this as the child's problem.

What the young person is told, and what is kept from them. There are three separate audiences in a child case: the child, the parents, and the institution. The consent conversation must establish, before anything else, what the child will be told about who sees what. A twelve-year-old is entitled to know whether what she says will be reported to her mother, and a therapist who does not tell her is not protecting her; she is teaching her, accurately, that adults cannot be trusted with information.

The four-part script for a child's consent conversation.

  1. "Your mum and dad have asked me to talk with you. You do not have to talk about anything you do not want to. If you want to stop, you can say so and we will stop."
  2. "What you say here stays here, unless I am worried that somebody is being hurt — including you. If that happens I will tell you before I tell anyone else."
  3. "I talk to other people about my work, with their names hidden, so that I can learn to do it better. I will not use your name."
  4. "Is there anything you want to ask me before we start?"

The age at which each part lands. Fifty-five percent of adolescent self-disclosure depends on part 2 being true and being believed. A therapist who says it and then breaks it without warning has done more damage than one who never offered it.

2.4 The first session: consent in practice

The consent conversation in a first session takes twelve to fifteen minutes and is the best investment in the hour. The sequence below is used throughout the course.

Minute 1 — Name the arrangement. "Before we start, I want to tell you how I work, and then you can decide whether you want to go on. Nothing you say in the next ten minutes goes into a record unless you decide to continue."

Minutes 2 to 5 — Who the client is. "I am going to be working with you as a couple. That means the relationship is my client, and I will not take sides with either of you. It also means that if one of you telephones me, I will tell the other that you did, without telling them what you said."

Minutes 5 to 9 — The confidentiality limits, with examples. "What you tell me stays here, with four exceptions. If I think you are in danger of seriously hurting yourself, I have to act. If I think someone else is in danger from you, I have to act. If a child is being hurt, the law requires me to report it. And if a court orders me to produce my records, I have to obey. In the first three, I will tell you what I am doing before I do it, unless telling you would put someone in danger."

Minutes 9 to 11 — Recording and supervision. "I record some sessions, and I take my work to a supervisor who is also a therapist. Your first names are used; your surname is not. The recording is deleted after six months. You can refuse the recording and still be treated."

Minutes 11 to 13 — Fees and practicalities. Fee, cancellation, who pays, what happens if it is late.

Minute 13 — The secrets policy. "There is one more thing, and it is the most important sentence of the hour. If one of you asks to see me alone, I will say yes. But I will not keep a secret that affects this relationship, because a therapy built on a secret is a therapy that is not working. If something like that comes up, I will tell you both that it has come up, and I will help whoever it is to decide how to say it. Is that acceptable to each of you?"

Minutes 14 to 15 — The question that tests it. "Tell me, in your own words, what you have understood about who I would tell if you told me something dangerous." Then record the answer. The recording of their words is the evidence of consent; the signature is not.

Why the sequence is fixed. Because the four limits are the parts families do not hear, and they are heard only if they are given last, with examples, after the clinician has established that they are not looking for a reason to report. A therapist who begins with the limits has begun with a threat.

2.4a The sentence that has to be said, and the four ways of saying it badly

Of the fifteen minutes of a first session's consent conversation, one sentence does most of the work, and it is the one trainees most often soften. It is the secrets policy.

The sentence. "If one of you sees me alone and tells me something that affects this relationship, I will not keep it. I will tell you both that something has been said, and I will help whoever it is to decide how to say it — but I will not carry it."

The four bad versions, and why each fails.

"There are no secrets in this therapy." False, and heard as a prohibition. Couples test it in the first month, discover that the therapist does keep ordinary confidences, and conclude that the rule was rhetorical.

"Anything you tell me individually, I may share." A licence without limits. The family hears "she will tell him", which suppresses the individual disclosure entirely, and the therapist loses the material that would have been most useful.

"We can discuss it if it comes up." The most common and the most damaging, because it is a deferral of the question that must be answered before the question arises. When it arises, the therapist is negotiating under pressure with a person who has already disclosed.

"I will use my clinical judgement." True, and useless to a family, because it tells them the rule is the therapist's discretion. Every subsequent decision will be experienced as arbitrary.

Why the good version works. It is specific about what the therapist will do (tell both that something has been said), it preserves the discloser's control over the content (help them decide how to say it), and it names the limit (the secret will not be carried). Three commitments, all of which can be kept, which is what distinguishes a policy from a warning.

The follow-up that tests whether it landed. "If your wife told me something on Tuesday that she did not want you to know, what do you think I would do?" A husband who can answer accurately has consented; one who cannot has heard the words and not the arrangement. The answer is worth recording, in his words.

2.5 Case: the family who signed and did not understand

The referral. The Baloguns — Femi, 55, Funke, 49, and Femi's mother, 81, with dementia — were referred eight months ago by a physician at the private hospital where Mama is seen. The original referral was for "carer stress". The family attended four sessions and stopped.

Why the case is in this week. The family has now returned, and has made a complaint: that the clinic "told the hospital things about us without asking". The complaint is being handled by the clinic manager, and the file has been sent to the therapist's supervisor.

What the file contains. A two-page consent form, signed by Femi in the first session. No date of explanation. No record that the limits of confidentiality were discussed. No record that supervision was mentioned. A note in session three: "spoke to Dr Adeyemi at the hospital re: Mrs Balogun's care — agreed to share summary."

What actually happened. In session three, the therapist telephoned the physician at the hospital to ask about Mama's medication, because Funke had said she was "sleeping all day". In the course of the call she described the household: that the daughter-in-law was exhausted, that there was a question about whether Mama could be left alone, that the family had "not been entirely honest with the physician about how much help they have". The physician recorded this in Mama's hospital file. A receptionist who knew the family read it and mentioned it to a relative. The family learned of it from the relative.

The analysis, through the seven steps.

Step 1 — The ethical question. Not "was the telephone call wrong?" but: what did this family consent to, what did the therapist owe the physician and the family respectively, and what does the clinic owe them now that a harm has occurred?

Step 2 — The facts. The consent form covered confidentiality in general terms. It did not name third-party consultation. The therapist believed she had "implied consent" because the family had asked her to liaise with the hospital. What the family had asked for was that she telephone about medication; they had not agreed to a description of the household. The disclosure reached a relative through a third party — the receptionist — which the therapist could not have foreseen and which she is nonetheless responsible for having made possible.

Step 3 — The parties. Mama: her clinical information was shared with her physician, which is defensible, and her family's private business was shared, which is not. Funke: the description of her exhaustion, and of the family's "not being honest", is now in a hospital file. Femi: he signed a form he did not read. The physician: he received information he did not ask for and recorded it. The relative: now holds information the family did not choose to give. The clinic: a complaint, and an exposure.

Step 4 — The frameworks. Deontology: the consent did not cover this disclosure, and the duty was to obtain it. Consequence: the therapist's goal — better medication for Mama — was legitimate and could have been achieved by asking about medication alone. Virtue: would she have made the call if the family were in the room? Care: the family's trust was the treatment's only asset, and it has been spent on a telephone call. Justice: the person most affected, Funke, was the person with least standing, and it is her description that is now circulating.

Step 5 — Consultation. The supervisor, the clinic manager, and — critically — the clinician's own professional indemnity provider, because a complaint has been made. This is not an admission of fault; it is the ordinary procedure.

Step 6 — Decision and action. Three things, in this order.

First, the apology and the account. The therapist meets the family — all three adults, including Mama, who is told in simple terms — and does four things: states what she did, without euphemism ("I telephoned the doctor about Mama's medication, and while I was on the telephone I described how things are at home, and I should not have done that"); states what she should have done (asked their permission first, and described only the medication); apologises without qualification; and states what she will do now (write to the physician asking that the household description be removed or annotated as not consented to, and confirm in writing to the family).

Second, the record. An incident note in the file: what happened, what was disclosed, to whom, the harm identified, the action taken, and the review date. Not a defence; an account.

Third, the practice change. The clinic's consent form now includes a named item: "I may need to speak to other professionals involved in your care. I will ask your permission before doing so, and I will tell you what I intend to say." The therapist's own practice changes in a way that is recorded in her supervision notes.

Step 7 — Review. At three months: has the letter to the physician been answered; is the annotation in place; has the family returned; and has the therapist made any further third-party calls without permission? The answer to the last question is the one that determines whether this was an incident or a pattern.

What the case teaches that the code does not. Four things.

  1. "Liaising with the hospital" is not consent to describe a household. Consent is specific. A family that asks you to discuss medication has consented to a discussion of medication, and the scope of consent is the scope of the request.
