  2. The disclosure you make is the disclosure that circulates. The therapist made one telephone call to one physician. The information reached a receptionist, a relative, and now a complaint. A clinician who thinks in terms of "one person" has not understood how information moves in a small community.
  3. The signature is the weakest part of the file. Had the therapist written four lines in session one — what she explained, in whose words it was repeated back, and what the family agreed — the complaint would still have been made and would have been answerable.
  4. The apology is a clinical act. The family returned after it, which they would not have done after a letter from a manager.

2.6 Transcript: the consent conversation that failed, and the one that worked

Two transcripts. The first is the original first session, reconstructed from the therapist's memory in supervision. The second is the repeat, eight months later, with the same family.

The original, in five turns.

T1 Therapist: Before we start, I have a form for you to sign. It is about confidentiality. — The disclosure of the document rather than of the arrangement. The family now believes the form is the consent. T2 Femi: (signs without reading) Where do I sign? T3 Therapist: There. (pause) So, tell me about your mother. — Two minutes and eleven seconds into a therapy that will last four sessions, and the four limits of confidentiality have not been mentioned. T4 Funke: She is not sleeping. She wanders at night. I am the one who — T5 Therapist: (interrupting) And does the doctor know about the wandering? — The question that leads, three sessions later, to the telephone call, and that shows the therapist was already thinking about the physician on day one. The failure was not a lapse; it was a pattern set in the first five minutes.

The repeat, eight months later, in nine turns.

T1 Therapist: Before we talk about anything, I want to tell you how I work, and I want to tell you what I got wrong last time. — The apology first. The family has come back in order to hear it, and everything else will be heard through it. T2 Femi: (nods) T3 Therapist: I telephoned your doctor about your mother's medication, which you had asked me to do. I also described how things are at home, which you had not asked me to do, and which I should have asked about first. I am sorry. — Four sentences: what she did, what was authorised, what was not, the apology. No excuse, and no "but". T4 Funke: (after a pause) It was the receptionist who told my sister. T5 Therapist: I did not know that. It makes it worse, and it is my responsibility, because I made the call. — The acknowledgement without defence. The temptation here is to explain the receptionist's confidentiality duty; that explanation would be accurate and would move the responsibility off the therapist and onto a stranger. T6 Femi: So what happens now? T7 Therapist: Three things. I have written to the doctor asking that the part about our home be removed from the file, and I will give you a copy of that letter. I have written in your file what happened, and you can see it. And I am going to explain to you now what I should have explained then, and then you can decide whether you want to go on with me. — The three-part remedy: repair the record, own the record, and re-offer consent. The third is the one that makes it a clinical act rather than a complaint process. T8 Therapist: (the full eight components, in plain language, over eleven minutes, with each adult asked to repeat back what they had understood) T9 Mama: (in Yoruba, through Funke) She says she wants to know who will know about her. — The question that the first conversation never made possible, asked by the person whose file it is.

2.6a The record of consent, written out

The single most useful paragraph a clinician will ever write, and the one that is almost never written. It goes at the end of the first session's note, takes ninety seconds, and looks like this.

"Consent explained orally, in English and Yoruba, to both parents and to the paternal aunt. Covered: the client is the couple, with the children seen within the family's treatment; no secrets policy, stated as a promise to tell both if a secret arises and to help the discloser decide how to say it; supervision (first names only, clinic supervisor, Dr O.); audio recording of sessions 1–4, to be deleted at six months, family may refuse without affecting treatment; fee ₦X per session, 24 hours' notice for cancellation; limits of confidentiality — risk to self, risk to others, child protection, court order — given with examples. Mother repeated back: 'if I tell you my husband is hitting me you will not tell him without asking me first' — confirmed, with the exception that if children are at risk the report is made regardless and she will be told. Father repeated back the supervision arrangement. Both agreed. Written summary in English provided."

Why each element is there. The language (proves it was accessible), the audience (proves who was informed), the content (proves what was covered), the teach-back (proves understanding rather than transmission), the correction in the mother's restatement (proves the clinician did not accept a misunderstanding), and the written summary (proves something continues after the session).

What the paragraph is not. It is not a defence prepared for a tribunal. It is the clinician's own record of an agreement she intends to keep, and its practical function is that she can reread it in session six, when she has forgotten what she promised.

The habit worth forming. Write it in the session, before the family leaves, and read it back to them: "this is what I am writing down about what we agreed — is it right?" Two sentences of confirmation, and the consent is real.

2.7 Laboratory: drafting the consent document

Aim. To produce a one-page informed-consent document for couple therapy that a family with limited literacy could actually use, and to defend every sentence in it.

Procedure. Students draft the document in forty minutes against the eight components. They then exchange with a partner who plays a family member reading it for the first time, and who must answer three questions from the document alone: who is your client? who would be told if I said I was afraid of my husband? and what happens if I miss a session? If the document cannot answer all three, it is rewritten in the session. The last twenty minutes is a plenary in which two documents are read aloud and the class marks them.

Marking rubric (20 marks).

Criterion0–12–34–5
The eight componentsFour or fewer presentSix or seven presentAll eight, in plain language, with the exceptions given as examples
Who the client isUnclear or absentNamed, but the implications are notNamed, with what it means for confidentiality and for the individual sessions
Plain languageClinical vocabulary, long sentencesMostly plain, with lapsesReadable by a twelve-year-old; no sentence over twenty words; the three test questions answerable
The secrets policyAbsentStated as a ruleStated as a rule and as a promise about how it will be applied
The record of consentSignature onlyA note that consent was explainedA note recording what was explained, what the family said back, and who witnessed it

The distinction that carries most of the marks. The top band requires the document to state the secrets policy as a promise about conduct — "if this happens, I will do this" — rather than as a rule. A rule tells the family what is forbidden; a promise tells them what the clinician will do. Families comply with promises and litigate rules.

The three sentences that fail most often. "Confidentiality will be maintained in accordance with professional standards" (means nothing to a family). "Information may be shared with relevant third parties where clinically indicated" (a licence to do what the Balogun therapist did). And "the therapist reserves the right to breach confidentiality in exceptional circumstances" (a threat with no content). All three are rewritten in the laboratory, and the rewrites are the most useful pages the students take away.

2.8 Six errors

Error 1 — Treating the signature as the consent. It is evidence of a process, not the process. A file with a signature and no note of what was explained is a file that will not survive a complaint.

Error 2 — Disclosing the document instead of the arrangement. Handing over a form and saying "it is about confidentiality" is not a consent conversation. The family learns what they need by being told, in speech, with examples, and being asked to say it back.

Error 3 — Leaving the limits until they are needed. A family that learns about the reporting duty after a disclosure has been made to them has been ambushed. The limits are disclosed at the start, and again as the case requires.

Error 4 — Not mentioning supervision and recording. The most commonly breached obligation in training clinics, and the one most easily fixed. "I take my work to a supervisor" is a sentence, not a form.

Error 5 — Assuming one parent's consent covers the household. Custody arrangements, guardianship, and the standing of a grandmother who is raising a child all affect who can consent. Ask, record the answer, and where it is unclear, obtain both.

Error 6 — Writing the consent note from memory. The note must be written in the session, in the family's words, and dated. A note reconstructed a week later is worth very little, and a tribunal will notice the distinction.

2.9 Culture, faith and consent

Consent is not always an individual act. In many of the households this book is written for, a decision to enter therapy is made by a family, and the person who signs is the person with standing rather than the person most affected. A clinician who insists on individual consent from each adult will sometimes be told what they want to hear and will not have consent in any meaningful sense. The workable procedure is to obtain the signature from the person with standing and to speak directly to each adult, including the grandmother and the adolescent, asking each whether they agree. The record then says both things.

The head of household and the wife's separate consent. A woman may be in treatment because her husband arranged it, and may not feel able to refuse. This is a consent problem, and its signs are the woman who says nothing in the first session and looks at her husband before answering. The clinical response is not to refuse treatment; it is to see her alone once, early, and ask whether she wants to be here. The answer is the consent.

Faith, authority and the referral route. Where a family has come because a pastor or an imam advised it, the authority in the room is partly elsewhere. The consent conversation should establish who else will be told about the treatment, because in these households the answer is frequently the religious community, and a family that assumes the therapist will brief the pastor and discovers otherwise will feel exposed.

Language and the interpreter. A consent conversation conducted through an interpreter is valid, provided the interpreter is briefed (Week 13 of MFT 703) and the family member is not used. A husband interpreting for his wife in a consent conversation about confidentiality is a contradiction in terms.

DEBATE — "A family that cannot read the consent form cannot give informed consent, and treatment should not begin." Argue for: consent requires understanding, a document is the standard evidence of understanding, and proceeding without it exposes the family to treatment they did not agree to. Argue against: literacy is not capacity, oral consent with a witness is a recognised and lawful route, and refusing treatment on the grounds of literacy denies the service to the people who need it most. Then write the clause you would put in your own consent policy to cover the oral route, and say what evidence you would keep.

2.10 Evidence note

What the research supports. The relationship between documentation quality and disciplinary outcomes is among the most consistent findings in professional regulation: inadequate records appear in a large minority of substantiated complaints, and in many the record failure is the finding rather than the clinical act. Studies of informed consent consistently find that recall of consent information is poor — typically less than half of what was explained is remembered a week later, and much less where the information is complex or the person is distressed — which supports the use of plain language, written summaries and teach-back methods. Teach-back, in which the client repeats the explanation in their own words, reliably improves comprehension.

What is weaker. The evidence on whether better consent processes improve clinical outcomes is thin. Consent documentation improves defensibility, and it plausibly improves the therapeutic alliance, but the trials are few and the outcome measures are satisfaction rather than change.

What is not supported. That a signature is sufficient. That longer forms produce better consent — the evidence points the other way, with comprehension falling as length rises. And that the limits of confidentiality deter families from disclosing — the finding is the reverse, that clear disclosure increases trust, which is why the sequence in 2.4 puts the limits after the clinician has established the relationship rather than before.

The matrix cell. Development is probabilistic and non-linear; the same input produces different outcomes depending on timing and context. The same consent form, explained to one family in a first session and posted to another with an appointment letter, produces consent in one case and a complaint in the other.

2.11 Key terms

Code of ethics — a professional body's enforceable statement of minimum standards.

Informed consent — agreement to treatment given by a person who understands what is proposed, its risks, its alternatives and its limits, and who is free to refuse.

Assent — a child's or young person's agreement to treatment, given in language they understand, where the legal consent rests with a parent or guardian.

Capacity — the ability to understand and weigh information relevant to a decision; distinct from age and from literacy.

Mature minor — a young person judged able to consent to treatment without a parent, under some jurisdictions' law.

Teach-back — asking the client to restate the explanation in their own words, as a check on comprehension.

Therapeutic secret — information held by the therapist from one member of a couple or family, known to the other members not to be shared.

Disclosure — the giving of information; not the same as consent, which requires understanding and agreement.

Witness — a person present at an oral consent conversation who can later attest to what was said.

Scope of consent — the limits of what was agreed; consent to discuss medication is not consent to describe a household.

2.12 Self-test

  1. Name the ten core obligations and the two that generate most complaints.
  2. Why is the signature the weakest part of the consent file?
  3. Give the eight components of informed consent for couple therapy.
  4. What is the difference between consent and assent, and why does the distinction matter clinically?
  5. In the Balogun case, what exactly did the family consent to, and what was disclosed beyond it?
  6. What are the four limitations of confidentiality, and how should they be framed to a family?
  7. What is teach-back, and why does it matter more than the form?
  8. What would you write in the record to evidence that consent happened?

Answers. (1) Competence; informed consent; confidentiality; multiple relationships; sexual intimacy; fees; advertising; research ethics; cultural competence and non-discrimination; legal compliance — the two that generate most complaints are inadequate records and failure to document consent. (2) Because it evidences a transaction rather than an understanding; a family can sign a document they cannot read and have consented to nothing, and a tribunal will say so. (3) Who the client is; who is seen and in what combination; the confidentiality and secrets policy; recording and supervision; fees and cancellation; the limits of confidentiality; the complaints route; the right to withdraw. (4) Consent is the legal authorisation, held by an adult or a guardian; assent is the young person's agreement, which is not legally required and is clinically decisive, because a child who did not assent withdraws by the third session. (5) Consent was to discussion of Mama's medication with her physician; the disclosure included the daughter-in-law's exhaustion, the family's handling of care, and a judgement that they had not been honest — none of which was within the scope. (6) Risk to self, risk to others, abuse of a child or vulnerable adult, and a court order — framed as promises about what the clinician will do ("I will tell you before I tell anyone"), with examples rather than abstractions. (7) Asking the client to restate the explanation in their own words; it matters because recall of consent information is poor and because the client's words, recorded, are the evidence of understanding. (8) What was explained; in what language and by whom; what the family said back; who witnessed it; the date; and what was agreed.

2.13 Reflective task

Find a consent form — any clinic's, any profession's — and read it as though you were a frightened parent with two children and no time. Then answer three questions: what does it tell you that you need to know? what does it fail to tell you? and what would you have to ask, out loud, to understand what will happen to your information? Most students find that the document answers the clinic's questions and not the family's, which is the difference between a release and a consent.

2.14 Further reading

  • AAMFT Code of Ethics — read the current version, and the interpretive statements, not a summary.
  • APA Ethical Principles of Psychologists and Code of Conduct — for comparison on the three issues where the codes disagree.
  • Beauchamp, T. and Childress, J. on informed consent — the analytical account of the eight components.
  • Schenker, Y. et al. on teach-back — the evidence on comprehension and recall.
  • Gutheil, T. and Gabbard, G. on boundary violations — the documentation chapter, which is the most useful practical text in this area.

Next: Week 3 — Confidentiality in the multiperson system.

Week 3 — Confidentiality in the multiperson system

There is no single confidentiality in family work; there is a policy you negotiate.

In individual therapy confidentiality is a wall with four doors. In family therapy it is a room with several people in it, each of whom has a different claim on the information, and the therapist's task is not to defend a wall but to design and run a room. That is a different skill, and the codes — written for the wall — describe it badly.

Three workable models.

ModelRuleAdvantagesRisks
No-secretsAnything told individually may be shared with the coupleSimple, transparentIndividual may withhold important material
Full secrecyIndividual disclosures are kept privateProtects individual therapyCreates a therapeutic secret that distorts the couple work
Selective (negotiated)The therapist and client agree in advance what will be sharedFlexible and realisticRequires skill and documentation

Most experienced couple therapists operate a modified no-secrets policy: they will not keep a secret that materially affects the couple's work, and they tell each partner this before the first individual session. This protects both the relationship and the therapist.

The distinction that makes the policy workable: privacy is not secrecy. Privacy is information a person chooses not to share, and it is legitimate — a client's history of sexual abuse, her feelings about her mother-in-law, what she earns. Secrecy is information that is being kept from someone in a way that distorts the treatment: an affair in progress, a plan to leave, a diagnosis that the other partner believes has been excluded. A policy that cannot distinguish the two will be experienced by the family as a demand for total exposure, and they will keep everything.

Why the therapist cannot be neutral about a therapeutic secret. A secret is not a piece of information stored; it is a structural feature of the therapy. Once the therapist knows something the other party does not, every intervention is affected: she cannot ask a question that would lead there, she cannot respond naturally to an account given in good faith, and she is, in the technical sense, deceiving one client on another's behalf. A therapy in that condition is not couple therapy; it is two individual therapies conducted in the same room.

Documentation. Record the policy in the file, in the family's words if possible, and note the date it was explained. If a secret arises, record what you did and why.

— During an individual session, a wife discloses that she has been having an affair for two years and has no intention of ending it or telling her husband. No one is at physical risk. Under a no-secrets policy, you tell her at the outset of the individual session that this cannot stay private if the couple work is to continue; you then help her decide how and when to disclose, with a deadline. Under a selective policy, you would negotiate what can be shared. What you must not do is carry the secret silently for months while conducting "couple therapy".

3.1 The lecture in ninety minutes

MinutesSegmentWhat the lecturer doesWhat students do
0–8The wall and the roomWhy individual confidentiality does not transfer to family workDraw the information map of a family they know
8–20The three modelsEach model, its logic, its failure modeChoose a model for four different referral types, with reasons
20–32Privacy against secrecyThe distinction, worked on eight examplesSort eight disclosures into privacy or secrecy
32–44The modified no-secrets policyThe four parts of the sentence, and how to say itRehearse the sentence in pairs, twice each
44–56Who else holds informationSchools, courts, employers, insurers, religious bodiesMap the information flow for a school referral
56–68The individual session inside family workWhy it is offered, how it is structured, how it endsScript the first two minutes of an individual session
68–80When a secret has to moveThe five-step process, with the affair caseRun the process in role, with a resistant discloser
80–90DocumentationWhat goes in the file, and what does notWrite the note for the case

The teaching decision that makes the week work. The week turns on one demonstration: the lecturer places three chairs, sits in one, and plays the therapist who has just been told about the affair. She then conducts twenty minutes of ordinary couple therapy — asking the husband about his week, responding to the wife's account of a disagreement — while the class watches the impossibility accumulate. The exercise is uncomfortable and it is unforgettable: by the end, every student has seen that the therapist is lying, not by commission but by the shape of her questions.

The sentence the lecture must land. "A secret does not sit in a drawer. It changes every question you can ask, and after a month it has changed the therapy."

3.2 Five concepts that organise the week

1. The client is a construction, not a fact. In family work "the client" is whatever the therapist and the family have agreed it is, and that agreement determines who holds the confidentiality. A couple can be the client, with each partner a participant; a family can be the client, with the children participants; an individual can be the client, with the family seen as context. Each construction is legitimate; none is neutral; and the therapist must choose one and say so.

2. Confidentiality is owed to the system, not distributed among its members. If the couple is the client, the confidentiality is held by the couple jointly, which means neither partner can demand it against the other. This is the sentence that resolves most confusion: "I cannot keep something from the client, and the client is both of you."

3. Privacy is a legitimate asymmetry. Members of a family know different things about each other, and that is ordinary. A therapist is not obliged to equalise knowledge, and a policy that requires it is not a confidentiality policy but a transparency rule, which is a different instrument with different costs.

4. Secrets distort, and distortion is measurable. The practical test of whether a secret is a therapeutic secret is whether it changes what the therapist does. If she has stopped asking about the weekends, or begun to steer away from a topic, or finds herself relieved when a session ends early, the secret is already operating and the policy has failed.

5. Institutions are third parties with their own claims. A school, a court, an employer, an insurer, a child protection agency and a religious body all have information claims, and each has a different legal standing. The therapist's obligation is to map them at the start, tell the family who they are, and never to discover one in the middle of a case.

3.2a The modified no-secrets policy, in four parts

The policy is a sentence with four components, and each component prevents a specific failure.

Part 1 — The promise of individual sessions. "Either of you may ask to see me alone, and I will usually say yes." Without this, the family believes there are no individual sessions, and the request, when it comes, is experienced as a breach.

Part 2 — The limit. "But I will not keep a secret that affects this relationship." The limit is stated in terms of effect, not of content, because content cannot be predicted and effect can be judged.

Part 3 — What the therapist will do. "If something like that comes up, I will tell you both that something has been said — not what — and I will help whoever it is to decide how and when to say it." The distinction between telling that something was said and telling what was said is the mechanism that makes the policy usable.

Part 4 — The deadline. "And I will ask for it to be said here, within a stated time, rather than leaving it open." A secret with no deadline is a secret; a secret with a deadline is a task. The deadline is negotiated but not indefinite, and the therapist writes it down.

The four failure modes the parts prevent. Without part 1, the request for an individual session feels like a rupture. Without part 2, the therapist carries secrets by accident. Without part 3, the disclosure of that something was said becomes a guessing game that is worse than the secret. Without part 4, the process has no end and the family settles into a stable, comfortable dishonesty.

3.3 The method: deciding where a disclosure goes

Six questions, asked in order, whenever a family member tells the therapist something in confidence. The method takes about five minutes of thinking and it converts an alarm into a decision.

Question 1 — Who is the client, as agreed? The answer determines everything else. If the couple is the client, the disclosure belongs to the couple and cannot be kept from one member indefinitely. If the individual is the client and the family is context, the disclosure is hers to control.

Question 2 — Is anyone at risk? A yes takes the case out of this method and into Week 8 or 9. Risk is always the first question, because the confidentiality policy does not survive it and the family was told so at the start.

Question 3 — Is this privacy or secrecy? Test it with one question: does knowing this change what I do in the joint sessions? If yes, it is secrecy, whatever its subject. If no, it is privacy and it stays.

Question 4 — Who else's information is in it? Most disclosures are not about one person. An affair involves a third party; a diagnosis involves a family's history; a plan to leave involves children. The therapist is not only holding the confider's information, and the other people in it have claims.

Question 5 — What happens if it moves, and when? Who will be told, by whom, in what words, with whom present, and on what date. The therapist's job is to make this a plan rather than an event.

Question 6 — What is my part, and what is not? The therapist does not disclose the secret; the client does. The therapist's role is to insist that it moves, to help plan how, to be present, and to manage the session afterwards. A therapist who takes on the disclosure has taken the responsibility for it, and the client has been relieved of the only act that could repair the relationship.

The rule that governs the six. The therapist never lies. She may decline to answer, and she must say that she is declining and why: "I am not going to answer that, and I want to tell you that I am not, rather than let you think I have." The distinction between a refusal and a lie is the whole of the professional position.

3.3a The individual session inside family work

An individual session within a couple or family treatment is a clinical instrument with a purpose, a structure, and an end. Used badly, it is the mechanism by which therapy is destroyed.

Why offer one.

  • To assess risk in private, where a partner's presence would make disclosure impossible.
  • To hear one person's account of something they cannot say in front of the other — usually fear, shame, or the wish to leave.
  • To prepare a person to say something in the joint session, which is the most common and most useful reason.
  • To assess for coercive control, which cannot be assessed with the controller present.

The rule that governs the offer. Never hold an individual session with one member of a couple or family without having stated the policy, in front of both, before the first one. The session is not the problem; the undisclosed session is.

The structure, in five minutes and forty. First two minutes: restate the policy — "you know I will not keep a secret that affects the relationship; is there anything you want to say in that knowledge?" Middle thirty: listen, assess, and do not ask leading questions about the other partner's conduct. Last eight: agree what, if anything, moves, and how. The session does not end with a half-disclosure and a reassurance.

The sentence that ends it well. "So: what I understand is that you want to leave, and you have not told him, and you are not ready to. I am not going to tell him. I am going to ask you to tell him, here, within two weeks, and I will help you plan what you say. If you have not told him in two weeks, I will tell him only that something has been said and that he should ask you — and I will tell you first that I am doing it."

What makes an individual session unsafe. Three things. It is offered without the policy being stated. It becomes the only place where anything true is said, which is the classic pattern of a couple therapy that has quietly become two individual therapies. Or it is used to gather information about the other partner, which converts the therapist into an investigator.

3.4 Confidentiality and the institutions around the family

A family does not arrive alone. The information map below is drawn on the whiteboard in the first session of any case with an institutional referrer, and it frequently changes the treatment.

The school. Holds attendance, behaviour, academic and safeguarding records. Has a legitimate interest in the child's functioning, no right to the therapy's content, and a tendency to expect a report. The therapist's position: the school may be given what the family agrees to, and where the school is the referrer it must be told at the outset what it will and will not receive.

The court. Has power to compel records and testimony, and is the only party on the list that can override the family's wishes entirely. (Week 11.)

The employer, the insurer, the employee assistance programme. Frequently the payer, and frequently the party with the weakest claim and the strongest expectation. The consent conversation must establish what the payer will be told: usually attendance and nothing else.

The child protection agency. Has statutory powers that vary by jurisdiction and frequently override confidentiality. The family must be told, at the start, in plain language, what the therapist is obliged to report and to whom.

The religious body. Rarely has a legal claim and frequently has a social one. Where a pastor or imam referred the family, or is supporting them, the question of what the therapist will tell the religious authority must be settled early, because families in these households often assume the therapist will brief the clergy and may be distressed to discover otherwise — or, worse, may assume confidentiality and discover the briefing.

The extended family. The most consequential party in many of these cases and the least considered. The grandmother, the uncle who pays, the aunt who is a nurse: each may be told something by a family member and each may act on it. The therapist cannot control this and should not pretend to; the workable sentence is "what is said in this room may be repeated outside it by any of us, and I cannot prevent that. What I can control is what I say."

The rule for the map. Draw it in the first session, name every party, and ask the family who has a claim in their view. The map is then recorded, and every subsequent request for information is answered by reference to it rather than improvised.

3.4a Four scenarios at the boundary

The categories are easy in the abstract and hard at four in the afternoon. The four scenarios below are the ones that most often divide a supervision group.

Scenario one — the past affair. A husband discloses, in an individual session, an affair that ended six years ago, before the couple's second child was born. He is not going to tell his wife and says it is "over and irrelevant". Analysis: secrecy, not privacy, because it changes what the therapist does — she can no longer explore the wife's account of the last six years without knowing that the husband holds a different history. The end of the affair does not end the secret's effect; it only removes one of the reasons for urgency. The policy applies, the deadline is set, and the therapist is honest that the disclosure may be worse for the marriage than the affair was.

Scenario two — the diagnosis. A wife discloses a new diagnosis of a chronic illness, and asks that her husband not be told yet. Analysis: this is closer to privacy, and the test settles it — the opposite: knowing this does change what the therapist does, but the change is in her attention rather than her honesty, and the question of who knows the diagnosis belongs, first, to the patient. The exception is where the illness bears directly on the treatment's subject matter, as it would if the couple presented with the husband's complaint that his wife has "lost interest". Then the content of the illness can remain private and its effect must be speakable: "there is something medical going on that she is dealing with, and it is relevant to what you are describing; she will tell you when she is ready." That is not a lie and it is not a disclosure.

Scenario three — the wished-for pregnancy. A woman in a couple treatment discloses that she has stopped her contraception without telling her husband. Analysis: the clearest kind of secrecy, because the treatment is about the couple's reproductive decisions and the therapist would be participating in a deception about their subject. It is also a case where the therapist's own views about reproductive autonomy will be activated, and the professional requirement is to state the limit without moralising about the choice.

Scenario four — the adult child's secret. A mother in a family treatment discloses that her 24-year-old son has been using drugs, and asks the therapist not to raise it with the son, who is also attending. Analysis: the son is an adult and a client; his mother's disclosure is information about him that he has not given. The therapist cannot promise the mother silence, because she is now conducting a family treatment in which she knows something the son does not know she knows — and she cannot disclose it to him either, because the mother told her. The workable resolution is the mother's own act: "I need you to tell him that you know, and I will help you do it, because I cannot work with the two of you while he thinks this is not being spoken about."

The pattern in all four. The answer is rarely "tell" or "do not tell". It is who tells, in what words, by when, with the therapist doing what.

3.4b The supervisor and the secret

A therapist holding a secret needs supervision and frequently avoids it, because the secret feels like a failure and because describing it means saying it aloud. This is the mechanism by which secrets damage therapists: the case that most needs discussing is the one least discussed.

The rule. Any case in which the therapist is holding information from a client — even legitimately, even temporarily — is presented in supervision within the month.

What supervision is for, in these cases. Not permission, which supervision cannot give. Three things: (1) a check on whether the therapist's reasoning is clinical or personal, since the wish to protect a client from the consequences of disclosure is often the therapist's own; (2) an outside view on the risk analysis, which deteriorates under pressure; (3) the documentation, because a supervisor who has read the reasoning becomes the strongest evidence that the decision was considered.

What the supervisor should not do. Instruct the therapist to disclose. The decision and the responsibility belong to the treating clinician, and a supervisor who directs it removes the judgement from the person who will have to defend it.

The audit question for the therapist. At the end of each month: is there anything I know that one of my clients does not know I know? If the answer is yes and there is no written plan with a date, the case needs supervision this week, not next.

3.5 Case: two years, no intention

Referral. Mrs Nkiru Adeleke, 36, a bank branch operations manager, telephones about her marriage. "We have not spoken properly in three years. I want to know whether it can be saved." Her husband, Segun, 39, a pharmacist, attends the first session reluctantly: "she decided we needed this, so here we are."

The household. Nkiru and Segun, married eleven years. Two children, 8 and 5. Segun's mother lives ten minutes away and is "very involved". Nkiru travels for work two or three nights a month.

The first session. The presenting complaint is "we are like flatmates". Segun's example: "she is on her phone until midnight". Nkiru's example: "he asks me about the children and never about my day." There is no violence, no substance misuse, no financial crisis. The therapist states the modified no-secrets policy, in the four parts, in the first session, and both agree. The note records it.

Session two — the individual request. Nkiru telephones: "I need to see you alone." The therapist offers the next appointment, and sees her for forty minutes. In the first eight minutes, Nkiru says: "I have been seeing someone for two years. He is a colleague. I am not going to stop and I am not going to tell Segun. I want to know whether I can save my marriage at the same time."

The application of the six questions.

1. Who is the client? The couple. The policy was stated to both and agreed by both. Nkiru was told, in Segun's presence, that a secret affecting the relationship would not be kept.

2. Is anyone at risk? No physical risk. There is a risk of an STI to Segun, which is a health risk and not a safety risk, and it is relevant — a therapist who treats this as a purely relational matter has missed something. There is a risk to the children's stability, which is a future harm rather than a current danger.

3. Privacy or secrecy? Secrecy, unambiguously. The test — does knowing this change what I do in the joint sessions? — is answered in one word: yes. Every question about the weekends, about her travel, about her withdrawal becomes unaskable.

4. Whose information is it? Nkiru's, Segun's, the children's, and the third party's — who has a wife of his own, which Nkiru mentions in passing and which the therapist does not pursue. The information is not one person's property.

5. What happens if it moves, and when? The plan has to be made now, in this session, or the policy is decoration.

6. What is my part? To insist that it moves, to help plan how, to be present, and not to disclose it.

The intervention, in the session, in sequence.

First, the reminder, not as a threat but as a fact. "When we started, I told you both that I would not keep a secret that affects the marriage. I meant it then and I mean it now. So I am going to say what I can and cannot do, and then we will decide what happens."

Second, the question that establishes the delay. "When did you decide that you were not going to tell him?" The answer is "about a year ago". The therapist names it: "so for a year you have known, and I have known for ten minutes. That is the difference I have to work with."

Third, the refusal to collude, stated as a limit on the therapist rather than on the client. "I am not able to run a couple therapy in which I know something of this size and your husband does not. Not because of a rule — because I would be lying to him every week, and he would be paying me to do it."

Fourth, the three options, laid out plainly. (a) She tells Segun, with the therapist's help, in a session, by a stated date. (b) She ends the couple therapy here, and the therapist explains to Segun that the couple work cannot continue — without giving a reason — which is honest and leaves Segun with a mystery. (c) The therapist tells Segun that something has been disclosed which he needs to discuss with his wife, and Nkiru tells him the content herself. Option (b) is included deliberately: a client who is not ready must have an exit that is not a lie.

Fifth, the deadline and the recording. Nkiru chooses (a), with a date three weeks out and one session in between to plan the words. The therapist writes it in the note: "client disclosed an ongoing relationship of two years; reminded of the policy agreed at session one; client agreed to disclose to her husband by [date], with therapist support; if not disclosed by that date, therapist will inform husband that a disclosure has been made and that he should ask his wife."

Sixth, the health question, which is separate and cannot wait three weeks. "There is one thing I am going to ask you to do this week, and it is not about your marriage. Get a sexual health check, and tell me you have done it. If you will not tell him, you still cannot leave him exposed." Nkiru agrees, and does it, and reports at the next session.

The disclosure session, in outline. Nkiru tells Segun in the fourth session, in the therapist's room, in eleven sentences that she had rehearsed with the therapist in the third. Segun's response is not what anyone predicted: he is not surprised. He says, "I knew something. I thought it was my fault." The session that follows is not about the affair; it is about a three-year silence in which both of them were protecting the other from a truth they each half-knew.

What the case teaches.

  1. The policy must be stated before it is needed. Nkiru's decision to disclose was made in a session in which the alternative was clearly worse — ending the therapy — and that alternative existed only because the policy had been agreed in session one.
  2. A deadline converts a secret into a task. Without a date, the therapist would have carried it for months, and the therapy would have become the deception the policy exists to prevent.
  3. The health question is not part of the confidentiality decision and must not be lost in it. A therapist absorbed in the ethics of disclosure can forget that a third party is being exposed to an infection.
  4. The predicted catastrophe is frequently not the actual one. The disclosure did not end the marriage; it ended a silence. That outcome is not guaranteed and it is common, and a therapist who assumes the worst will delay the disclosure in order to protect a relationship that is already being damaged by the delay.

3.5a When the client refuses the deadline

A real and frequent outcome, and the case in which the therapist's policy is actually tested.

What refusal looks like. "I am not going to tell him, and I am not going to stop seeing you. You said you would help me, and now you are threatening me." The accusation of betrayal is genuine and it is also the pressure that most therapists find hardest to withstand.

What the therapist says. Four sentences, in this order. "I am not threatening you; I am telling you what I can and cannot do, which is the same thing I told you on the first day." "I will not tell him what you have told me — that has not changed." "What I will do, on the date we agreed, is tell him that something has been disclosed that he needs to raise with you." "And I will keep working with you, either way, because you are not being abandoned for telling me the truth."

Why the fourth sentence matters more than the other three. The client's fear is that honesty will cost her the therapist. If it does, the lesson she takes is that honesty is dangerous — which is precisely the lesson that produced the affair's secrecy in the first place.

What if the client ends the therapy instead? Then the therapist writes to confirm the ending, offers to meet the husband to explain that the couple work has stopped without explaining why, and records the reasoning in full. The letter matters: it is the evidence that the decision was the client's, and it is the only document that will exist if the husband later complains.

The rule underneath all of this. The therapist's obligation is not to the secret and not to the marriage. It is to the honesty of the treatment she is conducting. A treatment she cannot conduct honestly is one she must not continue, and saying so is the professional act.

3.6 Transcript: the individual session, minutes seven to twenty

Fifteen turns from the session in the case, compressed. The annotation marks each move against the six questions.

T1 Nkiru: I have been seeing someone for two years. He is a colleague. I am not going to stop and I am not going to tell Segun. — Three facts and a decision, delivered quickly, which is the manner of someone who has rehearsed the opening and intends to control the frame. T2 Therapist: (pause) Thank you for telling me. (pause) You know what I said on the first day, in front of Segun, about secrets. — No shock, no reassurance, and an immediate return to the agreement. The pause before "thank you" is deliberate: it acknowledges the disclosure without approving of it. T3 Nkiru: I know. But you cannot tell him. It is my life. T4 Therapist: It is your life. And it is also my therapy, and I cannot run it with this in it. — The move that keeps the conflict where it belongs. The therapist does not argue about the ethics of the affair; she states a limit on her own practice, which is not negotiable by the client's view of marriage. T5 Nkiru: So you are going to tell him. T6 Therapist: No. I am not going to tell him what you have told me, and I am not going to tell him anything at all today. — The distinction that the client has not yet heard: the therapist's limit is on carrying the secret, not on disclosing it. T7 Nkiru: Then what? T8 Therapist: Then we have a choice to make, and I want you to make it rather than me. Three options. (lays them out) — Options rather than a demand. The client is being given control of the manner, which is the only thing she can be given control of, the fact having been established. T9 Nkiru: (long pause) If I tell him, I lose everything. T10 Therapist: You may. I do not know. What I know is that if you do not, you have already lost something, and it has been going on for a year. — The honest answer rather than the comforting one. Note that the therapist says "I do not know", which buys the credibility for the sentence that follows. T11 Nkiru: (crying) I did not plan this. It started because I could not talk to him. He is not a bad man. T12 Therapist: (after a pause) I believe you. And that is the thing we have to work out — not whether he is a bad man, but what happened in this marriage that made it impossible for you to say what you needed. (pause) But not today. Today is about whether you can tell him the truth, because nothing else can be worked out until you can. — The reframe: the affair is the presenting event and the marriage is the subject. The therapist promises the second conversation and does not have it now, which is what makes the promise credible. T13 Nkiru: (pause) If I do it, will you be there? T14 Therapist: Yes. In this room, with me in it, and after that we will do the hardest session of your life and I will still be here in the following week. — The commitment, which is the thing that makes the disclosure survivable, and which is not a professional obligation and is the whole of the therapeutic relationship in one sentence. T15 Nkiru: (after a long pause) Give me three weeks.

What the transcript shows. Three things that a policy document cannot teach. First, the therapist never moralises about the affair, which keeps the client in the room. Second, she refuses twice with the same sentence, and the repetition matters — a limit stated once is heard as an opening position. Third, she ends the session on the client's own deadline, which means the client leaves with an obligation she set rather than one imposed.

3.7 Laboratory: the secrets protocol

Aim. To design, in writing, a confidentiality and secrets protocol for a named service, and to defend its four parts under pressure from a client who is testing it.

Procedure. Groups of three: therapist, client, observer. Each group is given a service description (a school counselling service; a private couple practice; a church-based clinic; a hospital family therapy unit) and must produce a one-page protocol in thirty minutes. The client role is then briefed to test the protocol in three ways — by asking for an individual session before the policy has been stated, by disclosing a secret and refusing the deadline, and by asking the therapist to lie to a third party. Twenty minutes of role-play. The observer marks the therapist's adherence to the protocol the group itself wrote, which is the point of the exercise: most protocols are unwritable as written.

Marking rubric (20 marks).

Criterion0–12–34–5
The four partsTwo or fewer presentThree presentAll four, specific, with the action the therapist will take at each point
Privacy against secrecyBoth treated the sameDistinguished, but the test is not statedDistinguished with the operational test, and examples from practice
The institutionsNot addressedNamedMapped, with what each party may be told and who decides
Under pressureAbandoned the protocol when testedHeld it, but arguedHeld it, stated the limit as a limit on the therapist, and offered the client control of the manner
DocumentationNoneA note existsThe note records what was said, the agreement, the date, and the contingency

The distinction that carries most of the marks. The top band requires the student to hold the protocol without moralising. Groups routinely win the argument and lose the client: a therapist who says "you are being dishonest" has stated a true thing and has ended the possibility of the disclosure.

3.8 Six errors

Error 1 — Stating the policy after the secret. The policy is worth nothing once the secret exists, because the client reasonably hears a change of rules. It is stated in session one, in front of everyone, and recorded.

Error 2 — Confusing privacy with secrecy and demanding total transparency. A policy that requires the disclosure of everything produces a family who disclose nothing. The two categories must be distinguished in the explanation, with examples from ordinary life.

Error 3 — Carrying the secret "for now". The phrase that has ended more couple therapies than any single act. There is no "for now"; there is a date, or there is a lie.

Error 4 — Telling the client's secret to protect the client from the consequences. The therapist who telephones the husband "for her own good" has broken a confidence and taken away the only act that could have repaired the relationship.

Error 5 — Refusing the individual session altogether. A therapist who will not see one partner alone cannot assess risk, cannot prepare a disclosure, and cannot hear the thing that is most true. The individual session is not the danger; the unstated one is.

Error 6 — Forgetting the third parties inside the secret. An affair involves another household; a diagnosis involves a family history; a plan to leave involves children. A confidentiality analysis that counts only the people in the room has missed most of the people in the case.

3.9 Culture, faith and the multiperson system

Confidentiality is a Western construct in some of these households. The idea that an individual holds information that a spouse has no right to is not universally shared, and in many of the families this book is written for the operative assumption is that a married couple has one information economy. A therapist who announces an individual confidentiality without explanation will be heard as introducing a foreign and suspicious practice — "why would you need to speak to my wife alone?"

The translation that works. Two sentences. First, the practical one: "I see each of you alone once, because there are things people cannot say in front of a husband or a wife, and I need to know them to help." Second, the limit: "anything that affects the marriage, I will not keep." The family that understands why the individual session exists agrees to it far more often than one that has been told only that it is a right.

The elder and the family's information. Where a grandmother or an uncle holds authority in the household, they may expect to be told what happens in sessions, and their expectation may be shared by the family. The workable position is not refusal but specification: "I will tell you what I am doing and whether it is working. I will not tell you what is said in the room." Most elders accept this, because it gives them the thing they actually want, which is to know that something is being done.

Faith communities and the circulation of information. A pastor who refers a family will frequently expect a report, and the family may expect the pastor to receive one. Both expectations must be addressed in the first session, and the answer recorded. Where a religious authority is part of the family's support, the therapist may offer a summary with the family's written agreement, which is a legitimate arrangement and is almost never offered.

Shame and the disclosure of family business. In a shame-oriented community, a disclosure inside the family may become a disclosure in the community, and the family knows this better than the therapist does. That is a reason to plan the disclosure carefully and is not a reason to avoid it — and it is a reason to ask, before any disclosure is planned, who outside the household will come to know.

DEBATE — "In couple therapy, the therapist should never hold information from either partner." Argue for: the client is the couple, deception is incompatible with therapy, and a therapist who holds a secret is no longer working with a couple. Argue against: privacy is a condition of individual disclosure, a strict no-secrets rule drives the most important material underground, and a woman who cannot speak freely about her own fear will not disclose it at all. Then state the policy you would actually operate, and the case that would make you change it.

3.9a A record of the decision, written out

The note is the therapist's only defence and the family's only protection. It is written the same day, and it does four things: records the policy, records the disclosure, records the reasoning, and records the plan. An example, adapted from the Adeleke case.

Session 4 (individual, 40 min). Client N.A. disclosed an ongoing relationship of approximately two years with a colleague. Client states she does not intend to end it and does not intend to tell her husband. Policy. Modified no-secrets policy stated to both partners in session 1 and agreed by both; copy in file. Policy restated to client at the start of this session. Client acknowledged it. Reasoning. Client system is the couple. Disclosure affects the couple treatment and would require the therapist to avoid material relevant to the presenting complaint; not compatible with continued couple work. No current risk of violence identified. Risk of STI transmission to the husband noted and addressed separately (below). No statutory duty engaged. Third parties: the other party's spouse is not a client and has no claim on the file. Plan. Client elected to disclose to her husband in session 5, with therapist support. Rehearsal agreed for session 5 opening. If disclosure has not occurred by [date], therapist will inform the husband that a disclosure has been made and that he should raise it with his wife, having first told the client that she will do so. Client informed of this contingency and stated she understood it. Health. Therapist advised a sexual health screen this week, independent of the disclosure plan. Client agreed. To be confirmed at session 5. Supervision. Case to be presented at next supervision, [date]. Signed. [Therapist], [date], [time].

Why each part exists. The policy paragraph establishes that the client was told before she spoke, which is the difference between a trap and an agreement. The reasoning paragraph shows that the decision was clinical rather than moral, which is what a regulator reads for. The plan paragraph, with the date, is what makes the note a record of action rather than a record of concern. The health paragraph prevents the most common omission in these cases. And the supervision line is the strongest single sentence in the note, because it shows the therapist did not decide alone.

The version not to write. "Client disclosed an affair; discussed confidentiality." That note has no reasoning, no date, no plan, and no policy — and if the husband complains in a year, it establishes only that the therapist knew.

3.10 Evidence note

What the research supports. Survey and vignette studies consistently find that couple and family therapists report operating modified no-secrets policies and consider them more workable than either rigid alternative, while acknowledging that the policy is difficult to enact under pressure. The clinical literature on "therapeutic secrets" is largely theoretical, but it converges on the observation that a secret held by the therapist changes the therapist's behaviour — avoidance of topics, reduced spontaneity, and a measurable drop in the couple's engagement — which is consistent with the triadic model of the secret as a third participant.

What is weaker. There is almost no empirical work on outcomes. No trial has compared no-secrets, full secrecy and negotiated policies, and the question may be unanswerable by trial design because the policy is chosen partly by the population: a practice serving couples in high-conflict or coercive relationships will reasonably operate differently from one serving voluntary, low-risk couples.

What is not supported. That a no-secrets policy is the standard of care in any enforceable sense — the codes do not require it. That holding a secret is always harmful — some privacy is protective, particularly in cases involving past abuse or a partner's history of violence. And that the policy should be applied identically across cultures, which the cross-cultural literature on family information economies does not support.

The matrix cell. Development is probabilistic and non-linear; the same input produces different outcomes depending on timing and context. The same secret, disclosed in session two with a therapist who has stated the policy and has a supervisor, and disclosed in session twenty with a therapist who has never mentioned it, produces two different treatments and two different families.

3.11 Key terms

Confidentiality — the professional duty not to disclose information given in the treatment relationship, subject to specified exceptions.

Privacy — information a person legitimately chooses not to share; compatible with treatment.

Therapeutic secret — information held from one member of a couple or family that materially affects the treatment.

Modified no-secrets policy — a stated policy that individual disclosures will not be kept where they affect the couple or family work, with a process for their disclosure.

Client system — the person or persons agreed to be the client; the holder of the confidentiality.

Third-party claim — an institution's or individual's claim on information, legal or social.

Information map — a drawn record of who holds what information about a family, and who decides.

Deadline — the dated commitment that converts a secret into a task.

Collusion — an arrangement in which the therapist's silence serves the client's avoidance.

Duty to warn / protect — the legal or ethical obligation to act on a serious threat to an identifiable third party; jurisdiction-dependent.

3.12 Self-test

  1. Why does individual confidentiality not transfer to couple therapy?
  2. State the four parts of the modified no-secrets policy, and the failure each prevents.
  3. Give the operational test that distinguishes privacy from secrecy.
  4. What is the difference between telling a partner that something has been said and telling them what?
  5. In the Adeleke case, why was the health question separated from the confidentiality decision?
  6. What would you do if the client refused the deadline?
  7. Name five institutions with claims on a family's information, and what each may legitimately be told.
  8. Why is "carrying the secret for now" the most damaging option?

Answers. (1) Because the client is the couple jointly, so neither partner can demand confidentiality against the other, and a therapist holding information from one member is deceiving a client on another's behalf. (2) The promise of individual sessions (prevents the request being experienced as a rupture); the limit stated in terms of effect (prevents accidental secrecy); the commitment to tell both that something has been said (prevents the guessing game); the deadline (prevents a stable dishonesty). (3) Does knowing this change what I do in the joint sessions? If yes, it is secrecy. (4) Telling that preserves the discloser's control of the content and the timing, and it converts the therapist's position from deception to pressure; telling what removes the client's only remaining act of repair. (5) Because the exposure of a third party to infection is a health matter with its own urgency, and a therapist absorbed in the ethics of disclosure can lose it. (6) Hold the limit, do not disclose the content, state the date, inform the partner on the date that a disclosure has been made, and keep working with the client so that honesty does not cost her the therapist. (7) The school (attendance and functioning, not therapy content); the court (whatever is compelled); the insurer or employer (usually attendance only); child protection (what the statute requires); the religious body (a summary with written agreement, or nothing); and the extended family, who have no claim and considerable social power. (8) Because there is no "for now": the secret is either moving toward disclosure or it is being carried, and a carried secret changes every question the therapist can ask.

3.13 Reflective task

Think of a time you were told something in confidence that you could not keep from someone who needed to know. Write down what you did, and then answer: which of the six questions did you answer correctly at the time, and which did you answer afterwards? Most people find that the ethical reasoning came after the decision, which is normal — and it is the reason this course insists that the policy is stated before the case arrives.

3.14 Further reading

  • Gutheil, T. and Gabbard, G. on secrets in couple and family therapy — the clearest treatment of the triadic problem.
  • Margolin, G. on confidentiality in family research and therapy — the empirical discussion of the policy options.
  • Doherty, W. and Boss, P. on family ethics — the relational account that underlies this week.
  • AAMFT interpretive statements on confidentiality — the shortest authoritative source on the limits.
  • Kirmayer, L. on the cultural shaping of disclosure — relevant to the section on family information economies.

Next: Week 4 — Secrets I: the individual disclosure.

Week 4 — Secrets I: the individual disclosure

Learning outcomes. By the end of this week the student can (1) distinguish the ethics of a secret told in individual therapy from the ethics of a secret told in couple therapy; (2) apply a seven-step protocol to an individual disclosure; (3) assess what a secret is doing for the person who keeps it, not only what it is hiding; (4) plan, rehearse and record a disclosure without coercing it; and (5) identify the countertransference that being trusted with a secret reliably produces.

The distinction this week exists to teach

Week 3 dealt with the couple as client. The therapist there had a policy — agreed in advance, applied when the secret arrived — and the policy converted the secret into a task with a date. This week deals with the harder and commoner situation: a person in individual therapy tells the therapist something that concerns someone who is not in the room and is not a client.

The temptation is to import the couple framework wholesale, and it is a mistake. In couple therapy the therapist has a duty to the system, and the system's honesty is a condition of the treatment. In individual therapy the therapist has a duty to one person, and the person's honesty is a condition of the treatment too — but the person's privacy is hers to spend, and the therapist has no standing to demand disclosure to a third party who is not a client at all.

What the therapist does have standing to do is something narrower and more demanding: to insist that the secret be visible inside the therapy. The distinction is the whole of the week.

  • The therapist may not tell the husband. He is not a client, has no claim on the file, and telling him would be a plain breach.
  • The therapist may not pretend the secret does not exist. A treatment conducted around an unmentionable fact is a treatment of the presenting complaint only, and it will fail in a way that looks like the client's fault.
  • The therapist's working question is therefore not should this be disclosed? but what is this secret doing to you, and what would it take for you to be able to put it down?

The classification table. Before responding to any disclosure, classify it. The classification determines what happens in the next five minutes.

TypeExamplesWhat it obliges
ImmaterialA surprise party; a gift being planned; an ordinary private preferenceNothing. Keep it, and do not make it clinical
Material, no riskA past termination; a debt; a diagnosis; an affair that has ended; a family history kept quietWork with the cost of secrecy; plan disclosure if the client chooses it; never coerce
Third-party riskAn undisclosed infection; a credible threat; a plan to harmAssess; follow legal and public-health duties; document; disclose only what the duty requires
Child or vulnerable adultAbuse, neglect, exposure to dangerReport under the statute; inform the client what will be reported
Risk to selfSuicidal intent, self-harm, a planRisk assessment and safety planning; break confidentiality only as required

Note what is not on the table: "immoral" is not a category, and "the client's family would want to know" is not a category. The therapist's own moral judgement of the secret is not an input to the classification, and the most common error in this week's work is a classification driven by the therapist's disapproval.

4.1 The lecture in ninety minutes

MinutesContentTeaching form
0–10The individual case: why Week 3's framework does not transferTwo contrasting vignettes, voted on before analysis
10–25Five concepts: burden, avoidance, function, countertransference, self-concealmentLecture with board diagram
25–40The seven-step protocol; the classification table applied to five disclosuresLive sorting exercise
40–55The cost ledger and the two-futures exerciseDemonstration with a volunteer, then pairs
55–70The Bisi case, in fullCase presentation, students hold the decision
70–80The transcript, annotatedRead aloud in three voices
80–90Six errors; the record; what supervision is forShort lecture, closing question

4.2 Five concepts that organise the week

Concept 1 — Keeping a secret is work, and the work is paid for in the body. The disclosure literature is consistent on this point: concealment requires continuous vigilance — monitoring what one says, to whom, in front of whom — and that vigilance has measurable costs in physiological arousal, sleep, intrusive thought and sense of authenticity. The clinical implication is not "therefore disclose". It is that the client's reported symptoms may be the cost of the concealment rather than the effects of the concealed event, and that this distinction changes the treatment entirely.

Concept 2 — Secrecy is maintained by avoidance, and avoidance generalises. The client avoids one topic; then avoids the conversations adjacent to it; then avoids the people with whom the topic might arise. In therapy the generalisation is visible as a narrowing of what can be discussed, and it typically looks like a treatment that "has gone flat" rather than like concealment. The therapist's first task is to notice the narrowing, which requires asking about it directly.

Concept 3 — The secret has a function, and it is usually protective. It prevents a divorce, a beating, a loss of standing in a family, a mother's distress. A therapist who treats the secret only as a problem to be solved is asking a client to give up protection before any substitute exists. The function is assessed, named aloud, and taken seriously — and the assessment frequently reveals that the protection is partly illusory.

Concept 4 — Being trusted with a secret produces a reliable countertransference. It is flattering, it creates a special bond, and it invites the therapist to become the client's only ally — which is precisely the position from which the therapist stops challenging her. The signs are familiar: the therapist looks forward to the session, feels protective, has a private understanding with the client, and has begun to dislike the absent husband on the client's evidence alone. Each sign is a prompt to supervision.

Concept 5 — What the client conceals from the therapist matters as much as what she discloses. Self- concealment — the general tendency to hide personal information — predicts poorer outcomes and weaker therapeutic alliances, and it operates on the therapist too. A client who has told you about a past termination may still be concealing the reason she chose you, or the fact that she has already consulted a lawyer. The therapist's own honesty about the limits of what she can work with is the main instrument against this.

4.2a What a secret looks like before it is told

Most secrets are visible for several sessions before they are disclosed, and the therapist's capacity to notice them is a skill that can be trained. The markers below are drawn from the transcript evidence on concealment and from ordinary clinical observation.

Marker 1 — The generic answer. Asked about her marriage, the client who is holding something says "he is a good man" and offers no incident. Detail is where the truth lives; a client who produces adjectives and no events is either not attending to her relationship or not able to report it.

Marker 2 — The topic that never recurs. Every other subject in a client's life will return across six sessions. The one that appears once, in session one, and is never mentioned again, is usually the one that is being managed. The therapist's record should note what is missing as carefully as what is present.

Marker 3 — The time gap. A narrative with a hole in it — school, then marriage, with five years missing — is a narrative with a secret in it. The therapist does not immediately probe the hole. She notes it, and she returns to it when the alliance can bear the question.

Marker 4 — The body's answer. The chest, the throat, the hand that goes to the mouth, the change in breathing when a name is mentioned. These are not proof of anything and they are information, and they belong in the note as observation rather than as inference.

Marker 5 — The abrupt generalisation. The client who is talking about something specific and suddenly becomes abstract ("well, everybody has something they do not talk about") has just come close to it and has stepped away. The move is worth naming gently: "you went somewhere else just then."

Marker 6 — The pre-emptive question. "You are not going to tell anyone what I say, are you?" — asked before anything has been said, and usually asked more than once. The client is not testing the policy; she is preparing to use it.

How the therapist uses the markers. She does not accuse. She makes the space available: "is there anything you have been carrying that we have not got to?" — asked once, plainly, and then left. The question is an invitation and not a demand, and the client who declines it has learned that the door is open, which is what makes the later disclosure possible.

4.3a When the client is a child or an adolescent

The protocol changes, because the client's capacity to choose is limited and the parents' claim is real.

The default. A minor's confidentiality is real and is not absolute. The therapist tells the child, in the first session and in language appropriate to the age, what will be shared with parents and what will not. The standard formulation: "what you tell me stays with me, unless I think you or someone else is in danger, or unless I think you need help that only your parents can give — and if I ever have to tell them something, I will tell you first what I am going to say."

The common error. Promising a child total secrecy, which the therapist cannot keep and which, when broken, teaches the child the exact lesson the therapy exists to prevent.

The adolescent's material. An adolescent disclosing sexual activity, a relationship, or a plan to leave home is disclosing material that parents will want and frequently have a legitimate interest in. The therapist's position: the content stays confidential where there is no risk; the fact that the young person is dealing with something significant may be shared with the parents in general terms, with the young person's knowledge. Week 2's four-part script for consent with a young person is the template.

Where the child holds a family secret. A nine-year-old who says "do not tell my mum that daddy shouts" is disclosing something about the household. The therapist assesses risk first, then asks the child what she is most afraid would happen if it were known, then works with the parents on the pattern without attributing it to the child. The child must not be made the source, because a child who is the source of a parental crisis has been given a burden that will outlast the therapy.

4.3 The seven-step protocol for an individual disclosure

Step 1 — Receive it without reacting. The most consequential fifteen seconds in the session. The client has taken the largest risk she will take in months, and she is reading the therapist's face for the verdict. Any visible judgement — surprise, disapproval, concern so sharp it reads as alarm — teaches her that the disclosure was a mistake. The therapist's response is small and warm: "thank you for telling me", a pause, and then a question that keeps her talking.

Step 2 — Classify. Run the table. Is anyone at risk? Is a child involved? Is a duty engaged? This takes seconds and can be done while the client is still speaking. If any risk category applies, Week 8 or 9 governs and the session changes shape immediately.

Step 3 — Establish the history of the secret. How long has she held it, who else knows, has she ever tried to tell, and what happened. This is not small talk. The answers tell the therapist whether she is dealing with a long-maintained concealment (which will have shaped her relationships) or a recent event (which will not have had time to).

Step 4 — Assess the function. The question is: "what does keeping this do for you?" The answers are specific and are usually accurate — it protects her marriage, her mother, her standing, her job. The follow-up is the one that does the work: "and what does it cost you to keep it?" The client has almost always done this accounting privately and has never been asked to say it aloud.

Step 5 — Name the cost without demanding the remedy. The therapist reflects what she has heard, makes the link between the concealment and the presenting symptoms, and then does not prescribe. "So the sleep, and the knot in your chest — I wonder whether they are the price of carrying this alone." The client is now holding a formulation of her own problem rather than a secret.

Step 6 — Offer options, and let her choose. Five, in ascending order of exposure: keep it and work on managing it; tell one other person outside the marriage; tell the husband with the therapist's help in planning; tell the husband in a session with the therapist present; or defer with a review date. The deferral option is essential: without it, the client who is not ready has only the choice between exposure and flight.

Step 7 — Record and review. The note records the classification, the reasoning, the client's decision, and the review date. The review is a real appointment, and the therapist raises it.

What is absent from the protocol. Any step at which the therapist decides for the client. This is the difference from Week 3, and it is not a difference of squeamishness: the therapist has no authority over the client's relationship with her husband, and the attempt to exercise it converts treatment into moral direction.

4.4 Four techniques

Technique 1 — The cost ledger. A two-column sheet. Left: what keeping the secret protects. Right: what it costs — sleep, intimacy, the topics that cannot be raised, the moments of panic at a family gathering, the doctor's question that cannot be answered honestly. The client fills it at home between sessions. The sheet does the work that a therapist's argument cannot do, because the client produces both columns herself. In practice the left column is short and stable and the right column is long and growing, which is the finding that shifts the decision.

Technique 2 — The two futures. A structured imagination exercise, ten minutes, eyes open. The client describes in detail the morning three months after she has told him: where she is, what the room looks like, what he said, what happened to her body, and what she does next. Then the same morning in three years if she has not told him. The exercise is not a prediction and is not offered as one.
