Its function is to convert a vague dread into a set of specific fears, each of which can then be examined — and frequently to reveal that the feared outcome that has kept her silent for eleven years has never once been described in detail.

Technique 3 — The smallest true sentence. Many clients cannot contemplate the disclosure because they are imagining a full confession. The technique constructs the smallest sentence that is true and complete: "there is something about my past I have never told you and I want to tell you." Eleven words. The rehearsal is of these eleven words and the silence after them, and nothing else — because the client's real fear is not the content but the moment before it, and the moment can be practised.

Technique 4 — The audience question. "Who, other than your husband, would this change things with?" In a family where a mother-in-law, a pastor, or a brother has a stake, the client is often protecting the secret from four people while nominating one. Naming the full audience reduces the fantasy to a specific list and sometimes shows that the disclosure is smaller than she thinks — or larger.

A note on rehearsal. Where a client decides to disclose, the therapist rehearses with her: the opening sentence, the two most likely responses, and the sentence she will use if the conversation goes badly. The rehearsal is not preparation for a performance; it is the demonstration that she has options inside the conversation, which is what fear removes.

4.4a The case: Bisi Oyelaran, and what the therapist did not do

Referral. Bisi Oyelaran, 29, a primary school teacher, self-referred for "sleep and worry". Two children, 3 and 1. Husband Dayo, 33, a bank officer. Married four years. No prior therapy.

Sessions 1–4. The presenting problem is insomnia, a sensation of pressure in the chest, and irritability with the children. There is no identifiable stressor of the usual kind: work is stable, the marriage described as "fine", the children healthy. Bisi's mother died two years earlier and the therapist pursues bereavement, which produces some material and no improvement. In session 4 the therapist notices the pattern the reader should have noticed in session 2 — every account of the marriage is generic. "He is a good man" recurs five times. There is no anecdote, no argument, no detail.

The moment, session 5, minute twenty-two. "I have to tell you something and then I will probably not come back." The therapist says nothing except "I am listening." Bisi then discloses: at 21, in her final year at college, she was pregnant and had a termination. Her mother knew and arranged it. No one else has ever known. Dayo's family are members of a church where this is "the worst thing a woman can do", and his mother has begun asking when the third child will come, and has suggested a fertility test.

The classification, made in the moment. No risk to anyone. No child protection question. A material secret with no third-party risk. The protocol applies.

Step 1 in practice. The therapist said: "thank you for telling me, and I want you to know that I am not going anywhere." Bisi cried for four minutes. Nothing was said. This is not a failure of technique; it is the technique.

Step 3, the history. Eleven years. Two people knew — Bisi and her mother, and her mother is dead. Since the death, no one knows. The therapist names this: "so for two years you have been the only person in the world who knows this." Bisi said, "yes", and then, "I had not thought of it like that."

Step 4, the function and the cost. The function: it protects the marriage from Dayo's family, and it protects her from Dayo's judgement, which she describes as "he would not shout, he would go quiet, and then he would tell his mother." The cost, elicited over two sessions: she cannot answer questions about her twenties; she avoids her college friends; she has refused two invitations to speak at church; she has begun to dread intercourse because "it is the one place I think about it"; and the chest pressure began the week her mother-in-law first mentioned the fertility test.

Step 5, the naming. "You have been carrying this alone for two years, and the fear of being found out has started to organise your life — who you see, what you say, even when you are close to your husband. I do not think the sleep is the problem. I think the sleep is the bill."

Step 6, the options, offered without pressure. Bisi rejected disclosure to Dayo outright, and said so with contempt for the suggestion. The therapist did not argue. Bisi chose a version of the first option — keep it, work on managing it — with a review date in six weeks, and one addition of her own: she wanted to tell one person, a cousin in Ibadan, "so that somebody alive knows".

The decision the therapist did not make. The therapist did not tell Bisi that she must tell her husband, did not imply that a good marriage requires it, and did not treat the delay as a failure of courage. She said what she thought: that the secret was costing her more each year, that the cost would rise when the fertility question became a medical process, and that if Bisi ever wanted to tell him she would help her plan it and would be in the room if wanted.

Sessions 6–12. The work changed. The presenting symptoms were reframed as the cost of concealment, and the treatment became about how Bisi managed the secret rather than about the secret. She wrote the cousin a letter she did not send, then sent it. The chest pressure reduced over five weeks. In session 10 she said, of her own accord, "I think I will tell him before the test", and the therapist said "tell me what you would want to say", and the rehearsal began. She told him in session 14, at home, and came to the next session with the two of them. The therapist then saw them twice as a couple — with a new consent conversation, because the client system had changed.

What the case teaches.

  1. The therapist's own urgency is the main threat to the work. Had she pressed in session 5, Bisi would have left, and the secret would have gone back underground with a new piece of evidence: that telling costs you the person you told.
  2. The symptom was the bill. Reframing the presenting complaint as the cost of concealment is the single most useful intervention in this week's material, and it is available without any disclosure at all.
  3. The disclosure was eventually made, and not because it was required. It was made when the cost of the secret exceeded the cost of the telling, and the therapist's job was to make that comparison visible and to be present when the balance tipped.
  4. The client system changed, and the paperwork had to change with it. When Dayo joined, the consent conversation was redone from the beginning, and the record was updated. A therapist who simply continues on the old consent has made a documentary error that no amount of good clinical work repairs.

4.5 Transcript: receiving the disclosure

Session 5, minutes twenty-two to forty. Bisi has just said "I have to tell you something and then I will probably not come back." Annotation after each turn.

T1 Therapist: (pause) I am listening. — Four words. No question, no reassurance, no "of course you will come back". The client has announced an exit; the therapist does not argue with it, which is what keeps her in the room. T2 Bisi: (long pause, then quickly) When I was twenty-one, in my final year, I was pregnant. My mother took me. I have never told anybody. Dayo does not know. His family — you do not know his family. If they knew they would — (stops) T3 Therapist: Thank you for telling me. (pause) And I want you to know that I am not going anywhere. — Step 1. The two sentences are doing different jobs: the first acknowledges the risk she has taken; the second answers the fear she actually voiced, which was about being abandoned by the therapist, not about the termination. T4 Bisi: (crying) You are not going to say anything? T5 Therapist: Not yet. (pause) I would rather hear the whole of it than start talking. — The refusal to reassure is the hardest move in the transcript and the most important. Reassurance at this point ("that was not your fault", "God forgives") replaces the client's account with the therapist's, and the client learns that the subject must be managed. T6 Bisi: There is nothing else. It was eleven years ago. It is finished. T7 Therapist: Eleven years. (pause) Who knows? T8 Bisi: My mother. Only my mother. T9 Therapist: And your mother died two years ago. — Step 3. The therapist has done the arithmetic and says it plainly. The client's face changes; this is the first moment in which the isolation is visible to her. T10 Bisi: (pause) Yes. So now it is only me. T11 Therapist: (pause) So for two years you have been the only person alive who knows this. — The reflection that does more than any interpretation. It is accurate, it is short, and it is not a judgement. T12 Bisi: (long pause) I did not think of it like that. I thought — I thought I was managing it. T13 Therapist: You have been managing it. (pause) Tell me what managing it looks like. — The question that opens the cost ledger without the therapist having to propose it. T14 Bisi: I do not talk about university. If somebody asks, I say it was fine. I do not go to the reunions. I stopped singing in the choir because — (stops) — because I thought if I stood up there, everybody would know. T15 Therapist: Everybody would know. — Reflecting the exact phrase, which is the technique that gets the client to hear her own logic. The therapist does not correct it. The correction is available later and is worth nothing now. T16 Bisi: I know it is not reasonable. T17 Therapist: I did not say it was not reasonable. I said I heard it. — A small correction, and a necessary one: the client has begun to argue with herself, and self-argument is the mechanism by which the secret has been kept. T18 Bisi: (pause) His mother has started talking about a test. She wants to know why we are not expecting. And I sit there and I — (stops) T19 Therapist: And you sit there holding all of it. — The link between the secret and the presenting symptom, made in the client's own language, four minutes after the disclosure and without a formulation. T20 Bisi: (quietly) The chest started then.

Minute forty, the close. The therapist did not offer a plan in this session. She said: "This is a great deal to have said aloud, and I think we should stop here rather than start solving it. I want you to come next week. We will look at what you want to do about this — and there are several things you can do, not one." Bisi attended the following week.

What the transcript shows.

  • Restraint is the intervention. Of the twenty turns, the therapist asks four questions and makes three reflections. She interprets nothing, reassures nothing, and prescribes nothing.
  • The exact phrase. Reflecting the client's own words (turns 15 and 19) produces more than any formulation the therapist could have offered, because the client cannot argue with her own sentence.
  • The ending is deliberate. Closing before problem-solving prevents the session from becoming a negotiation about disclosure in the first hour of the client's exposure, and it makes the next appointment the container for the decision.

4.6a The record of an individual disclosure, written out

Two notes, from the Bisi case, at the two moments that matter. The first records the disclosure; the second records the decision at review.

Session 5 (50 min). Client disclosed a termination at age 21, during her final year at university. Her mother arranged and knew; mother died two years ago; no other person has known. Client states her husband and his family do not know and that she fears disclosure would end the marriage and expose her to her mother-in-law and her church community. Classification. Material, no current risk to client, child, or third party. No statutory duty engaged. No risk indicators on direct enquiry. Response. No advice or plan offered in this session; disclosure received, history and audience explored, link noted between the client's reported chest symptoms and the recent fertility questions from her mother-in-law. Session closed early by design; next appointment confirmed and attended. Plan. Options to be discussed at session 6 (keep and manage; tell one other person; tell husband with planning; tell husband in session; defer with review). Client informed there are several options, not one. Supervision. Case to be presented [date] — countertransference noted: therapist aware of a wish to protect client from her husband's family.
Session 11 (50 min). Review held as scheduled. Client reports: has told one person (cousin, Ibadan); chest symptoms reduced; sleep improved from approximately four to six hours. Client states she now intends to tell her husband before any fertility investigation begins, and asks for help planning. Rehearsal begun with the opening sentence; two likely responses identified; client to bring a written note to session 12 if she wishes. Client informed that if she wishes the therapist to be present, the therapist will attend; client has not yet decided. Client system unchanged at this date. Review at session 13.

What the two notes have in common. Both record the classification, the reasoning, the client's decision, the next step and the supervision. Neither contains a judgement of the client's choice. Neither records an instruction.

Why the second note matters as much as the first. It is the evidence that the deferral was a plan and not an abandonment. A file containing the first note alone tells a reader that the therapist knew about a secret and did nothing for six sessions; the two notes together tell the truth, which is that the client was given time, the work continued, and the decision was hers.

The one sentence that must never appear. "Client declined to disclose; discussed." It is accurate, it is useless, and it will be read by a regulator as an absence of clinical thinking.

4.6 Laboratory: the disclosure rehearsal

Aim. To practise receiving a disclosure without reacting, classify it correctly, and offer options without coercion — assessed by an observer against six observable behaviours.

Set-up. Triads, three rounds of twelve minutes each, so everyone plays all three roles. Each round the "client" draws a card. The cards, which should be prepared in advance:

CardDisclosureClassification
A"I have been paying a loan for two years and my husband does not know."Material, no risk
B"I have not told my wife that I lost my job six weeks ago."Material, no risk; possible financial risk
C"My brother has been staying with us and he has been in prison."Material; consider third parties
D"I have been having thoughts about not being here."Risk to self — stop the exercise and follow Week 8
E"My husband's cousin touched me when I was sixteen and I have never told anyone in the family."Material; consider historical abuse reporting rules
F"I know my daughter's husband hits her and she has asked me not to tell anyone."Risk to a third party — Week 9

Cards D and F are the ones that matter pedagogically: the student who continues the counselling protocol after drawing them has failed the round outright, however warm the response.

Observable behaviours (the observer marks each as present, partial or absent).

  1. Did the therapist allow a silence after the disclosure, before speaking?
  2. Did the therapist thank the client, and say something that addresses abandonment?
  3. Did the therapist ask about history and audience before proposing anything?
  4. Did the therapist ask about function and cost?
  5. Did the therapist offer at least three options, including a deferral with a review date?
  6. Did the therapist avoid reassurance, advice, and any statement of what the client "should" do?

Marking rubric (20 marks).

Criterion0–12–34–5
ReceptionReacted, advised or reassuredPaused, but then filled the silenceReceived without reaction; acknowledged risk; addressed abandonment
ClassificationDid not classifyClassified, but the wrong categoryCorrect category, and changed protocol where the card required it
Function and costNeither exploredOne exploredBoth explored in the client's language, with the link to the presenting complaint
OptionsGave one option or a demandSeveral, all leading to disclosureSeveral, including deferral and review, with the choice left where it belongs
RecordNothingNarrative onlyClassification, reasoning, client's decision and review date

Debrief question for every triad. "What did you want the client to do, and where did that wish show in what you said?" Almost every student discovers the wish in the pause before their first question.

4.7 Six errors

Error 1 — Reassuring in the first minute. "That must have been terrible", "you were very young", "God is merciful" — each is meant kindly and each takes the story away from the client. The client's task at that moment is to finish her account; the therapist's task is to make room for it.

Error 2 — Classifying by the therapist's moral reaction. The therapist who finds an affair or a termination disturbing will classify it as high-priority and press for disclosure. The classification table has no moral criterion, and its absence is deliberate.

Error 3 — Turning the first session of disclosure into a decision. The client has spent years holding something and has now said it aloud, which is exhausting. A therapist who wants a plan within twenty minutes is working at her own pace, not the client's.

Error 4 — Confusing "I cannot work around this" with "you must tell". The therapist's limit is about her own capacity to conduct an honest treatment, and it is legitimate. It is not a demand that the client disclose to a third party who is not a client, and the two are frequently conflated.

Error 5 — Becoming the keeper of the secret. The therapist who agrees to hold it, protects the client from the husband, and begins to manage the household's information has been recruited. The sign is that the therapist is now thinking about the husband as an obstacle.

Error 6 — Forgetting to review. The deferral option is safe only if the review is real. A deferral without a date becomes a permanent arrangement, and the secret has quietly been installed in the treatment.

4.4b Four sentences that help a client decide

The therapist's most useful contribution to a disclosure decision is not advice; it is a small number of sentences that make the decision thinkable. These four, used in this order across two sessions, do most of the work.

First — "you do not have to decide today." The client has usually arrived at the session believing that telling the therapist means being pushed. Removing the pressure lowers the arousal enough for thinking to become possible, and it is also true.

Second — "there is more than one way to do this." The client's imagination contains a single scenario: sitting opposite her husband, saying everything, and watching the marriage end. The therapist's job is to populate the field — a letter, a session, a trusted third person present, a partial disclosure of the fact rather than the detail, a staged disclosure over weeks. Clients almost never generate these options alone, because fear narrows the field to one.

Third — "what is the worst thing you are imagining, in detail?" Not "how do you feel?" — which produces adjectives — but a request for the specific scene. The scene is then examined: what he says, what she says next, who else is told, what happens in the following week. The examination does two things. It usually reveals that the imagined catastrophe has no script beyond the first minute. And where the catastrophe is real — where the husband has been violent, or where the family would remove the children — the examination reveals that too, and the plan changes accordingly.

Fourth — "whatever you decide, I will still be working with you." The sentence that makes honesty survivable and the one most often left unsaid. It is also the sentence the therapist must be able to keep, which is why the limit in Week 3's couple policy is different from the position here.

4.7a The audit question, and the end of the week

At the end of every week in which a disclosure has been received, the therapist answers four questions in writing, in the file, in under a hundred words.

  1. What do I now know that the people around this client do not know? The inventory. It is usually longer than the therapist expects.
  2. Is anything moving, or is this now installed? A plan with a date is moving. A deferral with a review date is moving slowly. A deferral without a date is installed.
  3. Who is paying for the concealment, and how much? If the answer is only the client, the situation is stable. If the answer includes a child, a partner's health, or a third party's exposure, the situation is not stable and the deferral must be renegotiated.
  4. Have I taken this to supervision? If not, why not — and the honest answer is frequently that the therapist does not want to be told to press, which is itself the material for supervision.

The closing point of the week. The student who finishes this week believing that the right answer is "the client should disclose" has not learned the week's content. The right answer is that the therapist maintains a relationship in which the client can tell the truth about what she is carrying, keeps her own conduct honest, never becomes a party to the concealment, and holds the client's choice even when the choice is to wait. That is a harder position to occupy than either directive — and it is the position the codes describe when they say that the therapist's duty is to the client's welfare and self-determination, and not to the therapist's view of the client's marriage.

4.8 Culture, faith and the secret

Shame is social before it is private. In many of the families this book serves, a secret is not held to protect the individual's self-image but to protect a household's standing. Bisi's fear was not that Dayo would think less of her; it was that Dayo would go quiet and then tell his mother, and that the information would then travel through a church and a compound. The therapist who assesses the risk as "her husband's reaction" has mis-specified it, and the mis-specification makes the client's fear look irrational when it is not.

Confession has an address. Where a client's framework includes confession — to a priest, a pastor, an imam, an elder — the therapist should ask, early, whether the client has confessed, and to whom, and what happened. Three outcomes are common and each is clinically useful. The client has confessed and found absolution, and the secret's burden is much lower than the therapist assumed. The client has confessed and been given a penance that made the secrecy worse, such as a direction never to speak of it again. Or the client has not confessed because the confession would require a public act she cannot perform, and the therapy is now carrying a religious obligation as well as a personal one.

The church as an information network. In congregations where testimony is public and pastoral care is layered through small groups, a disclosure has an audience that the client knows better than the therapist. The practical consequence: ask who would come to know, and do not assume the answer is one person. In Bisi's case the audience was a mother-in-law, a women's fellowship, and a choir.

The in-law's claim. In many households the mother-in-law holds a genuine and socially recognised claim on information about fertility. A therapist who treats this as intrusion will alienate the client, who may agree with the claim even while resenting it. The workable formulation is to name the claim as real and to separate it from the decision: "she has a claim to be told something; she does not have a claim to be told this, and the choice of what she is told is yours."

Reproductive history and the honour frame. Where a premarital pregnancy or a termination is framed as an offence against family honour rather than as a personal decision, the disclosure's danger is not disproportionate — it is accurately perceived. The therapist's obligation is not to talk the client out of the danger but to help her assess it with precision and to plan for the realistic worst case, including where she would go and who would take her in.

What not to do. Do not import the language of "authenticity" and "living your truth" into a household where the cost of disclosure may be housing, custody, or violence. Do not assume that the Western therapeutic consensus — disclosure is good for you — describes the client's situation. And do not assume the opposite either: many clients in these households disclose, survive it, and are relieved.

4.8a The therapist's own secrets

A neglected part of the topic, and a frequent source of impairment.

The therapist is not a sealed vessel. Therapists hold their own material — a divorce, a miscarriage, a parent's illness, a history of abuse, a child in difficulty — and the case that resembles it will activate it. The professional obligation is not to be unaffected. It is to know what is activated, to keep it out of the client's material, and to take it to supervision.

The three failure modes. Over-identification: the therapist's own secret shapes what she hears, so that the client's situation becomes the therapist's situation and the client's options narrow to the ones the therapist chose. Compensatory rescue: the therapist protects the client from the outcome she herself suffered. Displacement: the therapist's unprocessed material appears in the case as unusual intensity — the session that runs over, the client she thinks about at night, the case she discusses with her own partner.

Self-disclosure as a clinical decision. When the therapist discloses something of her own, the criteria are narrow: it must serve the client's work, it must be brief, it must be about the therapist's experience rather than the client's, and it must not require the client to take care of the therapist. "I have sat with this kind of decision myself, and I know it is not simple" meets the criteria. A twenty-minute account of the therapist's own divorce does not, and it makes the client a witness rather than a patient.

The question for the therapist. Before each session with the client who activates you: whose material is this? If the answer is unclear, the case goes to supervision and, where the activation is severe, the therapist considers whether the case should be transferred — which is a professional act and not a confession of inadequacy.

4.9 Evidence note

What the research supports. The concealment literature is substantial. Experimental work finds that holding a secret produces intrusive thinking about it and consumes cognitive capacity; that keeping secrets is experienced as effortful and fatiguing; that self-concealment is associated with greater distress and poorer help-seeking; and that disclosure interventions produce modest but reliable improvements in health and wellbeing. In psychotherapy research, clients' concealment predicts weaker alliances and worse outcomes, and client-rated honesty about what they have not said is a better predictor of progress than the amount they have said.

What is weaker. The evidence is largely about the cost of concealment in general, not about the right moment to disclose to a spouse. There are no trials of disclosure timing in couple or family treatment, and the prescriptive literature — tell within X sessions — is clinical convention rather than evidence.

What is not supported. That disclosure is always beneficial: the literature is clear that disclosure to a hostile or unsupportive recipient can worsen outcomes, which is precisely the situation many clients in this week's material accurately fear. That a therapist can predict the outcome of a disclosure. And that a secret unspoken to the therapist cannot be worked with — the treatment can address the management of a secret without its content, and sometimes must.

The matrix cell. Development is probabilistic and non-linear; the same input produces different outcomes depending on timing and context. Bisi at 21, in a family where her mother could act, and Bisi at 29, with two children, a mother-in-law and a church, is the same event in two different systems, and the disclosure carries two different prices.

4.10 Key terms

Individual disclosure — information given in individual therapy concerning a person who is not a client.

Concealment cost — the practical and physiological price of maintaining a secret.

Function — what the secret protects; assessed before any plan is proposed.

Self-concealment — a general disposition to hide personal information; predicts poorer therapy outcomes.

Deferral with review — an agreed postponement with a dated review; the alternative to disclosure and flight.

Smallest true sentence — the shortest complete disclosure, used in rehearsal.

Cost ledger — a two-column client exercise weighing protection against price.

Countertransference to the secret — the therapist's protective, flattered or allied response to being trusted.

Client system change — the addition of a new client (a partner, a child) requiring a new consent conversation.

4.11 Self-test

  1. Why does the modified no-secrets policy of Week 3 not transfer to individual therapy?
  2. State the five categories of the classification table and give one example of each.
  3. What are the three signs that a therapist has been recruited as keeper of the secret?
  4. Describe the cost ledger and explain why the client fills it rather than the therapist.
  5. What is the smallest true sentence, and what is it for?
  6. In the Bisi case, what did the therapist not do, and why does each omission matter?
  7. What must be recorded after an individual disclosure, in five parts?
  8. What changes when a new client joins an existing treatment?

Answers. (1) Because the client is the individual, so the therapist owes confidentiality to one person and has no standing to require disclosure to a non-client; the therapist's legitimate limit is on her own capacity to conduct the treatment, not on the client's conduct. (2) Immaterial (a surprise party); material with no risk (a past termination); third-party risk (an undisclosed infection); child or vulnerable adult (abuse); risk to self (suicidal intent). (3) The therapist looks forward to the session unduly; she feels protective of the client against the absent partner; and she is managing the household's information. (4) A two-column sheet of what the secret protects and what it costs, completed at home, because the client's own production of the right-hand column is more persuasive than any argument the therapist could make. (5) "There is something about my past I have never told you and I want to tell you" — eleven words used in rehearsal, because what the client fears is the moment before the disclosure rather than its content. (6) She did not reassure in the first minute (which would have replaced the client's account with her own); did not propose a plan in the first session (working at the therapist's pace); did not tell Bisi she must disclose (which would have ended the therapy); and did not treat the delay as failure. (7) Classification; reasoning; the client's decision; the review date; and the supervision plan. (8) A new consent conversation, an amended confidentiality agreement, a new record of who is the client, and a re-negotiation of what will be shared between the individual and joint work.

4.12 Reflective task

Write two hundred words on the last secret you were told. Do not describe the content. Answer three questions: what did you feel towards the person who told you; what did you want them to do next; and did that wish appear anywhere in how you responded? Keep the page. You will want it when the first case in your practice reproduces the feeling.

4.13 Further reading

  • Pennebaker, J. on disclosure and health — the empirical basis for the cost-of-concealment formulation.
  • Slepian, M. on the psychology of secrecy — the experimental work on mind-wandering and concealment burden.
  • Larson, D. and Chastain, R. on self-concealment — the construct that predicts therapy process.
  • Doherty, W. on moral conflicts in therapy — relevant to the discipline of not moralising.
  • Any current text on professional ethics in counselling for the treatment of client concealment from the therapist.

Next: Week 5 — Secrets II: infidelity, paternity and HIV status.

Week 5 — Secrets II: infidelity, paternity and HIV status

Learning outcomes. By the end of this week the student can (1) conduct the first four sessions after the discovery of an affair without either minimising the betrayal or treating the marriage as finished; (2) sequence the clinical and legal tasks when paternity is in question; (3) identify the jurisdiction's rule on disclosure of a communicable condition and follow the correct pathway where a partner is at risk; (4) decide, with reasons, whether the affair partner's spouse should be told; and (5) document all three in a form that would survive a regulator's reading.

Three secrets, three legal overlays

Week 4 dealt with the individual secret in general. This week deals with three specific secrets that arrive with predictable regularity and that each carry a legal overlay the therapist must recognise — not to practise law, but to know when to stop and refer.

SecretClinical taskLegal overlayWho else is exposed
InfidelityContain the disclosure; treat the betrayal; rebuild or endNot an offence in most jurisdictions; relevant to divorce, custody and maintenanceThe affair partner, and the affair partner's spouse
PaternitySupport the family through a factual discovery with enormous relational meaningPresumption of paternity, maintenance, custody, registration of birth, the child's rightsThe child, the presumed father, the biological father, both extended families
HIV and communicable diseaseSupport disclosure; treat the fear, the shame and the couple's sexual negotiationPublic-health law; in some jurisdictions a criminal offence to transmit or to expose knowinglyThe partner, and any previous or subsequent partner

The three are grouped because they share a structure: an established fact, a person who does not know it, and a third party who may be harmed by the continuing silence. Week 3's policy governs how the therapist holds the secret; this week governs what the therapist does with it.

The first thing to establish, always, is jurisdiction. The single most common ethical error in this week's material is a therapist applying a rule learned in one country to a family in another. On paternity, the presumption of legitimacy and the rules on registration differ substantially. On HIV, some jurisdictions impose a duty to disclose to an identifiable partner, some impose a duty to warn a public health authority, some criminalise non-disclosure, and some do none of these. On infidelity, adultery remains a criminal offence in a number of jurisdictions and is a ground for divorce in most. The therapist's obligation is to know the local rule for the three cases, to have checked it within the past year, and to have it in writing in the practice manual — not to reconstruct it from memory in a session.

The second thing is to know what the therapist does not do. She does not advise on divorce, on custody, on maintenance, on the registration of a birth, or on whether a client should be tested. She does not tell a client what the law requires of the client — that is legal advice, and where the stakes are this high it must come from a lawyer. She may say what her own duties are, because those are her duties and the client is entitled to know them. The distinction is clean and it is frequently blurred by therapists who have read one article.

5.1 The lecture in ninety minutes

MinutesContentTeaching form
0–8The three secrets and the common structureBoard diagram: fact, unknowing person, exposed third party
8–20Five concepts: betrayal trauma, the disclosure scene, the legal overlay, exposure, the third party's claimLecture
20–38Infidelity: the four-session protocolDemonstration, then pairs on the opening sentence
38–52Paternity: the sequence, and where the therapist stopsCase vignette, students write the referral question
52–66HIV: jurisdiction, pathway, and the safe disclosure planLocal rule quiz; group mapping of the disclosure pathway
66–76The Adegoke case and the disclosure sessionCase presentation with students as the couple
76–86Six errors; the documentationShort lecture
86–90Closing question: whose secret is it?Written answer, collected

5.2 Five concepts that organise the week

Concept 1 — Betrayal trauma is a trauma, and it is not a metaphor. The discovery of an affair produces a response with the signature of traumatic stress: intrusive images, hypervigilance to cues, avoidance, sleep disruption, and a compulsive need for detail. This matters clinically because the therapist who treats the discovery as an understandable upset will under-treat it. The partner who has just found out is not merely angry; she is in an acute stress response, and the first task is stabilisation rather than communication training.

Concept 2 — The disclosure scene is the treatment's foundation, and it happens once. The first conversation in which the affair is discussed will be remembered verbatim by both partners for years. What is said, whether the details are given, whether the affair partner is named, and whether the therapist is present or absent determines the shape of everything that follows. The therapist therefore prepares this conversation as carefully as any intervention in the book.

Concept 3 — The legal overlay changes what can be promised. A therapist who promises a client that nothing said in the room will reach a court has made a promise she may not be able to keep, because records can be subpoenaed and because in some jurisdictions the therapist's notes are discoverable in custody proceedings. The correct formulation, given at the start: "what we say here is confidential, and there are limits — one of them is that a court can compel records. If that ever happens, I will tell you and I will fight for as much confidentiality as the law allows." (Week 11 develops this.)

Concept 4 — Exposure is the therapist's signature question. Not "what should the client do?" but "who is exposed by the continuation of this silence, and how serious is the exposure?" An undisclosed affair exposes the affair partner's spouse to the same betrayal and, if there is any sexual contact outside the primary relationship, to infection. An undisclosed paternity exposes a child to a factual discovery later, at an age when it is more damaging. An undisclosed HIV status exposes a partner to infection. The seriousness of the exposure determines how much urgency the therapist brings.

Concept 5 — The third party has no claim on the therapy and a real claim on the truth. The affair partner's spouse is not a client, has no right to the file, and cannot be contacted by the therapist without the therapist's own breach of confidence. She also has a genuine moral claim to information that affects her marriage and her health. The two facts are both true, and the therapist must hold them without resolving the tension by pretending one is absent.

5.2a Infidelity: the four-session protocol

Session one — stabilise, do not adjudicate.

  • Both partners are seen, usually separately first, for twenty minutes each, then together briefly.
  • The injured partner is assessed for acute stress: sleep, intrusiveness, functioning, and suicidal ideation, which is not rare after discovery.
  • The involved partner is assessed for the character of the affair: how long, how often, whether it is ongoing, whether there is a pregnancy, whether money has moved, and whether the affair partner is threatening disclosure.
  • The therapist does not ask "why did you do this?" in session one. The question invites a defence that then has to be dismantled, and it is the wrong question at the wrong moment.
  • The therapist states the frame: no decisions about the marriage for six weeks; individual sessions permitted, with Week 3's policy; and the disclosure conversation will be structured, not improvised.

Session two — the disclosure scene.

Structure it as five questions, answered by the involved partner, in this order, with the therapist controlling pace:

  1. What happened? The facts, in outline — when it began, when it ended, how often. Not sexual detail.
  2. Who knows? Including the affair partner's spouse, and any friend who has been told.
  3. What did you tell me about it? The lies told to the injured partner during the affair, named specifically. This is the part that is hardest for the involved partner and the most necessary for the injured one, because the injured partner's injury is not only the sex; it is the deception she participated in unknowingly.
  4. What are you going to do now? Continuation, ending, disclosure of the affair to the affair partner's spouse, and the practical steps — the message sent, the block, the change of arrangements.
  5. What do you need from me? Addressed to the injured partner; the answer is usually "I need it to stop", and frequently "I need to know you chose me".

The detail question. The injured partner will ask for details, and the answer is not "tell everything" or "tell nothing", it is the distinction between facts necessary for safety and choice and images that will not leave. Necessary: how long, whether protection was used, whether there is an ongoing contact, whether the affair partner has been told about the marriage. Not necessary, and harmful: positions, comparisons, what the other person looks like. The therapist says this to both of them, before the questions begin, and both partners usually accept it — because the injured partner's real question is not anatomical.

Session three — the meaning.

Only now, after the facts are held and the exposure has been managed, does the question "what was this about?" become answerable. The therapist's stance must be exact: the meaning is not a cause, and the cause is not an excuse. "I want to understand what was happening in your life when this started, and I am not asking so that we can blame your wife for it. I am asking because whatever made the affair possible will still be there when it is over." The distinction is the one thing that makes the third session survivable, and it must be said aloud.

Session four — the choice.

The marriage is named as a decision rather than a default. Both partners are asked, separately and then together, whether they intend to attempt repair. Where one partner will not, the therapist does not argue; she helps them separate without the affair becoming the whole of the story. Where both will, the treatment moves to the repair tasks: full transparency for a defined period, the naming of what was lost, the rebuilding of trust as a set of testable commitments rather than a feeling, and the explicit rejection of "we are past it now" as a route to a silent recurrence.

The temporal frame that matters. The literature on affair recovery converges on a rough shape: an acute phase of three to six weeks in which nothing is decided; a middle phase of several months in which the meaning is worked and trust is rebuilt through consistency; and a long tail in which anniversaries and discoveries of new detail re-open the injury. A therapist who promises the couple that "in twelve sessions you will be through this" has promised something false and has given them a schedule to fail against.

5.2b Paternity: the sequence, and where the therapist stops

The situation. A father learns, or suspects, that a child he is raising is not biologically his. Or a mother learns that the man she named as the father is not. Or an adult child learns, from a commercial DNA test, that her father is not her father. Each of these arrives in a therapist's office and each is treated by the therapist as a family disclosure with a legal dimension.

Step 1 — Establish what is known, and how. Suspicion, an admission, a test, or a document. A suspicion the client has carried for nine years is not the same fact as a test result, and the clinical work differs enormously. The therapist's first question, "what do you actually know, and how do you know it?", is answered incompletely about half the time.

Step 2 — Establish who knows. The mother, the man, the child, the extended family, a lawyer. In the commonest configuration, two adults know and a child does not, and the therapist's client is one of the two.

Step 3 — Establish the child's position. Age, developmental stage, relationship with the presumed father, relationship with the biological father if any, and what the child has been told. The child's welfare is the organising criterion and not the parents' comfort. In many jurisdictions the child has a legal right to know the identity of a parent, and in almost all of them the child's interests will be weighed in any later dispute.

Step 4 — Refer for legal advice, and say why. The referral question is specific: what are the client's obligations and rights in respect of maintenance, custody, registration and the child's knowledge? The therapist does not answer this. She does something more useful: she writes the question down for the client so that the client can put it to a lawyer, and she records that she did so.

Step 5 — Sequence the disclosure, if there is to be one. The order matters and there is no universally correct one. The options: mother tells the father, then both tell the child; the father tells the child without the mother; a family session with the therapist present; or deferral until the child reaches an agreed age. Each has costs. What the therapist can do is insist that the decision is made deliberately, with the child's interest stated aloud, rather than by drift.

Step 6 — Be present for the child's question. Children ask the questions adults avoid: "so who is my real father?" and "does he still love me?" The therapist's preparatory work with the parents is to write answers to those two questions in advance, in words a child can hear, and to agree that the child's relationship with the man who raised him will be described as a relationship and not as a technicality.

Where the therapist stops. At the door of the courtroom. Everything about maintenance, access, guardianship and registration belongs to the lawyer; everything about the child's experience, the parents' grief, the family's story and the disclosure conversation belongs to the therapist.

The vignette. Kola, 47, a school principal, attends alone. He says his wife has told him, in an argument, that their second child, now 14, is not his. He has said nothing to the child and has not slept in three weeks. He wants to know whether to ask for a test.

The therapist's first session does four things and none of them is advice. She stabilises — sleep, work, the suicidal ideation he mentions in passing and then retracts, which is assessed properly. She establishes what he knows: his wife's statement in anger, no test, no corroboration. She names the two questions that are currently fused in his mind and must be separated: is it true? and what do I do about my son? And she writes, for him, the questions for a lawyer: what is his legal position if the child is not his; what obligations continue; what rights does he have; what happens if he seeks a test without the mother's consent.

The clinical work over eight sessions is not about the test. It is about the fact that Kola has not been able to look at his son for three weeks, and that the boy has noticed. The most urgent task in the case is that a fourteen-year-old is being silently abandoned by a father who is waiting for a fact. Whatever the fact turns out to be, that is the harm that is happening now.

5.3 HIV and other communicable conditions

The clinical reality. A client discloses that he is living with HIV and has not told his wife. He is on treatment and virally suppressed. Or a client discloses that her husband is living with HIV and has not told her. Or a client discloses that he has been diagnosed with hepatitis and does not intend to tell his partner. In each case the therapist holds information whose non-disclosure exposes an identifiable person to a serious condition.

Step 1 — Know the rule before the case arrives. The therapist's practice manual should contain four answers, reviewed annually: (a) is there a statutory duty to disclose to a public health authority, and within what period? (b) is there a statutory duty or permission to disclose to an identifiable partner, and to whom? (c) is non-disclosure or exposure a criminal offence, and does the therapist's knowledge create any liability? (d) does the jurisdiction operate a partner-notification service, and how is it accessed? Four questions, four sentences. The therapist who cannot answer them is not competent to hold this case.

Step 2 — Assess the actual risk. The therapist needs, not rumour: the client's treatment status, whether viral suppression has been achieved and documented, whether protection is used, and what the partner knows. In many of these cases the risk is far lower than the client's fear, and in some it is higher than the therapist's first assumption. The risk assessment is not the therapist's opinion; it is what the client's physician has documented, and part of the therapist's work is to get the client to a physician.

Step 3 — Separate shame from risk. The commonest presentation is a client who is not avoiding disclosure because of danger but because of shame and the fear of being seen as dirty, unfaithful, or diseased. Naming this distinction changes the work: "I am not asking you to disclose because you have done something wrong. I am asking you because there is a person who could be infected and who is not being given the chance to protect herself."

Step 4 — Use the partner-notification route if it exists. Where a service exists, it can be used anonymously or with the client's involvement, and it frequently resolves the case: the partner is told by a trained worker, the client is spared the first sentence, and the therapist's dilemma dissolves. This route is under-used simply because therapists do not know it exists.

Step 5 — Plan the disclosure hour by hour. Where the client will disclose, the plan is specific: when — not "soon", but a date and a time when the house is empty; who — alone, or with a trusted person; what — the diagnosis, the treatment, the risk to the partner, and the offer to attend a clinic together; and what if — the two worst-case responses, each with a plan, including where the client sleeps that night.

Step 6 — Where the client refuses and the risk remains. Then the therapist states her position in terms of her own conduct, in writing, and seeks immediate supervision and legal advice if the jurisdiction imposes a duty. Three options, in order of preference: the client discloses within a stated period; the client authorises partner notification; or the therapist discloses what the law requires and tells the client in advance that she will. What the therapist may not do is continue as though nothing has happened, because in this case the continuation of silence has a named potential victim and a documented date.

The vignette. Mrs Ifeoma Nwosu, 38, a seamstress, attends for anxiety. In session four she discloses that her husband, Emeka, was diagnosed eight months ago, is not on treatment, has refused to tell her, and she learned of it from a pharmacy receipt. Her question is not whether to tell him she knows; it is whether to leave. The therapist's tasks here are inverted: the exposed person is the client, the secret's holder is the absent husband, and the clinical work includes her own testing, her own risk, her own safety and the decision about the marriage. The therapist does not contact the husband. She supports the client in her own disclosure if the client chooses it, assists her to a clinic, and records the reasoning. The case then moves into Week 9's territory, because a woman whose husband has concealed a diagnosis for eight months is a woman whose household may become unsafe when she says so.

5.3a The third party's spouse: the question with no clean answer

The question. The client's affair partner is married. Her husband does not know. Does the therapist do anything?

What is not available. The therapist cannot contact him. That would be a disclosure of her client's confidential information to a stranger, and it is prohibited regardless of the moral merits. Any answer that begins "the therapist should tell him" has misunderstood the ethics of the role.

What is available. Four options, in ascending order of intrusiveness, and the therapist should be able to name all four and say which she would press for and why:

  1. The client tells him. The cleanest, rarest, and the one the therapist should raise first, explicitly and without embarrassment: "he is being deceived in the same way your wife was. Is that something you are willing to end?" Many clients have not considered it, having classified the affair partner's marriage as not their business.
  2. The client tells the affair partner that he will tell, or that she will, within a stated period. A pressure that is applied to the right person.
  3. The couple decides together, which happens more often than clinicians expect once the injured partner is past the acute phase: the betrayed spouse frequently wants the other household to know, and the decision is then a joint one that the therapist can support.
  4. Nobody tells him, and the therapist records that the question was raised, what the couple decided, and why. This is a defensible outcome and it must be a decision rather than an omission.

The safety filter that overrides the order. Where the affair partner's husband is known to be violent, the disclosure may expose the affair partner and the client to serious harm. In that case the correct answer is frequently option 4 with a documented reasoning, and the therapist who presses for disclosure has imported a moral position into a risk calculation.

The therapist's own view. The therapist is entitled to have one and is not entitled to impose it. The workable practice is to state it once, briefly, as a question rather than a verdict — "have you thought about what happens to his wife?" — and then to work with the answer.

5.4 Anatomy of the disclosure session

A structure for the session in which the affair is first discussed with both partners present. Ninety minutes, not fifty, and scheduled deliberately rather than arising inside a routine session.

PhaseMinutesWhat the therapist does
Frame0–8Names the purpose; states the rules (no detail beyond the necessary; no leaving the room; a break available on request); reminds both that no decision about the marriage is being made today
The account8–30The involved partner gives the outline, using the five questions. The therapist controls the pace and stops the account when it moves into detail that serves no safety function
The questions30–55The injured partner asks; the therapist filters — "that is a detail; ask instead what you need to know to feel safe"
The response55–70The injured partner's reaction, uninterrupted. The therapist does not manage it, soften it, or interpret it
The commitments70–85Concrete, verifiable, dated: contact ended, method of proof, transparency arrangements, who else is told
The close85–90Both partners state one thing they will do before the next session; the therapist summarises in five sentences and records them

The three rules of the room. First, the involved partner answers; the therapist does not answer for him. Second, the injured partner's anger is not moderated in this session — it is the appropriate response and moderating it protects the wrong person. Third, the session ends with commitments rather than with feelings, because commitments are what the injured partner actually needs and feelings are what the involved partner finds easier to offer.

5.5 Case: the Okonkwos, second year

The household. Ada Okonkwo, 35, a pharmacist; Chuka Okonkwo, 40, a logistics manager; Ebuka, 10. They were the family in Week 1, where the clinic's policy on individual disclosures was explained, recorded and agreed.

The referral. Ada telephones on a Tuesday morning. "I found something on his phone. I need to see you before I do anything." The therapist offers a same-day individual appointment, states the policy again at the start of it, and Ada agrees to it a second time.

What Ada knows. A message thread on Chuka's phone, seen four days ago. A woman's name, Nkechi. Messages that are affectionate and explicit, spanning roughly seven months. Ada has photographed eleven messages, has told no one, and has not confronted him. She has not slept more than four hours in four nights and has twice been unable to drive because of shaking. She has begun looking up divorce lawyers on her phone and has not called one.

The assessment in that session.

  • Acute stress: intrusive images, hypervigilance, sleep loss, appetite loss, intrusive checking of his phone at night. Not suicidal, but passive ideation on direct enquiry ("sometimes I think I should just disappear") which is assessed and planned for, and reviewed at every subsequent session.
  • Risk: no history of physical violence. Chuka has never been violent; he is, in Ada's description, "the one who goes quiet". Ada's own anger is substantial and she is frightened of it.
  • Disclosure history: the children know nothing. Ada's mother knows nothing. Ada's sister knows "something is wrong".
  • Immediate functioning: she cannot concentrate at work and has taken two days of leave.

The therapist's four tasks in that first session. Stabilise (sleep, food, the name of a person she can call at night). Separate the questions (is it happening; is it over; what do I do; who do I tell — four questions, and they cannot be answered in one week). Establish what she needs to know (whether it is ongoing, whether protection is used, whether there is a pregnancy, whether money has moved — the safety questions, and no others). And set the frame for the next session: "you will not decide anything this week. You will come back on Thursday, and we will decide what to do about the conversation with him."

Session two, three days later — the question of the individual session. Ada wants to see the therapist alone again, indefinitely, "to decide what I am going to do". The therapist applies Week 3: the couple is the client; individual sessions are permitted and are not a parallel therapy; and the plan must be that the disclosure moves into the couple's room. Ada agrees, with one condition — she wants the disclosure to happen in the therapist's office, not at home, because "at home I will either scream or say nothing".

Session three — Chuka alone. He attends, having been told by Ada only that she has "seen something" and that they have an appointment. He is guarded, then, at minute nineteen, says: "I know why I am here."

What he discloses: a relationship of seven months with a colleague, Nkechi, who is also married and has two children. He says it ended "in a manner of speaking" three weeks ago. He has not told Ada. He says he intends to, "when things are calmer". He says his marriage has been "dead for two years". He asks, twice, whether the therapist will tell Ada.

The therapist's answers, in order. "I will not tell her what you have told me. And I am not willing to hold this while I sit with both of you — which means it needs to move, and the shape of that is what we will decide now." She does not accept "when things are calmer", because there is no calmer. She asks the safety questions: protection, pregnancy, money, and the affair partner's marriage. She asks what he has told Ada about the last seven months — the lies, specifically — because that is the material the disclosure session will have to address. And she says the sentence that determines the case: "you will tell her, in my room, on Thursday. I will help you prepare what you say. She has already seen the messages, so this is not a revelation; it is the truth after four days of her not knowing whether she is going mad."

Session four — the disclosure session, ninety minutes. Both present. Ada speaks first, for six minutes, without interruption: what she saw, what the four days have been like, and the sentence she has rehearsed — "what I need from today is not an explanation, it is the truth." Chuka then answers the five questions. He gives the outline: seven months, a colleague, twice-weekly, ending three weeks ago — although Ada's messages suggest contact nine days ago, which he then corrects. He names the lies: the late nights, the "team retreat", the weekend he said he was with his brother. He states what he has done: message sent ending it, contact blocked, and an agreement to move teams at work next month. He states what he has not done: he has not told Nkechi's husband. And he answers Ada's question, "why", with the sentence that becomes the centre of the treatment: "because I did not want to be the man who had failed, so I stopped telling you anything."

The detail question, in the session. Ada asks, at minute forty, "was she better than me?" The therapist intervenes, exactly as the protocol requires: "that is a detail, and I am going to stop it — not to protect him, but because you will carry the answer for years and it will not tell you anything you need. What you need to know is whether he wants to be married to you, and that is a question he has to answer." Ada accepts this, and later says it was the most useful sentence in the session.

The commitments at minute seventy. Four, all dated. Chuka sends Ada a written account of the timeline by Saturday. He books and attends an STI screen within ten days and shows her the result. He agrees to leave his phone in the kitchen at night for three months. He agrees to a joint session every week for eight weeks. And on the fourth point — telling Nkechi's husband — he agrees to decide by the following Thursday, and the therapist records the question as open.

The next eight sessions. The treatment is not about the affair. It is about the two years in which Chuka stopped telling Ada anything, and about his belief that a man who admits failure loses his household, which is his father's belief and which he has never examined. Ada's work is separate and harder: she has to decide whether she can live with a man who lied competently for seven months, and the therapist's job is to keep that question open rather than to sell the marriage. In session eleven Ada says, "I do not trust him. I have decided to see whether trust comes back." That sentence, not the affair, is the case's turning point.

What the case teaches.

  1. The individual session was the instrument, not the problem. Without three individual sessions — Ada's two and Chuka's one — there would have been no prepared disclosure, and the conversation at home would have been the screaming match Ada predicted.
  2. The policy was stated in Week 1 and applied in Week 5. The therapist could hold Chuka to a disclosure because the clinic's policy had been explained to both of them a year earlier and recorded. A therapist who improvised the policy on the day would have been negotiating from nothing.
  3. The safety questions are asked of both partners, separately. Ada was asked about violence, ideation and functioning; Chuka was asked about protection, pregnancy, money and the affair partner's marriage. Neither set of questions would have been answerable with both in the room.
  4. The disclosure session was structured, and the structure was the therapy. Frame, account, questions, response, commitments, close: six phases and ninety minutes, and none of it was improvised.
  5. The treatment's subject was not the affair. The affair was the event; the marriage's two-year silence was the case. Finding that distinction is the clinical work of session three.

5.6 Transcript: the refusal of "when things are calmer"

Session three, Chuka alone, minutes twenty-two to forty. Annotated.

C1 Chuka: I will tell her. When things are calmer. She is not in a state to hear it now. C2 Therapist: (pause) There is no calmer. — Four words, and the refusal to accept the most common postponement in this literature. "When things are calmer" is not a plan; it is a description of the client's hope that the problem will become smaller on its own. C3 Chuka: You do not know her. She will — (stops) C4 Therapist: I do know her a little. I saw her on Tuesday. (pause) She has not slept in four nights and she has been photographing your phone. She is already in it, Chuka. The only question is whether she is in it alone. — The move that ends the postponement: not an argument about duty, but a description of what the delay is actually costing the person the client says he is protecting. C5 Chuka: (long pause) I did not think of it like that. C6 Therapist: Most people do not. (pause) Tell me about the last seven months. Not the relationship — the things you said to her. — The pivot from the affair to the deception. This is the technical heart of the session: the affair is what happened; the lies are what the disclosure must address. C7 Chuka: I said I was working late. I said the retreat was in Abuja. I said — (pause) — I said I was tired. That one was true. C8 Therapist: So there was a true thing in it. — A reflection that does something precise: it neither excuses nor condemns, and it registers that the client's marriage was not simply a lie. C9 Chuka: (quickly) It is not her fault. Ada. It is not her fault. C10 Therapist: I did not say it was. (pause) But you have just told me you were afraid of being the man who failed. Where does that come from? — The question that opens the third session's real material, asked without any suggestion that it excuses the affair. Note that the therapist first refuses the implication (C10, first sentence) and then asks the question, in that order — reversing the order would have sounded like an excuse. C11 Chuka: My father. He was — he lost his job when I was twelve, and my mother's brothers took over the house. I watched him become nothing. In our place, a man who cannot provide is — he is not a man. C12 Therapist: (pause) And in seven months, did you become him? C13 Chuka: (long pause) Every time I came home. — The sentence the case will be built on, arrived at in session three, without the therapist having proposed it. C14 Therapist: Then we have something to work with. (pause) On Thursday you will tell Ada the truth about the seven months, and I will be in the room, and this — what you have just said — is what we will be working on afterwards. — The two-part close: the disclosure is not negotiable, and the treatment is promised. Both parts are necessary; either alone fails.

What the transcript shows. The refusal of the postponement took eleven words and no moral argument. The relevant contrast is with the therapist who says "you have an obligation to tell her", which invites a debate about obligations; the therapist here says what the delay is doing, which cannot be debated because it is observable.

5.7 Laboratory: three secrets, three protocols

Aim. To produce, in ninety minutes, three one-page protocols — infidelity, paternity, HIV — for a named practice, each with the clinical sequence, the referral trigger, the documentation and the jurisdictional question.

Procedure. Groups of four, each group assigned one secret, twenty minutes to draft, then ten minutes to present to the other groups, who must find the missing element. Each group must also write the sentence it would use to explain the limit of confidentiality to a client in that case.

Marks (20).

Criterion0–12–34–5
SequenceNo orderOrder present but not justifiedOrder justified by exposure and by the client's capacity
Referral triggerNoneVague ("refer if needed")A specific question for a specific professional, written out
JurisdictionNot addressedMentionedThe four questions named, with a source and a review date
DocumentationNothingNarrativeClassification, reasoning, decision, date, supervision
The sentenceMoralising or evasiveAccurate but coldAccurate, brief, and delivered without blame

The commonest failure. Groups write "the therapist should advise the client to disclose". That is advice about a third party's conduct, it is outside the role, and it is marked in the lowest band however sound the underlying ethics.

5.8 Six errors

Error 1 — Adjudicating the affair. "What he did was wrong" is true, and said in session one it makes the therapist the injured partner's ally and the involved partner's judge — and the involved partner, who is necessary to the repair, stops speaking.

Error 2 — Answering the detail questions. The injured partner asks, the therapist permits, and both carry images for years. The filter — facts necessary for safety and choice — must be applied by the therapist, in advance, out loud.

Error 3 — Treating paternity as a therapy question. The therapist who opines on maintenance, custody or registration has left her competence and possibly her indemnity. The referral is written down, with the question specified.

Error 4 — Assuming the local HIV rule from memory. The rules differ, they change, and the consequences of being wrong are criminal. Four questions in the practice manual, reviewed annually.

Error 5 — Continuing a treatment that is being conducted around an undisclosed exposure. The therapist who sees the couple weekly while the husband's diagnosis remains hidden from the wife is not neutral; she is a party to the exposure.

Error 6 — Deciding the third-party question by omission. The affair partner's spouse is either told, or the question is raised and recorded as decided against. What is not permissible is never raising it.

5.9 Culture, faith and the three secrets

Infidelity and the shape of the household. In a polygynous or extended household, the definition of infidelity is not the therapist's to set. A second relationship may be adultery to one wife, a breach of an agreed arrangement to another, and a family matter to be resolved by elders to a third. The therapist's first task is to ask what the couple's own understanding was — what was promised, to whom, and in front of whom — because the injury is measured against the promise and not against the therapist's model of marriage.

The elders' jurisdiction. Where a family council has authority, the couple may want the matter resolved there rather than in the therapy room, and the therapist may be asked to attend or to report. Attending a family council is sometimes possible and requires a clear contract about confidentiality; reporting to it almost never is. The workable answer is that the therapist will help the couple prepare what they will say to the elders.

Paternity and lineage. In households where lineage carries inheritance, naming and belonging, the disclosure of non-paternity is not only a relational event; it can alter a child's standing in a family and a community. This raises the stakes and does not change the criterion — the child's welfare — but it changes the timing, the audience and the planning. The therapist who treats it as a Western-style family secret has underestimated the case.

HIV, blame and the church. Where a diagnosis is read theologically — as judgement, as a test of faith, or as a punishment on a household — disclosure carries a second injury. Congregational responses vary from extraordinary care to exclusion, and the client knows which is likely in her own congregation better than the therapist does. Asking "if this became known in your church, what would happen?" produces information that belongs in the risk assessment.

The communitarian objection. "In our culture, family matters are not discussed outside." This is true and it is not an answer to the exposure question. The therapist's response separates the two: "I agree that this is family business. The question I am asking is not whether to keep it in the family — it is whether the person who is being exposed is inside the information or outside it."

DEBATE — "When a client refuses to disclose an HIV-positive status to a partner, the therapist's duty to the partner overrides confidentiality." Argue for: an identifiable person is at risk, the client's refusal converts the therapist into a participant, and public health law in many jurisdictions supports disclosure. Argue against: disclosure by the therapist destroys the confidence on which testing and treatment depend, the partner-notification route exists precisely to avoid it, and the therapist's duty is defined by statute and not by the therapist's conscience. Then state the rule you would work under and the jurisdiction you would work in.

5.9a The referral note to a lawyer

Therapists refer to lawyers constantly and badly. A referral that says "client needs legal advice" produces a lawyer who cannot begin. A referral that specifies the question produces advice the client can use.

The four questions worth writing down. First: what is the client's legal position in respect of the children if the facts are as stated? Second: what must the client do, or refrain from doing, in the next thirty days to avoid prejudicing that position? Third: what records exist, and can any of them be compelled? Fourth: what does the client need to know about the timing of any disclosure?

What the therapist does not put in the note. Her clinical opinion of the parties, her view of who is at fault, and any speculation about facts the client has not established. The note is a question, not a case formulation, and the lawyer's file may become a document in the proceedings.

What the therapist records afterwards. That the referral was made, on what date, with what question, and that the client was advised to obtain advice before making any decision with legal consequences. Six lines. They are the difference between a therapist who acted within her competence and one who did not.

5.10 Evidence note

What the research supports. Affair recovery research describes a reasonably consistent trajectory of acute stress, meaning-making and rebuild-or-end, with the injured partner's intrusive symptoms typically prominent for weeks and the couple's trust rebuilt through consistent verifiable behaviour rather than through reassurance. Disclosure of HIV status is associated with better treatment engagement and lower transmission risk, and partner-notification services increase disclosure rates; the public health literature is clear that supporting voluntary disclosure outperforms mandatory approaches on most measures. The research on secrecy in families shows that maintained family secrets are associated with poorer wellbeing in the person holding them, and that disclosure is most damaging when it is discovered rather than told.

What is weaker. There are no trials determining the optimal content of an affair disclosure, and the clinical debate between full disclosure and a facts-only approach is not settled empirically. Paternity disclosure has a small qualitative literature and almost no outcome data, largely because the situations are heterogeneous.

What is not supported. That a couple cannot recover from an affair — the outcome data on couple therapy following infidelity is modest but positive. That disclosure of HIV status always improves relationships — it can precipitate violence, which is why safety planning precedes disclosure. And that the therapist's judgement about the moral seriousness of the secret should determine the urgency; the exposure assessment does that.

The matrix cell. Development is probabilistic and non-linear; the same input produces different outcomes depending on timing and context. Seven months of an affair discovered by a phone message, and seven months disclosed by a client in session twenty, are the same fact with two different prognoses.

5.11 Key terms

Betrayal trauma — the traumatic stress response to the discovery of a significant relational betrayal.

Disclosure scene — the first structured conversation in which the affair is discussed with both partners.

Exposure assessment — the identification of who is put at risk by the continuation of a silence.

Presumption of paternity — the legal presumption that a child born within a marriage is the husband's; jurisdiction-specific.

Partner notification — the public-health route by which a partner is informed of exposure, anonymously or with the index client's involvement.

Viral suppression — the reduction of viral load to undetectable levels; relevant to risk assessment and not a substitute for disclosure.

Safety filter — the rule that where disclosure would create a risk of violence, the risk governs the plan.

Third-party claim — the moral (not legal) claim of a non-client on information that affects them.

5.12 Self-test

  1. State the common structure of the three secrets in this week.
  2. What are the four questions the practice manual must answer on communicable disease?
  3. Which detail questions may the therapist permit, and which must she stop?
  4. Why is "when things are calmer" not a plan?
  5. Name the four options for the affair partner's spouse, and the filter that overrides them.
  6. What does the therapist do when paternity is in question and the client asks for advice about custody?
  7. Give the six phases of the disclosure session with their timings.
  8. In the Okonkwo case, what was the treatment actually about?

Answers. (1) An established fact, a person who does not know it, and a third party exposed by the continuing silence. (2) Whether there is a duty to a public health authority and within what period; whether there is a duty or permission to disclose to an identifiable partner; whether non-disclosure or exposure is an offence; and whether a partner-notification service exists and how it is accessed. (3) Permit facts necessary for safety and choice — duration, protection, whether contact continues, whether the affair partner's household is affected; stop questions about sexual practice, comparison, and the other person's appearance. (4) Because it names a state of affairs that will not arrive on its own and does not specify who does what by when; the delay is itself the harm. (5) The client tells him; the client tells the affair partner that he will tell; the couple decides together; or nobody tells him and the reasoning is recorded. The safety filter — a risk of violence — overrides all four. (6) She says she does not give legal advice, writes the client's question down for a lawyer, and records that she did so. (7) Frame 0–8; account 8–30; questions 30–55; response 55–70; commitments 70–85; close 85–90. (8) The two years in which Chuka stopped telling Ada anything, and his belief that a man who admits failure loses his household.

5.13 Reflective task

Write three hundred words on the following: a couple arrives having discovered an affair, and you find yourself hoping they will separate. What in your own history is producing that hope, and how would you know if it had begun to shape your questions? Do not resolve the question. Locate it.

5.14 Further reading

  • Glass, S. on the anatomy of affairs and on Not "Just Friends" — the standard clinical account of boundaries and affair types.
