  • Gottman, J. on trust and betrayal in couples — the framework for the trust-rebuilding tasks.
  • Perel, E. on the meaning of affairs — the counterweight to purely behavioural accounts, useful for the third session's work.
  • The WHO guidance on HIV partner notification and the UNAIDS materials on disclosure — the source for the pathway in section 5.3.
  • Any current local statute or professional guidance on communicable disease disclosure, read in the original and filed in the practice manual.
  • A family law primer for your jurisdiction, for the reference list and not for advice.

Next: Week 6 — Boundaries and multiple relationships.

Week 6 — Boundaries and multiple relationships

Learning outcomes. By the end of this week the student can (1) distinguish a boundary crossing from a boundary violation and explain why the difference is the whole of the topic; (2) apply the four tests to any multiple relationship and reach a defensible decision; (3) manage the unavoidable dual relationships of small- community practice without either pretending they do not exist or abandoning the client; (4) state and defend a personal rule for touch, self-disclosure, gifts, bartering and digital contact; and (5) recognise and manage sexual attraction to a client as a professional event rather than a personal failing.

The distinction the week is built on

A boundary crossing is a departure from the usual frame that is made for the client's benefit, is discussed, and is reversible. A boundary violation is a departure that serves the therapist, is concealed, and often is not reversible. The two look similar in a textbook and are entirely different in a file.

CrossingViolation
Whose needThe client'sThe therapist's
DisclosedTo the client, to supervision, to the recordConcealed, or minimised
ReversibleYes — the frame can be restoredRarely; the relationship is altered
ExampleA fifteen-minute extension in a crisis, noted and discussedSessions that routinely run long because the therapist enjoys them
ExampleAttending a former client's wedding after discharge, with the frame discussedAttending a current client's family event as a guest
ExampleAccepting a bowl of fruit at the end of a course of treatmentAccepting a car

The pedagogy follows from this: students who are taught boundaries as prohibitions become anxious and rigid, and rigid therapists fail their clients at exactly the moments when flexibility is clinical. Students who are taught the crossing/violation distinction can think, and thinking is what the situation will require.

Why this topic is examined so heavily in the codes. Because boundary violations are the commonest route by which therapists harm clients, and because the sequence is well documented: a gradual erosion, always initiated by small kindnesses, always rationalised as therapeutic, and almost always preceded by isolation from supervision. The literature on therapist sexual misconduct finds that the great majority of cases were preceded by non-sexual boundary crossings over months, and that colleagues often noticed and said nothing.

The error that costs most in this subject. Teaching the topic as though it were about sex. It is about power, and the sexual cases are the endpoint of a sequence that begins with the therapist becoming special.

6.1 The lecture in ninety minutes

MinutesContentTeaching form
0–10Crossing and violation: five paired examples, sorted by the class before any theoryLive sorting
10–22Five concepts: power, role conflict, the slope, the small community, the felt senseLecture, board diagram
22–40The four tests and the decision matrix, applied to eight scenariosSmall groups, then plenary votes
40–55The unavoidable dual relationship: the community contractDemonstration of the opening conversation
55–70Touch, gifts, barter, home visits, digital contact: the rule and the rationaleLecture with cases
70–80Attraction: recognition, disclosure to supervision, and the decision treeShort lecture; written anonymous question
80–90The Alabi caseCase presentation, students decide

6.2 Five concepts that organise the week

Concept 1 — Boundaries exist because the relationship is asymmetric. The client tells the therapist what she has told no one; the therapist tells the client almost nothing. The client depends on the therapist's judgement; the therapist depends on the client for a fee. That asymmetry is not an unfortunate feature of therapy; it is the mechanism by which therapy works, and every boundary in this week's material exists to protect it. A boundary that is justified by "professionalism" in the abstract will not survive pressure. A boundary justified by the asymmetry will, because the therapist can see what it protects.

Concept 2 — Role conflict degrades judgement before it degrades conduct. The therapist who is also the client's landlord does not begin by acting badly; she begins by thinking differently. She notices that she is reluctant to challenge the client about the rent, or that she is unusually warm about the client's financial difficulty. The judgement goes first. This is why role conflicts are examined at the point they are created and not at the point they produce harm.

Concept 3 — The slope is real, and it is walked one small step at a time. No therapist decides to have a sexual relationship with a client. A therapist decides to take one phone call, then to meet for coffee "to discuss the crisis", then to accept a lift, and at each point the previous step has made the next one seem continuous. The clinical use of the concept is not to frighten students but to give them a rule: the sequence is interrupted at the first crossing, when it is cheap, and the interruption is a conversation rather than an act of will.

Concept 4 — In small communities, multiple relationships are not avoidable; they are manageable. In a city of eight million a therapist can decline a case and the client will find someone else. In a town of thirty thousand with two trained therapists, refusing the mayor's family can mean the family receives no service. The ethical question therefore shifts from may I avoid this? to how do I manage this honestly? — which is a harder question and the one this week spends most of its time on.

Concept 5 — "It felt right" is not a test. The felt sense is a poor guide in this domain because the therapist's warmth, the client's gratitude and the pleasure of being needed all feel like evidence of a good relationship. The test is external: whose need is served, what would supervision say, what would the client say in five years, and is it in the record?

6.2b The five-minute boundary decision

The four tests produce a decision, and in practice the therapist has five minutes and no matrix to hand. The compressed version, used in the room, is four questions asked in this order.

Question one — what would I have to stop doing? The concrete loss is the fastest indicator of a role conflict. A therapist who realises that she could no longer challenge the client about money has found the conflict without needing a theory.

Question two — who would know first? If the answer is "nobody", the arrangement has already failed. Every defensible dual relationship in this chapter was declared before it began; every indefensible one was discovered.

Question three — can the client say no? A favour, an offer of employment, a discount: each is a proposal made by a person the client depends on. If the client cannot refuse without cost, the arrangement is exploitation whatever its intention.

Question four — what is my sentence? The therapist must be able to say, in two sentences and without embarrassment, what the arrangement is and why. A clinician who cannot construct this sentence has not decided anything; she has drifted, and drift is the mechanism of the whole chapter.

What to do with the answer in the five minutes. Three outcomes only. Proceed with the contract write-up. Decline with the four-part script. Or say "I need to think about this and I will tell you on Thursday", which is always available, is never wrong, and is the answer most often forgotten under pressure.

The one arrangement that cannot be decided in five minutes. Any proposal that involves money, employment, or a sexual or romantic dimension — including a proposal that arrives from the client, obliquely, in a sentence that could be read two ways. Those go to supervision before any answer is given, and the client is told that the therapist is thinking about it, which is true and which is not a rejection.

6.2a The four tests, and the decision they produce

The four tests from the scaffold are the right ones. What follows is how to use them so that they produce a decision rather than a discussion.

  1. Objectivity. Could the second relationship impair my judgement about this client's treatment? Not "will it" — could it. The answer is almost always yes in some degree, and the useful question is what specifically would change.
  2. Exploitation. Could the second relationship exploit the power difference? A loan to a client, a business partnership, a favour that the client cannot refuse — each is exploitation even where the therapist's motive is generous.
  3. Foresight. Would the client experience this as exploitative later? This is the test that is easiest to answer dishonestly and most useful when answered in the client's voice. "In three years, telling a friend about her therapy, how would she describe this?"
  4. Avoidability. Is the second relationship avoidable without depriving the client of the service? A gift from a bereaved family is not really avoidable; a business partnership is.

The decision matrix.

Objectivity impaired?Exploitation possible?Avoidable?Decision
NoNoYesDecline, briefly and without a lecture
SlightlyNoYesDecline; discuss in supervision
YesNoNoProceed with a written contract and supervision review
YesYesNoProceed only with a formal risk management plan, documented, reviewed monthly
YesYesYesDo not proceed. Transfer the case

The three columns that matter. Written contract: the conversation with the client about how the two roles will be handled, recorded in the file in the client's presence and with the client's agreement. Supervision review: a named supervisor who knows about the arrangement and a date at which it is reconsidered. Transfer: the option that therapists resist and that is frequently correct.

The sentence for declining. "I am not able to take this on, and I want to tell you why so that it does not feel like a rejection: I know your family socially, and that would make it hard for me to be a good therapist to you. I can give you two names." Brief, honest, and it does not invite negotiation. The therapist who explains at length has made the client responsible for accepting the refusal.

6.3 The unavoidable multiple relationship: the community contract

Where the therapist and client will inevitably meet in another role — the same church, the same school, a shared extended family, a village — the work is not avoidance but an explicit contract, made at the start, recorded, and revisited.

The four elements of the contract.

  1. The naming. "We are going to see each other at church, and at your sister's shop, and probably at the market. I want us to agree now how we will handle that, so that neither of us is caught out."
  2. The greeting rule. Agreed in advance: the therapist will greet the client normally and will not refer to therapy; the client may decide whether to acknowledge the therapist first; and the therapist will follow the client's lead in public. This small rule prevents the commonest injury, which is a client left standing in a market while her therapist pretends not to see her.
  3. The information rule. What the therapist will do with information acquired in the community role. "If your mother tells me at church that you have lost your job, I will not raise it in session as though you had told me. I will wait for you to bring it, or I will ask whether you want to talk about it." This is the single most useful clause in the contract and almost never written.
  4. The review. A date at which the contract is looked at again, because the community roles change — the client's daughter joins the therapist's son's class, the client is elected to the church committee that employs the therapist's husband.

What the contract does not solve. It does not remove the judgement problem. A therapist who is a deacon and a therapist in the same congregation has two sources of authority over a client, and the contract manages the boundary without dissolving the role conflict. Where the conflict is structural — the therapist holds a leadership role in a community the client belongs to — referral is the better answer, and saying so is not a failure.

6.3a The role conflicts that recur

The client who is a colleague. A fellow therapist, a nurse, a teacher in the same school. The specific hazards: the colleague knows the therapist's reputation, may be supervisee or supervisor, and will meet the therapist professionally after treatment ends. The management: an explicit discussion of what will happen at professional meetings, no treatment of the colleague by a person who has authority over them, and no use of the colleague's clinical knowledge in the treatment.

The client who is a student. Power is at its maximum and cannot be contracted away. A therapist who teaches the client, examines the client, or supervises the client's placement should not also treat them, and the correct answer is referral. Where a training clinic has no alternative, the treatment is conducted by someone with no assessment authority over the student, and this is written down.

The client referred by a friend. The friend will ask how it is going. The therapist's answer, agreed in advance with the client: "she is attending and I am not able to discuss her." Said once to the friend, in the first week, without apology. The alternative — "it is going well" — is a disclosure.

The client whose family employs the therapist's spouse. A genuine conflict of interest in small economies. The test: would the therapist be able to challenge the client's behaviour if the client's family were paying her household? Where the answer is no, transfer.

The former client who becomes a friend. The codes differ, and the safest practice is that the relationship does not convert, or converts only after a substantial interval and with the recognition that the client may return to treatment. A former client who is now a friend cannot come back as a client, and the therapist should say so at the point the friendship is contemplated rather than at the point it is needed.

The client who becomes famous, or whose case becomes public. The therapist's obligation not to use the material does not lapse when the case is in the newspapers, and the temptation is at its maximum precisely then.

6.3b The referral conversation, scripted

Declining is a technique, and like any technique it improves with rehearsal. The four-part script below takes about two minutes and leaves the client with a service rather than a refusal.

Part one — the decision, stated first. "I have thought about this and I am not going to be able to take the work on." First, because a client who hears the explanation first spends it looking for the loophole.

Part two — the reason, in terms of the client's interest. "The reason is that I know your family outside this room. If I were your therapist I would already know things about your husband, and you would be watching what you said to me. That would make the therapy worse, not better."

Part three — the alternatives, specific. Two names, with a sentence about each, and an offer to telephone ahead: "Dr Bello works with couples and has a clinic on Tuesdays; Mrs Ojo is closer to you and works evenings. I can call either of them today and tell them that you will be in touch, without telling them anything about you."

Part four — the door. "If neither works, come back to me and I will find you a third name. What I will not do is leave you with nothing."

What the client usually says, and the answer. "Is it because of something I did?" — "No. It is about me and where I worship, and nothing about you." "You are the only one who understands." — "I have met you twice; what you are responding to is that somebody listened. Someone else will listen, and they will be able to hear more than I can."

6.4 Touch, gifts, self-disclosure, barter, home visits and the telephone

Each of these is sometimes therapeutic and always requires a rationale that can be spoken aloud in supervision. The rule for each is stated here as a personal rule, because a therapist who has not pre-decided will decide inside the moment, and the moment is designed to make the generous option feel right.

Touch. The rule: touch is used only where it is a recognised part of the therapeutic method being practised, or where it is the ordinary human response to acute distress and is brief and public. A hand on the shoulder of a woman who has just been told her husband is dying, in a room with a window, is not a boundary violation. A twenty-minute embrace at the end of every session is. The rationale: touch cannot be retracted, is interpreted through the client's history — particularly where the client has been abused — and is the commonest first step in the documented sequence toward sexual misconduct.

Gifts. The rule: small, culturally appropriate, given at a transition (the end of treatment, a bereavement, a birth), accepted with thanks and recorded. Anything of substantial value, anything given early in treatment, and anything that creates an obligation is declined with an explanation that protects the giver. The test: is the gift a communication or a transaction? In this region, food and cloth carry meaning and refusing them can be an insult; the therapist who declines a bowl of fruit to prove a point has damaged an alliance for an abstraction. The recorded note is short: "client brought fruit at the end of the eighth session; accepted, meaning discussed, recorded."

Self-disclosure. The rule: brief, about the therapist's experience rather than the client's, serving the client's work, and never requiring the client to care for the therapist. The test: if the client responded by consoling the therapist, would the disclosure have been appropriate? If not, it should not be made.

Barter. The rule: almost never. Where a client has no money and the alternative is no treatment, a barter arrangement may be ethical, and it requires a written value, a stated duration, and a supervision review. The specific hazard is that the client's service is now a clinical topic — the therapist who accepts tailoring from a client will have opinions about the tailoring.

Home visits. The rule: a home visit is a clinical intervention with a purpose, a time limit, and a documented rationale. Family therapy occasionally requires it: a home reveals what a clinic cannot. The visit is arranged in advance, attended with a plan, and recorded, and the therapist does not accept hospitality beyond water, does not stay for a meal unless the meal is the intervention, and does not go alone where safety is uncertain.

The telephone and the message. The rule: the channel is specified at the start, with its limits — "you may call between nine and six; if it is urgent and I am not available, go to the emergency number; I do not respond to messages after nine at night." The rule exists because the alternative is a therapist who is available at all hours to a client who has no other support, which is not a boundary the therapist can sustain and not a service the client can rely on.

Digital contact. The rule: no personal social media contact with current clients; no searching for a client online without a clinical reason and a record of it; no clinical material over unencrypted channels; and an explicit statement in the first session of what the therapist will and will not do online. The specific hazard of the photograph: a client's social media reveals information the therapist did not obtain clinically and cannot forget. Where the therapist has seen it, the fact is recorded, because the alternative is a treatment silently shaped by material the client never gave.

6.4a The boundary log

A one-page document, kept by the therapist, reviewed monthly in supervision. Five columns: date; the crossing; whose need it served; what was said about it to the client and to the supervisor; and whether the frame was restored.

Why a log rather than a memory. Because crossings are forgettable and their accumulation is the phenomenon worth noticing. A therapist who can see eleven small extensions over four months is looking at a pattern that no single session reveals, and the pattern is the early part of the slope.

What belongs in it. The session that ran twenty minutes over because a client disclosed a bereavement. The gift that was accepted. The lift home given in the rain. The client's child's school fee that the therapist "lent". The text message answered at eleven at night. Each is small, and the log's purpose is not to punish any of them.

What the review asks. Three questions. Is the same client appearing repeatedly? Is the same kind of crossing recurring with different clients, which points at the therapist rather than the case? And has anything been crossed that was not told to supervision, which is the only entry that is a violation rather than a crossing?

6.5 Sexual boundaries, and what to do with attraction

The absolute. Sexual contact with a current client is prohibited in every code this book draws on and is a criminal offence in many jurisdictions. There is no clinical argument, no cultural exception, and no case in which it is defensible. Sexual contact with a former client is prohibited by most codes for a stated period — commonly two years, sometimes five, sometimes permanently — and the period is a minimum rather than a permission.

Why the prohibition is absolute. Because consent is compromised by the asymmetry, and because the harm is severe and durable. Clients who have been sexually involved with a therapist show outcomes comparable to those of survivors of sexual assault by someone trusted: betrayal, confusion about their own responsibility, damage to their capacity to trust a helper, and in a substantial proportion, a subsequent suicide attempt. This is the single most harmful thing a therapist can do, and the research on it is not ambiguous.

Attraction is not the problem. Most therapists will feel drawn to a client at some point in a career, and the feeling is neither a symptom of impairment nor a predictor of misconduct. The problem is silence about it. The documented pathway to violation runs through secrecy: the therapist who cannot say "I am aware of feeling drawn to this client" to a supervisor is the therapist at risk.

What to do, concretely, in the week it happens.

  1. Name it to yourself and write one line in a private note. The act of writing reduces the intensity and creates a record that can be discussed.
  2. Tell supervision, in the same words: "I am aware of attraction to this client and I want to talk about how it is affecting the work." Supervisors should make this easy by asking routinely, because the first time it is said should not be the first time it is possible.
  3. Increase structure, not distance. Do not abruptly become cold, which the client experiences as unexplained rejection and which often produces the pursuit the therapist fears. Keep the frame, keep the notes, keep the seating, and keep the session ending on time.
  4. Examine the material. Attraction frequently carries information: the client resembles someone; the client is providing the admiration the therapist's life is not; the case is a repetition of an earlier one. The information is clinically useful and belongs to the therapist's own work, not to the client.
  5. Refer, if the feeling is not manageable. Not as a punishment. The sentence: "I think you would be better served by a colleague, and I want to tell you that this is about my capacity and not about you."

Where a client declares love. The response is kind, brief, unambiguous, and does not interpret the client's feeling as pathology. "I am not able to be that for you, and I am not going to pretend I do not understand what you are asking. What I can be is your therapist, and I think that is worth more to you than the other thing." Then the clinical work: what the declaration is about, which is usually loneliness, gratitude, or a history in which care and romance have been the same thing.

6.5a What the codes actually say, and where they differ

The codes converge on the absolute prohibition and diverge on almost everything else, which is why a therapist moving between jurisdictions must read the version that governs her.

On sexual contact with current clients. Universal prohibition, and in a growing number of jurisdictions a criminal matter rather than a professional one. There is no divergence to report.

On sexual contact with former clients. The divergence is real: some codes impose a fixed interval — two years, sometimes five — others require the therapist to demonstrate that the contact is not exploitative, and some prohibit it permanently where the treatment was long or the client vulnerable. A therapist in a jurisdiction with a two-year rule who treats the interval as permission has misunderstood the rule, which is a minimum evidentiary threshold and never a warrant.

On non-sexual multiple relationships. The codes permit them where they are not exploitative and do not impair judgement or risk harm, and they require the therapist to document the reasoning. What none of them supplies is a list, and the absence is deliberate: the categories are local.

On gifts. Most codes say nothing explicit and the professional bodies' guidance varies from "small and culturally appropriate" to a value threshold in local currency. The therapist should know her own body's wording because a complaint will be assessed against it.

On barter. Permitted in some codes where the arrangement is not exploitative, is entered at the client's initiative, and is documented; discouraged in others.

On social media. The most recent guidance and the least settled. The consistent threads: no personal accounts for clinical use, no searching without clinical justification, and a written statement to the client at intake.

On touch. Almost entirely absent, which is why the reasoning in section 6.4 has to be constructed from the general principles rather than looked up.

6.6 Case: the Alabis, and the deacon's wife

The setting. A private practice in a state capital, and a church of about three hundred members where the therapist has worshipped for eleven years, sings in the choir, and is known to almost everyone as "the counsellor".

The referral. Mrs Yetunde Alabi, 44, a school administrator, is referred by her physician for depression following a miscarriage at fourteen weeks. Her husband, Deacon Samuel Alabi, 49, is a church officer and a friend of the therapist's husband. The couple have three children, 17, 14 and 9. The referral arrives by telephone from the physician, who does not know the therapist's church.

The first decision, before the first session. The therapist recognises the name and applies the four tests. Objectivity: impaired — she knows Samuel socially, has opinions about him, and has heard him preach. Exploitation: possible — she holds lay religious standing in a congregation where the family worships. Avoidability: yes, in principle; there are four other therapists within reasonable distance. Foresight: the client, in three years, describing this therapy to a friend, would say "she is in my church". The matrix says: decline or transfer.

What actually happened, and why it is instructive. The therapist telephoned the physician and said she could not take the referral, giving the reason in one sentence. The physician asked whether that was not excessive: the family had asked for her by name. The therapist said it was not excessive and gave two names. The family, through Samuel, telephoned the therapist directly three days later and asked her to reconsider — "we would not be comfortable with a stranger; you are one of us."

The therapist's decision at that point is the pedagogically interesting one. She did not simply repeat the refusal. She offered a consultation: one meeting, with both of them, for the sole purpose of explaining the conflict, giving the two referrals, and — this is the part that matters — asking whether they wanted her to speak to the named therapist in advance so that the family would not have to begin from nothing. She did not take the case.

Why the refusal was right, in four sentences. Because the client's miscarriage, her grief and whatever is turning in her marriage will include material about her husband that she must be able to say to someone who is not in his network. Because the therapist would meet Samuel at a men's fellowship on Saturday and would be carrying his wife's account of him. Because a church of three hundred people cannot keep a secret, and the therapist's own presence in the congregation would make the client's attendance a piece of church news. And because the standing the therapist holds in that church is a form of authority the client cannot decline.

The alternative that was considered and rejected. The therapist considered taking the case with a community contract — the four elements of section 6.3 — and rejected it, for one reason: the contract manages the encounters and cannot manage the information. In a congregation, Samuel's friends will discuss his wife's depression with him, and the therapist will be present when they do.

The consultation, in practice. Fifty minutes, both present. The therapist said four things: that she was not going to be the therapist and that the reason was the church, not the family; that she was sorry, because she knew the refusal felt like a rejection and it was not one; that she would give two names and would speak to either of them if the family wished; and that she would do one more thing, which was to be clear that she would never discuss the family's business with anyone at church, including her own husband. The last sentence did more clinical work than the referrals.

The follow-up that makes the case worth teaching. Six weeks later, at a church event, the therapist met Yetunde. Yetunde said, in front of two other women, "I am seeing Dr Bello now. It is going well." The therapist said, "I am glad", and asked nothing. That is the contract, honoured without a rule being written, and it is what the family needed from her: a person who would hold the boundary in public without making them feel handled.

What the case teaches.

  1. The first decision is made before the first session, and it is made on the basis of the referral information, not on the basis of the client's preference.
  2. Refusal is a clinical act and it has a technique. Brief, specific, non-moralising, with alternatives offered and one offer of bridging.
  3. The family's request to be seen by a known person is not evidence that the arrangement is acceptable. It is evidence that they do not know what the arrangement would cost them. The therapist knows, which is why the decision is hers.
  4. The pressure to accept comes disguised as cultural competence. "You are one of us" is a real claim in a communal setting, and the answer is not to deny it but to explain why it makes the therapy harder rather than easier.

6.7 Transcript: the request to be a character witness

From a different case, the same week's material. A client, Mrs Ngozi Eze, 38, has been in treatment for eight months following her husband's desertion. She is in a custody dispute. Minutes thirty-one to forty-two.

E1 Client: My lawyer says I should ask you. He says a letter from you would help. Or you could come. E2 Therapist: (pause) Tell me what your lawyer wants me to say. E3 Client: That I am a good mother. That I am not — that I am not unstable. He said a therapist's letter carries weight. E4 Therapist: It does. (pause) And I am not going to write it, and I want to explain why properly rather than just say no. — The refusal is immediate, and the explanation is promised. The order matters: a delayed refusal invites the client to argue, and an unexplained refusal is heard as abandonment. E5 Client: Because of confidentiality. E6 Therapist: Partly. (pause) The bigger reason is that I would stop being your therapist the moment I became your witness. — The distinction between the rule and the reason. Clients can argue with a rule; they cannot easily argue with the loss of their therapist. E7 Client: I do not understand. E8 Therapist: If I write that letter, then everything we do from today is affected by it. I would be watching what I write in your notes in case a judge reads them. And you would be watching me. Our sessions would become a place where you perform being well, because I am the person who has to convince a court. That is not a therapy any more. — The mechanism, described concretely. This is the paragraph that most students remember, because it is the one that shows them why the rule exists rather than merely that it does. E9 Client: (pause) So what do I do? He is going to say I am unstable. He has been saying it for a year. E10 Therapist: I cannot be your witness. I can do three things. I can write a letter that says only that you have been in treatment with me since [date] and that you have attended regularly — nothing about your parenting, nothing about my opinion. I can help you find an independent expert who can assess you properly, which is what the court actually needs. And I can keep doing the work we are doing. — Three options, and the first is a partial accommodation that stays inside the role: a factual letter, not an opinion. E11 Client: A letter that says nothing. E12 Therapist: A letter that says something true and does not pretend to be an assessment. (pause) What your lawyer wants is my opinion. My opinion is not evidence, it is my impression of you in a room. The court needs someone who has done an assessment. — The distinction between clinical impression and forensic opinion, which is the whole of Week 11's territory arriving early and being handled in eleven words. E13 Client: (long pause) All right. (pause) I was afraid you would say no and I would have nothing. E14 Therapist: You have the treatment. (pause) And I understand that it does not feel like much this week. — The acknowledgement, which does not soften the refusal and does not leave the client alone with it.

What the transcript shows. The refusal is made in four turns without a lecture, the reason is given in terms of what the client would lose, and a partial accommodation is offered that is genuinely inside the role. The commonest failure in this scenario is not the refusal; it is the therapist who agrees "just this once" and then discovers, eight months later, that the file has become a document written for a judge.

6.8 Laboratory: the boundary audit

Aim. To produce a written map of the student's own current and foreseeable role conflicts, and three pre-decided responses.

Procedure. Each student completes a one-page audit in twenty minutes, using the grid below. Then, in pairs, each student presents their three highest-risk situations and the partner plays the colleague who says, "surely in this case you would make an exception?" Ten minutes each way. The exercise is assessed on the pre-decisions, not on the analysis.

DomainQuestionMy current facts
CommunityWhere am I known, and to whom?
FamilyWhose relatives, in-laws or household members might present?
WorkDoes my other employment create authority over potential clients?
MoneyAre there debts, investments or business interests that could meet a client?
FaithDo I hold a role with authority in a congregation?
DigitalWhat is discoverable about me, and what do I do when a client appears in my feed?
PhysicalIn what settings do I meet clients outside the room?

Marks (20).

Criterion0–12–34–5
CompletenessThree domains or fewerFive or sixAll seven, with specifics
Pre-decision"I will decide when it happens"A general intentionA specific sentence the student would actually say
Under pressureException granted in the role-playHeld the line but argued at lengthHeld the line briefly and offered an alternative inside the role
RecordNo recordDescription onlyThe contract, the rationale and the review date

The observation to make in the debrief. Almost every student, when pressed, finds a reason why their own case is the exception. That is the exact skill the topic requires them to notice in themselves.

6.9 Six errors

Error 1 — Teaching boundaries as prohibitions. The result is a therapist who refuses a dying client's hand and a bereaved family's bowl of fruit, and who has confused rigour with coldness. The test is whose need, whose record, and what would supervision say.

Error 2 — Declining without a reason or with a lecture. "I am not able to" without an explanation reads as rejection and costs the client the referral; a five-minute account of the ethics of dual relationships makes the client responsible for the refusal.

Error 3 — Accepting an unavoidable dual relationship without a contract. In a small community, taking the case is often right; taking it without the four-element contract, recorded, is not.

Error 4 — Treating the community encounter as if it can be handled by a rule. The client who is ignored in a market will not complain; she will stop attending. The greeting rule is agreed in advance precisely because the moment is unrehearsable.

Error 5 — Concealing attraction. The pathway to the worst outcome in the whole of this book begins with a therapist deciding that the feeling is private. It is not private; it is material for supervision.

Error 6 — Converting a clinical relationship into a forensic one without noticing. The letter, the report, the attendance at a hearing: each is a role change, and each is made in a moment when the client desperately wants it and the therapist wants to help.

6.9a Four complaints, and what each teaches

Complaint one — the therapist attended the client's father's funeral. The client had asked her to come; the therapist went, stood at the back, and did not speak to the family. Finding: a crossing, appropriately handled and recorded, and the complaint, brought by a relative who thought it excessive, was not upheld. Lesson: the record and the rationale are what distinguish this from a violation.

Complaint two — the therapist employed the client as a cleaner. Six months into treatment, an arrangement that saved the client's household from eviction and gave the therapist a cleaner. Finding: a violation of the role, notwithstanding the good motive. Lesson: the exploitation test is about power, and a client cannot refuse an offer from her therapist.

Complaint three — the therapist did not greet the client at a wedding. Finding: no misconduct and a genuine injury. Lesson: the greeting rule exists for this; the client's experience of being unrecognised is a clinical event even where the boundary was correct.

Complaint four — the therapist's husband was the client's bank manager. No contact between them about the client, and the client discovered the connection at a social event. Finding: a conflict that should have been declared at intake. Lesson: the obligation is disclosure at the point the conflict is known, not management after the client finds out.

6.10 Culture, faith and the boundaries of a communal practice

"In our culture, we are all family." The claim is accurate and it is not an argument against boundaries; it is an argument for better ones. In a communal setting, refusing a gift, declining to eat, or maintaining formal distance marks the therapist as a stranger, and a stranger cannot treat a family's shame. The professional task is to be inside the community's forms and outside its information flows: to eat the food, greet the elder, attend the funeral, and still not discuss the client's marriage with the aunt who asks.

Gifts as relationship, not transaction. In many households a gift is a statement about the relationship, and its refusal is a statement in reply. A therapist who declines everything has said, in the only language available to the family, that they are not in a relationship. The workable practice: accept what is small and edible, share it if the setting allows, name its meaning in the session ("this is your mother saying thank you, and I will tell her myself"), and record it. Decline what is valuable, early in treatment, or obliging, and give the reason in terms the family can accept: "if I took this I could not be fair to you."

The pastor-therapist and the imam-therapist. Where the therapist holds religious office as well as clinical training, the two roles have different rules and the same client. The religious role includes moral authority and often an obligation to the congregation; the clinical role includes confidentiality and no moral authority. A person holding both should decide, before the case, which role is operating in the room, say so to the client, and not move between them inside a session. The most serious version of this problem is the confession that is also a clinical disclosure, made to a person who holds both offices.

Touch across cultures. In a society where a hand on the arm is ordinary comfort, refusing it can read as contempt, and where it is not ordinary, offering it can read as intrusion. The therapist's guide is not a general rule but the client's own idiom, and the safest method is to let the client's physical language set the register and to keep any touch brief, visible and purposeful.

Home visits and hospitality. Where treatment in a clinic is foreign and treatment in the home is normal, the home visit is a clinical necessity. Its rules remain: a purpose, a time limit, a record, and a clear statement that the therapist is there as a therapist even though she has been received as a guest. The meal is the hardest part: accepting it is usually right, staying for three hours is not.

The boundary that the community will test hardest. Not sex and not gifts — the request for advice about another family member. "You know my sister's son, he is in trouble, tell me what to do." The answer is warm and firm: "bring him, or tell her to call me, and I will help properly. I cannot advise you about somebody who is not in the room." Said once, it establishes the practice; said apologetically, it establishes that the boundary is negotiable.

DEBATE — "In a small community, the therapist who refuses a dual relationship is prioritising professional comfort over the client's access to care." Argue for: access is an ethical good, the alternatives may be nonexistent, and rigid refusal can be a way of protecting the therapist's reputation. Argue against: the harm from impaired judgement falls on the client, the community contract does not solve information flow, and the therapist's discomfort is often an accurate signal. Then state the conditions under which you would take a case you knew to be a dual relationship.

6.11 Evidence note

What the research supports. The research on therapist sexual misconduct is the most robust in this area: the harm is severe, the pathway is preceded by non-sexual boundary crossings, and the isolation of the therapist from colleagues is a consistent precursor. Studies of small-community and rural practice find multiple relationships to be normal rather than exceptional, and identify the management strategies — explicit contracting, supervision, clear public rules — that practitioners report as workable. Research on attraction finds it common and rarely acted upon, with the principal risk factor being the therapist's inability to discuss it.

What is weaker. There is very little outcome research on non-sexual dual relationships, and the field's guidance is largely consensus-based, which is why the codes are worded in terms of avoiding harm and examining whether the relationship could impair judgement rather than in terms of prohibition.

What is not supported. That any dual relationship is unethical — the codes do not say so, and in small communities the position would make practice impossible. That a boundary crossing is a sign of misconduct. And that attraction, handled openly in supervision, predicts any poor outcome at all.

The matrix cell. Development is probabilistic and non-linear; the same input produces different outcomes depending on timing and context. The same gift, accepted in session two rather than at the end of treatment, is a different act with a different meaning.

6.12 Key terms

Boundary crossing — a departure from the usual frame for the client's benefit, disclosed and reversible.

Boundary violation — a departure serving the therapist, concealed and often irreversible.

Multiple relationship — a second significant relationship with a client or someone close to the client.

Role conflict — competing obligations arising from two roles held towards the same person.

Community contract — the four-element agreement (naming, greeting, information, review) made where encounters are unavoidable.

Barter — exchange of services for treatment; permissible only in narrow conditions with records and review.

Forensic role — the therapist's role as witness or expert; incompatible with the treating role.

Foresight test — the question of how the client would describe the arrangement in three years.

6.13 Self-test

  1. Give three paired examples distinguishing crossing from violation.
  2. State the four tests and the decision each column produces.
  3. What are the four elements of the community contract?
  4. Why is the greeting rule in the contract, and what does it prevent?
  5. What is the correct response to a request to be a character witness, and why is a factual letter permitted?
  6. What are the five steps for managing attraction, and why is structure preferred to distance?
  7. Why is "it felt right" not a test?

Answers. (1) Extending a session in a crisis versus routinely over-running because the therapist enjoys it; accepting fruit at discharge versus accepting a car; attending a former client's wedding with the frame discussed versus attending a current client's family party. (2) Objectivity, exploitation, foresight, avoidability — the combination producing decline, contract with supervision, risk-managed continuation, or transfer. (3) Naming the encounters; the greeting rule; the information rule; the review date. (4) To prevent the client being ignored in public and experiencing the therapist's silence as a repudiation. (5) Decline the opinion, explain the role change, offer a factual letter (dates and attendance) and referral to an independent assessor; because a factual letter discloses only what is agreed and does not convert the therapist into a witness. (6) Name it, tell supervision, increase structure, examine the material, refer if unmanageable; because distance is experienced as unexplained rejection and often provokes the pursuit the therapist fears. (7) Because warmth, gratitude and being needed all feel like evidence of a good relationship, so the guide must be external: whose need, what supervision would say, and what the client would say afterwards.

6.14 Reflective task

Write two hundred words answering one question honestly: which of the seven audit domains is your weakest, and what is the case that would find it? Name the client you can already imagine. That client is the one this week was written for.

6.15 Further reading

  • Pope, K. and Sonne, J. on sexual attraction and boundary violations — the standard empirical and clinical treatment.
  • Gabbard, G. on boundaries and the therapist's self — the analytic account of the slope.
  • Zur, O. on dual relationships in small communities — the strongest argument for a contextual rather than prohibitive approach.
  • AAMFT and equivalent codes, the sections on multiple relationships and sexual contact, read in the original.
  • Any current guidance on remote and digital practice in your jurisdiction.

Next: Week 7 — Competence, supervision and referral.

Week 7 — Competence, supervision and referral

Learning outcomes. By the end of this week the student can (1) define competence in its four dimensions and explain why "I have read about it" is not one of them; (2) recognise the six signals that a case is beyond her competence, including the ones that are felt rather than reasoned; (3) describe cultural humility as a competence and its limits as a substitute for training; (4) construct a supervision contract and bring material to supervision that is actually useful; and (5) conduct a referral without abandoning the client.

Competence is an ethical obligation before it is a personal quality

The codes all contain a version of the same sentence: the therapist practises within the boundaries of her competence and refers when a client's needs exceed it. The sentence is easy to sign and hard to enact, because the therapist's competence is not visible to her. Nobody feels incompetent while being incompetent; the feeling associated with incompetence is usually confidence, because confidence is what the absence of feedback produces.

Three facts organise the week.

First, competence is specific. A therapist may be highly competent with couples in acute conflict and not competent with a couple in which one partner is psychotic. Competence is not a level; it is a set of domains, each with a boundary, and the boundary is discovered by noticing where the work stops going well.

Second, competence decays. Skills that are not used are lost, knowledge in most areas of this field has a half-life of a few years, and a therapist who qualified in 2010 and has not read since is competent in the practice of 2010. Continuing professional development is therefore not an administrative requirement; it is the maintenance of the licence to practise at all.

Third, competence is maintained by a relationship, not by an intention. The mechanism by which therapists discover their limits is supervision and consultation. A therapist without supervision is not a therapist with an independent practice; she is a therapist with an unexamined one.

DimensionWhat it meansHow it is evidencedHow it fails
KnowledgeThe theory, evidence and law relevant to the problemTraining, reading, CPD recordsConfident error; outdated method
SkillThe capacity to enact the methodObserved practice, recorded sessions, supervisionKnowing what to do and not being able to do it
ExperienceHaving done it, repeatedly, with feedbackCase logs, supervision historyThe first case treated as routine
AppropriatenessPersonal and cultural fit for this clientSelf-knowledge, supervision, client feedbackThe therapist's history driving the treatment

The fourth row is the one that gets skipped. A therapist can be knowledgeable, skilled and experienced and still be the wrong person for this client — the man working intensively with sexual abuse who has not addressed his own; the therapist whose marriage is collapsing treating a couple in crisis; the young clinician whose authority is contested in a family where age confers standing; the therapist who cannot hear a mother's account because it is her own mother's account. Appropriateness is assessed before the case and re-assessed when the work stalls, and the assessment is not a matter of self-confidence.

7.1 The lecture in ninety minutes

MinutesContentTeaching form
0–10Competence: four dimensions, and why confidence is not evidenceTwo vignettes: confident and wrong, uncertain and right
10–22Five concepts: scope, specificity, decay, the novice problem, supervision as the mechanismLecture
22–35Six signals that the case is beyond youStudents add their own, anonymously
35–50Cultural humility and the interpreterDemonstration: a session through an interpreter
50–65Supervision: contract, material, the unsafe supervisorPair work on supervision contracts
65–78Referral: the six-step process and the conversation that clients hateRole-play with observers
78–90The Ogundipe case: recognising psychosis in a counselling roomCase, with students holding the referral decision

7.2 Five concepts that organise the week

Concept 1 — Scope of practice and scope of competence are different, and the first is legal. Scope of practice is what the licence permits; scope of competence is what the therapist can actually do well. Practising at the edge of the licence and outside the competence is lawful and unethical at the same time, and it is the commonest form of misconduct in ordinary practice — not predation, but well-meant over-reach.

Concept 2 — Competence is domain-specific, and the domains are smaller than therapists think. "I work with families" is not a domain. "I work with families in which a parent has a chronic illness and the children are school-age" is closer. The specificity matters because the referral question — "can I do this?" — is answerable only at that granularity.

Concept 3 — The novice's problem is not ignorance; it is the absence of a signal. Expertise research is consistent: beginners do not know what they do not know, because the cues that would tell them are the ones training is supposed to install. The practical consequence is that the therapist's own assessment of her competence is least reliable precisely where it matters most, and that an external check is not a luxury.

Concept 4 — Competence decays quietly. There is no moment at which a therapist becomes incompetent; there is a drift, accelerated by isolation, by a busy practice, and by success. The maintenance mechanism is deliberate: reading, case discussion, recorded sessions reviewed with a peer, and a CPD plan that names the gaps.

Concept 5 — Supervision is the profession's immune system. Not the therapist's personal support, not a requirement to be satisfied, but the mechanism by which the field detects its own errors. A therapist who brings only her successes to supervision has disabled it, and she is the one it was designed to protect.

7.2a The six signals that the case is beyond you

Signals, not proofs. Each of them can occur in a case that is being handled well, and the accumulation of two or three is a prompt to supervision and a fourth is a prompt to refer.

Signal 1 — You dread the session. Not the ordinary reluctance before a hard session; a low, anticipatory dread that begins the day before. It usually means the therapist does not know what to do and has not admitted it.

Signal 2 — You are reading the night before in order to be able to conduct the session. Reading is good. Reading instead of supervision, and reading during the treatment, means the treatment is being improvised from the literature in real time. That is a legitimate way to learn a narrow technique with supervision and an illegitimate way to hold a case alone.

Signal 3 — You have not presented the case in supervision. The strongest indicator in the list. Therapists present the cases they are proud of and the cases that are interesting; they avoid the ones in which they suspect they are failing. If a case has been in the practice for three months and has never been discussed, ask why.

Signal 4 — You feel relief when the client cancels. The relief is information. It frequently accompanies cases in which the therapist is out of depth, or in which the therapist dislikes the client, or both.

Signal 5 — The work has stopped moving and you cannot say why. Competence has an observable signature: the treatment produces movement in the agreed direction, or the therapist can account for why not. A case that is flat for eight sessions and cannot be explained is a case that is being held by somebody who does not know what to do next.

Signal 6 — The client's presentation contains material you have never been trained to assess. Psychosis, dissociation, an eating disorder, a developmental disorder, a child's sexualised behaviour, a neurological change. Recognising the material is a competence in itself; the correct response to recognition is not treatment but referral and, frequently, medical assessment.

The rule for using the signals. The therapist does not act on the signals alone. She writes them down, presents the case, and takes the supervisor's view. What she does not do is wait for the signals to become an adverse event.

7.2b The development plan that actually works

Continuing development fails when it is a list of courses and succeeds when it is a set of narrow, observable targets with a person attached to each.

The four-column plan. Target: a specific capability, described so that someone could watch for it — "conduct a structured risk assessment for suicide and defend the formulation in supervision". Evidence: how the capability will be observed — recordings, a supervisor's report, three consecutive cases discussed. Support: who is involved — the supervisor, the peer group, a short course with a follow-up consultation. Date: when it will be reviewed — a fixed month.

The failing version. "Improve my work with trauma." Nobody can observe it, no one is responsible for it, and no date exists. Six months later the therapist has attended two webinars and can report no change.

The two ratios worth knowing. First, that a therapist improves mainly on the cases she discusses, so the number of cases reviewed per year is a better measure of development than the number of courses attended. Second, that skills decay without use, so the plan should include maintenance of capabilities the therapist already has and is not currently using — the couples work set aside while a practice fills with individual referrals, the children's work not touched since training.

The annual question. Once a year, in writing: which capability did I lose this year, and which did I gain? The answer is the development plan. It is also, for most therapists, the first honest audit of their practice they have conducted.

7.3 Cultural humility, and the interpreter

The problem with the phrase "cultural competence". It implies a body of knowledge about groups, acquired and then held. The trouble is that it encourages two errors: treating a person as an instance of a culture, and believing that knowledge of a group's customs is the same as the capacity to work with a person from that group. The alternative framing — cultural humility — is not vaguer; it is more demanding. It says the therapist must be able to (a) recognise her own frame as one frame among many, (b) ask the client what her world means to her without assuming, (c) bear being wrong and corrected, (d) notice where her own cultural position gives her authority the client cannot refuse, and (e) seek specific knowledge where it is needed, which is a competence like any other.

What cultural humility is not. It is not an excuse for inexpertise. "I work with everyone" is not a cultural position; it is a refusal to specify. And it is not a substitute for language, which brings us to the interpreter.

The interpreter: four rules.

  1. The interpreter is a professional, not a bystander. Introduce them, explain their role to the family, agree that they will interpret faithfully and not summarise, and state that they are bound by confidentiality.
  2. Speak to the client, not to the interpreter. First person, eye contact with the family, and no asides about the family in the third person. The asides are the commonest failure and the family hears the tone if not the words.
  3. Never use a family member as interpreter. A child interpreting a parent's disclosure, or a husband interpreting a wife's account of his violence, is a distortion and sometimes a danger.
  4. Build in time. Everything takes roughly twice as long, and the session should be scheduled at ninety minutes rather than fifty. An interpreted session compressed into fifty minutes is a session in which the therapist will skip precisely the material that is hardest to say in two languages.

When there is no interpreter. A therapist who does not share the client's language and proceeds anyway is working outside her competence by definition. In many settings the honest answer is to say so, to find a colleague who shares the language, or to work with the family's nominated interpreter under the four rules above, documented as a compromise rather than presented as a method.

The interpreter's own exposure. Interpreting a disclosure of violence, sexual abuse or a diagnosis places a burden on the interpreter, who is frequently a member of the same community and may know the family. The therapist should check on this, offer the interpreter the option to withdraw from a particular case, and where possible use an interpreter from outside the community for sensitive material. Very little of this appears in the training literature and almost all of it matters in practice.

7.3a The supervision contract

Supervision fails for contractual reasons far more often than for relational ones. The contract is one page and it answers six questions.

  1. What is the supervisor's authority? Does she have responsibility for the cases, or is she a consultant to the therapist's own decisions? The difference determines who carries the liability, and it should be stated at the first meeting rather than discovered in a crisis.
  2. How often, for how long, and in what form? Weekly or fortnightly; one hour; individual, group, or live. "Whenever I need it" is not supervision; it is consultation, which is useful and is not the same thing.
  3. What will be brought? A written list of current cases, at least one case in detail, at least one recorded or verbatim excerpt each month, and every case in which the therapist is holding a secret, considering referral, or aware of attraction. The list is the supervisor's protection as much as the therapist's.
  4. What will be recorded? A note by the supervisor for each session, and the therapist's own record in the case file that the case was discussed. A regulator investigating a case will ask when it was last supervised, and the answer must be documentary.
  5. How will disagreement be handled? The supervisor's view is an opinion; the therapist's decision is the clinical act. Where they disagree about a decision with legal consequences, the disagreement is recorded and, where necessary, a second opinion is obtained. Neither party should pretend the tension does not exist.
  6. How will it end? A planned ending, with the cases handed over, and a review of the therapist's development. Supervision that simply stops is the commonest way for a therapist to become isolated.

What to bring, concretely. Three questions are more useful than a narrative: what am I not seeing? what did I do that I would not want a colleague to have watched? and what am I avoiding? The third question produces the material more reliably than any case description.

The unsafe supervisor. Three patterns, each of which requires the therapist to change supervisor. The supervisor who needs to be admired: the therapist learns to present well and stops presenting honestly. The supervisor who takes over the case: the therapist becomes an assistant and stops developing judgement. The supervisor who colludes: the pair agree that the client is difficult, the spouse is impossible, the agency is hopeless, and nothing is examined. A supervisor who has never disagreed with the therapist in a year is not supervising.

Group supervision. Valuable and hazardous. It is valuable because eight minds notice more, and hazardous because of the exhibition and the audience: a therapist presenting a failure to a group will manage the group's opinion, and members will perform their competence for each other. The protections are a standing agenda, a rule that no one advises without asking what is wanted, a named facilitator, and confidentiality rules in writing.

Where supervision does not exist. Many settings, including large parts of the private sector in this region, provide none. The ethical position is that the therapist must obtain it anyway, in whatever form is available: a peer consultation group of three or four, a paid monthly consultation, a reciprocal arrangement with a colleague that is conducted on a schedule rather than when convenient. The absence of an employer-provided supervisor does not remove the obligation; it relocates it.

7.3b Peer consultation, and the rule of three

A peer consultation group of three therapists, meeting monthly for two hours, is the most cost-effective competence maintenance available to a practising clinician. The rules that make it work are few.

  • A fixed membership and a fixed date. Groups that meet when needed do not meet.
  • One case per member per meeting, prepared, and presented in a standard format: presentation, what has been tried, what the therapist wants, and the question she is avoiding.
  • No advice without permission. The presenting therapist asks for what she wants: reflections, ideas, or a decision.
  • Written notes, kept by the presenter, in the case file.
  • A rule about confidentiality that includes the members' own material, since the group will discuss their cases and their reactions.
  • A rotating external review twice a year, in which one session is given to a senior colleague to observe. The group that never lets an outsider in develops its own blind spots, like any system.

The rule of three is the minimum: one therapist alone cannot check her own judgement, and two can settle into a shared blind spot. Three produces a triangle in the technical sense, which is to say a structure in which one member can be observed by the other two.

7.3c Supervision in the first two years

The period in which supervision matters most is the one in which it is easiest to avoid, because a newly qualified therapist is expected to be competent and is frequently employed in a setting with no supervision structure.

The four things a new therapist should insist on. A named supervisor with a written contract; a weekly hour, protected in the diary; permission to record sessions, which is the only way skill can be observed; and a standing agreement that any case in which the therapist feels out of depth is brought within the week rather than at the next scheduled meeting.

The four things a new therapist should not do. Practise alone: a solo practice in the first two years is an avoidable risk to clients and to the therapist. Accept cases at the edge of competence in order to build a practice. Treat the supervision hour as a report on attendance. And conceal the first serious error, which is the error that supervision exists to catch and which, once concealed, shapes the therapist's practice thereafter.

The first adverse event. Every therapist will eventually have one: a client who deteriorates, a disclosure that was missed, a suicide attempt, a death. The professional response is a structured review — what happened, what was known, what was decided, what would be done differently — conducted with the supervisor and recorded. The unprofessional response is silence, which converts an event into a secret and a secret into a pattern.

7.4 Referral: the six steps

Step 1 — Recognise, and write it down. The recognition usually arrives as one of the six signals. The discipline is to record the thought in the file on the day it occurs: "considered whether this case is within my competence to treat; concerns about [X]; to discuss in supervision." That note changes the character of everything that follows, because a therapist who has recorded a doubt has discharged part of the duty and a therapist who has not has concealed it.

Step 2 — Decide, with the supervisor, what kind of referral. Four kinds, and they are not the same.

KindMeaningExample
AdditiveAnother professional joins; the therapist continuesMedical review added to psychological treatment for depression
ConsultativeAnother professional advises; the therapist treatsA psychiatrist advises on medication while the therapist continues
TransferThe case moves entirelyA disclosure of psychosis in a couple therapist's office
EscalationA statutory pathway is triggeredSuspected child abuse

Most incompetence situations require additive or consultative referral, and therapists skip to transfer because transfer is easier to justify and harder to bear. Transferring a case that could have been treated with advice deprives the client of the therapist who knows her.

Step 3 — Name it to the client, without shame and without euphemism. The template: "I want to talk about something in our work. I think you need something I am not the best person to provide, and I want to tell you what it is and why." Then the specific gap: "what you are describing needs a psychiatric assessment, and I am not qualified to do that. It is not that I am giving up on you."

Step 4 — Make it warm. A name on a piece of paper is a cold referral and most clients do not follow it up. The warm referral: the therapist telephones the receiving professional with the client's written consent, describes the situation and the question, and then gives the client the name of a person who is expecting her. Where the receiving service is a public clinic with a queue, the therapist finds out when to attend and what to bring, and writes it down.

Step 5 — Bridge, if the client needs it. One or two sessions while the referral is arranged, with the client's agreement, is not a failure to refer; it is the prevention of a gap. The bridge has a stated end: "we will meet twice more while you get an appointment, and then we will finish."

Step 6 — Follow up once, and close properly. One telephone call after the transfer, to confirm attendance and to say that the therapist is glad. Then a closing session, with a summary, an offer of return if the situation changes, and a note in the file. A referral without a closing is an abandonment with paperwork.

The client who refuses the referral. Common, and often rational: the referred service is expensive, far away, stigmatised, or staffed by someone the client distrusts. The therapist's response is not to insist and not to collude. She names the risk of continuing: "I can keep seeing you, and I want to be honest that I would be guessing at the part that matters. If you will not go, I will still see you, and I will say so in my notes." The decision is the client's, the honesty is the therapist's, and the record must show that the choice was informed.

The refusal to refer. The other failure. A therapist who will not refer because the client will be upset, or because the case is interesting, or because she is the only person the family trusts, has placed her own needs above the client's. The test: if a colleague described this case to me, would I say she should refer it? Therapists answer this question accurately about other people's cases and inaccurately about their own.

Referral in a crisis. Where the need is urgent — psychosis, suicidal intent with a plan, a child at risk — the referral is not a suggestion but an arrangement made in the room: the call is made with the client present, the appointment is fixed, transport is discussed, and where the client will not go and the risk is grave, the statutory pathway or the emergency service is used and the client is told what is being done and why.

7.4a The referral letter

Six lines, written the same day, sent with the client's written consent, and copied to the file. Longer letters are read less carefully and disclose more than the purpose requires.

Re: [name], [date of birth]. Client attended [n] sessions with me between [dates]. Reason for referral: [the question, in one sentence, e.g. "assessment of persecutory beliefs of three months' duration and one episode of apparent unresponsiveness"]. Relevant history: [two or three lines]. Risk: [current, one line]. Client has consented to this referral and to my speaking with you. I am available on [number]. Yours, [name], [qualification].

The rule about diagnoses in the letter. The referring therapist states observations and her formulation, and does not present her formulation as a finding. "I have been working with a formulation of depression; the features below do not fit it" is honest. "Client has psychosis" is a diagnosis the therapist is not entitled to make, and if she is wrong it follows the client into a file.

The rule about the client's own words. Quote them, briefly and accurately. "Client states that his supervisor 'has been replaced by someone who is not him'" is worth more to a receiving psychiatrist than three paragraphs of the therapist's adjectives.

7.5 Case: Mr Ogundipe, and the diagnosis the therapist did not make

Referral. Femi Ogundipe, 52, a customs officer, self-refers for "stress and marriage problems". His wife telephoned first and said he had "changed"; he attends reluctantly. Married twenty-four years, four children, the youngest 15.

Sessions 1–3, as the therapist recorded them. Low mood, poor sleep, loss of interest, irritability with his wife, and a complaint that his colleagues "are talking about me". The therapist formulates depression with a relational precipitant, begins behavioural activation, and refers the couple for joint work in week four.

What the therapist missed, in the record. Three details, each recorded and none interrogated.

  1. In session two, Ogundipe said that the neighbours had installed a device to listen to his conversations, and that he had found "a mark" on his car. The therapist wrote "client reports feeling watched; discuss trust".
  2. In session three, his account of a meeting at work was difficult to follow: he moved between subjects, said his supervisor had been "replaced by someone who is not him", and laughed at a point in the narrative that was not funny.
  3. His wife, in the joint session, said he had stopped eating food she cooked, on the grounds that "it tastes different", and had twice slept in the car.

The joint session that changed the case. In week five, Mrs Ogundipe said, in her husband's presence, that he had not slept for three nights and had been praying aloud until dawn. Ogundipe stood up, said "you have all decided to make me mad", and left the room. He returned eleven minutes later, calm and apologetic, with no memory of the preceding half hour.

What the therapist did, in the order she did it.

  1. She stopped the couple work. This is the first and most important act: a treatment that requires two participants to be lucid could not continue while one of them was intermittently not.
  2. She saw Ogundipe alone the following day, with a structured screen for psychotic symptoms, which she had not been trained to use and had read the previous night. That is not competence; it is a first-aid measure, and it is better than nothing. She found persecutory beliefs held with conviction, a period of apparent unresponsiveness that she could not account for, and no insight.
  3. She telephoned the psychiatric service the same afternoon, with written consent, described the presentation and asked for an urgent assessment. She wrote the question down: "is this a first episode of psychosis, and does he need admission?"
  4. She told Ogundipe what she was doing before she did it, in the presence of his wife, and she used the sentence that made it possible: "I am not saying you are mad. I am saying that something is happening to your mind that I am not qualified to assess, and I am not willing to guess."
  5. She attended the psychiatric appointment with the couple at the client's request, contributed the record, and handed the case over.

The outcome. Ogundipe was assessed and admitted for three weeks; the working diagnosis was a psychotic episode with a likely organic contribution, and he was investigated accordingly. He returned to the practice eleven months later, at his own request, for couple work — with a psychiatrist's letter, a stable medication regime, and a therapist who now had a supervision arrangement with a psychiatrist for exactly this kind of question.

What the case teaches.

  1. The odd detail is the diagnosis. "Feeling watched" is not a symptom of depression; "the supervisor has been replaced by someone who is not him" is not a marital complaint. Both were in the record within three sessions and neither was pursued. The failure was not ignorance of psychiatry; it was the therapist's assumption that the formulation she had was sufficient.
  2. The couple's presence concealed the symptoms. In joint sessions Ogundipe was polite, coherent for twenty minutes and defended by his own narrative; his wife's account was the only evidence and the therapist had classified it as marital criticism.
  3. The competence that was required was recognition, not treatment. No part of this case needed the therapist to be a psychiatrist. It needed her to notice, to ask, to telephone, and to say "I am not qualified to assess this" without shame.
  4. The referral preserved the relationship. Ogundipe returned, which is the outcome that the cold referral rarely produces.

7.6 Transcript: the referral conversation

Minutes four to sixteen of the session after the joint session. Annotated.

R1 Therapist: I want to talk about something, and I am going to say it plainly because I think you will hear it better plainly than gently. — The warning that a serious statement is coming. It buys attention and it treats the client as an adult. R2 Ogundipe: You want to stop. R3 Therapist: I want to change what we are doing. (pause) What I have seen in the last two sessions is not something I am trained to assess. — The distinction between stopping and changing, offered first, before the content. R4 Ogundipe: What did I do? R5 Therapist: Nothing. (pause) This is not about your behaviour. There are experiences you have described to me — the neighbours, the mark on the car, the night you do not remember — and those are not things a counsellor assesses. A doctor needs to look at them, and I want to arrange that this week. — The content named, in his own words, without a diagnostic label. R6 Ogundipe: (quietly) So you think I am mad. R7 Therapist: (pause) I think something is happening in your mind that is frightening you and that I cannot explain. (pause) "Mad" is a word people use to end a conversation. I am trying to start one. — The refusal of the label and the refusal of the euphemism, in the same answer. R8 Ogundipe: My job. If they hear — R9 Therapist: That is a real fear and we will deal with it. (pause) The psychiatrist I am going to call works in a private clinic; the note goes to your file there and nowhere else. If the assessment leads to treatment, we will talk about what your employer is told, which in most cases is nothing. — A practical concern answered specifically, which is what makes the rest credible. R10 Ogundipe: And my wife? She will say I told you — R11 Therapist: Your wife has been saying it for months and you have been telling her she is wrong. (pause) What I am doing is taking her seriously, which does not mean she is right about everything. — The move that converts the wife from accuser to ally, and that requires the therapist to say something slightly uncomfortable. R12 Ogundipe: (long pause) Will you tell them what I said? The thing about the car? R13 Therapist: With your permission, yes, because it is the reason for the referral. Without your permission, I will tell them what I observe and I will tell you that I have done so. — The honest answer, which is more useful than reassurance and which models the disclosure policy of Week 3 in a medical setting.
