R14 Ogundipe: (pause) Can you be there? R15 Therapist: If you want me there, I will be there. — Three seconds of commitment that determine whether he attends.

What the transcript shows. Nine of the fifteen turns are the client's fears, and each is answered specifically rather than globally. The therapist never says "it will be fine". She says what she will do, what she will not do, and what the client's choices are — and the referral holds because the fear of being labelled was answered with a procedure rather than with reassurance.

7.7 Laboratory: the competence audit and the referral rehearsal

Part A — the audit (thirty minutes, individual, written). The student lists fifteen presenting problems likely in her intended practice, and for each: a competence rating out of five, the evidence for the rating (how many cases, with what supervision), and the named service or supervisor she would refer to. The two lowest ratings become the year's development plan; the two highest ratings are interrogated by a partner, who asks "when did you last have feedback on that?"

Presenting problemRating (1–5)EvidenceReferral destination
Couple conflict with no violence
Couple conflict with a violent partner
Child with behavioural difficulty
Adolescent self-harm
Suspected psychosis
Grief and bereavement
Sexual difficulty in a couple
Family with a member who has dementia
Substance misuse
Disclosure of historical abuse

Part B — the referral rehearsal (forty minutes, triads). Each triad receives one card: refer for a psychiatric assessment; refer because the client's child needs a specialist; refer because the therapist's own history makes the case inappropriate; refer because the client needs a service the therapist does not provide (legal aid, housing). The therapist has twelve minutes to tell the client, name the gap without shame, offer a warm handover, and survive the client's anger. The observer marks the six elements.

Marks (20).

Criterion0–12–34–5
RecognitionDid not identify the gapIdentified it lateIdentified it early and recorded it
The namingEuphemism or self-blameClear but coldClear, specific, unashamed, and about the task not the person
WarmthA name on paperContact offeredTelephone call made, appointment arranged, logistics given
The client's angerDefended or collapsedEnduredReceived, named, and the referral held
The recordNothingDescriptionReasoning, the referral question, the consent, the follow-up date

The marking note. The lowest band applies to the therapist who, when the client protests, offers to "keep seeing you as well". That sentence is the commonest way in which a correct referral is undone, and it is a failure of the criterion on warmth — because the therapist has made the client's comfort the criterion rather than the client's care.

7.8 Six errors

Error 1 — Confusing scope of practice with scope of competence. "I am licensed to see families" is true and is not an answer to "should I be seeing this family?".

Error 2 — Treating the first case of a kind as routine. The first psychotic presentation, the first disclosure of abuse, the first family in which a child has died: each requires supervision in advance, and each is frequently held alone because the therapist does not want to appear inexperienced.

Error 3 — The cold referral. A name and a telephone number, given at the end of a session, with no call and no follow-up. Most clients do not attend, and the therapist records a referral that did not happen.

Error 4 — The referral that becomes an abandonment. No closing session, no summary, no offer to return. The client experiences the referral as a rejection and does not seek help again.

Error 5 — Supervision that is a performance. The therapist brings the case that will make her look thoughtful and leaves out the one she is failing. The supervisor cannot supervise what she is not told, and the client carries the cost.

Error 6 — Cultural humility used as an excuse. "I did not want to impose my framework" is not a reason for failing to assess, to ask, or to refer. Humility is a stance in enquiry; it is not an absence of method.

7.8a The case the therapist kept

The counter-case to section 7.5, and the more common failure.

A therapist in a small practice sees a woman for eighteen months for what is recorded as "anxiety and marital difficulty". The woman has a history of childhood sexual abuse that emerges in month four. The therapist has no training in trauma treatment, has read two books, and continues. The treatment is supportive, kind, and static; the client's nightmares do not change and her marriage deteriorates. In month fifteen the client discloses that she has been cutting her thighs for a year and has not told anyone.

The signals that were present and unheeded. The dread before sessions, recorded nowhere. The case never presented in the peer group, because the therapist "did not want to be told to refer". The night-before reading that the therapist experienced as diligence. The flat trajectory that the therapist attributed to the client's "complexity".

What competence would have required, at month four. A supervision conversation about whether trauma-focused work was within the therapist's scope; an additive referral to a trauma-trained clinician, with the therapist continuing the marital work; or a transfer with a bridge. None of these requires abandoning the client, and all of them require the therapist to say out loud that she did not know how to treat what had emerged.

The lesson, stated as a rule. The case that the therapist cannot bring herself to discuss is the case that most needs discussing, and the reluctance is the most reliable signal in this chapter. It is also the signal that therapists are least likely to act on, because acting on it requires admitting, in front of a senior colleague, that a client has been receiving a treatment that was not working.

7.9 Culture, faith and the referral that is refused

The traditional healer. In much of this region, families consult a traditional healer, a spiritualist, or an elder before or alongside a therapist, and the therapist who ignores this will be working in parallel with a treatment she knows nothing about. The professional position is neither endorsement nor contempt but enquiry: what has been done, by whom, what was said, what it cost, and what the family believes about it. Where the family attributes the problem to a spiritual cause and the therapist's formulation is psychological, the two are not necessarily in conflict, and the therapist who insists on the conflict loses the family.

Collaboration, with limits. Collaboration with a traditional practitioner is possible and sometimes excellent, particularly where the practitioner holds the family's trust and the therapist holds the clinical method. It requires a clear agreement about confidentiality, a shared understanding of who is treating what, and a willingness to withdraw if the practitioner's methods involve harm — beating, confinement, deprivation, or "deliverance" practices that endanger a child. Where harm is involved, the statutory duty is engaged and the family's framework is not a defence.

Referral to a religious authority. Families frequently ask the therapist to refer them to a pastor or imam for the spiritual part of a problem, and sometimes the therapist should. The distinction worth teaching: refer for pastoral care where the client wants it and it is additive; do not refer instead of the clinical assessment where the clinical need is real. A therapist who sends a depressed client to a pastor in place of treatment has not respected the culture; she has failed to treat.

When the therapist's referral is refused on religious grounds. "We do not take these things to doctors." The response is not argument and not capitulation. It is specificity: what exactly is feared (stigma, gossip, medication, admission, cost), what the family would accept (a physician rather than a psychiatrist, a female doctor, a private clinic, a single consultation), and what the therapist's own limit is. Where a child's welfare is at stake, the limit stops being negotiable.

The elder's veto. In some households a senior man will decide whether the family continues in treatment, and the therapist will never meet him. The clinical response is to ask the client what the elder believes is happening, and to offer a route by which the elder's view can be heard — a letter, a meeting, a family session — because a treatment that the household's authority has not consented to will end at the elder's first objection.

The therapist's own faith. A therapist whose framework is religious will be tempted to offer it, and a therapist whose framework is secular will be tempted to correct the family's. The competent position is that the therapist's metaphysics is not the treatment, and that the client's is material. What the therapist believes about God is not a clinical instrument; what the client believes is.

DEBATE — "A therapist who shares a client's culture is always better placed to treat that client than one who does not." Argue for: shared language, shared shame, shared knowledge of what a family will and will not tolerate, and the credibility that comes with it. Argue against: over-assumed understanding, the impossibility of disclosing family business to a person from the same community, the blind spots that similarity produces, and the client's need for someone outside the system. Then describe the case in which you would deliberately choose an outsider.

7.9a When the therapist is the only service

A situation common in this region and almost absent from the training literature: the therapist is the only clinician within four hours' travel, and the client cannot be referred anywhere.

The four moves available. Extend scope carefully, with supervision at a distance: a monthly telephone consultation with a specialist, a recorded session reviewed by email, a named consultant for the specific domain. Use the medical system for what it can do: the general practitioner, the district hospital, the community health worker — each can carry part of the assessment even where no psychiatrist exists. Train a local resource: a nurse, a teacher, a catechist who can be taught a structured screen and a referral pathway. Travel: the therapist goes to the specialist twice a year with a caseload, which is cheaper than pretending.

What must be recorded. That no local service existed; what alternatives were considered; what the remote consultation advised; and what the client was told about the limits. The record is not defensive paperwork; it is the only evidence that the decision was a considered one rather than a convenience.

What must not happen. The therapist treating outside her competence while describing the situation as "cultural context" or "the reality of practice". Necessity changes the risk calculation; it does not change the obligation to seek advice, to record, and to tell the client the truth about what is and is not available.

7.10 Evidence note

What the research supports. Therapist effects are substantial and consistent: outcomes vary more between therapists than between the treatments they claim to deliver, and the most robust correlates of better outcomes are skill in alliance management, deliberate practice, and the use of feedback. Supervision is associated with therapist development in qualitative and survey research, and feedback-informed practice improves outcomes and reduces deterioration, particularly with clients who are not progressing. Training in cultural formulation is associated with better engagement but not reliably with better outcomes, which supports the humility framing rather than a knowledge-acquisition framing.

What is weaker. The direct evidence that supervision improves client outcomes is surprisingly thin, because supervision is nearly universal and therefore hard to study by comparison. The evidence on interpreter use is mostly about error rates and satisfaction rather than clinical outcomes.

What is not supported. That experience alone produces competence; the evidence on therapist development shows that many therapists plateau early and that self-assessed competence correlates poorly with observed skill. That cultural matching reliably improves outcomes — the findings are mixed and the mechanisms are unclear. And that a referral is a neutral act; clients experience it as a judgement on themselves, and the evidence on drop-out after referral is consistent with that.

The matrix cell. Development is probabilistic and non-linear; the same input produces different outcomes depending on timing and context. The same referral, made in week two and made in week twenty, is a different clinical act — the first is a formality, the second is a rejection after an investment.

7.11 Key terms

Scope of practice — what the licence permits.

Scope of competence — what the therapist can do to an adequate standard, specified by domain.

Cultural humility — a stance of enquiry about one's own frame and the client's, with the capacity to be corrected.

Additive referral — another professional joins and treatment continues.

Consultative referral — advice obtained; the therapist continues.

Transfer — the case moves entirely.

Warm handover — a direct contact between professionals, with consent, so that the client arrives expected.

Bridge session — a planned continuation while a referral is arranged, with a stated end date.

Signal — a felt or observed indicator that the case may exceed competence; not proof and not to be ignored.

7.12 Self-test

  1. Name the four dimensions of competence and give a failure of each.
  2. Why is confidence a poor indicator of competence?
  3. List the six signals that a case is beyond you.
  4. What are the four rules for working with an interpreter, and why is a family member never acceptable?
  5. Distinguish additive, consultative and transfer referrals with an example of each.
  6. What are the six elements of a referral that the client actually acts on?
  7. What is the "refusal to refer", and what test detects it?
  8. In the Ogundipe case, what were the three missed details and what did each signify?

Answers. (1) Knowledge — confident error from outdated method; skill — knowing the technique and being unable to enact it; experience — treating the first case of a kind as routine; appropriateness — the therapist's own history or position driving the treatment. (2) Because competence deficits remove the cues by which they would be detected, and because confidence rises fastest where feedback is absent. (3) Dread before the session; reading the night before in order to conduct it; the case never presented in supervision; relief when the client cancels; a treatment that has been flat and unexplained; material the therapist was never trained to assess. (4) Introduce and contract the interpreter as a professional; speak to the client in the first person; never use a family member; allow double time. A family member is never acceptable because their own position in the family distorts what is said, and because a child or spouse cannot safely carry another member's disclosure. (5) Additive — a physician reviews medication while therapy continues; consultative — a psychiatrist advises and the therapist treats; transfer — a first episode of psychosis moves to a psychiatric service. (6) Recognition recorded; the kind of referral chosen; the gap named without shame; a warm contact made; a bridge if needed; a follow-up and a closing. (7) Continuing a case because the client will be upset or because the therapist is the only person trusted; the test is what the therapist would say if a colleague described the case to her. (8) The belief that neighbours were watching (persecutory belief); the incoherent account of the meeting ("replaced by someone who is not him"); the refusal of his wife's cooking and sleeping in the car (a change in behaviour with a possible delusional basis) — together, an emerging psychosis that the depression formulation concealed.

7.13 Reflective task

Write one page on the following: name the client you would least like to be referred. Describe that client's presenting problem, and then answer honestly whether your reluctance is about competence or about your own history. Keep the page; it is the beginning of your development plan.

7.14 Further reading

  • Rønnestad, M. and Ladany, N. on the development of therapists over the career — the evidence on plateauing and on what distinguishes therapists who keep improving.
  • Miller, S., Hubble, M. and Duncan, B. on feedback-informed practice — the strongest argument that outcomes can be improved with routine measurement.
  • Falender, C. and Shafranske, E. on clinical supervision — the standard text on the supervisory relationship and contract.
  • Tervalon, M. and Murray-García, J. on cultural humility — the original formulation, worth reading in the source.
  • Any current local guidance on the use of interpreters in health and social care.
  • Your professional body's continuing development requirements, read as a minimum and not as a standard.

Next: Week 8 — Risk I: suicide and self-harm.

Week 8 — Risk I: suicide and self-harm

Learning outcomes. By the end of this week the student can (1) ask about suicide directly, specifically and without euphemism, and explain why asking does not increase risk; (2) conduct and document the ten-element assessment and formulate risk at one of three levels; (3) write a safety plan with a client rather than for one; (4) manage a disclosure of risk inside a family treatment without turning the session into an interrogation; and (5) break confidentiality for risk in a way that preserves the relationship.

The standard, stated once

Ask directly. Ask specifically. Ask about means. Document what you asked and what you were told. There is no evidence that asking about suicide increases risk; there is substantial evidence that clinicians do not ask, that they ask indirectly when they do, and that the indirect question produces a denial.

The four sentences that are wrong, and the one that is right.

SaidHeardProblem
"You are not thinking of doing anything silly, are you?""Say no""Silly" invites denial and insults the person
"Do you ever feel like you want to end it all?""That is a metaphor"Vague; produces a vague answer
"You would not harm yourself, would you?""I am being watched"Leading; the answer is in the question
"Are you having thoughts of suicide?""Yes or no"Better, but a closed door
"Have you had thoughts of ending your own life — and if so, how often, and what have you thought about doing?""This person can hear it"Correct: direct, specific, and open

Why family therapists get this wrong more often than individual therapists. Because in family work the therapist is managing multiple people's distress at once, and risk appears as one thread among many. The parent who says "she is being dramatic" and the adolescent who says "I am fine" produce a session that is busy, warm and dangerous. The discipline is structural: risk is assessed individually, in every case that presents with the features below, whatever the presenting complaint.

When to assess, as a minimum. Any presentation with: hopelessness, a recent loss, a previous attempt, a family history of suicide, a chronic illness or pain, substance misuse, a disclosure of self-harm, an adolescent with school refusal or sudden withdrawal, a client in a violent relationship, a client who has just been left, and any client about whom the therapist has a private unease she cannot name. The last is on the list because it is frequently correct.

LevelPictureAction
LowIdeation absent or fleeting, no plan, no intent, good support, reasons for livingDocument, safety plan, review at next session, monitor
ModerateIdeation with frequency, some intent, no plan or vague plan, inconsistent support, treatableSafety plan, means restriction, notify a support person with consent, increase frequency, refer for assessment
HighIdeation with plan and intent, access to means, previous attempt, agitation, intoxication, no supportDo not leave the person alone; arrange urgent psychiatric assessment or emergency care; inform family; document contemporaneously

8.1 The lecture in ninety minutes

MinutesContentTeaching form
0–8The standard, and the four wrong sentencesStudents write their own opening question, then compare
8–25Five concepts: state not trait, the assessment is an intervention, means, family as resource and hazard, documentationLecture
25–45The ten elements, each with its wordingDemonstration; then triads practise three elements each
45–55Formulation and the three levelsFive vignettes, voted and defended
55–65Self-harm: function, contagion, family responseLecture with a case
65–78The safety plan, written with the clientLive construction with a volunteer
78–90The Suleiman case: an adolescent, a plan, and two parentsCase, students decide what the mother is told and when

8.2 Five concepts that organise the week

Concept 1 — Risk is a state, not a trait. The same person assessed in March and in July is two different patients. Risk rises with agitation, insomnia, intoxication, shame, loss of a relationship, and access to means, and falls with sleep, support and a reason to stay alive. The implication for practice is that an assessment is a snapshot with a date and a review, not a verdict filed once.

Concept 2 — The assessment is itself an intervention. A careful, direct, non-judgemental enquiry reduces isolation, communicates that the therapist can bear the material, and frequently produces the first honest conversation the person has had. This is why the quality of the asking matters clinically and not only forensically: a bad assessment does harm and a good one does some good.

Concept 3 — Means matter more than intent. The difference between a serious attempt and a death is often distance and method. Means restriction — locking medication, removing a firearm, agreeing that a friend holds the pesticide — is the intervention with the best evidence in the whole of this week's material, and it is the one most often omitted because it feels intrusive.

Concept 4 — The family is both the resource and the hazard. The family is the most reliable source of monitoring and the most common source of the precipitants: conflict, shame, rejection, violence, and the loss of a relationship within the household. A family therapist is therefore unusually well placed to intervene and unusually at risk of talking the risk down.

Concept 5 — Documentation is a clinical act, not a defensive one. The record of what was asked, what was answered, how risk was formulated, who was informed and when it will be reviewed is what allows the next clinician to continue the work. It is also the only thing that distinguishes a considered decision from a negligent one when the outcome is bad, which it sometimes will be.

8.2a The ten elements, with wording

The elements are the standard list. What is usually missing from teaching is the sentence to use for each.

1. Ideation. "In the last two weeks, have you had thoughts of ending your own life?" Then: how often, how long does it last, can you stop it, does it come on its own or when something happens.

2. Plan. "Have you thought about how you would do it?" Then: where, when, have you prepared anything, have you been to the place. A plan that has been rehearsed is a different level of risk from a plan that has been imagined.

3. Intent. "Do you intend to act on this?" The question is uncomfortable and it is the one that most changes the formulation. A second question follows: "what has stopped you so far?"

4. Means. "Do you have access to — " and then name the means the client has already mentioned, plus the common ones in the setting: medication in the house, pesticides, a firearm, a height, a vehicle, a rope. Ask what is in the house and who else can reach it.

5. Previous attempts. "Have you ever tried before?" Then: what, when, what happened, did anyone know. The strongest single predictor, and frequently not in the referral.

6. Protective factors. "What keeps you going?" — children, a mother, faith, a dog, work, a plan for next year. Ask for specifics and record them in the client's words, because they are the raw material of the safety plan.

7. Substance use and impulsivity. "How much are you drinking?" and "do you do things quickly when you are upset?" Intoxication and impulsivity are the two factors that convert ideation into action within hours.

8. Family history. "Has anyone in your family died by suicide, or tried?" The question requires care and it must be asked; the answer frequently reframes the case.

9. Current stressors and recent losses. "What has happened in the last three months?" — a separation, a dismissal, a debt, a diagnosis, a death, an arrest, a public humiliation. Ask what the client expects to happen next, because the anticipation of a loss is as potent as the loss.

10. Disclosure and support network. "Who knows about this?" and "who could you call at two in the morning?" The answers determine whether a safety plan is possible or whether the plan must be a clinical arrangement.

The follow-up question students forget. "What would make it worse this week?" The answer is the plan: the anniversary, the court date, the visit from the husband, the results of the test. A risk formulation that does not name the date on which risk will rise is a formulation that will be wrong within seven days.

8.2b Formulation, and the sentence that carries it

An assessment produces a level, and the level produces actions. What makes the work defensible is the sentence that connects them.

The template. "Risk is assessed as [level] on [date]. This is based on [ideation, plan, intent, means, history]. Protective factors are [specific]. The precipitants in the next two weeks are [specific dates or events]. Actions taken: [means restriction, notification, referral, frequency of contact]. Review: [date]."

Worked example.

Risk assessed as moderate on 14 March. Daily ideation over three weeks, no plan, no stated intent, prior overdose at 17. Access to a large quantity of her mother's medication in an unlocked cupboard. Protective factors: two children, a sister she telephones daily, church attendance. Precipitant: custody hearing on 26 March. Actions: medication moved to the sister's house today with the client's agreement; sister informed with consent; weekly sessions increased to twice weekly until the hearing; psychiatric assessment requested, appointment 19 March; safety plan written and copied. Review: 17 March.

Why this format works. It records the reasoning rather than the conclusion, so a reader can see that the assessment was made rather than assumed. It names the dates, so the review is real. And it records the client's consent to each notification, which is the detail that distinguishes a collaborative intervention from a unilateral one.

What must be added where the client refuses consent to notify. "Client declined notification of her sister. Discussed the additional risk of secrecy; client maintained refusal. Therapist notes the increased risk and will review at the next contact. Emergency contact details held." Recorded refusal is not misconduct; unrecorded refusal becomes, in a subsequent inquiry, an omission.

The three things a formulation must never contain. A promise — "client contracted not to harm herself" — which is worthless and which no client can make. A prediction — "client is not at risk" — which no clinician can make. And a euphemism — "client has some passive thoughts" — which conceals rather than records.

8.2c Ten things that raise risk in a family case

The formulation is built from the elements, and the elements are easy to gather and easy to leave unweighted. The ten items below are the ones that most often change a moderate assessment into a high one, and each is under-detected in family work because it appears as ordinary family material.

  1. A recent humiliation or exposure — an arrest, a pregnancy discovered, a debt made public, a shaming in church or at work. The days immediately after an exposure are the days of highest risk.
  2. The end of a relationship in the last six weeks, including the end that the client has not yet accepted.
  3. A court date, a hearing, or a custody decision in the next fortnight. Ask for the date; it belongs in the formulation and it sets the review.
  4. Insomnia of more than three nights. Agitation and sleeplessness are the states in which ideation becomes action.
  5. Access to a method that is unfamiliar to the therapist — pesticides in a farming household, a relative's firearm, a well, a river the client has mentioned twice.
  6. A family history that nobody has asked about, including a death that the family describes as an accident.
  7. A client who has become the family's carer — for a parent with dementia, a disabled sibling, a dying spouse. The burden plus the loss of the role is a combination that appears in the files after the event.
  8. A client who has disclosed abuse and been disbelieved by the family member she told.
  9. A client who is taking medication and has stopped it, particularly where the family has discouraged it.
  10. The therapist's own unease, without a reason. It is on the list because it is data: an experienced clinician's inarticulate concern is a signal that something in the presentation does not fit the formulation.

8.3 Self-harm: what it is, and what it is not

The distinction that matters clinically. Suicidal behaviour is intended to end life. Non-suicidal self-injury is intended to relieve intolerable feeling, to punish the self, to communicate, or to feel real, and is not intended to kill. The distinction is not absolute — self-injury is a risk factor for suicide, and the two occur in the same person — and it changes the response: self-injury requires an assessment of function and an alternative coping repertoire, and suicide requires a risk assessment and a safety plan.

The four functions, in the words clients use.

  1. To feel something, or to feel nothing. "When I cut, everything goes quiet for a while."
  2. To punish. "I deserved it." Frequently following abuse, and frequently accompanied by shame.
  3. To communicate. "Nobody would listen unless they saw something." Common in adolescents in families where distress is not permitted.
  4. To control. "It is the one thing that is mine." Common where the person's life is otherwise governed by others.

The countertransference. Self-injury produces strong reactions in therapists, and the reactions are usually one of three: rescue (the therapist becomes the person who must stop it, and the client's autonomy disappears), disgust (the therapist's own history or her culture's response to mutilation), or fascination (the case becomes the therapist's most interesting one). All three are material for supervision, and all three change the treatment.

What the therapist does in the session. Asks about the injury without recoiling; asks about the function without interpreting it; asks about the moment before, in detail, because the sequence feeling — act — relief — shame contains the points at which alternatives can be introduced; and does not make the stopping of self-injury a condition of the therapy. A therapist who says "you must stop" has added a rule to a problem that is caused by rules.

The family's response. The commonest sequence in a family: discovery, horror, prohibition, concealment. The adolescent learns to hide more carefully and to injure in places that do not show. The therapist's work with the parents is to convert horror into monitoring and prohibition into an agreement about what will happen when the urge arrives — usually a plan involving a person and a place rather than a rule.

Contagion. In school and peer settings, self-injury and suicidal behaviour cluster. Two implications. First, group work with adolescents who self-injure should be conducted with attention to comparison and to explicit method discussion; some services avoid mixed groups entirely. Second, in a family with two adolescents, the therapist should expect the second to be affected and should ask.

8.3a The safety plan, written with the client

A safety plan is not a no-suicide contract. It is a written sequence the client will follow as the urge rises, and it is written with the client, in her language, on paper she keeps.

The six steps, in order.

  1. My warning signs. The thoughts, feelings, images and behaviours that come before the urge. In the client's words: "I stop answering messages", "I start thinking about my mother".
  2. Things I can do on my own. Two or three, specific and available: a shower, walking to the junction, a particular playlist, prayer. Not "think positive".
  3. People and places that distract me. Named people, with numbers, and places that are open at night.
  4. People I can ask for help. Two or three names, with numbers, and an agreed script: "I am not all right, can you be with me for an hour?"
  5. Professionals and services. The therapist's number with its limits, the clinic, the emergency department, the crisis line. Written in full, not abbreviated.
  6. Making the environment safe. The means, and what will be done about them today: medication with the sister, the razor blades removed, the pesticide taken to the farm, the key to the roof given to the neighbour.

The rules for writing it. In the room, on paper, in the client's handwriting where possible; the client keeps a copy and the file keeps a copy; the client's own language, not the therapist's; reviewed at each session initially and at every change of circumstance; and the client's agreement sought at each step, because a plan imposed by the therapist is a plan the client will not use.

A worked plan, for Amina, 15, whose case follows in 8.5.

Warning signs: I stop coming out of my room. I feel like I am behind glass. I start thinking nobody would notice. On my own: cold shower; the playlist Aunty Bisi made; walk to the corner and back. Distraction: Halima (mother) in the kitchen; sit with her while she cooks. Fatima next door. Ask for help: Halima — "I am having the bad thoughts again, please sit with me". Fatima — "can I come and sit at your place". Professionals: Dr Okafor (therapist) 0803…, weekdays 9–6; Amina's clinic 0805…; Emergency 112. Safe environment: mother's medication now in a locked box with the key kept by Halima's sister; Amina's blades given to the therapist in session; no access to the roof (padlock replaced 12 March).

8.4 Risk inside a family treatment

The structural rule. Assess individually, agree what will be shared, then facilitate the sharing. Three steps, in that order, every time. The therapist who assesses in front of the family gets a performance; the therapist who shares without agreement loses the young person; the therapist who neither assesses nor shares is the case in the file that ends badly.

Step 1 — individually. The client is seen alone, the assessment is conducted in full, and the safety plan is written. The parents or partner are told that the therapist is seeing the person alone and why, in general terms, before the session — never after.

Step 2 — agree what is shared. The conversation has three parts. What the therapist must tell, and why: the risk, because the household is the means of supervision. What the client will tell herself: the detail, the words, the timing. And what will not be told: the content of the therapy, the things said about the parents, the history. The agreement is written down, in the note and, where the client wishes, on paper for the family.

Step 3 — facilitate. The client speaks first, in the joint session, with the therapist present. If she cannot, the therapist says what was agreed and the client confirms it. The family's reaction is then worked with: the father's anger, the mother's guilt, the sibling's confusion. The session ends with the household's part of the safety plan — who checks the medication cupboard, who is called, what happens at night.

The three family configurations that require specific care.

The parent who is suicidal. The children are not told the content. They are told what changes: "your mother is ill and is being treated; she will be in hospital for a few days; this is not your fault and it is not because of anything you did." The children's need for a truthful, age-appropriate account is a clinical task and it is frequently skipped because the adults are overwhelmed.

The adolescent who self-injures. The parents are told the fact, not the diary. The prohibition response is anticipated and converted, in the session, into an agreement about the next occasion. The school is involved only with the young person's knowledge and, where the law requires it, with her being told first.

The child who discloses. The child's disclosure of abuse is Week 9's territory and the same structure applies: the child is seen alone, told what will happen next, and told who will be told and in what words before anyone is told.

8.4a Breaking confidentiality for risk, in words

The moment is unavoidable and it is where many therapists lose a client. The four-part statement, delivered before the call is made rather than after.

  1. The commitment, restated. "I told you at the start that there are things I cannot keep, and this is one of them."
  2. The specific content. "What I am going to tell your mother is that you have been thinking about taking her tablets, and that we have made a plan. I am not going to tell her what you said about her, or about your father, or anything about the sessions."
  3. The reason. "Because the tablets are in the house, and because she can do something I cannot do from here, which is to be awake at two in the morning."
  4. The offer. "You can be in the room when I tell her, or I can tell her and you can come in afterwards. Those are the two choices and I would rather you chose."

What the client usually says. "You promised." The answer is not a defence of the policy. It is: "I promised, and I told you at the time what the exception was. I am keeping the promise that matters more, which is that I would not let this happen without doing something."

What must never happen. Telling the family and then telling the client. The sequence converts a professional duty into a betrayal, and the client who learns of it afterwards will not disclose again — to this therapist or to any other.

8.4b When the family will not cooperate with the plan

The plan assumes a household that will act. Frequently it will not: the mother who says "she is doing it for attention", the father who refuses to lock anything because "a man's house is not a prison", the husband who dismisses his wife's ideation as manipulation.

The four responses, in order. First, ask what the objection is about, because it is usually about something specific: the father hears an accusation of his parenting, the mother hears that she has failed. Second, offer a version of the plan that does not require them to admit anything — locking a cupboard because the children are small, not because a daughter is suicidal. Third, find another adult — an aunt, a teacher, a neighbour, a religious leader — and build the monitoring around that person, with the client's knowledge. Fourth, and where the risk is high, state the position plainly: "I am telling you what I think needs to happen because your daughter's life is at stake. If it does not happen, I will record that I asked and I will keep asking."

What not to do. Do not discharge the family because they will not comply. Do not accept the plan's collapse in silence. And do not turn the work into a campaign against the parents, which will cost the adolescent the only household she has.

8.5 Case: Amina Suleiman, fifteen

Referral. The school counsellor telephones: a teacher noticed cuts on the forearm of Amina Suleiman, 15, a senior secondary student. Amina has refused to explain them. The mother, Halima, 41, a trader, is telephoned and attends with Amina the following day. The father, Yusuf, 47, is a long-distance driver and is away; he will attend when he returns in three weeks.

The household. Halima and Yusuf, married nineteen years. Amina, 15; Ibrahim, 11. The family is Muslim; Halima's sister Bisi lives twenty minutes away. Yusuf is frequently absent for two to three weeks at a time.

Session one, with mother and daughter. Amina is silent, arms covered, answering in single words. Halima does the talking: Amina has been "difficult" since the new school year, has stopped eating with the family, and has been found crying in the bathroom. Halima's own account includes a period of depression two years earlier after a miscarriage, for which she took medication and did not continue.

The therapist does three things in this session and none of them is to ask about the cuts in front of the mother. She establishes the presenting problem in the family's terms. She explains the structure — she will see Amina alone first, then the family, and she explains why, in front of both, so that the individual session is not a secret. And she says what she will not keep: "if I think Amina is in danger, I will tell you, and I will tell her first that I am going to." Halima agrees. Amina looks up for the first time.

Session two, Amina alone, fifty minutes. For fifteen minutes Amina says nothing of substance. The therapist does not fill the silence with questions; she says, at minute twelve, "I am not going to make you show me your arm. I am going to ask you some questions that I ask everybody, and you can tell me to stop." Then the assessment.

  • Ideation: yes, most days for about four months. "I think about not being here."
  • Plan: yes. Her mother's remaining tablets, in the cupboard above the sink. She has taken them out twice and put them back.
  • Intent: "I do not know. Sometimes I do." She has written a note, on her phone, unsent, four weeks ago.
  • Means: the tablets, and the roof of the building, which has a door that is not locked.
  • Previous attempts: none. Two episodes of cutting, both in the last three months, both on the forearm, both after arguments at home.
  • Protective factors: Ibrahim, her brother — "he would find me"; her aunt Bisi; prayer; the possibility of finishing school.
  • Substance use: none. Impulsivity: high — she describes decisions made "in one minute".
  • Family history: none known; the therapist does not ask in detail and records that she will.
  • Stressors: a change of school; her father's absences; an argument six weeks earlier in which Yusuf said she had "brought shame" by being seen with a boy; and the discovery that her mother had been "sick" and had hidden it.
  • Network: nobody knows. Her closest friend has been told "some of it".

The formulation. High risk on the day of assessment: ideation with plan and some intent, means accessible in the house, a recent humiliating event at home, high impulsivity, and no adult who knows. Protective factors substantial: her brother, her aunt, her faith, school.

What the therapist did, in order, within that session.

  1. She told Amina that she would be telling her mother, that evening, and exactly what she would say — that Amina has thoughts of ending her life, that there are tablets in the house, and that they need a plan. She did not tell her mother about the note, the boy, or the contents of the session.
  2. She offered Amina the choice of being in the room. Amina chose to be present.
  3. She telephoned the clinic's psychiatric service and made an appointment for two days later.
  4. She wrote the safety plan with Amina (8.3a) and gave her a copy.
  5. She asked Amina to hand over her blades, which Amina did, and recorded the receipt of them.
  6. She saw Halima for twenty minutes, with Amina present, and used the four-part statement of 8.4a. Halima's response was silence, then weeping, then, "I did not know. I thought she was angry with me."
  7. She asked Halima to move the medication that night and to lock the roof door; Halima telephoned her sister and the medication went to Bisi's house the same evening.
  8. She wrote the note the same day: the ten elements, the level, the actions, the review date, and the consent recorded.

Sessions three to eight. The psychiatric assessment finds a moderate depressive episode without psychosis; no medication is started, and weekly therapy plus review is agreed. The family sessions, with Ibrahim present for two of them, work on the household's rules about distress: the family in which feelings were "not discussed", the father's shaming sentence, the mother's concealed illness, and the brother who had noticed everything and had been told nothing.

The father's return, session six. Yusuf attends, having been briefed by telephone by the therapist with Amina's knowledge. His first response is anger — at the school, at the boy, at the therapist for "putting ideas in her head". The therapist does not defend herself. She asks one question: "what did you want for her?" He answers, "a good life, a reputation". The therapist says, "those are not the same thing, and I think one of them is killing her." It is the most direct sentence in the case, and it is delivered after five sessions of alliance-building and in the presence of the family. Yusuf is silent for a long time; then he says, "what do I do?"

Week twelve. Amina has not cut for nine weeks. The ideation has reduced to occasional, without plan. The family has a rule about the roof door and the medication, and Ibrahim knows what to do if his sister is "behind glass". Yusuf has changed his work pattern to be home one week in three.

What the case teaches.

  1. The assessment was done alone and the sharing was planned. Had the therapist asked about the tablets in front of Halima, she would have received "I would never do that to my mother", and the plan would have remained in the cupboard.
  2. The disclosure to the mother was specific and limited. Amina knew exactly what would be said. That is what made the second and third disclosures possible.
  3. Means restriction was the intervention. Eight of the therapist's actions in that first session were administrative; the one that most reduced the risk was a locked box in another house.
  4. The family's culture was the terrain, not the obstacle. Yusuf's concern for reputation was not contemptible; it was the value he had organised his life around. Naming it, and separating it from his daughter's life, was possible only after five sessions of being taken seriously.
  5. The mother's own history was in the case from session one. Halima's untreated depression and her concealment of it were both the reason for Amina's silence and a risk in their own right. The therapist referred Halima for her own treatment in week seven.

8.6 Transcript: telling a fifteen-year-old that her mother will be told

Session two, minutes thirty-eight to forty-nine. Annotated.

A1 Therapist: Amina, I have to tell you something and it is not a punishment. — The opening that distinguishes a duty from a consequence. Without it, everything that follows is heard as the therapist taking the mother's side. A2 Amina: You are going to tell her. A3 Therapist: Yes. (pause) Tonight, before you leave. — The fact confirmed immediately, with the timing. Delay in this conversation produces a fantasy of delay in the disclosure. A4 Amina: You said you would not. A5 Therapist: I said I would not tell her what we talk about. I also said that if I thought you were in danger I would tell her. (pause) You are in danger. The tablets are in the kitchen. — Both halves of the original agreement restated, in her words. A therapist who only restates the exception sounds as though she is invoking a technicality. A6 Amina: (crying) She will ask me why. A7 Therapist: She will. (pause) And you do not have to answer that tonight. Tonight is about the tablets. — The separation of the urgent from the important, which is what makes the disclosure bearable. The reason can wait; the means cannot. A8 Amina: She will cry and then she will be angry and then she will tell my father. A9 Therapist: She may tell your father. (pause) I am going to ask her not to telephone him tonight, and to let us decide together what he is told and by whom. I cannot promise she will agree. — An honest answer about a third party's behaviour, which is more reassuring than a promise the therapist cannot keep. A10 Amina: Can I be there? A11 Therapist: I was going to offer you that. (pause) You can be in the room, or you can wait outside and come in after I have said the first part. — The choice, given in the terms of 8.4a. The client's only remaining control is the manner, and it is enough. A12 Amina: I want to be there. But I do not want to talk. A13 Therapist: You do not have to talk. (pause) I will say the words, and you can nod. — The permission that makes her presence possible. A client who is required to speak in that room will not attend it. A14 Amina: (long pause) Are you going to tell her about the note? A15 Therapist: No. (pause) That is between us, and it is not what she needs tonight. — A limited, kept promise at the moment of breaking a larger one. The therapist's credibility rests on the second promise being exactly as reliable as the first.

What the transcript shows. Four of the seven therapist turns are about what will and will not be said, and none of them is a reassurance. The client's objections are answered with facts about procedure. The one thing the therapist does not do is apologise — "I am sorry, I have to" — which would present the disclosure as the therapist's misfortune and invite the client to console her.

8.7 Laboratory: the observed risk assessment

The exercise. Fifteen minutes with a trained actor, observed, pass or fail, and it counts towards the observed risk assessment component of the module. The actor is briefed with a presentation and a script that contains three opportunities to minimise and one moment of direct disclosure that must not be missed.

The six requirements. The student must (1) ask directly about ideation; (2) ask about plan, intent and means; (3) ask about previous attempts and protective factors; (4) ask what will make it worse in the next two weeks; (5) formulate a level aloud and justify it; (6) begin a safety plan with the client.

CriterionFailPassDistinction
DirectnessEuphemism, or never askedAsked directly onceAsked directly, then followed up on frequency, controllability and content
CoverageFewer than six elementsSix to eightAll ten, with wording that produced specific answers
MeansNot askedAskedAsked, and a specific action agreed today
FormulationNone, or a labelA level with reasonsA level, a justification, a precipitate date and a review date
Safety planSuggested onlyBegunWritten with the client, in her language, with means addressed
The relationshipClient defensive or silencedClient engagedClient discloses more at fifteen minutes than at five

The three most common failures observed. Asking the question once and accepting the first denial. Skipping means because the conversation has become emotional. And ending the assessment without a review date, which turns a snapshot into a verdict.

The debrief question. "What did you feel when she said she had thought about it?" Students report fear, relief, sadness, and a wish to reassure. Each of those is a signal of what the student will do under pressure, and each is worth naming before it is enacted.

8.7a Telephone risk, and the session that is not a session

A large proportion of risk work in a family practice happens on the telephone, and the telephone has its own method.

What to establish in the first four minutes. Where the client is, whether anyone is with her, whether she is safe for the next hour, and whether she has taken anything or is about to. Nothing else matters until those four answers exist.

What to do with the answers. If she has taken something, the call becomes a call for an ambulance and the therapist stays on the line, with the address written down and repeated aloud. If she has not, the call becomes a short assessment of the elements that can be assessed by voice — ideation, plan, intent, means, and who is nearby — followed by a decision: bring her in, send someone to her, or agree a contact in fifteen minutes.

The three things a telephone assessment cannot do. It cannot see the room, the tablets, the injury, or the other person in the house. It cannot establish whether the client is alone and says she is not. And it cannot end the call — the therapist who says "call me if you need me" and puts the phone down has handed the risk back at the moment it was highest.

The note. Written immediately after the call, before the next appointment, with the time of the call, the four opening answers, the action taken, and the time of the follow-up contact. Notes written the next morning lose the times, and the times are the evidence.

8.8 Six errors

Error 1 — The indirect question. "You are not thinking of doing anything silly?" The question is designed to be answered with a denial, by the therapist as much as by the client.

Error 2 — Assessing in front of the family. The parent says "she is being dramatic" and the assessment ends before it begins. Individual assessment is a structural requirement, not a preference.

Error 3 — The no-harm contract. "Will you promise me you will not do anything until we meet?" It has no evidentiary or clinical value, it shifts responsibility to a person who cannot carry it, and it has been followed by deaths in cases where it was recorded as protective.

Error 4 — Means left in the house. The assessment identifies the tablets and the plan records them, and nothing is done about them. This is the single most common omission in the files of completed suicides.

Error 5 — Telling the family before telling the client. The disclosure is made in a telephone call "for safety" and the client learns of it afterwards. The relationship ends, and the next clinician receives a client who will not disclose.

Error 6 — Treating self-harm as a behaviour to be stopped. The prohibition produces concealment, the concealment removes the client from view, and the risk rises while the file says the behaviour has stopped.

8.9 Culture, faith and suicide

The religious frame is not an obstacle to the assessment; it is material within it. In households where suicide is understood as a sin and a shame upon the family, the client's ideation will be concealed with particular skill, and the protective factors will include the very belief that produces the shame. The therapist asks about both: what the client believes would happen to her soul, and what she believes would happen to her mother. The answers frequently reveal that the religious prohibition is holding the person alive and that the social consequence is pushing her toward death, at the same time.

Confession and concealment. A client who has confessed suicidal thoughts to a religious authority and been told to pray more has been given a penance without an assessment. The therapist's response is not to criticise the authority but to make the assessment: "what did you tell him, and what did he say, and did anyone ask you whether you had a plan?"

The family's reputation after a death. Where a death by suicide carries lasting stigma, the family's grief work includes managing the community's account of the death. This is a real clinical task and it is regularly ignored by models of bereavement developed elsewhere. The therapist helps the family decide what will be said, to whom, and by whom, and treats the family's need for a survivable story as legitimate.

Postvention. After a death by suicide, the family's risk rises: bereaved relatives have elevated rates of suicide, and the risk is highest in the first year and among parents and partners. The therapist's postvention work with a family includes assessing each member, naming the risk explicitly with the adults, and agreeing who will watch whom in the first three months. It is the case in which the therapist is most likely to be invited to comfort and least likely to assess.

The therapist's own exposure. A death by suicide in a therapist's caseload is an occupational hazard of clinical work and it produces a characteristic response: a review of every session, a conviction of responsibility, and either an increase in defensive practice or a withdrawal from risk entirely. The professional response is a structured review with a supervisor, a written account of the care provided, and support. The unprofessional response is silence, which converts one death into a pattern of avoidance that affects every subsequent client.

DEBATE — "When a competent adolescent refuses to allow her parents to be told about her suicidal thoughts, the therapist should respect her refusal." Argue for: the young person's developing autonomy, the evidence that forced disclosure ends engagement, and the clinical value of being the one adult who does not betray her. Argue against: the household is the only available supervision, the means are in the house, and the law in most jurisdictions permits or requires parental involvement. Then state the threshold at which you would override the young person, and who you would tell first.

8.9a The therapist's own history of suicide

Many clinicians have lost someone to suicide, have survived an attempt themselves, or both, and the training literature treats this as though it did not exist.

The rule. A therapist with an unprocessed personal history in this area is at risk of two opposite errors: the under-assessment that comes from familiarity ("she sounds like I did, and I am fine"), and the over-reaction that comes from fear ("I cannot take a client with ideation at all"). Both are clinical problems and neither is a character flaw.

The questions to ask yourself, in writing, once. Whose death or attempt is nearest to this case? What do I do when a client describes a method I have thought about? Am I assessing this client or managing my own fear? The answers determine whether the work needs consultation, a change of caseload, or personal therapy.

What is not acceptable. Practising without supervision in this domain while carrying an unexamined history, and disclosing the history to the client in a way that makes her responsible for the therapist's distress. "I lost my own brother, so I understand" is occasionally the most useful sentence in a session and is more often the moment at which the client stops being the patient.

8.10 Evidence note

What the research supports. Asking about suicide does not increase risk, and screening improves detection. Previous attempt is the strongest single predictor. Means restriction reduces suicide rates — the evidence from pesticide regulation, firearm access and medication packaging is among the strongest in the field. Safety planning interventions reduce subsequent suicidal behaviour relative to usual care, and brief contact interventions after discharge reduce repetition. The therapeutic alliance is associated with reduced suicidal ideation in treatment studies.

What is weaker. Risk assessment tools have modest predictive value at the individual level and should not be used as a substitute for clinical formulation; the field has largely accepted that prediction is not achievable and that formulation, safety planning and continuity of care are the useful targets. The evidence base for no-harm contracts is essentially null and their use is discouraged.

What is not supported. That hospitalisation prevents suicide in every high-risk case — the post-discharge period is the period of highest risk. That a client who denies ideation is safe. That a low score on an instrument settles the question.

The matrix cell. Development is probabilistic and non-linear; the same input produces different outcomes depending on timing and context. The same disclosure of ideation in a week when the client's mother is away and the tablets are unlocked, and in a week when the household is stable, are two different risks.

8.11 Key terms

Ideation — thoughts of ending one's life; assessed for frequency, intensity, controllability and content.

Plan — a specified method, place or time; rehearsed plans indicate higher risk.

Means restriction — reducing access to lethal methods; the intervention with the strongest evidence base.

Safety plan — a written, client-authored sequence of steps from warning sign to emergency contact.

Non-suicidal self-injury — self-harm without intent to die, performed for relief, punishment, communication or control.

Postvention — the support and risk management provided after a death by suicide.

Formulation — the clinical reasoning that links the assessment to a level of risk and a set of actions.

8.11a Three sentences that are remembered

The sentence that opens the assessment. "I ask everybody this, because it matters and because people are usually relieved to be asked: have you had thoughts of ending your own life?" The framing does two things — it normalises the question and it names the relief, which is usually accurate.

The sentence that survives a denial. "All right. And if it came back, who would you tell?" A denial is information, not a conclusion, and the follow-up question keeps the door open without an interrogation.

The sentence that ends a session safely. "Before you go: what is the plan for tonight, and who is in the house with you?" Asked at the door, it is heard as care rather than as assessment, and it produces information that the earlier questions did not.

8.12 Self-test

  1. Give the correct wording for asking about suicidal ideation, and explain why the four common versions fail.
  2. State the ten elements of the assessment, and the follow-up question most often forgotten.
  3. What are the actions attached to each of the three levels?
  4. Why is a no-harm contract not a safety plan?
  5. Give the six steps of a safety plan.
  6. In the Suleiman case, what four actions most reduced the risk, and why?
  7. What is non-suicidal self-injury, and what are its four functions?
  8. What is postvention, and why is the family at elevated risk?

Answers. (1) "Have you had thoughts of ending your own life — and if so, how often, and what have you thought about doing?" The common versions use euphemism, vagueness, leading phrasing, or a closed question, each of which invites denial or produces an unusable answer. (2) Ideation, plan, intent, means, previous attempts, protective factors, substance use and impulsivity, family history, current stressors and recent losses, disclosure and support network; the forgotten question is what will make it worse in the next two weeks. (3) Low — document, plan, review; moderate — plan, means restriction, notify with consent, increase frequency, refer; high — do not leave the person alone, urgent psychiatric or emergency care, inform family, document contemporaneously. (4) Because a contract extracts a promise from someone who cannot guarantee it and produces no actions, whereas a plan specifies who does what, when, with what means removed. (5) Warning signs; things I can do alone; people and places that distract; people I can ask for help; professionals and services; making the environment safe. (6) The individual assessment, which produced the information; telling the mother with Amina's knowledge and a limited content; the removal of the medication to another house; and the locked roof door — the last two being means restriction, the intervention with the strongest evidence. (7) Self-harm without intent to die, performed to relieve intolerable feeling, to punish, to communicate, or to control. (8) The support and risk management of those bereaved by suicide; because bereaved relatives, particularly parents and partners, have elevated suicide rates, highest in the first year.

8.13 Reflective task

Write the note you would want a coroner to read about a client you were worried about. Then write the note you actually keep. The difference between the two is the measure of your documentation practice.

8.14 Further reading

  • Stanley, B. and Brown, G. on safety planning — the source of the six-step structure used here.
  • Jobes, D. on the collaborative assessment and management of suicidality — the best-developed clinical approach to formulation and alliance.
  • Hawton, K. and colleagues on self-harm in adolescents — the epidemiology and the evidence on repetition.
  • The WHO suicide prevention materials, particularly on means restriction at population level.
  • Any current national guideline on the assessment and management of suicide risk in your jurisdiction.

Next: Week 9 — Risk II: intimate partner violence and child protection.

Week 9 — Risk II: intimate partner violence and child protection

Learning outcomes. By the end of this week the student can (1) distinguish situational couple violence from coercive control and explain why the distinction changes the treatment; (2) screen each partner separately with direct questions, and document a positive screen; (3) identify the factors associated with lethal risk and act on them; (4) explain to a couple why conjoint work is contraindicated and what is offered instead; (5) build a safety plan with a victim that does not require her to leave; and (6) discharge the statutory duty to report a child protection concern correctly and in the right order.

The distinction on which everything depends

Situational couple violenceCoercive control
PatternMutual, low-level, escalates under stress; both partners can be aggressiveOne partner dominates systematically; the violence is one instrument among many
FearSituational, episodicChronic; the victim is afraid most of the time
MechanismsArguments that escalateSurveillance, isolation, financial control, threats, sexual coercion, rules, humiliation
The victim's view"We both lose control""I have to be careful all the time"
Couple therapyMay be appropriate after assessmentContraindicated; risk increases after conjoint sessions
ResponseCommunication and de-escalation work; individual safety planSafety planning, advocacy, protection; individual work only

Why the distinction is not academic. Conjoint work in a coercive control case does three harmful things. It treats the violence as a communication problem, which the perpetrator welcomes. It gives the perpetrator access to the victim's disclosures, because everything said in the room is available to him afterwards. And it produces retaliation: the literature and the practice literature agree that the period after a conjoint session is a period of elevated risk, and the retaliation is frequently about something said in the session.

The family therapist's structural problem. Couple and family therapy is the modality most often requested by couples in which control is present, because the request itself is part of the control: "we need help communicating" is a formulation in which the violence does not appear. The therapist who accepts the referral at face value will conduct twelve sessions of communication training in a household where one partner is deciding what the other may say.

9.1 The lecture in ninety minutes

MinutesContentTeaching form
0–10The two patterns; how the referral conceals the secondTwo referral letters, students identify which is which
10–25Five concepts: control, lethality, separation, the structural problem, the child as victimLecture
25–40Screening: who, when, how, and what to do with a positiveDemonstration, then paired practice of the four questions
40–55The lethality factors, in plain languageCard sort; each student ranks five cases
55–70Ending conjoint work, and what to offer insteadRole-play of the conversation with a couple
70–80The reporting duty: threshold, route, order of operationsLocal rules quiz
80–90The Okafor caseCase, with students deciding what is reported and to whom

9.2 Five concepts that organise the week

Concept 1 — Coercive control is a pattern of domination, not a series of incidents. The incidents are the visible part; the substance is the regulation of the other person's daily life — what she wears, whom she sees, whether she works, whether she has money, whether her phone is checked. The clinical implication is that an absence of recent violence is not an absence of danger, and that the therapist must ask about control, not only about hitting.

Concept 2 — Lethality is assessable, and the factors are specific. Strangulation, threats to kill, access to a weapon, stalking, separation, pregnancy, escalation in frequency or severity, a history of violence towards others, and a partner who has been violent during sex. Each raises the risk of homicide substantially. The therapist does not need a scored instrument to ask about them, and should know the local one if it exists.

Concept 3 — Separation is the period of highest danger. The weeks after a woman leaves, or after she says she will, are the weeks in which the risk of homicide peaks. This inverts the assumption of many helpers, who treat leaving as the solution; leaving is a process with a peak, and the safety planning must cover the leaving rather than begin after it.

Concept 4 — The therapy setting is a site of risk, not a sanctuary. Appointment times are known, waiting rooms are public, text messages are visible, and a partner who is controlling will want to attend every session. The practice must be designed with this in mind: separate contact details, notes that cannot be read at home, a protocol for what to say if the partner telephones, and a receptionist who has been trained.

Concept 5 — The child is a victim of the control, not only a witness. Exposure to the control of one parent by another is a form of harm in its own right, recognised in child protection frameworks in most jurisdictions. The child's presence changes the therapist's duties, the risk formulation and the plan.

9.2a Screening: who, when, how

Who. Every adult seen in a couple or family case, at least once, alone. Not "where indicated", because indication is what the screening is for.

When. In the first two sessions, before any conjoint work, and again if the presentation changes — a new injury, a new fear, a partner who has become more attentive to the therapy.

How. Alone, in a room the partner cannot see into, with an explanation of why the question is being asked. The four questions, in this order:

  1. "Has your partner ever hit, kicked, pushed, slapped or otherwise hurt you?"
  2. "Has your partner ever made you afraid — not angry, afraid?"
  3. "Has your partner ever forced you to do something sexual that you did not want?"
  4. "Do you feel safe going home today?"

The follow-ups that produce the information.

  • "What is the worst thing he has ever done?" The answer is frequently not in the first four questions.
  • "Has he ever put his hands around your neck, or made you unable to breathe?" Strangulation is under-reported and is the single strongest non-fatal predictor of homicide.
  • "Has he ever said he would kill you, or the children?"
  • "Does he check your phone, your money, where you go?"
  • "Has the violence got more frequent or worse in the last year?"
  • "What happens after he has been violent — does he apologise, blame you, promise, or nothing?"

What to do with a positive screen. Thank the client, name what she has described, and ask what she wants. Then the sequence: assess lethality; agree immediate safety for today; discuss what the therapist must do (children, statutory duties); provide information about services without making attendance a condition; record; and plan the next contact. The full safety-planning method is in 9.3a.

What not to do. Do not ask in front of the partner. Do not ask as part of a checklist read aloud to both. Do not respond with "have you considered leaving?" as the first sentence, which is heard as "your judgement has been poor". And do not offer couple therapy as the response to a positive screen.

9.2b The lethality factors, in the language of a session

The list below is the one a family therapist should be able to run through without notes. None of them is a prediction; each of them changes the urgency.

1. Strangulation. Ask: "has he ever put his hands around your neck, or covered your mouth and nose?" A woman who has been strangled is at substantially elevated risk of being killed, and the assault frequently leaves no visible injury and is not reported to the police.

2. Threats to kill. Ask for the words. "I will kill you and nobody will find you" is a different fact from "I was angry".

3. Access to a weapon. A firearm, a machete, a knife kept in the bedroom. Ask what is in the house.

4. Separation, or the threat of it. Ask: "have you told him you want to leave, or has he said he would not allow it?" The answer sets the risk of the next month.

5. Stalking and monitoring. Following, checking the phone, tracking the car, arriving unannounced, calling the therapist. Monitoring is a marker of the partner's need for control and of his capacity to find her.

6. Escalation. More frequent, more severe, or new forms. Ask for a comparison with a year ago.

7. Pregnancy and the postnatal period. Violence frequently begins or escalates in pregnancy, and the risk of homicide is elevated.

8. Sexual violence within the relationship. Ask directly; the question is rarely asked and frequently answered.

9. A history of violence outside the home — towards previous partners, children, strangers, animals.

10. The partner's own instability — substance misuse, a recent job loss, a mental illness, a court case, jealousy that has become fixed. Combined with a weapon and a separation, this is the configuration that appears in the files after a death.

How to use the list in a session. Not as an interrogation. Three or four questions, chosen for what the client has already said, in a tone that says "these are questions I ask everybody". The client who is asked about strangulation by a therapist who is not shocked will answer, and the answer may be the first time she has said it aloud.

9.2c The practice that is safe to be a client in

Safety is a property of the practice, not only of the therapist's intentions. Six arrangements, each cheap and each routinely absent.

  1. A second contact number that the partner does not know. A client's contact details must be recorded with a note on who may be telephoned and what may be said. The receptionist who telephones a house and asks for "Mrs Okafor about her appointment" has disclosed the therapy.
  2. A receptionist who has been briefed. The person at the front desk is part of the clinical service. She needs two sentences: do not confirm whether a person is a client; if a man arrives asking for his wife, say you will check and get the therapist.
  3. A waiting room that is not a corridor. Where a couple is in a violent relationship, the waiting room is where the confrontation happens. Separate entrances, staggered appointment times, or the client waiting in the car and being fetched.
  4. A protocol for the partner's telephone call. Written down: what may be said, who says it, and the note that is made afterwards. Usually: "I can confirm that she had an appointment and I cannot discuss anything else."
  5. Notes that cannot be read at home. If the client takes a receipt, an appointment card or a leaflet home, it will be found. The practice's answer: nothing written goes home unless the client asks for it.
  6. A named person to call at night. Not the therapist's personal number as a general service, and not a promise of availability. A named duty line, written on a card, with the hours stated.

The test. Ask the receptionist, or the answering service, what she would say if a man telephoned and said, "my wife comes to you, I want to know when her appointment is". If the answer is not rehearsed, the practice is not ready for this week's cases.

9.3 Ending conjoint work: the conversation and the alternative

The therapist has screened both partners, and the screen is positive. The couple is expecting a session about communication. The task is to change the treatment without losing the victim.

The principles. Do not confront the perpetrator with the accusation in front of the victim, because she will pay for it. Do not tell the victim's account to the perpetrator. Do not use the word "violence" unless the victim has used it in front of him, because the disclosure of what she said in the individual session may itself be dangerous.

The script, with both present.

  1. "I have met each of you separately, as I said I would, and I have decided that the work I do — seeing couples together — is not the right thing for you at the moment."
  2. "The reason is that I do not think this is a communication problem, and couple work can make things worse when it is not."
  3. "Here is what I am offering instead: individual sessions for each of you, with me or with a colleague, and a referral for [the relevant service]."
  4. "I want to be clear about one thing: I will not be discussing with either of you what the other has said to me."

The predictable responses, and the answers.

  • The perpetrator's anger: "why does she get her own sessions?" — "Because I need to see each of you alone to do this work, and that is the same for both of you." (The symmetry is real and is stated without disclosing anything.)
  • The victim's fear: she may not want to end conjoint work, because the conjoint sessions are the only place she is safe from him. This is a genuine clinical dilemma. The resolution is not to continue conjoint sessions but to increase the frequency and reliability of individual contact, and to help her build other safe contacts.
  • The couple's refusal: "we came for couple therapy". Then the therapist states the limit as a limit on her own practice: "I am not able to do the work you are asking for, because I think it would put one of you at risk. I would rather lose the case than do that."

What is offered instead, and why each element matters.

OfferPurpose
Individual sessions with the same therapist, for the victimContinuity and a safe place to plan
Individual work with a different therapist for the perpetratorAvoids the therapist holding both accounts and being triangulated
Referral to specialist advocacy or a domestic violence servicePractical help the therapist cannot provide: housing, police, legal, money
A referral to a perpetrators' programme where one existsThe only intervention with evidence of effect, and only where attendance is monitored
A child protection referral where children are presentThe statutory duty, discharged separately from the couple's treatment

The therapist who continues couple work anyway. The commonest reason is the therapist's belief in the relationship or in her own skill. The second commonest is the victim's request. Neither is a justification, and the note written after an injury will not read well: "continued conjoint work despite disclosure of coercive control in individual session of 4 March."

9.3a Safety planning with a victim

The framing sentence. "I am not going to tell you to leave. Whether you leave is your decision and you know your situation better than I do. What I want to do is make sure that if you ever need to leave quickly, you can."

That sentence is the foundation, because the victim has usually been told by professionals, relatives and her husband what to do, and the therapist who does not add to the list becomes usable.

The plan, in eight parts.
