  1. The documents. Identity card, birth certificates, the children's records, the marriage certificate, bank cards, a copy of any protection order. Kept in a place he does not know, or with a trusted person.
  2. The money. A small amount saved, a separate account if possible, and knowledge of where to get emergency assistance.
  3. The bag. A packed bag at a neighbour's or a sister's house: clothes for the children, medication, a phone charger, a copy of documents.
  4. The code word. One word, texted to one person, meaning "come now" or "call the police". Agreed in advance, with a person who will act.
  5. The route. Where she goes, how she gets there, and what she does if the first place is not available. Two options, not one.
  6. The children. Where they are at each hour of the day, who collects them, and what the school will do if he arrives.
  7. The phone. A second phone or a saved number; the emergency number on speed dial; and the practice's policy about what the therapist will say if he answers her phone.
  8. The review. The plan is reviewed at each contact, because circumstances change and because the plan that was made in March may be wrong in June.

What the therapist does not do. Does not hold the bag, the documents or the money. Does not promise to be reachable at all hours. Does not contact the perpetrator or mediate. Does not tell the family, the church, or the employer without consent, except where a child protection duty requires it.

The danger of the written plan. A safety plan found in a handbag or on a phone can escalate the violence. The practical solution is that the victim keeps the information in her head or in a form that can be explained away, and the therapist keeps the record; the copy given to the client is minimised and, where necessary, not given at all.

9.4 Child protection: recognition, threshold, duty

The four categories. Physical abuse; sexual abuse; emotional abuse; neglect. In most jurisdictions the definition also includes exposure to domestic violence and, increasingly, exposure to harmful practices. The therapist's task is not to determine whether abuse occurred — that is the job of the investigative authority — but to recognise a concern at the threshold the law sets, and to report it.

The threshold in plain language. A concern is reportable when there is a reasonable suspicion that a child is being harmed or is at risk of significant harm. Not proof; suspicion. The therapist does not need to be right; she needs to be reasonable, and the reasonableness is judged on what she knew and when, not on the eventual outcome.

What raises a concern in ordinary family work.

  • A disclosure by a child, however tentative or retracted.
  • An injury with an inconsistent explanation, or an explanation that changes.
  • Sexualised behaviour in a young child that is developmentally unexpected.
  • A child who is frightened of a specific adult, or who will not go home.
  • A parent's account of violence towards a child, including "discipline" that leaves injury.
  • Exposure to the coercive control described in 9.2 — a child who sees it, hears it, or is used in it.
  • Chronic neglect: hunger, untreated illness, absence from school, a child left with inadequate care.
  • A child who discloses that an adult has asked him to keep a secret that frightens him.

The reporting route in Nigeria, and the general principle. The Child Rights Act 2003 has been adopted by many but not all states, and state child protection laws vary; the practical route is usually the state ministry responsible for social welfare or the state child protection unit, the police family support unit, or a designated safeguarding lead in a school or hospital. The therapist should have the four local numbers in her practice manual, verified within the past year, and should know which of them operates at night.

The order of operations, which matters more than the route.

  1. Ensure immediate safety. If the child is in danger now, the emergency services come first and the paperwork second.
  2. Record what was said, in the child's words, before speaking to anyone else, with the date and time.
  3. Consult — a supervisor, a designated safeguarding lead, a child protection adviser — where consultation is available within the hour. Consultation does not delay a report that is required.
  4. Report. The call is made by the therapist, recorded with the time, the name of the person spoken to and the advice received.
  5. Tell the parents, unless telling them would endanger the child. The default is transparency, and the exception is where a parent is the alleged abuser or the parent's response would expose the child to further harm or to pressure to retract. The decision is recorded with reasons.
  6. Tell the child what will happen, in words she can hold: who will be spoken to, what she will be asked, and that she has not done anything wrong.
  7. Continue the treatment if possible, and record the referral and the outcome.

What the therapist does not do. Does not investigate. Does not interview the child repeatedly to establish the facts, which contaminates the evidential process and harms the child. Does not confront the alleged abuser. Does not promise the child confidentiality. And does not accept the family's assurance that "it will not happen again" as a reason not to report.

9.4a The child's disclosure: what to say, and what not to say

What to say. Four things, in this order. "Thank you for telling me." "You have not done anything wrong." "What you have told me is important and I am going to have to tell someone who can help — I will tell you who and what I am going to say before I do it." "I am not going to ask you to tell the story again and again."

What not to say. "Are you sure?" "Why did you not tell me before?" "Did he touch you here?" — the leading question that puts the words in the child's mouth. "Promise me you will tell me if it happens again." And "this will be our secret", which is the sentence that makes the child complicit and which the therapist of all people must not say.

Recording. Verbatim, in quotation marks, including the child's own vocabulary and the questions that were asked. "She said 'he does a bad thing to me at night'. I asked 'can you tell me what happens?' She said 'he comes to my bed and I pretend to sleep'." That record is markedly more useful than "child disclosed abuse".

The child's retraction. Common, and frequently the product of pressure at home. A retraction is recorded, reported and not treated as a resolution. The therapist's note: "child retracted the account on 12 April, having spent the weekend with the maternal grandmother; the original disclosure stands recorded and the referral continues."

The non-abusing parent. Frequently the person who brings the child, frequently disbelieving, frequently the target of pressure from the extended family to "settle it". The therapist's work with her is specific: what she believes, what she has been told by whom, what she is afraid of, and what she will do if the child is disbelieved by the family. Her capacity to protect is the single best predictor of the child's outcome, and supporting it is the most useful clinical act available.

9.4b When the report is made against the family's wishes

The family will be angry, and the anger is understandable: they came for help and received an investigation.

What the therapist says. "I know this is not what you wanted. I am required to report what I am told when a child may be at risk, and I have done it. I am telling you now, before anyone contacts you, because I do not want you to be surprised." Then the specifics: who was called, what was said, what is likely to happen next.

What the therapist does not apologise for. The report itself. An apology — "I am so sorry, I had no choice" — converts a professional duty into a personal betrayal and invites the family to treat the therapist as the source of the problem. The better sentence: "I did what the law requires, and I am still your therapist if you want me to be."

When the family leaves. A proportion will. The therapist records the reasoning, offers the referral, and does not pursue. A family that leaves because a report was made may return, and the file should make that possible: a closing summary, a note that the door is open, and no editorial about the family's decision.

Where the alleged abuser is a client. The therapist may be treating the perpetrator — frequently a father in a family case — and now has a duty to the child that conflicts with the duty to the client. The resolution is not subtle: the child's safety governs. The therapist reports, tells the client that she has done so and why, and considers whether she can continue to treat him. In many cases she cannot, and the transfer is the correct outcome.

9.4c The four cases that must not be missed

Four presentations in which the child protection duty is engaged and in which family therapists most often hesitate.

The one that is "discipline". A father describes beating his nine-year-old with a cable "because he is stubborn". He is not asking for help with it; he is describing ordinary parenting. The therapist's duty is engaged if the law's threshold is met, and the conversation is not about whether the father loves his son. The therapist says what she must do, and does it.

The one that is "cultural". A family requests a practice that is harmful to a child — a cutting, a beating as initiation, a marriage of a fifteen-year-old. The therapist does not adjudicate the culture. She applies the law, states it plainly, reports where required, and works with whatever part of the family will work with her.

The one where the child is used. A mother reports that her husband's family have taken her son to another state and will not return him. This may be abduction, custody dispute, or child protection matter; the therapist does not decide which, and she does telephone the authorities and record the advice.

The one that is a disclosure by an adult. A client in individual therapy says, in passing, that her brother "does things" to her eight-year-old niece. The duty is engaged for the niece, who is not a client and has never been seen. The therapist reports, tells the client that she is doing so, and works with the client's guilt afterwards.

The common thread. In each of the four, the therapist's hesitation is about the relationship with the adults. The child is not in the room. That is exactly why the duty exists, and why the decision cannot be left to the clinical judgement of the person who is fond of the family.

9.5 Case: the Okafors, and the referral that said "communication"

Referral. Blessing Okafor, 34, a hairdresser, telephones a couple practice: "we need help with communication. My husband has agreed to come." Emeka Okafor, 41, a spare-parts trader, attends the first session. Children: Chidi, 6, and Ada, 2.

Session one, both present. The complaint is that "she is always angry and she shouts in front of the children", and "he does not listen". The therapist, following the practice protocol, explains that she will see each of them alone once before beginning conjoint work, and gives the reason: it is how she gets to know each person. Emeka objects mildly, is reassured, and agrees.

Session two, Blessing alone, and what she discloses.

  • Physical violence for nine years, beginning in the first year of marriage. Pushing, slapping, a beating during her second pregnancy that led to a threatened miscarriage.
  • Strangulation, twice, the most recent eight months ago.
  • Threats: "he says he will kill me and take the children to his village, and nobody will ask him any questions."
  • Control: he keeps her bank card, checks her phone every night, decides whether she may visit her mother, and has twice arrived at her salon and sat outside.
  • Sexual coercion: "sometimes I let him so that he will not be angry."
  • Isolation: her mother lives in another state; her closest friend has stopped visiting because of him.
  • Threats about the children: "he says if I leave he will make sure I never see them."

The assessment. Coercive control with high lethality indicators: strangulation, a specific threat to kill, a threat to remove the children, monitoring of her movements, escalation during pregnancy, and recent escalation in frequency. There is also exposure of the children to the violence: Chidi has been present at four incidents and has begun wetting the bed.

The therapist's actions in that session, in order.

  1. She did not ask "why have you not left". She said: "thank you for telling me. None of this is your fault."
  2. She assessed immediate safety: "are you safe going home today?" Blessing said she was, "as long as I do not annoy him".
  3. She asked about the children and made the note that establishes the child protection question: present at four incidents, bedwetting since the last one.
  4. She explained what she must do. She would not be reporting to the police — that is the client's decision — but she was obliged to report the children's exposure to the state child protection unit, and she would tell Blessing exactly what she would say and when. She was clear that the report concerned the children, not the marriage.
  5. She made the report the same afternoon, after a supervision call, and recorded the name of the officer and the advice.
  6. She arranged a second individual session in three days, and gave Blessing the practice's emergency number and a code phrase — "I am calling about the hair appointment" — that would tell the therapist that she needed to talk and could not speak freely.
  7. She documented the safety plan in the 9.3a form, with the documents left with Blessing's aunt and a bag at a neighbour's house.

Session three — the conjoint session that did not happen. Emeka attends expecting the couple session and is told, with Blessing present, that the therapist will not be conducting conjoint work. The therapist used the script of 9.3, and Emeka's response was as predicted: anger, then a demand to know what his wife had said, then a threat to withdraw from the process altogether.

The therapist's answer, which is the one that matters. "I am not going to tell you what she said. What I will tell you is my clinical view, which is that the two of you working on communication in this room would not be safe, and I am not willing to do it. I will see you individually, and I will give you the name of a programme that works with men in your situation."

Emeka did not attend individual sessions. Blessing attended eleven over four months.

The child protection process. A social worker visited the home twice. No removal. A case was opened and the children were seen at school. Blessing was asked, by the social worker, whether she wanted to leave; she said she did not, yet. The therapist supported her in that answer in the recorded conversation with the social worker, which is the point at which many therapists instead advocate for the outcome they believe in.

Month five — the escalation the plan anticipated. Blessing telephoned at 9.40 p.m. from her sister's house. He had found the bag at the neighbour's, had been "calm", and then had thrown a chair through the window of the room where Chidi was sleeping. She had left with both children. The therapist's role in that call was three things: confirm immediate safety, direct her to the police station and the social worker, and be on the telephone the following morning when she made the statement. She did not go to the house, did not confront Emeka, did not offer her own home.

The outcome, which is not a happy ending and is a good outcome. Blessing did not return. A protection order was applied for with the help of a legal aid organisation. Emeka was charged and the case is before a court. Blessing attended therapy for a further eight months, and the children were seen by a child therapist. The therapist's own note after the night call reads, in part: "the plan made in session two covered the leaving. It did not cover what he would do when he found the bag. The bag should have been somewhere he had no reason to go."

What the case teaches.

  1. The referral said "communication" and the case was coercive control. The screening was the only thing that found it, and the screening was a practice protocol rather than a clinical hunch.
  2. The individual session was the instrument of safety, and the conjoint session was the instrument of danger. Everything the case required came out of the individual sessions; everything that would have been harmful would have happened in the joint one.
  3. The child protection report was separate from the marriage. It concerned the children's exposure, it was made with the mother's knowledge, and it did not require her to leave or to make a statement against her husband.
  4. The therapist did not tell her to leave, and she left. The decision was hers, made five months later, and it was made because the plan and the relationship were in place.
  5. The plan had a hole, and the therapist recorded it. The bag's location was a foreseeable mistake, the note says so, and that note is what a supervisor needs in order to improve the practice's protocol.

9.6 Transcript: "I am not going to tell you what she said"

Session three, minutes six to nineteen, with both present. Annotated.

B1 Emeka: So what did she tell you? Because she has been telling people things. B2 Therapist: I am not going to tell you what she said. (pause) I am going to tell you what I have decided. — The refusal and the substitution, in one breath. The refusal alone leaves a vacuum that he will fill with suspicion; the substitution gives the session a subject. B3 Emeka: So you have decided something without me. B4 Therapist: I have. (pause) I have decided that I will not see the two of you together. — Ownership of the decision. The therapist does not say "the practice's policy requires", which would be true and would invite him to argue with an absent authority. B5 Emeka: Why? B6 Therapist: Because I do not think this is a communication problem, and because couple work in a situation like yours can make things more dangerous afterwards. — "A situation like yours" rather than "your violence": the therapist has not disclosed the individual session's content and has not accused him, and the meaning is unmistakable. B7 Emeka: (to Blessing) You told her I hit you. B8 Therapist: (to Emeka, immediately) I am talking to you now, not to her. — The intervention that protects the victim in the room. If the therapist lets that question land, the session has become a place where the violence will be settled afterwards. B9 Emeka: (standing) I am not paying for this. B10 Therapist: You do not have to. (pause) Sit down if you want to hear what I am offering, or leave now and I will write to you. — The choice, without pursuit and without a threat. The therapist does not stand, does not raise her voice, and does not move towards the door. B11 Emeka: (after a pause, sitting) What are you offering. B12 Therapist: Individual sessions with me, or with a colleague if you prefer. A referral to a programme for men who want to change how they handle anger and control. And no conjoint sessions until that work has been done. — The offer is real, and the referral is to a programme with an evidence base rather than to "anger management", which is a different and largely ineffective thing. B13 Emeka: And her? B14 Therapist: She has the same offer. (pause) I want to be clear that I will not be passing messages between you. — The symmetry that is true and that protects the individual work, and the explicit refusal of the mediator role, which is the role a controlling partner will try to install.

What the transcript shows. The therapist never discloses, never accuses and never argues. She states decisions in the first person, protects the victim in the room with one sentence, and offers a real alternative. The one moment of real risk is B7, and the response to it is the shortest turn in the transcript.

9.7 Laboratory: the screening interview, observed

The exercise. Each student conducts a twelve-minute individual screening interview with an actor playing one of four briefed roles, and is then asked two minutes of questions by the observer. The roles are deliberately varied: a woman who minimises, a woman who is not being abused but whose partner is controlling about money, a man who is being abused by his wife and will not say so, and a woman who discloses strangulation in the last minute.

What is assessed. Whether the questions are asked at all; whether they are asked directly; whether the follow-ups are used; whether the lethality factors are covered; whether the therapist's response to a disclosure increases or reduces the client's willingness to say more; and whether the safety planning begins.

Marks (20).

Criterion0–12–34–5
ScreeningNot askedAsked in general termsAll four questions asked directly, with the strangulation follow-up
ResponseShock, advice, or "why did you not leave"Neutral but passiveNames what was described, states it is not her fault, asks what she wants
LethalityNot coveredPartly coveredStrangulation, threats, weapons, separation, escalation, children — all covered naturally
SafetyNoneAdvice givenA plan begun, with the "I am not telling you to leave" frame
DutyNot mentionedMentioned vaguelyThe child protection duty stated specifically, with what will be reported and when

The most common failure. The student stops after the first question because the answer is "no". The screening is not a checklist; it is a conversation in which the second and third questions do most of the work.

9.8 Six errors

Error 1 — Screening in front of the partner. The commonest and most dangerous error in the whole of couple work. The screening is individual, always, and the reason is stated so that it does not look like suspicion.

Error 2 — Accepting the couple's formulation. "We need communication help" is the referral, not the case. The formulation belongs to the assessment.

Error 3 — Continuing conjoint work after a disclosure of control. Sometimes because the victim begs for it, sometimes because the therapist believes she can manage the sessions. The sessions become the place where the violence is negotiated and, afterwards, enacted.

Error 4 — Telling her to leave. She has been told by her mother, her pastor and her husband. Her judgement is the only asset she has, and the therapist who overrides it has joined the list.

Error 5 — The report without the conversation. The call is made and the family learns of it from a social worker at the door. The relationship, and frequently the treatment, ends there.

Error 6 — The child interview conducted to establish the facts. Repeated questioning contaminates the evidence, harms the child, and does the investigator's job badly. One account, recorded verbatim, then the report.

9.9 Culture, faith and the household's rules about violence

The extended family's jurisdiction. In many of the households this book serves, a marital dispute is settled by elders, and a wife who takes it outside the family is breaking a rule that the family enforces. The therapist's question — "who in the family knows, and what did they say?" — is therefore not background; it is the risk assessment. A family council that has decided the matter is closed is a family council that will return the wife to the house.

Bride price and the language of ownership. Where a marriage involved a bride price, the language of the household may include the idea that the wife has been "paid for", and the husband's family may treat a separation as a debt. The therapist does not need to argue with the custom; she needs to know that the client may be hearing an obligation where the therapist hears a crime, and to make the distinction explicit: "the family may speak about what was paid. That does not change what is allowed to be done to you."

Religious teaching on submission. A client may hold a theology in which endurance is a virtue and divorce is a sin, and she may hold it sincerely. The therapist does not argue with the theology. She asks three questions: what does your faith say about what is being done to you; what does it say about your children watching; and who in your church would help if you told them? The answers frequently produce an internal permission the therapist could never have supplied.

The church as a resource and as a hazard. A congregation can provide practical help — a place to stay, money, childcare — and can also direct a woman back to a violent husband in the name of reconciliation. The therapist's task is to find out which, before recommending that the client tell her pastor.

The man's own shame. The perpetrator in many of these cases is not a stranger to shame; he is a man who has failed at work, at money, at status, and whose violence is the one arena in which he is not powerless. This is an explanation and not an excuse, and it matters clinically because a programme that addresses shame has a better chance than one that addresses anger.

The child's discipline. Physical discipline is normative in many households, and the boundary between discipline and abuse is set by the law and by injury, not by the therapist's cultural preference. The Child Rights Act and state laws define the threshold; the therapist applies it and does not negotiate it in the session, while recognising that the parent who is reported for a beating will experience the report as an assault on his parenthood.

DEBATE — "A therapist who tells a woman to leave a violent marriage is doing her job; one who does not is colluding." Argue for: safety is the first duty, the therapist has information the woman cannot have, and neutrality in the face of violence is complicity. Argue against: the woman's judgement about her own risk is more accurate than the therapist's, leaving is the period of greatest danger, and the decision must remain hers if the plan is to be used. Then write the sentence you would actually say, and the case in which you would depart from it.

9.10 Evidence note

What the research supports. The distinction between coercive control and situational violence is well established in the specialist literature and in practice guidance, and conjoint treatment is contraindicated where control is present. Strangulation is a strong predictor of subsequent homicide. Separation is a period of elevated risk. Specialist advocacy and safety planning improve outcomes for victims, and the evidence on perpetrator programmes is modest but consistent for court-mandated attendance. Screening increases identification; the evidence that screening alone improves outcomes is weaker, which is why screening must be paired with a response.

What is weaker. Prediction of lethality at the individual level remains limited. The evidence base on couple therapy in situational violence is small and contested. Child protection interventions have mixed evidence, with outcomes depending heavily on the quality of the service rather than the model.

What is not supported. That leaving resolves the risk — it often raises it in the short term. That a couple who present voluntarily are unlikely to have violence in the relationship. That a perpetrator's attendance at an anger management course changes behaviour. And that a child who is "too young to understand" is unaffected by exposure.

The matrix cell. Development is probabilistic and non-linear; the same input produces different outcomes depending on timing and context. The same disclosure of strangulation in a first session and in a twelfth changes the case entirely, because in the twelfth the therapist has already conducted six conjoint sessions.

9.11 Key terms

Coercive control — a pattern of domination through surveillance, isolation, threats and violence.

Situational couple violence — mutual, episodic aggression arising in conflict, without a pattern of control.

Lethality factors — the specific features associated with elevated homicide risk, including strangulation, threats to kill, weapons and separation.

Safety plan — an agreed set of preparations and contacts enabling a victim to act quickly if needed.

Advocacy — specialist practical support from a domestic violence service.

Threshold — the level of concern at which a report is required; reasonable suspicion of significant harm.

Exposure — a child's experience of violence between adults in the household, treated as harm in many frameworks.

9.11a The note that protects the next clinician

Five lines, written after every case in which violence or a child protection concern has appeared.

Assessed individually on [date]. Client disclosed [strangulation on two occasions, most recent eight months ago; threats to kill; monitoring of phone and movements]. Lethality factors present: strangulation, threats, separation threat, escalation. Conjoint work contraindicated; couple informed [date], with reasons; individual work offered to both. Child protection referral made to [agency], [time], officer [name], advice received: [ ]. Safety plan agreed and recorded. Emergency contact protocol explained. Review [date]. Supervision [date].

The five lines exist for a reason that is not defensive: the next clinician, the social worker, the court and the therapist herself in six months all need to know what was known, when it was known, and what was done.

9.12 Self-test

  1. Give four differences between situational couple violence and coercive control.
  2. Why is conjoint work contraindicated in coercive control, and what are the three harms it produces?
  3. State the four screening questions and three follow-ups.
  4. Name six lethality factors and the one most strongly associated with homicide.
  5. What is the "I am not going to tell you to leave" frame, and why does it work?
  6. Give the eight parts of a safety plan.
  7. What is the threshold for a child protection report, and what are the seven steps of the order of operations?
  8. In the Okafor case, what did the therapist refuse to do, and why did each refusal matter?

Answers. (1) Pattern (mutual and episodic versus systematic domination); fear (situational versus chronic); mechanism (escalating arguments versus surveillance, isolation, money, threats); treatment (conjoint may be appropriate versus contraindicated). (2) Because it treats violence as a communication problem, gives the perpetrator access to the victim's disclosures, and produces retaliation after the session. (3) Hit, kick, push; made you afraid; forced sexual acts; safe going home today. Follow-ups: worst thing he has done; hands around your neck; threats to kill; escalation; monitoring. (4) Strangulation, threats to kill, access to a weapon, separation, stalking, escalation, pregnancy, sexual violence, history of violence outside the home, perpetrator instability; strangulation is the strongest non-fatal predictor. (5) An explicit refusal to direct the client, which preserves her judgement and makes the therapist usable; it works because she has been instructed by everyone else and the therapist's refusal distinguishes her. (6) Documents, money, bag, code word, route, children, phone, review. (7) Reasonable suspicion of significant harm. Order: immediate safety; record verbatim; consult; report; tell the parents unless that endangers the child; tell the child; continue treatment and record. (8) She refused to disclose Blessing's account, to accuse Emeka in the session, and to conduct conjoint sessions — protecting the victim from retaliation, preserving the confidentiality that made the disclosure possible, and removing the setting in which the violence would have been negotiated.

9.13 Reflective task

Write two hundred and fifty words on this question: a woman tells you she is not ready to leave and asks you not to raise it again. What do you say, and what do you record? Then answer honestly which of the two — saying it or recording it — you would find harder, and why. The difficulty is usually about helplessness, and helplessness is the feeling that produces either control or withdrawal in a therapist. Neither is the work.

9.14 Further reading

  • Stark, E. on coercive control — the foundational account, and the source of the distinction this week rests on.
  • Campbell, J. and colleagues on the danger assessment — the empirical basis for the lethality factors.
  • The WHO clinical guidance on responding to intimate partner violence — practical and internationally adaptable.
  • Any current national or state guidance on child protection reporting and the Child Rights Act, read alongside the state law that actually applies where you practise.
  • A domestic violence advocacy service's own materials — read what the advocates give to women, because your client will read them too.

Next: Week 10 — The legal framework: courts, statutes and the therapist's place in them.

Week 10 — The legal framework a clinician must know

Learning outcomes. By the end of this week the student can (1) describe the four marriage systems operating in Nigeria and the dissolution route attached to each; (2) identify the five areas of law that bear on family therapy and state what the therapist may and may not do in each; (3) apply the welfare principle and explain why a treating therapist should not conduct a forensic custody evaluation of her own client; (4) route a legal question out of the session correctly, with a written referral question; and (5) explain why documentation is a legal instrument.

Orientation, not legal advice

This week teaches legal literacy, not law. Nothing here is advice, statutes vary between states and are amended, and the therapist who relies on a textbook rather than on a lawyer in a live case has made a category error. The purpose of the week is narrower and entirely practical: that the therapist can recognise a legal question when it appears in a session, knows which area of law it belongs to, knows what she may answer and what she must not, and knows who to send it to with a question that a lawyer can answer.

Marriage in Nigeria exists in four overlapping forms: statutory marriage under the Marriage Act, customary marriage, Islamic marriage, and cohabitation without a legal marriage. A family may be married under more than one system at the same time, and the therapist who assumes a single legal framework will mis-hear the case.

SystemWhat the therapist needs to knowWhat to refer
Statutory (Marriage Act)Monogamous; dissolution by petition to the High Court under the Matrimonial Causes Act, with the two-year rule and recognised groundsGrounds, timing, procedure, financial orders, custody
CustomaryVaries by locality and by community; dissolution through customary process and customary courts; frequently no written recordValidity, dissolution, property consequences, the status of children
IslamicGoverned by Sharia law and Sharia courts in the states that operate them; dissolution by pronouncement, khul' or judicial processValidity, divorce, maintenance (nafaqah), custody (hadanah)
CohabitationNo legal marriage; limited statutory protection; the parties may believe they are marriedProperty, paternity, maintenance, inheritance

Why the plurality matters clinically, not only legally. A woman may believe she is married under the statute and discover that her husband's family has dissolved a customary marriage she did not know was the operative one. A man may marry a second wife under Islamic law and be committing bigamy under the statute. A couple may have had three ceremonies and one legal marriage. The therapist's question is always the same: which marriage, under which law, and what has been filed?

10.1 The lecture in ninety minutes

MinutesContentTeaching form
0–8Why the therapist needs legal literacy and must not give legal adviceTwo cases: one where the therapist answered, one where she referred
8–25The four systems and the dissolution routesDiagram on the board; students place six families on it
25–40Five areas of law and the boundary in eachTable, with a "may/may not" sort
40–55The welfare principle; the custody report; the role conflictTwo reports, one by a treating therapist and one by an evaluator, compared
55–70Routing a legal question: the five questions and the referral notePairs write a referral question from a case vignette
70–82The Adewale caseCase, with students deciding what the therapist does
82–90Documentation as evidenceOne file note rewritten in legal-proof form

10.2 Five concepts that organise the week

Concept 1 — Legal literacy is a competence; legal advice is a different profession. The therapist must know enough to recognise, to route and to record. She does not opine on whether a marriage is valid, whether a divorce will be granted, whether custody will be awarded, or what a court will do. The distinction is not modesty; it is the boundary of the licence, and crossing it exposes the client to wrong advice and the therapist to liability.

Concept 2 — Legal pluralism means the family may be operating in two systems at once. Statutory, customary and Islamic frameworks coexist, and the same family may have obligations under more than one. The clinical consequence is that a client's account of "what the family has decided" may be legally real in one system and irrelevant in another. The therapist does not resolve this; she establishes which forum is operating and refers the question.

Concept 3 — The welfare principle governs children's cases, and it is not the same as the parents' wishes or the therapist's view of the parents. Every custody and maintenance decision is made by reference to the child's best interests. The therapist's contribution is evidence about the child, not advocacy for a parent. This is why the therapist's clinical opinion — "she is a good mother" — is not what the court needs; the court needs facts about the child's care, stability, schooling and relationships, and an assessment conducted for the purpose.

Concept 4 — Clinical and forensic roles are incompatible in the same case. A treating therapist cannot conduct a custody evaluation of her own client: the alliance compromises the neutrality the court requires, the client's disclosures are not gathered for the purpose, and the therapist's opinion is shaped by months of one-sided narrative. The correct answer to the request is a factual letter and a referral to an independent evaluator (Week 6, section 6.7).

Concept 5 — Records are legal documents from the moment they are written. They can be subpoenaed, read aloud in court, quoted in a complaint, and inspected by a regulator. The note that records reasoning, dates, consent and actions is the therapist's evidence that she acted properly; the note that records impressions and speculation is the other side's evidence that she did not.

10.2a The five areas of law, with the boundary in each

1. Marriage and dissolution. The therapist may: ask which system or systems the marriage was contracted under, whether any petition or customary process has been started, and what the client wants. The therapist may not: advise on grounds, validity, timing or the likely outcome. Refer: to a family lawyer, with the question written down.

2. Children — custody, access, maintenance, guardianship. The therapist may: provide factual information with consent, support the child, and help the parents reach agreements that are not legally binding. The therapist may not: evaluate her own client's parenting for a court, recommend an outcome, or write a letter of support. Refer: to an independent forensic evaluator, and to a lawyer for maintenance and custody procedure.

3. Violence — criminal and protective. The Violence Against Persons (Prohibition) Act 2015 applies in the Federal Capital Territory and in the states that have adopted it; state laws vary in the protections they provide. The therapist may: explain her own reporting duties, support a client through a police statement, and refer to legal aid. The therapist may not: advise whether to report a crime, mediate a criminal matter, or promise an outcome.

4. Mental health legislation. The Mental Health Act 2021 reformed the framework nationally, and its adoption and implementation vary by state. It matters clinically because it governs involuntary assessment and treatment, and because a family may attempt to use it against a member. The therapist may: explain the process in general terms and support a client through an assessment. The therapist may not: initiate an involuntary process except through the statutory route and with legal advice.

5. Records and data protection. The Nigeria Data Protection Act 2023 and the professional codes impose duties about storage, access, retention and disclosure. The therapist may: hold records, share them with consent, and disclose where compelled or where a duty requires. The therapist may not: disclose without consent or legal authority, keep records indefinitely without a policy, or store clinical material in a form that can be read by a family member.

10.2b When litigation is pending: the therapy contract

The most demanding configuration in this week's material: the family is in court and the therapy is running at the same time. Four additions to the ordinary contract, agreed at the start and recorded.

1. The therapy is not part of the case. "This work is not evidence. If a court asks for my records, I will tell you and I will do what the law requires, and I will not be writing a report for either side." Stated at the start, it prevents the client recruiting the therapist as an ally, which is the commonest failure.

2. The court timetable is not the clinical timetable. The therapist asks for the dates of hearings and filings and writes them in the file. Sessions immediately before a hearing are different sessions — the client is mobilised, the children are frightened, and the material is often the material of the courtroom rather than of the family.

3. Nothing is said about the other party that cannot be said in front of them. This is a constraint on the therapist, not on the client: the therapist will not collude in building a case, and will name the collusion when it is invited ("you are asking me to agree that he is a bad father, and I am not going to do that").

4. The children are not witnesses. The therapist will not ask a child to report on a parent, will not carry messages, and will not tell a child what is happening in court beyond what the parents have agreed to tell them. Where the parents cannot agree, the therapist helps them compose a single short explanation, because the child who is told two stories is being asked to choose.

10.3 The plural system in the consulting room

The five configurations the therapist will meet.

  1. One marriage, one system. The couple married under the statute and will divorce under it. The path is legible and the therapist's task is to keep the clinical work separate from the litigation timetable.
  2. One marriage, two systems. A statutory marriage plus a customary ceremony, which many families treat as the "real" marriage and the registry as the formality. The dissolution question then becomes which forum the family will use, and the answer may differ between the husband's family and the wife's.
  3. Two marriages, sequentially. A customary marriage followed by a statutory one, or the reverse. The legal position of the first wife may be entirely different from what she believes.
  4. Two marriages, concurrently. An Islamic second marriage alongside a statutory first marriage, which may constitute bigamy under the statute — a criminal question, not a therapeutic one.
  5. No marriage. Cohabitation, frequently described by the family as marriage, with children. Property, maintenance and inheritance questions arise, and the client may have no legal status at all.

What the therapist does with the configuration. Four questions, asked without legal opinion. Under which law was this marriage contracted, and is there a certificate? Has anything been filed or pronounced — a petition, a customary dissolution, a pronouncement? Who in the family has been involved in that process, and does the client agree with what they have done? What does the client want — the marriage, the divorce, the children, the property, or clarity?

The clinical significance of the fourth question. Clients frequently do not know what they want from the legal process, because the legal process has been started by somebody else — a husband's family, a brother, an employer. The therapist's work is to help the client separate the question "what is happening to me" from "what do I want", because a client who has not answered the second will be represented by whoever answers it for her.

The woman with no forum. In some of these cases the client has no effective access to any court: no money, no lawyer, no standing in the customary process, and a family that has already decided. The therapist's role then is practical and clinical rather than legal: identify the legal aid providers that exist, connect her with a women's organisation that accompanies clients, and support the psychological work of living with an outcome she did not choose. Doing this well is not a lesser form of help than referring to a lawyer.

10.3a The client who has been to three forums

A configuration that appears often enough to be its own subsection. The client has been to a customary court, to a pastor, and to a police station, and has three different answers. She arrives believing that "nobody will help", which is frequently accurate in the sense that no single forum has authority over the whole of her problem.

What the therapist does first. Builds the chronology, because the client's account will be organised by emotion rather than by date. A single page, with dates, forum, decision and who was present, converts a story of chaos into a set of events. The page is the client's, written with the therapist, and it is the most useful document the case will produce.

What the therapist does second. Establishes which forum is authoritative for which question. A customary court may have decided a matter of family status; a police report may have been closed; a pastor may have pronounced a reconciliation that has no legal effect. The therapist does not decide; she writes the question for each.

What the therapist does third. Names the pattern to the client. "You have been to three places and each of them answered a different question. That is why it feels as though nobody has answered you." Naming it is often the first moment at which the client feels understood, and it is the foundation for the referral that follows.

What not to do. Do not tell the client that her customary process "does not count". It counted, to the people who conducted it, and to her; the legal question about its effect is separate, and dismissing the process confirms that the therapist does not understand her world.

10.4 The welfare principle and the custody report

The principle. In any decision concerning a child, the child's best interests are the paramount consideration. The therapist does not need to know the statutory wording; she needs to know that the child's interests are not the same as either parent's wishes, and that a court will weigh stability, care, education, health, the child's own views where age permits, and the child's relationships.

What a court typically asks for, and what a therapist can provide.

The court wantsThe treating therapist can supplyThe treating therapist cannot supply
Facts about the child's careAttendance, presenting problems, the child's own statements, with consentAn assessment of the other parent
The child's viewsWhat the child has said, in context, if the child consents or the law requiresA recommendation about residence
Stability and functioningObservations from sessionsA prediction of future parenting
Expert opinionNothingAn expert opinion — she is not the expert, and she is not independent

The factual letter, which is usually the right answer. On letterhead, with the client's written consent, it states: the dates of contact, the fact of attendance, the presenting problem in the client's words, and — only if the therapist judges it appropriate and the client agrees — a short statement of the clinical picture. It does not contain an opinion about parenting, an assessment of the other party, or a recommendation. It is dated, signed and sent to the court or the lawyer, and a copy goes in the file.

The request to appear. Where the therapist is subpoenaed she must attend and answer. Where she is asked to appear as an expert she must decline, and explain why: an expert opinion requires an assessment conducted for the court, independence from the parties, and a methodology that can be scrutinised. A treating therapist who offers an expert opinion in her own client's case is not being helpful; she is being replaced as a witness by an advocate.

The child's own voice. A child of twelve may express a preference, and the expression is evidence, not a decision. The therapist's task is to ensure the child is not placed in the position of choosing between parents, which is a burden that produces loyalty conflicts and lasting damage. The formulation that helps: "you do not have to choose. The adults will decide, and what you can tell me is what you want them to know about how it is for you."

The report that must never be written by a treating therapist. "In my opinion, the mother is the more capable parent and the children should reside with her." It is a forensic opinion, it is outside the therapist's role, it will be attacked in cross-examination, and it converts months of clinical work into a piece of advocacy that the other side can dismantle by showing the therapist met the father four times.

10.4a Routing a legal question: the five questions and the referral note

The moment it happens. A client says, "can he take the children from me?" or "if he has another wife, is our marriage not void?" or "my brother says I can divorce him for free in the customary court, is that true?" The therapist has two seconds in which her face will answer, and the answer must be a route rather than an opinion.

The five questions, in the session.

  1. "Has anything been filed?" A petition, a customary process, a police report, a maintenance application. The answer changes the urgency from "sometime" to "within days".
  2. "Do you have a lawyer?" If yes, the therapist's job is to help the client formulate the question for the lawyer. If no, the therapist's job is the referral, with the specific route.
  3. "What are you being asked to decide, and by when?" A court date, a family meeting, a deadline for a response. Dates are the therapist's business even where the law is not.
  4. "Who else is a party?" A husband's family, a co-wife, a child's guardian, an employer, an immigration authority. Multi-party cases need multi-party advice.
  5. "What do you want me to do?" The client may want an opinion, a letter, a witness, or a person to sit with her. Each is a different request, and only some of them are ones the therapist can meet.

The referral note, which is the deliverable of the whole week. Six lines, written in the session, handed to the client, copied to the file.

Re: [client], [date]. Thank you for seeing [client], who is considering/ facing [divorce petition / custody application / customary dissolution / maintenance claim]. She would like advice on: (1) the validity and effect of the [customary] dissolution pronounced on [date] in respect of a [statutory] marriage; (2) her position on residence of and access to the two children, aged 12 and 7; (3) whether maintenance can be sought and from which court. She is not able to fund private representation and has asked about legal aid. I am her therapist and am not able to advise on any of these matters. Yours, [name], [qualification], [contact].

Why the three numbered questions matter more than the letter's tone. A lawyer receiving "please advise on custody" will write a general letter that costs money and answers nothing. A lawyer receiving three numbered questions can answer them in fifteen minutes and will.

What goes in the file. The referral note, the date, the client's consent to the referral being made, and the question she asked in her own words. Nothing about the therapist's clinical view of the husband.

10.5 Building the referral network before you need it

Therapists build clinical networks and neglect legal ones, then improvise at the point of crisis. Five contacts, established in advance, each with a name and a number verified in the last year.

1. A family lawyer willing to take occasional pro bono or reduced-fee referrals, and willing to receive the six-line note of 10.4a rather than a telephone call in a panic.

2. A legal aid provider — the Legal Aid Council, a state scheme, a university law clinic, or a women's organisation with a legal desk. Legal aid is frequently available and almost never known about by the client.

3. The police family support unit at the nearest station, with the name of the officer in charge. This is the contact that determines whether a report is taken properly at eleven at night.

4. The state ministry responsible for social welfare, or the child protection unit, with the out-of-hours number if one exists (Week 9).

5. A mediator or a customary process you can respect. Not every case needs a court, and some families will use a mediation process that is cheaper, faster and more legitimate to them than litigation. The therapist should know which local processes are conducted fairly and which are instruments of the husband's family.

The maintenance of the network. One telephone call a year to each contact, and one case referred. A network that is only used is not a network; it is a list of strangers with whom the therapist is unfamiliar at the moment of greatest need.

10.6 Case: the Adewales, and the marriage that was also another marriage

The referral. Bola Adewale, 38, a schoolteacher, attends alone "for stress". She and Yinka, 42, a civil servant, have been married twelve years, with two children, 12 and 7. In session three she says, in passing, "his people have done what they wanted to do. They say we are no longer married."

What had happened. Bola and Yinka married in a church with a marriage certificate — a statutory marriage under the Marriage Act. In the eleventh year, following a dispute about money and Yinka's relationship with another woman, Yinka's family convened and pronounced a customary dissolution. Yinka moved out. He has continued to pay the school fees and has visited the children twice a month. He has told Bola that the marriage is over and that she should "go back to her family". Her brother has told her the same. She has no lawyer, has not been to any court, and believes that what the family pronounced is final.

The legal question, which is not the therapist's to answer. Whether a customary dissolution can dissolve a statutory marriage, and what Bola's position is in respect of the marriage, the children, maintenance and property. The therapist knows enough to know that this is a real question with a real answer, and does not know the answer.

What the therapist did.

  1. She asked the five questions. Nothing filed; no lawyer; the deadline perceived by Bola was her family's pressure to move out "before the school term"; parties included Yinka's family and hers; and what she wanted was "to know whether I am still married".
  2. She wrote the six-line referral note with three numbered questions, and telephoned a legal aid organisation she had a contact at, with Bola's consent, on the same day.
  3. She separated the three problems that Bola had fused: the legal status of the marriage; the practical question of money and children; and the emotional question of whether her husband had ever intended to remain. Only the third was the therapist's, and it had been invisible because the first two were consuming her.
  4. She did not tell her to fight or to accept. She said what she had said in other cases: "there is a legal question and I cannot answer it; there is a decision about what you want, and that is yours; and there is the grief, which is mine to work on with you."
  5. She recorded all of it, including that she had made no legal assessment.

What happened. A lawyer at the legal aid organisation advised that a customary dissolution does not dissolve a statutory marriage, and that Bola remained married under the statute; a petition would be required. Bola did not immediately petition. She spent four months in treatment deciding whether she wanted to, and in the fifth month she filed. The therapist supported her through the process and did no legal work at all.

The clinical work, which is the part students underestimate. Bola's presenting "stress" was the experience of having been told by two families what her life was. She had not been asked what she wanted since she was twenty-six. The therapy's central question — "what do you want?" — was answered for the first time in session nine, in these words: "I want to be a married woman or a divorced woman. I do not want to be this."

What the case teaches.

  1. Legal pluralism produces a particular kind of helplessness, and the helplessness is the clinical problem. The client who does not know whether she is married cannot plan, cannot grieve and cannot decide.
  2. The therapist's ignorance of the law is not a barrier when it is honest. "I do not know, and here is how we will find out" is a complete clinical intervention.
  3. The family's pronouncement is not the law. In this case the family's process was real to the family and had no effect on the statutory marriage. The therapist's refusal to accept the family's account as the legal fact changed the case, and she reached that refusal not by knowing the law but by knowing that a question existed.
  4. The three problems had to be separated before any of them could be worked. This is the single most useful move in the whole week, and it generalises to almost every case in which a family presents with a legal crisis.

10.6a The factual letter, written out

The letter that is almost always the right answer to "will you write something for my case". On letterhead, with the client's written consent, five short paragraphs.

Re: [client name], [date of birth]; consent to disclosure dated [date] I have seen [client] on [n] occasions between [date] and [date], at her request, for [presenting problem in the client's words]. She has attended [n] of [n] scheduled appointments. The clinical picture is of [one or two sentences: anxiety following separation; grief; difficulty sleeping]. I have not assessed her parenting capacity and I make no recommendation about residence or contact. [Client] has told me that [only if relevant and consented: she wishes to see her children; she is attending a support group]. I make no finding as to the truth of what she has told me. If the court requires information about the children's welfare, I would suggest an independent assessment; I am not able to provide one, as I am [client]'s treating therapist and my view would not be independent. Yours faithfully, [name], [qualification], [registration number], [contact].

Why the third paragraph is written that way. "I make no finding as to the truth" is the sentence that keeps a clinical letter from becoming a witness statement. Clients often ask for it to be removed, because it weakens the letter; the therapist explains that its presence is what makes the rest of the letter admissible as an account rather than challengeable as advocacy.

The refusal to add the sentence the client wants. "In my opinion she is a fit mother." The refusal is delivered with the alternative: "I can say what I have observed, and I cannot say that. If you need an opinion, you need someone the court will treat as independent, and here is how to ask for one."

10.7 Transcript: "can he take the children?"

Session five, minutes eighteen to thirty-one. Bola has just been told that she remains married under the statute.

D1 Bola: So can he take the children? His mother says they are his children because they bear his name. D2 Therapist: (pause) That is a legal question and I am not going to guess at it. (pause) I can tell you what I know and what I do not know. — The refusal, followed immediately by the structure of the answer. "I am not going to guess" is stronger than "I am not qualified", because it names what the therapist will not do rather than what she lacks. D3 Bola: But you must know. D4 Therapist: I know that there is a law about this, and that it is not the law his mother is describing. (pause) I do not know what a court would decide, and neither does she. — The two facts the therapist can honestly give: a law exists, and the family's version is not it. The second half is the clinically important one, because it interrupts the family's certainty without substituting the therapist's. D5 Bola: So I need a lawyer. D6 Therapist: You need a lawyer, and you needed one four months ago, and that is not your fault. (pause) I have a name, and I will telephone today if you want me to. — The acknowledgement that avoids blame, and the concrete offer. The client who is told "you should have got a lawyer" hears an accusation, and the accusation is almost always unjust. D7 Bola: I do not have money for a lawyer. D8 Therapist: There is legal aid, and I have checked that this organisation takes cases like yours. (pause) I am going to write the questions down for you, so that you are not paying for the first hour while he works out what you are asking. — The specific, prepared answer. Vague reassurance about legal aid is what most clients receive; a checked contact and three written questions is a service. D9 Bola: (long pause) If I am still married, then what has he been doing for a year? D10 Therapist: (pause) That is the question underneath the legal one, and it is the one we can work on. — The pivot to the clinical material, made explicit so that the client knows the therapist has heard it and has not dropped it. D11 Bola: He has been living as if I do not exist. And his family have been telling everyone in the compound that I am the one who refused. D12 Therapist: So you have been divorced in your street and married in the law. — The formulation that the rest of the case is built on, offered in one sentence and in the client's own terms. D13 Bola: (crying) Yes. (pause) That is exactly it. D14 Therapist: Then let us be clear about the three things we are dealing with: what the law says, which is your lawyer's; what you want, which is yours; and what it has been like to live in that gap, which is ours. — The separation of the three problems, stated aloud so that the client has a map she can use outside the room.

What the transcript shows. Seven turns, and in none of them does the therapist answer the legal question. In four of them she gives the client something usable: the existence of a law, the name of an organisation, three written questions, and a formulation of her situation that she recognises. The refusal is not the intervention; the substitution is.

10.8 Laboratory: legal triage and the written question

Part A — triage (twenty minutes, individual). Eight vignettes, each with a legal question embedded in clinical material. The student identifies: the area of law; whether the question is urgent (within seven days) or not; what the therapist may say; what the therapist must not say; and the referral destination.

VignetteEmbedded question
A wife says her husband's family "divorced" her by customValidity; effect on statutory marriage
A father asks whether he must keep paying school fees after separationMaintenance obligation
A mother says her husband has taken the children to his villageCustody; possible abduction
A client asks whether she can refuse her husband a second wifeNothing legal; a marital and religious question
A couple ask whether their church marriage is "real"Validity; registration
A client's employer demands a report about herData protection; consent
A client asks if he can be forced into psychiatric treatmentMental health legislation
A client asks who will get the houseProperty; matrimonial causes; customary law

Part B — the six-line note (twenty-five minutes, pairs). Each pair writes the referral note for one vignette, with three numbered questions, and reads it aloud. The group then marks it against four criteria: the questions are answerable; the note contains no legal opinion; it contains no clinical opinion that is not necessary; and it identifies the client's practical constraint (money, distance, language).

Marks (20).

Criterion0–12–34–5
IdentificationWrong area of lawRight area, no urgencyRight area, urgency and forum identified
BoundaryGave an opinionHedged opinionRefused clearly and gave something usable instead
The noteA name onlyA referralThree answerable questions, practical constraints named
RecordNoneDescriptionQuestion, consent, date, referral made, advice recorded

The comment to make in the debrief. The best notes in the room will be the shortest. Legal questions are answered by being narrowed.

10.9 Six errors

Error 1 — The reassuring opinion. "He cannot take the children; you are their mother." It is said kindly, it is frequently wrong, and the client will act on it. The error is not the warmth; it is the opinion.

Error 2 — Refusing to engage at all. "I am not a lawyer" said and nothing else offered leaves the client with the same problem and one fewer ally. The refusal must be followed by the route.

Error 3 — Treating the family's pronouncement as the legal fact. Customary processes are real to families and may have no legal effect. The therapist who accepts the family's version confirms the client's helplessness.

Error 4 — The letter of support. "I have known Mrs Adewale for a year and she is a devoted mother." It is not evidence, it will be cross-examined, and it has converted the therapist into a witness for one side.

Error 5 — The forensic evaluation conducted by the treating therapist. The role conflict described in 10.4, made worse by the therapist's belief that she knows the family better than an outside evaluator would.

Error 6 — The clinical note written for the file and read in court. Sarcasm, speculation, unverified allegation recorded as fact, and abbreviations that cannot be explained. The note is written for a reader who will be hostile. Write it that way.

10.9b The four questions a therapist asks her own lawyer

Therapists hesitate to consult a lawyer about their own practice because they assume it is expensive and that the question must be serious. In fact four questions account for most of the need, and each can be answered in a short consultation.

1. "May I disclose this, and to whom?" The records question. The answer depends on the statute, the consent and the order, and it changes with the jurisdiction.

2. "What must I do now that I have been served?" The subpoena question. The answer is almost always: verify, tell the client, obtain advice, produce only what is required, and keep a copy of everything produced.

3. "Am I obliged to report this?" The child protection and risk question, where the threshold is unclear.

4. "If I proceed as I intend, what is my exposure?" The advance question, asked before a decision rather than after it, and the one that most reduces the risk of a complaint.

The practice's arrangement. A retainer or a professional indemnity scheme that includes legal advice, and a named contact. The cost of the annual arrangement is a fraction of the cost of a single defended complaint, and the availability of advice changes the therapist's decisions in the direction of more honesty rather than less.

10.10 Culture, faith and the law the family actually uses

Customary process is not a lesser law; it is a different forum with different rules and real authority. In much of this region, a family's decision about a marriage is made in a room with elders, is binding in the eyes of everyone present, and is never written down. A therapist who treats this as "not real" will lose the family's confidence; a therapist who treats it as legally decisive will misinform the client. The correct position holds both: "what your family decided matters to them and to you. Whether it changes your legal position is a different question, and we will ask someone who knows."

The two forums and the confusion between them. Clients frequently assume that a customary dissolution ends a statutory marriage, that a church ceremony creates a legal marriage, that an Islamic marriage gives a second wife the same standing as the first, and that a father's family owns the children. Each assumption is common, each carries consequences, and none of them can be corrected by the therapist's opinion. It is corrected by a lawyer's letter and by the client's decision to act on it.

Religion, obedience and the right to leave. A client may believe that her faith forbids divorce and that her suffering is a cross. The therapist does not argue with the belief. She does three things: she finds out what the client's own religious authority teaches about cruelty and about release, which is frequently more permissive than the client assumes; she separates the religious question from the legal one, since a religious prohibition is not a legal incapacity; and she asks the question that opens the case: "if your faith permitted it, what would you want?"

The family's use of the law as a threat. "My husband says he will take the children and I will never see them." Sometimes the threat is knowledge of the law; more often it is a threat with no legal basis, issued to produce compliance. The therapist cannot assess which it is. She can do two things: name the threat as a threat, which reduces its power, and route the client to advice that will tell her the truth.

The honour dimension of a public case. Where the family's standing depends on the marriage being intact, a court case is a public declaration that it is not. Clients may therefore prefer a private, customary or mediated route even where a court would give them more. That preference is rational and the therapist should respect it, while making sure the client knows what she is giving up.

The male client and the law's reputational cost. A father who is seen to be fighting his wife in court may lose standing at work and in his family. The therapist working with such a father should not read his reluctance to litigate as indifference to his children.

DEBATE — "A family therapist should always advise a client to obtain legal advice before making any decision about the marriage." Argue for: information is a precondition of consent, the stakes are irreversible, and the therapist's silence can be read as endorsement of the family's version. Argue against: the client may not want legal advice, the referral may itself escalate the conflict, and the therapist's role is to support the client's own decision rather than to steer it. Then state the three cases in which you would insist.

10.11 Evidence note

What is known. Legal pluralism is a structural feature of family life in Nigeria and in much of sub-Saharan Africa, and the literature on family law in plural systems documents precisely the confusions described in this week: conflicting adjudications, women's differential access to forums, and the gap between customary practice and statutory protection. Research on help-seeking in domestic violence consistently finds that legal literacy is one of the strongest predictors of a woman taking protective action, and that the absence of accessible advice is a principal barrier. Studies of client experiences of court-involved therapy find that role confusion — treating clinician versus evaluator — is a common source of complaint and of harm.

What is weaker. There is little empirical work on the specific question of how therapists should handle legal plurality in the consulting room, and almost none on outcomes. The guidance in this week is therefore derived from professional ethics, from the structure of the law, and from practice rather than from trial evidence.

What is not supported. That a therapist's clinical opinion about parenting has predictive validity for custody outcomes. That legal advice from a non-lawyer is harmless if it is well-intentioned. And that referral to a lawyer inevitably escalates a family's conflict — in many cases it reduces it, because each party stops guessing.

The matrix cell. Development is probabilistic and non-linear; the same input produces different outcomes depending on timing and context. Legal advice given in week two, before positions have hardened, and in week twenty, after a customary dissolution has been pronounced and acted upon, produce two different families.

10.11a The six documents a family therapist should hold

Legal literacy is partly a matter of paperwork, and the paperwork is cheap.

  1. A consent form that names the limits of confidentiality, the duty to report, the possibility of records being compelled, and the identity of the client system — reviewed and re-signed when the system changes (Week 2).
  2. A privacy and records policy stating what is kept, where, for how long, who may see it, and how a request for access is handled.
  3. A disclosure protocol for requests from lawyers, courts, employers, insurers and the police: who decides, what is checked, what is recorded.
  4. A subpoena procedure, one page: verify the document, tell the client, contact the supervisor or indemnifier, seek legal advice, produce only what is required.
  5. A complaints procedure telling the client how to complain about the therapist, because a client who has a route to complain is a client less likely to litigate.
  6. A list of verified contacts — lawyer, legal aid, police family unit, social welfare, crisis line — with the date each was last checked.

The file that survives scrutiny is not the file with the most detail. It is the file in which every disclosure has a consent behind it, every referral has a question attached, and every decision has a date.

10.12 Key terms

Legal pluralism — the coexistence of statutory, customary and religious legal systems within one jurisdiction.

Matrimonial Causes Act — the statute governing statutory divorce in Nigeria, including the two-year rule and the recognised grounds.

Welfare principle — the rule that the child's best interests are the paramount consideration in decisions concerning children.

Forensic role — the role of an independent expert providing an opinion to a court.

VAPP Act 2015 — the Violence Against Persons (Prohibition) Act, applicable in the FCT and in adopting states.

Mental Health Act 2021 — the national framework for mental health care, with state-level variation in implementation.

Legal aid — publicly funded legal advice and representation; availability varies and is frequently unknown to clients.

10.13 Self-test

  1. Name the four marriage systems and the dissolution route for each.
  2. Give the boundary, for the therapist, in each of the five areas of law.
  3. What is the welfare principle, and what does it require of a therapist asked for a report?
  4. Why should a treating therapist not conduct a forensic custody evaluation of her own client?
  5. State the five questions that route a legal question.
  6. What is the six-line note, and why three numbered questions?
  7. In the Adewale case, what were the three problems, and which was the therapist's?
  8. Why is the family's pronouncement not the law, and why does that matter clinically?

Answers. (1) Statutory — petition to the High Court under the Matrimonial Causes Act; customary — customary process and customary courts, locality-specific; Islamic — pronouncement, khul' or Sharia court; cohabitation — no marriage, so limited statutory protection. (2) Marriage — may ask which system and what has been filed, may not advise on validity or grounds; children — may give factual information with consent, may not evaluate or recommend; violence — may explain her own duties and refer, may not advise on reporting or mediate a crime; mental health — may explain and support, may not initiate involuntary processes outside the statute; records — may hold and share with consent, may not disclose without authority or keep records indefinitely without policy. (3) The child's best interests are paramount; it requires the therapist to give facts about the child and to refuse an opinion about parenting or residence. (4) Because the alliance and the one-sided narrative compromise independence, the material was not gathered for the purpose, and the opinion will be dismantled in cross-examination. (5) Has anything been filed; do you have a lawyer; what must be decided and by when; who else is a party; what do you want me to do. (6) A short referral letter with three numbered, answerable questions; because a general request produces general advice that costs the client money and answers nothing. (7) The legal status of the marriage; the practical questions of money and children; and the emotional question of whether her husband had ever intended to stay — only the third was the therapist's. (8) Because a customary process may have no legal effect on a statutory marriage, and clinically because the client's belief that her life has been decided by others is the presenting problem; correcting it restores her capacity to decide.

10.9a The therapist in the witness box

Most family therapists will give evidence at some point, usually in a custody or protection matter, and the experience is unlike anything else in the work.

The four rules. Answer the question asked. Not the question behind it, not the one you would prefer, and not the narrative you have prepared. Say "I do not know" when you do not know, which is more credible than an improvisation and is frequently the honest answer. Do not defend your clinical decisions beyond the point of explanation. A therapist who argues with counsel is winning an argument and losing the case. Take your notes, and refer to them, because a witness who says "I would need to check my notes" and then reads them accurately is more reliable than one who reconstructs from memory.

The three questions that will be asked. "You only ever saw one side of this family, did you not?" "You are not a psychiatrist, are you?" "You formed the view that my client was the problem because that is what you were told." Each is answerable honestly and none requires a defence. The honest answers — "yes, I saw both parents but mostly the mother", "no, and I have not offered a psychiatric opinion", "I formed my view from what I observed and I recorded what I was told" — are stronger than any attempt to sound more authoritative than the therapist is.

The preparation. Read the file in full; write a chronology; identify the limits of your knowledge; discuss with your supervisor what you will say about the decisions you made; and remember that the court is not asking whether you are a good therapist. It is asking what you know.

10.14 Reflective task

Take one of your own family cases from placement, or the Adewale case, and write three hundred words separating the clinical question from the legal one. Then answer: which of the two did you spend more time thinking about, and why?
