Most trainees find that the legal question is more anxiety-provoking because it has a right answer and they do not know it — which is exactly the anxiety that produces either an opinion or a refusal, and neither is the work.

10.15 Further reading

  • The Marriage Act, the Matrimonial Causes Act and the Child Rights Act, read in the original and not in summary — they are shorter than students expect.
  • The Violence Against Persons (Prohibition) Act 2015, with attention to the states that have adopted it.
  • The Mental Health Act 2021 and the state-level implementation guidance where you practise.
  • Any current text on family law in Nigeria, for the reference section of the practice manual.
  • The Nigeria Data Protection Act 2023 and your professional body's record-keeping guidance.

Next: Week 11 — Records, subpoenas and the court.

Week 11 — Records, subpoenas and court

Learning outcomes. By the end of this week the student can (1) write a record that serves three readers — her future self, a colleague taking over, and a court; (2) distinguish fact, observation, clinical opinion and hypothesis in a note; (3) handle an access request from a client, a partner, an employer or a court; (4) respond to a subpoena in the correct order and with the correct advice; and (5) give evidence as a witness of fact without becoming an advocate.

The record is two things at once

It is the clinical memory of the treatment, and it is a legal document that may be read by people who have no interest in the therapist's good intentions. The two functions are compatible, and the note that serves the first serves the second: a note that records what was observed, decided and done is both the best clinical note and the best evidence. Notes fail when a therapist writes for one reader only — the impressionistic note written for herself, or the defensive note written for a regulator — and the failure shows in both places.

Write for three readers.

ReaderWhat they need
The therapist in six monthsWhat was the problem, what was tried, what happened, what is planned
A colleague taking over tonightThe risk, the current plan, who knows, what to do if telephoned
A courtFacts with dates, sources, decisions with reasons, consent, and no speculation

The four registers, which must never be mixed in a single sentence.

  1. Fact. "Client attended on 4 March. Her left forearm was bandaged."
  2. Observation. "She was tearful at the start of the session and calm by the end. She checked her phone four times."
  3. Clinical opinion. "In my view the presentation is consistent with an acute stress response following discovery of the affair."
  4. Hypothesis. "Possible that the husband's drinking is a factor; not yet explored."

A note that says "client is a manipulative woman who is clearly exaggerating her husband's behaviour" contains no register at all: it is a character judgement, it is unsupported, and it will be read aloud in a witness box.

11.1 The lecture in ninety minutes

MinutesContentTeaching form
0–8The three readers; two notes on the same session, comparedRead both, vote on which is defensible
8–22Five concepts: memory, hostile reader, four registers, minimum necessary, privilegeLecture
22–40Note structure, with a worked example written liveTherapist writes a note on the board from a vignette
40–52The multiperson record: whose file, whose secret, where it goesThree configurations, students place the material
52–65Access requests and the subpoena sequenceSimulation: a letter arrives, what do you do in the next hour
65–80Giving evidence: the four rules, the three questionsMock cross-examination with a colleague as counsel
80–90The Eze subpoenaCase, students decide what is produced and what is withheld

11.2 Five concepts that organise the week

Concept 1 — The record is the treatment's memory, and memory is a clinical instrument. Continuity of care depends on the next clinician being able to reconstruct what happened. A file of one-line entries ("seen; discussed anxiety; better") is not a record of a treatment; it is a record of attendance, and it cannot support a handover, a supervision, or a defence.

Concept 2 — Assume a hostile reader. Not because the reader is hostile, but because the only readers who matter in a dispute are the ones looking for inconsistency. Write as though the sentence will be read aloud by someone who wants it to mean something else, because one day it will be.

Concept 3 — The four registers must be labelled. Facts stated plainly, observations stated as observations, opinions introduced as opinions, hypotheses marked as hypotheses. The failure to distinguish them is the single commonest reason a clinical record damages the clinician who wrote it.

Concept 4 — Minimum necessary is the rule for every disclosure. To a court, to a school, to a physician, to a partner: disclose what is required for the purpose and nothing else, and record what was disclosed and why. A file sent in full to a school because the school asked for "the records" is a breach, whatever the school's intention.

Concept 5 — Privilege and confidentiality are different, and privilege is not the therapist's. Confidentiality is the professional duty not to disclose. Privilege is the client's legal protection against disclosure in proceedings. In most jurisdictions privilege is qualified: it can be waived by the client, overridden by statute, or displaced where the court orders production. The therapist cannot promise privilege, which is why the consent conversation must mention that records can be compelled.

11.2a The note, written out

The structure for a family session. Six headings, five to eight minutes of writing, and the note is done.

Attendees: Mrs Eze, Mr Eze (first 20 minutes only), therapist. Children not present. Presenting this session: Mr Eze raised the school's report of Chidi's absences; Mrs Eze stated she had not been told of the meeting. Risk screen: No disclosures of harm this session. Direct enquiry about safety at home — client states no violence in the last month; no suicidal ideation on direct enquiry. Interventions: Reviewed the previous week's agreement about school communication; both parents agreed a written message each Friday; therapist modelled the message and both rehearsed it. Response: Mr Eze left the session at minute 20 saying he had to return to work. Mrs Eze remained and stated that she had stopped telling him about school matters "because it always ends in a quarrel". Plan: Weekly sessions continue. Mrs Eze to attend alone next week, as agreed in the room. School's safeguarding lead to be told only that the family is attending; consent for that limited contact recorded.

The same session, badly written. "Good session. Couple engaged. Father defensive and clearly controlling; mother tearful and possibly depressed. Will continue." Four judgements, no facts, no plan, no consent, and each judgement is an assertion the therapist cannot support from the session.

The six things every note should contain. Date and attendees; the reason for the session; the risk screen, with the questions asked; what the therapist did; what the client(s) did in response; and the plan, with who was told what.

The eight things that must never appear.

  1. Character judgements — "manipulative", "attention-seeking", "a difficult woman".
  2. Speculation about third parties — "I suspect the grandmother is behind this".
  3. Unverified allegation recorded as fact — "mother is an alcoholic" when the source is the husband.
  4. Other clients' information.
  5. Secrets — an individual disclosure recorded in a file to which the other partner may have access.
  6. Jokes, irony, nicknames, and anything the therapist would not want read aloud.
  7. Abbreviations that cannot be expanded by a reader who is not the author.
  8. Opinions about the law or the likely outcome of a case.

The note written late. It happens, and the honest entry is "note written on [date] from memory of session on [date]". An undated, unmarked reconstruction presented as contemporaneous is the error, not the lateness.

11.2b The multiperson record: whose file is it?

The question that generates more complaints than any other in couple and family work.

The three configurations.

ConfigurationThe fileThe individual disclosures
Individual client, family as contextThe client's fileIn the file; the family has no access
Couple as client, one fileOne file, both namedIndividual disclosures recorded in a restricted section, accessible only to the therapist and supervisor
Family as client, children includedOne file with the children named as clientsChildren's disclosures recorded separately, with a note of who may see them

The restricted section: how it works in practice. A separate, clearly marked section of the record — in an electronic system, a separate note type with restricted access — containing material given in individual sessions that the other party has not been told. The therapist's clinical record is complete; the disclosure risk is contained. Where the practice has no electronic system, the equivalent is a sealed envelope referenced in the main file, with the practice policy stating what it contains and who may open it.

Why this matters more than it appears. In a couple case the partners may each have a right of access to the data held about them under data protection law, and the other partner's disclosures are data about them in part. The therapist who has recorded the wife's account of the husband in the joint file, in a form that identifies him, will find herself in the position of having to disclose it or to justify withholding it. The restricted section and the practice's policy are what make that possible.

What the note should say when restricting. "Individual session [date]: material recorded in restricted section per practice policy [reference]; client informed that this material will not be shared with [partner]; no information affecting safety withheld from the file." Three clauses, and they answer the question a regulator will ask.

11.2c The electronic record

Most practices now keep records electronically, and the electronic record creates five problems that the paper record did not.

1. The audit trail remembers everything. Every version, every deletion, every time a note was opened and by whom. A therapist who "tidied" a note six months after a session will be asked why, and "for clarity" is not an answer that survives the question.

2. The shared device. A practice laptop used at home, a phone with the notes app, an email with clinical content in it — each is a disclosure waiting to happen. The policy is simple: clinical records are kept on the clinical system, nothing clinical is in personal email, and no clinical material is on a personal phone.

3. The copy that leaves the building. A client who is sent a summary by email has a copy that the therapist cannot retrieve. Where a summary is sent, it is written for that purpose, marked as such, and recorded.

4. The template that writes the note. Templates improve completeness and produce homogeneity: every note says "client engaged well" because that is the first option in the dropdown. The discipline is to use the template's headings and the therapist's own sentences.

5. The access request that is a download. Where a client requests their data, the practice must be able to produce a coherent record rather than a raw export that includes other clients' names. The restricted section of 11.2b is, in an electronic system, an access permission — and the permission must be tested before it is needed.

11.3 Access requests: four kinds, four answers

1. The client's own request. Under data protection law the client is generally entitled to a copy of the data held about them, subject to exceptions. The therapist's task: verify identity, establish whose data is involved, redact third-party information, and respond within the statutory period. Where the therapist believes access would cause serious harm, the statutory exception may apply and legal advice should be taken before refusing.

2. The partner's request in a couple case. The partner is also a client in a jointly-held file, which is why the restricted section exists. The therapist discloses what is about that partner and does not disclose the other partner's individual material. The reasoning is recorded.

3. The employer's, insurer's or school's request. Usually limited to attendance, or to a report with written consent. The therapist discloses the minimum and records it. An employer who asks for "the notes" receives attendance only, and is told why.

4. The court's or the police's demand. Weeks 10 and 11's core business, and the subject of 11.4.

The letter that answers an access request. Five lines: what has been received, what will be provided, what will be withheld and on what basis, when it will be sent, and who to contact. The therapist does not argue in the letter; she states and records.

The request to "just tell me how she is doing". The commonest of all, made by a relative at a wedding. The answer is one sentence, delivered warmly: "I am not able to talk about that, and I know it is because you are worried about her."

11.3b The client who asks to see her file

The request arrives in three forms, and each is answered differently.

"What have you written about me?" The most straightforward, and the one that is frequently met with evasion. The answer: "I will show you." Reading the record together — the notes, the plan, the supervision entries — takes twenty minutes and does three things: it demonstrates that the therapist has been paying attention, it allows the client to correct factual errors, and it removes the sense that something is being written behind her back.

"I want a copy." A data protection request, answered within the statutory period, with third-party information redacted and a short covering letter. The therapist does not require a reason, does not negotiate, and does not express hurt. The one legitimate caution is where access would cause serious harm, which is a legal exception to be taken on advice and not a feeling to be acted on.

"My husband wants to see what you wrote about him." If he is a client in the same file, he may be entitled to what is about him. What is about his wife is not his, and the restricted section exists for that reason. The therapist replies in writing, stating what will be provided and what will not, with the basis.

The clinical value that is usually missed. Reading the file with a client is one of the few interventions that reliably produces a correction of the therapist's misunderstanding. Clients routinely say, at line three, "that is not what I meant" — and the correction is worth more than the twenty minutes.

11.4 The subpoena: what it is and what to do in the first hour

What it is. A court order requiring the production of documents or the attendance of a witness. It is not a request, it is not optional, and ignoring it is contempt. It is also not, in itself, an order that the therapist disclose everything in the file: it defines what is required, and the therapist's obligation is to comply with what it requires and nothing beyond.

The five checks, in the first hour.

  1. Is it valid and addressed to me? Names, addresses, the court, the case number, the date, and the signature or seal. A photocopied letter from a lawyer is a request, not an order, and the two are answered differently.
  2. What exactly does it require? Documents, attendance, or both; the date; and the scope — "all records relating to" is broader than "your notes of 4 March" and both must be read precisely.
  3. Has the client been told? Unless the order prohibits it, the client is informed immediately, in writing, and the therapist explains what will be produced.
  4. Who is advising me? The supervisor, the indemnity provider, the professional body and, where necessary, the therapist's own lawyer. The therapist does not respond alone to a legal process.
  5. What is the deadline, and is there time to object? Where the scope is excessive or the material privileged, the advice may be to seek to limit the order. That application is made by a lawyer and it is made before the deadline, not after.

The order of operations once advice is taken.

  • Produce only what is required, in the form required.
  • Keep a complete copy of everything produced, indexed, and mark the date it was produced.
  • Where something is withheld on advice, say so in writing and on what basis — a short privilege log.
  • Record the process in the file: what was received, when, what advice was taken from whom, what was produced, when, and to whom.
  • Tell the client what was produced, unless prohibited, and record that you did.

What not to do. Destroy or alter records, which is a serious offence and the end of the therapist's credibility. Produce the entire file because it is simpler. Ring the other side's lawyer to negotiate scope without advice. Or promise the client that nothing will be produced.

The extra care in family cases. The file frequently contains the other party's individual material and sometimes a child's. The therapist produces what the order requires about the client she treated, and where the order is broad enough to capture other people's disclosures, she raises the scope with her adviser. This is not obstruction; it is the discharge of duties owed to people who are not parties to the dispute.

11.4a Privilege, and the promise a therapist must not make

The distinction. Confidentiality is a duty the therapist owes. Privilege is a right the client holds, protecting communications from disclosure in legal proceedings. The therapist's obligation is to keep confidence; the client's privilege may be waived by the client and may be overridden by statute or by the court.

The four situations in which privilege will not save the file.

  1. Where the client waives it, frequently by putting their mental state in issue — a client who claims psychological injury, or who relies on the therapy as evidence of good parenting, has opened the door.
  2. Where a statute creates an exception, child protection being the commonest.
  3. Where the court orders production after considering any claim of privilege.
  4. Where the therapist is the subject of a complaint or proceeding, in which case the record is the evidence the therapist herself needs. The codes generally permit disclosure for the purpose of defending a complaint.

The consent conversation, revised. The Week 2 sentence — "this is confidential except for the limits I am about to describe" — should include one further clause in any case with a legal dimension: "and if a court ever orders me to produce my records, I will tell you, I will seek advice, and I will produce what the order requires."

What the therapist must never say. "Nothing you tell me can ever be used in court." It is false, it is frequently said to reassure, and it is the false promise on which a client later bases an allegation of betrayal.

11.4b The request from the school

The commonest third-party request in family work, and the one most often answered carelessly.

What the school actually needs, and what it does not. The school needs to know that the child is receiving support, whether there are practical implications for school life, and — where the school is a referrer — whether to continue the referral. It does not need the content of the therapy, the parents' accounts of each other, or a diagnosis the therapist is not qualified to give.

The three permitted answers. Nothing, where the family does not consent, except where a safeguarding duty requires otherwise. Attendance only, where the family consents to that and nothing more: "the family is attending and I have their consent to tell you that." A short agreed summary, written with the family, showing them the text before it is sent.

The safeguarding exception, handled carefully. Where the school's involvement is a child protection matter, the therapist may be required to share information with the safeguarding lead. That sharing is specific, recorded, and — unless telling the parents would endanger the child — done with the parents' knowledge.

The request that arrives by email at 4 p.m. on a Friday. The answer is not a reply. It is an acknowledgement that the request has been received, that consent will be checked on Monday, and that the therapist will respond in writing. Most breaches in small practices happen in the twenty minutes before a weekend.

11.5 Giving evidence

The role. A witness of fact reports what she saw, heard and did. She does not advocate, does not offer opinions outside her expertise, and does not answer the question she would have preferred. Where the therapist has also been appointed as an expert — rare, and never in her own client's case (Week 10) — the rules are different and the distinction must be stated at the outset.

The four rules, in the box.

  1. Answer the question that was asked. Briefly, then stop. The commonest error is the therapist who, anxious to be understood, continues into material that creates a new line of attack.
  2. Do not guess. "I do not know" and "I would need to check my notes" are complete and creditable answers. An improvisation under pressure is how witnesses are destroyed.
  3. Distinguish fact from opinion, and say which you are giving. "I observed X. My opinion, for what it is worth, is Y, and I should say I am not a psychiatrist."
  4. Do not defend the treatment. Counsel will invite the therapist to justify a decision. The answer is an explanation, not a defence: "I made that decision because at the time I had these three pieces of information, and I recorded it here."

The three questions that will come.

"You only ever saw one side, didn't you?" Answer honestly: how many sessions each party attended, and what the therapist did not have.

"You are not a doctor, are you?" Answer honestly, and state what the therapist is qualified to say — which is usually about the therapy, not the diagnosis.

"That is your opinion, and it is only an opinion." Agree, and say what it rests on: the sessions, the notes, the recorded statements, the supervision. The opinion that is supported by evidence survives this question; the opinion that is a feeling does not.

The preparation that matters. Read the whole file, not only the notes about the session in question. Write a chronology. Mark the three decisions you might be challenged on and be able to explain each in three sentences. And rehearse with a colleague playing counsel, because the first time the question is asked should not be in court.

The day itself. Take the file and the order. Arrive early. Dress as the court expects. Speak to the bench, not to counsel. Answer slowly. Ask for a break if you need one. And remember that the discomfort of the witness box is not evidence that the therapist did something wrong.

11.5a The report written before the hearing

Where the court asks for a report, the structure below produces something usable and honest. It is a report of fact and clinical opinion within the therapist's competence, not a custody evaluation (Week 10).

The six sections. The instruction: who asked for the report, what was asked, and the date. The sources: sessions, dates, who attended, documents seen, and what was not available. The history: brief, factual, and drawn from the record rather than from memory. The observations: what the therapist saw, in behavioural language. The clinical opinion: clearly labelled, bounded by the therapist's competence, and stating its limits. The limits: what the therapist cannot say — parenting capacity, the other parent, prognosis, the child's best interests.

The four sentences that keep a report inside its role. "I have not assessed [the other parent]." "I make no recommendation as to residence or contact." "This report is based on my contact with [client] over [n] sessions and on no other source." "I am willing to attend court and to answer questions about the treatment I provided."

What the report must not contain. A recommendation disguised as an observation ("she was consistently the more attentive parent, which I would expect to be relevant to residence"), the other party's alleged behaviour presented as fact, and any material from an individual session of a partner who has not consented.

11.6 Case: the subpoena in the Eze matter

Background. Mrs Ngozi Eze, 38, was in treatment for eight months following her husband's departure (Week 6, section 6.7, where she asked the therapist to be a character witness and was refused). She is now in a custody dispute over two children, 11 and 6.

The document. A subpoena duces tecum arrives by courier, addressed to the therapist, requiring "all notes, records, correspondence and reports relating to the treatment of Ngozi Eze" and her attendance on a date three weeks away. It is issued at the request of the husband's solicitor.

The five checks. Valid and properly addressed; scope extremely broad; client not yet told; supervisor available, indemnity provider contacted; deadline leaves eleven days to seek advice on scope.

What is in the file, and why it matters.

  1. The treatment notes across eight months, including Mrs Eze's account of the marriage, which is highly critical of her husband and includes allegations that have never been tested.
  2. Three entries in which she describes her mother-in-law supervising the children and says she "does not trust her".
  3. A note recording that Mrs Eze told the therapist, in an individual session, that she had once left the children with a neighbour overnight while she travelled to another city for a funeral. The therapist recorded it as a fact reported, not investigated.
  4. A letter the therapist wrote at Mrs Eze's request to the children's school, confirming that the family was attending therapy, with no clinical detail.
  5. A supervision note recording the therapist's view that Mrs Eze was "fragile but improving".
  6. Nothing about the husband, who was never a client.

The advice received. Produce items 1, 3, 4 and 5 in response to the order, with third-party identifiers redacted where they are not the subject of the proceedings (the neighbour's name, the mother-in-law's medical details, which appear in one sentence about her own health). Item 2 is producible, with the mother-in-law's health information redacted. Item 5's reference to "fragile" is producible and the therapist should expect to be asked about it. Nothing is withheld except the redactions, and the redactions are logged with reasons.

What the therapist did.

  1. Told Mrs Eze by telephone the same day, in the words: "your husband's lawyer has asked the court to order me to produce my records. I have to comply. I am going to tell you exactly what is in the file and what will be produced, and I am going to take advice first." She then read the relevant entries to her, over two sessions.
  2. Wrote to Mrs Eze confirming the conversation, the scope of the order, what would be produced, and the date.
  3. Took advice from her indemnity provider and, through them, a lawyer, on the redactions and on whether to seek to limit the scope. The advice was to produce with redactions and not to contest the scope, because the material was plainly relevant and a contested application would delay the hearing and damage the therapist's position.
  4. Produced an indexed bundle, paginated, with a covering letter stating what was produced, what was redacted, and the basis for each redaction.
  5. Attended and gave evidence.
  6. Recorded all six steps in the file, with dates and names.

The evidence, and the two hard moments.

The first. Counsel read out the note about the overnight stay with the neighbour. "You recorded that my client's wife left the children overnight with a neighbour, did you not?" The therapist answered: "I recorded what she told me. I did not investigate it and I made no finding." Counsel: "So you cannot say whether it is true." Therapist: "That is correct."

The second. Counsel read out the supervision note: "fragile but improving". "You considered her fragile." Therapist: "I wrote that in a supervision note eight months ago, in the context of her grief and her financial position. I also wrote 'improving', and the treatment continued for five months after that note. I can explain the context if the court wishes."

The outcome of the hearing, which is not the point of the case. Residence was ordered with the mother, with defined contact for the father. The therapist's evidence neither rescued nor destroyed the case, which is what evidence from a treating clinician usually does.

What the case teaches.

  1. The order was complied with, not resisted, and the compliance was careful. The therapist's protection was not argument but accuracy: an indexed bundle, logged redactions, and a recorded process.
  2. The client was told before she was surprised. The two sessions spent reading the file to her were the clinical work of the week, and probably prevented the treatment from ending.
  3. The notes she had written were the evidence. The "fragile but improving" note was the only moment of real difficulty, and it was survivable because it was a supervision note with a date and a context, not a character judgement in the clinical record.
  4. The file contained material about people who were not parties, which is the ordinary condition of family work and the reason the redaction log exists.
  5. Nothing was destroyed, altered or withheld without a reason on the record. Had any of those three happened, the case would have been about the therapist rather than about the children.

11.7 Transcript: fifteen minutes of cross-examination

Condensed from the hearing, with annotation.

X1 Counsel: You are not a psychiatrist, are you? X2 Therapist: No. I am a family therapist, registered with [body]. X3 Counsel: And you are not qualified to say whether my client's wife is a fit mother. X4 Therapist: I have not said that, in my notes or in this court. — Fourteen words. The answer corrects the premise without arguing with counsel, which is the difference between a witness and a combatant. X5 Counsel: You formed a view, though. X6 Therapist: I formed a clinical impression of a woman in grief, which is what I was treating. (pause) I recorded it and I have explained what it was based on. — The concession (yes, a view) followed by the limitation (of a particular thing), which is more credible than a denial. X7 Counsel: You never spoke to my client. X8 Therapist: I did not. He declined to attend. I wrote to him once, at his wife's request, offering an appointment, and I have the letter in my file. — The fact, plus the document, plus the correction of the implication that the therapist excluded him. X9 Counsel: Would it not have been better to insist? X10 Therapist: I cannot compel an adult to attend therapy. (pause) I can record that I invited him. — The answer to a "would it not have been better" question, which is counsel's favourite form: it invites the witness to criticise herself. The witness states what was possible. X11 Counsel: You recorded that she left the children with a neighbour overnight. That is a safeguarding matter, is it not? X12 Therapist: I recorded what she told me. I did not treat it as a safeguarding concern, because it was one night, with a known neighbour, and she told me herself without being asked. (pause) If I had thought a child was at risk I would have reported it, and I would have recorded that too. — The factual answer, the reasoning, and the statement of the duty — three elements in three sentences. X13 Counsel: With respect, that is your judgement. X14 Therapist: It is. And it is the judgement I am employed to make, and it is written down with the reasons. — The final turn of the transcript, and the one students should memorise: the witness owns the judgement, states its basis, and does not apologise for having made it.

What the transcript shows. The therapist's longest answer is forty words and most are under fifteen. She concedes what is true, corrects what is false, and never argues. The single most damaging thing that could have happened in that box was a therapist who sounded as though she were the client's advocate — and the discipline that prevented it was grammatical: she spoke about what she observed, in the past tense, and left the conclusions to the court.

11.8 Laboratory: the note lab and the subpoena drill

Part A — the note lab (thirty minutes). Students are given a ten-minute transcript of a family session containing: a risk disclosure, an individual aside, an argument between the parents, and a plan. Each writes the note in eight minutes. The notes are exchanged and marked against the "three readers" standard by a partner, who must answer three questions from the note alone: what is the risk, what was decided, and what would I do if the client telephoned tonight?

Criterion0–12–34–5
Fact and registerJudgements mixed with factsMostly factualEvery statement in the correct register
RiskNot recordedRecorded as a statementQuestions asked, answers given, level and plan recorded
The individual asideIn the main noteOmittedIn the restricted section, with the reason recorded
Plan and consentAbsentGeneralSpecific, dated, with who was told what and the consent
HandoverUnusableAdequateA colleague could take over tonight

Part B — the subpoena drill (thirty minutes). Each student receives one of four documents: a subpoena duces tecum, a letter from a solicitor requesting records "as a matter of urgency", a request from a school for "the records of the family", and a request from a client's employer. In twelve minutes they must write: the first five actions, the letter or response, and the file entry. The group then compares the four responses, which is the point of the exercise — the same instinct produces four different correct answers.

The four correct answers, in summary. Subpoena: verify, tell the client, take advice, produce what is required, log. Solicitor's letter: this is a request, not an order; consent or a court order is required; reply in writing. School: attendance only, with consent, unless a safeguarding duty requires more. Employer: attendance only, with consent, and a note explaining the limit to the employer.

Marks for Part B (10). Correct identification of the instrument; the client informed; advice taken; minimum necessary applied; the file entry complete.

11.8a The four-minute handover note

The note that matters most is the one written when a therapist goes on leave, becomes ill, or leaves a post. It is four minutes' work and almost never done.

The four lines per case.

  1. Who and why — the client system, the presenting problem, and the agreed focus.
  2. The current risk — the last assessment, the date, the plan, and who knows.
  3. The live issues — the secret being held, the referral pending, the court date, the person who has not been told something.
  4. What to do first — the call to make, the appointment to keep, the file to read.

What belongs at the top of the bundle. A single page listing every client with a risk flag, a court date, or an open protection matter, in date order. It is the page a covering colleague reads on the first evening.

Why it is an ethical duty and not an administrative one. Continuity of care is a client's interest. The therapist who leaves without a handover has transferred her caseload and abandoned its clinical responsibility, and the case in which this matters most is always the one with the highest risk — because that is the case with the most to record and the greatest reluctance to reduce it to four lines.

11.9 Six errors

Error 1 — The note that records a conclusion without its basis. "Client is depressed." The next clinician cannot tell whether this was an assessment, a diagnosis, or an impression, and neither can a court.

Error 2 — The individual disclosure in the joint file. It is the commonest documentary failure in couple work and it is created by an administrative habit rather than a decision.

Error 3 — Promising that the file can never be disclosed. It is false, it is said to reassure, and it converts a lawful disclosure into a betrayal.

Error 4 — Producing the entire file in response to a narrow order. It breaches duties owed to third parties and to the other partner, and it is usually done out of anxiety rather than necessity.

Error 5 — Altering or destroying records. It is a criminal offence in most jurisdictions, it is detectable, and it converts a defensible case into an indefensible one.

Error 6 — Advocacy in the witness box. The therapist who argues for the client's case has abandoned the only thing that made her evidence useful, which is that she was not a partisan.

11.9a Ten sentences that have been read back to a therapist in a hearing

Each of these was written in a file and each was quoted. They are collected because the pattern is instructive: none is a lie, and each is an assertion that the therapist could not support when asked.

  1. "Client is manipulative." Question asked: "On what evidence?"
  2. "Mother appears to prefer the son." Question: "Which sessions, and what did you observe?"
  3. "The marriage is characterised by his control." Question: "You never met him, did you?"
  4. "Client is not at risk." Question: "How could you know that?"
  5. "She is a devoted mother." Question: "That is an opinion, is it not?"
  6. "Father is in denial." Question: "Did you assess him?"
  7. "Client has borderline traits." Question: "Are you qualified to make that diagnosis?"
  8. "The family is enmeshed." Question: "What does that mean in observable terms?"
  9. "Client was intoxicated at session." Question: "Was this tested, or observed, and by whom?"
  10. "I am certain the disclosure is true." Question: "Certain on what basis?"

The rule that produces all ten errors. The sentence was written to capture a feeling, in a document that requires evidence. The remedy is not fewer opinions but labelled ones: "I observed X. My impression, which I have not tested, is Y."

11.10 Culture, faith and the record

"The family wants to see what you have written." In households where information is held collectively, the idea that a therapist keeps a private record is foreign and suspicious. The request may come from an elder, a husband, or a client herself who has been sent to fetch it. The answer is not a lecture on data protection. It is three sentences: what the record is (the therapist's working note), who may see it (the client, and a court if it orders), and what the therapist will do instead (summarise in the session what she has written, and let the client correct anything inaccurate).

The client's right to her own record. Where a client asks to see what is written about her, the request is usually legitimate and should not be treated as hostility. Reading the record with the client, in a session, is frequently therapeutic: it demonstrates that the therapist has understood, it corrects errors, and it makes the record collaborative rather than mysterious.

Privacy in a shared house. A record kept on a phone in a household where the husband checks the phone is not private. This is an argument for paper in a locked cabinet, for a practice system rather than a personal device, and for the practice policy that nothing clinical is stored on a personal phone. In the settings this book is written for, that is not a technicality; it is a safety matter.

Confession, records and religious authority. A religious leader who asks to see the therapist's notes about a member of the congregation is making a request that has no legal basis and considerable social force. The answer is the same as for any third party, delivered with respect: "I cannot discuss her with you. I can tell you that she is being seen, if she has agreed to that."

The family's own record. Many families keep their own documentation — letters, court papers, a written account of events. Where the therapist is shown it, the fact is recorded, because the file must show the source of information that reached the treatment from outside, and because the document may later be produced by the family and attributed to the therapy.

DEBATE — "A therapist's notes should be written on the assumption that the client will read them." Argue for: transparency, accuracy, the client's right of access, and the discipline it imposes. Argue against: the clinical record must contain hypotheses and observations not intended for the client, candour would be lost, and the therapeutic relationship would change. Then state your practice, and the note you would write differently if the debate changed your mind.

11.10a The retention, storage and destruction schedule

Three decisions that every practice must make in advance, because each of them is impossible to make well at the moment it arises.

Retention. How long is a record kept after the last contact? The answer is set by the professional body, by limitation periods for claims, and by the needs of former clients who may return. In most jurisdictions the practical range for adult records is seven to ten years, and for children's records until some years after the child's majority. The rule should be written, applied consistently, and stated in the privacy notice the client receives.

Storage. Who can reach the record; what happens if the therapist is ill, dies, or leaves the country; and where the backup lives. A practice of one is a practice with a single point of failure, and the "professional will" — a named colleague who can access records, contact clients and close the practice — is part of the duty to clients rather than an act of foresight.

Destruction. How, by whom, and with what record. A file that is destroyed is recorded as destroyed, with the date and the method, and the destruction is subject to a check that no request or proceeding is pending. The practitioner who shreds a file during a complaint has committed the offence described in Error 5.

The client's information. What the client is told at the start: what will be kept, for how long, who may see it, what happens on the therapist's death or retirement, and how the client can obtain a copy. Three sentences in the consent conversation, and a paragraph in the written information.

11.11 Evidence note

What is known. Studies of clinical records consistently find high rates of missing information on risk, consent and reasoning, and that record quality is improved by structured templates and by audit with feedback. Research on client access to records finds that reading them is usually experienced as neutral or positive and rarely harmful. In the forensic literature, the quality of the contemporaneous record is one of the strongest determinants of how a clinician's conduct is judged in a complaint or a hearing.

What is weaker. There is little research on the specific formats used in family therapy, and none on the restricted-section practice recommended here, which is derived from the structure of multiperson confidentiality rather than from outcome evidence.

What is not supported. That detailed notes protect a therapist regardless of content — a detailed note containing judgements is more dangerous than a sparse factual one. That the client's access to records is generally harmful. And that destroying or amending records ever improves a position.

The matrix cell. Development is probabilistic and non-linear; the same input produces different outcomes depending on timing and context. The same sentence, written in a supervision note and written in the clinical record, has two different lives in a courtroom.

11.12 Key terms

Contemporaneous note — a record made at or near the time of the event; its evidential weight is much greater.

Restricted section — a part of a couple or family file holding material that must not be disclosed to another client in the same case.

Privilege — the client's legal protection against disclosure in proceedings; qualified and waivable.

Subpoena duces tecum — an order to produce documents; distinct from a request by a party.

Redaction — the removal of specified information from a disclosed document, recorded with reasons.

Witness of fact — a witness reporting what she observed; contrasted with an expert witness offering opinion.

Minimum necessary — the principle that disclosure is limited to what the purpose requires.

11.12a The two-minute note review

A habit rather than a technique, and the cheapest quality improvement available: at the end of each working day, the therapist re-reads the notes she wrote and asks four questions of each.

  1. Could a colleague act on this tonight?
  2. Have I written a judgement where I should have written an observation?
  3. Have I recorded the risk questions I asked, or only the answers?
  4. Is there anything here I would not want read aloud?

Ten notes take two minutes and question 4 is the one that changes practice. A therapist who asks it daily stops writing the sentences that get read back in a hearing (section 11.9a) within about three weeks.

11.13 Self-test

  1. Name the three readers of a clinical note and what each needs.
  2. Give the four registers, with an example of each.
  3. State the six headings of a family session note.
  4. What is the restricted section, and what three clauses describe it?
  5. Give the five checks to perform in the first hour after a subpoena arrives.
  6. Distinguish confidentiality from privilege.
  7. Name the four situations in which privilege will not protect the file.
  8. Give the four rules for giving evidence, and explain why "I do not know" is a strong answer.

Answers. (1) The therapist in six months — problem, attempts, outcome, plan; a colleague taking over — risk, plan, who knows, what to do if telephoned; a court — facts with dates, decisions with reasons, consent, no speculation. (2) Fact: "client attended on 4 March"; observation: "she checked her phone four times"; opinion: "consistent with an acute stress response"; hypothesis: "possible that the husband's drinking is a factor, not yet explored". (3) Attendees; presenting this session; risk screen; interventions; response; plan. (4) A clearly marked part of a couple or family file holding individual-session material that must not be shared with the other client; described by its reference to practice policy, the client's knowledge that it will not be shared, and the statement that nothing affecting safety is withheld from the main file. (5) Validity and addressee; exact scope; whether the client has been told; who is advising; the deadline and whether there is time to object. (6) Confidentiality is the therapist's professional duty; privilege is the client's legal protection, waivable by the client and overridable by statute or court order. (7) Client waiver, frequently by putting mental state in issue; a statutory exception such as child protection; a court order; and proceedings in which the therapist is defending herself. (8) Answer the question asked; do not guess; distinguish fact from opinion; do not defend the treatment. "I do not know" is strong because it is credible, it prevents the improvisation that destroys witnesses, and no court expects a clinician to know everything.

11.14 Reflective task

Write one page on the following before you read further: which of the four registers do you default to when you are tired, and what does that default produce in a record that might one day be read aloud? Most therapists default to opinion, because opinion is what the mind produces when it is summarising quickly, and opinion is the least defensible thing in a file.

Take the last note you wrote in a placement or practice and read it as three people: as the therapist in six months, as a colleague on call tonight, and as counsel cross-examining you. Write a paragraph on what each of them would make of it. Then rewrite one sentence.

11.15 Further reading

  • Any current text on record keeping and the legal process in health and social care in your jurisdiction.
  • The Nigeria Data Protection Act 2023, and your professional body's guidance on records and access.
  • Gutheil, T. on the psychiatrist in court — despite the discipline, the clearest treatment of testifying as a clinician.
  • Your indemnity provider's guidance on subpoenas and on responding to requests for records — usually free and usually unread.
  • Court rules on the production of documents in family proceedings where you practise.

Next: Week 12 — Money, gifts and the economics of the relationship.

Week 12 — Money, gifts and the business of therapy

Learning outcomes. By the end of this week the student can (1) explain why the fee is clinical material and not an administrative detail; (2) conduct the fee conversation at intake, including the sliding scale and the cancellation policy, in five minutes; (3) manage non-payment clinically rather than avoiding it; (4) apply a decision procedure to any gift and record the outcome; and (5) identify the business pressures that produce unethical practice and state the limits that protect the client.

The subject nobody teaches and everybody practises

Money is the least discussed and most consistently present element of the therapeutic relationship. It is present at the first contact, in every session, in the waiting room, in the client's calculation of whether she can afford to continue, and in the therapist's calculation of whether she can afford to keep her. It carries meaning about worth, entitlement, dependency, shame, power and care — and because therapists are frequently uncomfortable with money, the meaning is usually not discussed at all.

The three-part claim of this week. First, the fee is a clinical instrument: it defines the frame, it makes the relationship professional, and it is one of the ways in which the client's difficulties with entitlement, need and worth appear in the room. Second, the therapist's own relationship with money is a source of error, and it is rarely examined. Third, a practice that is not financially viable will exploit its clients in ways that are rarely deliberate and always structural.

SituationClinical meaning, usuallyEthical requirement
A client who always pays lateHelplessness, resentment, a test of whether the therapist will chaseA stated policy, applied consistently, discussed as material
A client who pays in advance for ten sessionsFear of abandonment; an attempt to secure the therapistDiscuss what the payment means before accepting it
A client who asks for a reductionShame; a belief that she is not worth the full feeA written policy, a decision made once, recorded
A therapist who never chases a debtThe therapist's own discomfort with conflict or with greedSupervision; a policy applied by the practice rather than the person
A gift after a good outcomeGratitude; a wish to be remembered; obligationA decision procedure, a conversation, a record
A gift after a disclosure of shameAn attempt to repair the therapist's view of her; a paymentOften to be refused, with the meaning named

12.1 The lecture in ninety minutes

MinutesContentTeaching form
0–10Money as clinical material: three vignettes, and the therapist's discomfortAnonymous written answers to "what did your family teach you about money?"
10–22Five concepts: frame, transference, the therapist's money, sliding scale, viabilityLecture
22–38The intake fee conversation, scripted and rehearsedDemonstration, then triads
38–52Non-payment: the sequence, the limit, the endingCase, with students writing the sentence for month two
52–68Gifts: the four categories and the decision procedureCard sort of eight gifts; then the refusal script
68–80The business pressures: discounts, packages, marketing, billingLecture with four examples, students identify the risk
80–90The Nwankwo caseCase, students decide what is accepted, what is charged and what is said

12.2 Five concepts that organise the week

Concept 1 — The fee is part of the frame, and the frame is the treatment. A therapy has an agreed time, place, frequency, duration and payment. The payment is not the least of these: it is what makes the relationship a professional one rather than a friendship, and it is what allows the client to feel entitled to the therapist's attention. A client who is not paying, or who is paying unpredictably, is frequently a client who does not feel entitled to ask for anything.

Concept 2 — Money carries the client's history, and the therapist should expect it. The client who cannot ask for a reduction and stops attending; the client who insists on paying for the whole course in advance; the client who gives an expensive gift and then asks for a favour; the client who treats the fee as a debt of gratitude. Each is a repetition of something, and each becomes treatable when it is named.

Concept 3 — The therapist's own money is the largest unexamined variable in the room. Therapists under-charge because they are anxious about being seen as commercial, or because they do not believe their work is worth the fee, or because a client's poverty activates guilt. They over-charge, or cling to clients they should refer, when the practice is not viable. Neither is a moral failing; both are material for supervision, and neither is correctable by willpower.

Concept 4 — The sliding scale is an instrument of justice and a machine for resentment. A scale that is written, published internally, applied by rule and reviewed annually serves clients and protects the therapist. A scale that is negotiated case by case, in the room, according to how sorry the therapist feels, produces inconsistency, the client's discovery of another client's lower fee, and a therapist who is quietly furious.

Concept 5 — Viability is an ethical matter. A practice that cannot pay its rent will raise its fees abruptly, take clients it should refer, and cut the supervision that protects clients. The ethical practice is a sustainable one: priced to survive, with the pro bono work built into the budget rather than subtracted from it at the end of a difficult month.

12.2a The intake fee conversation, in five minutes

Six statements, made at the first contact, in the order below, and repeated in writing.

  1. The fee, plainly. "My fee is X per session, and sessions are fifty minutes." No apology, no explanation of overheads, no "I know it is expensive".
  2. What the fee includes, and what it does not. The session; correspondence with other professionals where consented; a written report where one is agreed. Not telephone calls between sessions, which are handled by agreement.
  3. When and how it is paid. "Payment is due at the end of each session, by transfer or in cash. I do not carry change." The mechanics are stated because ambiguity about mechanics is where resentment begins.
  4. Cancellations. "I require twenty-four hours' notice. If you cancel with less, or do not attend, the session is charged, because the time was held for you." Then the sentence that makes it acceptable: "if you are ill or there is an emergency, tell me and I will not charge you."
  5. The sliding scale, if there is one. "I have a small number of reduced-fee places. If you need one, tell me now or at any point, and I will tell you honestly whether one is available." The phrase "tell me honestly whether one is available" is important: it makes the scale a resource rather than a favour.
  6. What happens if payment becomes difficult. "If you ever cannot pay, tell me early. We will find a way forward, and I would much rather know than discover it." Said at the start, this sentence makes the later conversation possible.

A worked fee dialogue.

Client: How much do you charge? Therapist: X per session. Sessions are fifty minutes, and payment is at the end of each session. Client: (pause) That is — I am a teacher. Therapist: Then let us talk about it now rather than later. I have two reduced places at the moment; one is taken. (pause) What would be manageable for you? Client: I do not know. I do not want to be a charity case. Therapist: You are not. The reduced places exist because I would rather work with a full practice than an expensive empty one. (pause) If we agree a figure, we will review it in six months, and if your situation changes we will change it then.

Why the last sentence matters. A reduced fee without a review becomes permanent by default, and both parties know it. A stated review makes the arrangement a professional one and gives the client a way to increase the fee without having to raise it.

The three questions the therapist should be able to answer without flinching. What is my fee? Why is it that? And what will I do if this client cannot pay it? A therapist who cannot answer the second has not priced her work; she has inherited a number.

12.2b Non-payment: the sequence and the limit

The clinical problem. Non-payment is rarely about money alone. It is frequently about shame (the client who cannot say she cannot pay), about entitlement (the client who believes the therapist owes her), about a test (will you chase me, and does my worth depend on your not asking?), or about a genuine catastrophe. The therapist who chases aggressively and the therapist who never raises it both fail; the first humiliates, the second colludes and then resents.

The sequence, session by session.

At the first missed payment: ask, in the session, without accusation. "I noticed the last two sessions were not paid. Is something happening?" The question is about the client, not the debt.

At the second: name the pattern and the feeling. "This is the second month. I want to say what I notice, which is that you have not mentioned it, and I wonder whether it is difficult to talk about." Then the agreement: a date, an amount, and whether the sessions continue meanwhile.

At the third: the limit. "I am going to be honest with you. I cannot continue to hold sessions that are not paid, and I am not willing to end the therapy because of money without us having tried to solve it. Here are the options: a reduced fee for a period, a payment plan, a pause, or a referral to a service that is free."

At the fourth: the decision, made and recorded. Continuing indefinitely is not generosity; it is a slow resentment that the client will feel, and it ends with the therapist withdrawing at the worst possible moment.

The sentences that work, and the ones that do not.

SayNot
"I need to raise something about the account.""You have not paid."
"I would rather we talked about it than that I sent a letter.""This is a business, you know."
"What would be manageable?""I have been very patient."
"If we cannot agree, I will refer you and we will end properly.""I cannot help people who do not pay."

The clinical gain in the conversation. A client who has never been able to negotiate a debt with anyone — and this is common in clients whose financial lives are chaotic or controlled — has the experience of a relationship in which a difficulty is named, negotiated and survived. That is often worth more than the fee.

Ending for non-payment, done properly. A closing session, a written summary, a referral, an offer to return if circumstances change, and a record of the reasoning. Not a letter from an agency, and not silence.

12.2c The therapist who is owed money, and cannot say so

The countertransference of the unpaid fee is one of the most reliable in practice and one of the least discussed.

The four patterns.

  1. The rescuer. The therapist decides that the client's poverty is part of the problem and that charging is therefore cruel. Sessions continue, the fee is never discussed, and the therapist begins to feel quietly exploited. The client, sensing it, apologises for existing.
  2. The withdrawing therapist. The therapist begins to offer shorter sessions, fewer appointments, less attention. The client experiences a loss of interest and cannot say why.
  3. The punitive therapist. The debt becomes a moral matter. The therapist's tone becomes cool, and the client — who has usually been punished before — recognises the tone before she can name it.
  4. The avoider. The therapist genuinely does not think about the debt until the accountant raises it, and then sends a letter written in a completely different voice from the one used in the room.

The examination. Before the conversation, three questions in writing: what do I feel about being owed this money? What do I imagine the client feels? And what am I doing differently in the sessions since the debt began? The third question is usually answered with a specific change — the therapist has stopped challenging, or has started ending on time with unusual firmness — and that change is the clinical material.

The rule. The therapist's discomfort must not be paid for by the client, in either direction: neither by waiving the fee so that the therapist does not have to have the conversation, nor by extracting the money in a way that punishes her. The conversation is the only way through, and it is the therapist's to initiate.

12.3 Gifts: four categories and one procedure

Category 1 — the cultural token. Food, cloth, a small item given at a festival or a home visit. Value is modest, the meaning is relational, and refusing it can be experienced as rejection. Default: accept, name the meaning, record it, and do not treat it as a transaction.

Category 2 — the transitional gift. Given at the end of treatment, at a birth, at a bereavement, or after a significant improvement. Default: usually accept if modest; discuss what it means; record it. The discussion frequently produces the most important session of the final month.

Category 3 — the expensive gift. Value beyond a token; jewellery, electronics, a large sum, land. Default: decline, warmly, with an explanation that protects the giver's dignity: "I am very moved that you would do this, and I am not able to accept it. It would change something between us that I need to keep as it is."

Category 4 — the gift with strings. Any gift accompanied by a request, an expectation, or a narrative about what the therapist now owes. Default: decline, and name the string, because a gift that creates an obligation has already begun to distort the relationship.

The procedure, in five steps.

  1. Notice. Register the gift as a clinical event rather than an administrative one, and do not decide in the

doorway.

  1. Ask what it means. "What would it mean to you if I accepted this?" and, where relevant, "and what would it mean if I did not?" The answers are almost always more informative than the object.
  2. Decide against the category, not against the person's need. The therapist who accepts because the client will be hurt has made the client's reaction the criterion.
  3. Say the decision, once, warmly, and without a lecture. The lecture ("you know, ethically, I cannot") turns a gift into an embarrassment.
  4. Record it. One line: what was given, what was decided, what was discussed, and what happened to the object.

The gift that must be refused, whatever its value. A gift from a client who is in a coercive relationship and who is spending money her partner controls, because accepting it colludes in the control. A gift from a child client that has clearly been taken from a parent's belongings. A gift that is the client's only asset. And a gift given immediately after a disclosure of shame, because the therapist is being paid to stop seeing something.

The gift that is a communication. In many of these cases the gift says something the client cannot: "I am not only a problem", "you have been good to my mother", "I want you to remember me". Naming the communication — "I think this says something about how you want me to see you" — frequently makes the object unimportant, and the client may then be content for the therapist to keep it or return it.

What to do with what is accepted. Food is shared or eaten; cloth may be worn or kept; money is never accepted. Where the practice has a policy that accepted gifts become the property of the clinician, the policy is stated in writing at intake, which prevents the later argument about whether the therapist "kept" something.

12.3a Barter, pro bono, and the client who cannot pay at all

Barter. The exchange of goods or services for treatment. Permissible in narrow conditions: the client initiates; the value is agreed in writing; the arrangement is time-limited; it is supervised; and the therapist records the rationale. Prohibited in practice where the client's service is intimate, where the value is uncertain, or where the exchange would place the therapist in a position of judging the client's work.

The specific hazard of barter. The client's service becomes clinical material. The therapist who accepts tailoring from a client will have views about the tailoring, and the client will know it. What was a fee becomes a performance, and the therapy acquires a second arena in which the client can fail.

Pro bono. Treatment given without fee. It is a professional obligation in most codes, in some form, and it is best organised as a proportion of the practice — one or two places held open — rather than as an occasional decision made under pressure in the room. The client on a pro bono place is told that it is a place, not a favour, and that the arrangement is reviewed annually. That framing protects both parties from the gratitude that otherwise contaminates the work.

The client who cannot pay at all. Three options, in order of preference. A reduced or waived fee for a defined period, with a review. A referral to a free service, with a warm handover. A short piece of work — six sessions — focused on a single question, rather than an open-ended therapy that will collapse in month three.

The option that must be avoided. Continuing indefinitely without agreement, because the therapist cannot bear to raise it. The client will sense the unspoken arrangement, will begin to apologise for existing, and will leave without a closing. The ending that results is worse for her than the one the therapist was avoiding.

The practitioner's own arithmetic. A practice with four reduced places, each at half fee, has given away two full fees a week. That is a real contribution and it must be in the budget. The practitioner who gives away places ad hoc and then raises everyone's fee to compensate has made the poorest clients pay for the therapist's generosity to other clients.

12.3b The gift from the family, not the client

A distinct problem, and common in family work: the gift comes from a member of the family who is not the identified client — the grandmother, the uncle who pays, the child.

Why it is different. The giver may not be in treatment; the meaning may be about the family's hierarchy rather than about gratitude to the therapist; and accepting from one member can create a coalition that the other members can see.

The procedure. Treat it as the gift from a client, and add two steps. Establish who decided that the gift would be given, because a gift offered by a father on behalf of a family in which the mother is silent is a statement about who speaks. And where the giver is not a client, decline more readily: there is no therapeutic relationship to preserve, and the gift is frequently an attempt to establish one.

The child's gift. A drawing, a card, a stone from the school playground: accept it, thank the child, and where the child is in a family treatment, keep it visible for a session. A child who gives a gift is testing whether the therapist is a person who can be given to, and the answer matters clinically.

12.4 The business: eight pressures that produce unethical practice

1. The package discount. "Ten sessions for the price of eight." It is a commercial instrument in a clinical relationship, it creates pressure on the client to continue when she would have stopped, and it creates pressure on the therapist to keep her coming. Where a package is offered it must be refundable, optional, and never presented as a saving against distress.

2. The productivity target. An employer who requires a fixed number of clinical hours per week, or a fixed income from each clinician, creates an incentive to keep clients in treatment, to shorten sessions, or to accept clients outside competence. The individual therapist cannot change the system; she can record the pressure and refuse the three specific practices that harm clients — retention for revenue, treatment without supervision, and records written to satisfy a billing code.

3. Upcoding and creative billing. Describing a session in terms that maximise reimbursement, or recording a diagnosis that the client does not meet in order to satisfy an insurer. This is fraud, it is a disciplinary matter, and it also corrupts the clinical record — the diagnosis written for the payer becomes the diagnosis that shapes the treatment.

4. The referral arrangement. A payment, a commission or a reciprocal arrangement for referrals. In most jurisdictions it is prohibited, and in all of them it corrupts the one decision the client depends on: that the referral was made in her interest.

5. Testimonials and advertising. Client testimonials are prohibited or restricted in most codes, because consent to treatment is not consent to be advertised and because the testimonial is a disclosure. Claims of effectiveness that cannot be supported — "guaranteed results", "the only therapist who..." — are misleading conduct, regardless of the therapist's sincerity.

6. The product sold to clients. Books, courses, supplements, retreats. There is nothing inherently wrong with a therapist writing a book; there is a serious problem with recommending to a current client a product from which the therapist profits, without disclosure and without an alternative.

7. The consultancy overlap. The therapist who is engaged by an employer to advise about an employee who is also her client. The two roles have different clients and different duties; the conflict is structural and should be declined rather than managed.

8. The unpaid work that is expected. Reports for courts, letters for schools, telephone calls with social workers: frequently unbilled and frequently essential. A practice that does not price this work either does it resentfully or refuses it at the moment the client most needs it. The remedy is a stated fee for reports and correspondence, discussed at intake (12.2a), and applied without embarrassment.

12.4a The practice that survives without exploiting anyone

Five arrangements, each of which is a decision made in advance rather than in the moment.

Price for survival. The fee is set to cover the practice's costs, the therapist's income, supervision, insurance, continuing development and a margin for the unpaid work. A fee set to be "affordable" by an anxious therapist who has not done the arithmetic becomes a fee that rises unpredictably in year three.

Build the pro bono into the plan. One or two places, budgeted, reviewed annually, publicly described in the practice's information as part of how it works.

Charge for the work that is work. Reports, court attendance, extended telephone calls and meetings with other professionals have stated fees. The client is told in advance; the alternative is a therapist who resents the letter she is writing at ten at night.

Hold a no-show policy that is fair and enforced. Twenty-four hours, with a stated exception for emergency and illness, applied consistently, and discussed clinically when it recurs.

Keep supervision in the budget. It is the last item most practices cut and the first that protects clients. A practice that cannot afford supervision cannot afford to practise.

The annual review. Once a year: what is the fee, what does it cover, what is the pro bono allocation, what is the debt, and what did I do last year that I would not defend to a colleague? The review is the practice's equivalent of the record of consent — the evidence that the arrangements were chosen.

12.4b What the client is told, in writing

The money policy belongs in the written information the client receives at intake, in one page and in plain language. Six paragraphs.

The fee. The amount, the session length, and what it covers. Payment. When it is due, and how it is made. Cancellations. The notice required, the charge, and the exceptions. The sliding scale. That places exist, that they are limited, how they are allocated, and that they are reviewed. Difficulties. That the client should tell the therapist early if payment becomes difficult, and that the therapist would rather know than discover it. Records and confidentiality. A cross-reference to the consent document, and the statement that the record may be seen by the client and may be compelled by a court.

Why the written page matters more than the spoken conversation. The client under stress remembers about one-third of what is said in a first session. The page is read at home, sometimes twice, and frequently by a partner — which is itself a disclosure the therapist should have anticipated and stated: "you may show this page to whoever you wish; it contains nothing about your treatment."

12.5 Case: the Nwankwos, the fee, the goat and the plot

The family. Chuka Nwankwo, 44, a civil engineer; Ifeoma Nwankwo, 40, a trader in fabric; children 14 and 9. They were referred by their physician after the death of Ifeoma's mother, with whom the household had lived.

The first four months. Weekly couple sessions, then fortnightly. The presenting problem was the household's grief and the conflict between Ifeoma and her husband's sister, who had moved in "to help" and stayed. The work went well by every measure that matters: the conflict reduced, the children were brought for three sessions, and the grief was named.

Month five: the job. Chuka was told his contract would not be renewed. The family's income halved. In session eighteen he said, at the end, "we may need to reduce the sessions". The therapist's response was the sentence she had practised at intake: "thank you for telling me now rather than in three months. Let us talk about what is manageable."

The agreement. Sessions fortnightly rather than weekly; the fee reduced by forty per cent for six months; a review in month eleven. Recorded in the note and in a short letter to the family. The therapist's own note recorded the reason and the review date, which is the difference between a sliding scale and a favour.

Month seven: the goat. Ifeoma arrived with a live goat, tied, on the back of a neighbour's pickup, "for the therapist's family, because of what you did for my mother before she died". The therapist's first instinct was refusal — a live animal in a city clinic — and her second was to accept immediately so as not to shame her.

What she did. She neither accepted nor refused in the doorway. She said: "this is the most generous thing anyone has brought me, and I want to talk about it properly. Can we sit down for five minutes?" Then she asked what the gift meant. Ifeoma's answer: "in my place, when somebody does something for your mother, you do not say thank you with words."

The therapist then made a decision on the category rather than on the feeling: a live animal is not a token and its value is not trivial, and it also cannot be returned without humiliation. She proposed a third option: "I will accept it, and I will not take it home. I will ask you to sell it and to put the money towards the remaining fee — which means you have paid me, in the way your mother would have understood."

Ifeoma refused, then accepted, then cried, and the transaction became the subject of the best session of the treatment: a family that had been unable to receive help without paying for it, and a therapist who had found a way to let them pay.

Month eleven: the plot. Chuka's contract was renewed. He attended alone and said that a relation had given the family a plot of land on the outskirts of the city, and that they wanted to transfer it to the therapist "as a thanks, because the marriage would have ended".

The decision. Declined, immediately and unambiguously, with the explanation that protects: "I cannot accept this. It is worth more than the whole of the treatment, and if I took it, you would always know that I had, and I would always know it too. (pause) What I would like, if you want to mark this, is a letter. Not for the file, for me."

The letter. Chuka wrote two pages. It is not in the file, it is not evidence, and it was, the therapist later said, the only gift in twenty years of practice that she had wanted.

What the case teaches.

  1. The intake sentence made month five possible. "If you ever cannot pay, tell me early" was said at the start; without it, Chuka would have cancelled the sessions without explanation and the treatment would have ended silently.
  2. The decision was made on the category, not on the feeling. The goat was a live animal of nontrivial value; the plot was an asset. The therapist's discomfort and the family's generosity were both real and neither was the criterion.
  3. The third option is usually available. Accepting and refusing were not the only choices; "sell it and put the money towards the fee" preserved the meaning, protected the frame and gave the family a way to pay that their own culture recognised.
  4. The conversation about the gift was the therapy. Both gifts arrived at moments when the family was working out whether help could be received without debt — which was, in retrospect, the family's central problem and the reason the sister-in-law had stayed for two years.
  5. The refusal of the plot was the more generous act. The therapist who accepted it would have been, from that day, a person to whom the family owed everything, and the therapy would have ended in a way nobody could describe.

12.6 Transcript: the fee conversation at month two of non-payment

A different case, from the therapist's own practice log. Mrs Bello, 33, has attended eight sessions and has paid for three. Minutes twelve to twenty-four.

F1 Therapist: I want to raise something and I have been putting it off, which I am telling you because it is true. — The confession of reluctance, which disarms the accusation before it lands. F2 Client: The money. F3 Therapist: The money. (pause) Three sessions have been paid for and eight have happened. — The facts, without an adjective. "Eight have happened" rather than "you owe me for five" — the same information, without the debt-collector's grammar. F4 Client: I am sorry. I am so sorry. I will bring it next week. F5 Therapist: (pause) You have said that twice. (pause) I am not asking for an apology, and I am not going to pretend I have not noticed. — The pattern named, gently, and the apology declined, because the apology is the thing that prevents the conversation. F6 Client: (long pause) My husband controls the account. F7 Therapist: (pause) Tell me about that. — Three words. The fee conversation has just become the clinical material, which is what the concept in 12.2 predicted and what the therapist was waiting for. F8 Client: He gives me money for the house. If I ask for more he wants to know what for. He does not know I am coming here. F9 Therapist: So every session you have attended has been paid for out of — what? F10 Client: Food money. I take a little. F11 Therapist: (pause) So you have been buying your therapy with your children's food money, and you have not been able to tell me. — The reflection that names the cost without moralising.
