The client's face, recorded in the note, changed. F12 Client: (crying) I did not want you to think I was like that. F13 Therapist: Like what? F14 Client: Somebody who does not pay. F15 Therapist: (pause) We are going to change this today, and it is not going to be you paying more. (pause) First, I want to know whether coming here is safe for you. — The pivot that matters: the fee has revealed a safety question, and the question takes priority over the account.

What the transcript shows. The therapist raised the money, named the pattern, declined the apology, and then followed the material wherever it went. Within eleven minutes of the fee conversation, the case had become a case about financial control — which had been in the room for eight sessions and had not been visible because nobody had mentioned the money.

The outcome. The fee was waived for three months while a safety plan was made; the client's attendance was recorded as "no charge, agreed for safety reasons, reviewed monthly"; and the financial control became the central subject of the treatment. The therapist also changed her intake: the question "who knows that you are coming here?" now appears in the first session of every case.

12.6a The five sentences of the money conversation

Students ask for a script, and a script of five sentences is more useful than a policy document.

  1. "I want to raise something about money, and I have been putting it off." Every difficult conversation in this chapter begins with the therapist admitting the reluctance, which lowers the client's fear of being judged.
  2. "The facts are these." The number of sessions, the number paid, the amount outstanding. No adjectives.
  3. "What is happening?" A question about the client's situation rather than about her conduct, which is where the clinical material lives.
  4. "Here is what I can do, and here is what I cannot." The options, stated as the therapist's capacity rather than as the client's failure.
  5. "Whatever we agree, we will write it down and we will look at it again on [date]." The date that converts an arrangement into a plan.

What the five sentences do not include. An apology for asking, an explanation of the therapist's overheads, an account of how hard the therapist works, or a reassurance that the client is not the first person to do this. Each of those makes the exchange about the therapist.

The rehearsal. Practise the five sentences aloud before the session in which they will be needed. Therapists who have not said them aloud once will soften them in the room, and softening is what produces the ad hoc arrangement that becomes a resentment.

12.7 Laboratory: the fee policy and the gift decision

Part A — write the practice's money policy (thirty minutes). One page, six headings: fee and what it covers; payment mechanics; cancellation; sliding scale and how a place is allocated; pro bono allocation; debt and its limits. Then the group exchanges policies and each student applies another practice's policy to three cases. The exercise reliably produces the discovery that most policies cannot be applied by anyone other than their author, which is the definition of a policy that is really a preference.

Part B — the gift sort (fifteen minutes). Eight gifts, sorted into the four categories of 12.3, with a decision and a sentence for each.

GiftCategoryLikely decision
A bowl of fruit at the end of treatmentToken/transitionalAccept, record
A wrapped watch at session fourExpensive, earlyDecline
A live chicken, delivered to the clinicToken with practical problemsDiscuss, propose a third option
A child's drawingTransitionalAccept, display or keep
Money in an envelope after a good sessionNever acceptableDecline, discuss the meaning
A client's business stock as paymentBarterDiscuss, usually decline
A gift from a client in a controlling marriageCategory four — the string is the controlDecline, and assess safety
The offer to renovate the clinicExpensive, institutionalDecline, or refer to the practice's owner with disclosure

Part C — the refusal script (ten minutes). Each student writes three sentences for declining a watch, and reads them aloud. The group marks them on two criteria: dignity preserved, and no lecture.

Marks (20 total). Policy: applicable by a stranger (5), covers all six headings (5), states limits (5). Gift decisions: correct category for each and a defensible decision with a sentence (5).

The observation to make in the debrief. The best refusal scripts in the room will be the shortest, and every student will initially write one that explains the ethics of gifts to the client.

12.8 Six errors

Error 1 — Never mentioning the fee again after intake. The client who is struggling has to raise it herself, which the client least able to raise it will not do. The therapist must raise it, and the permission to do so was given at intake.

Error 2 — The ad hoc reduction. A lower fee granted in the room, to a client who asked persuasively, with no review and no record. It is discovered, it is resented, and it is unfair to every client who did not ask.

Error 3 — Chasing a debt by letter. The letter is appropriate at the end of a process, not at the beginning. Sent early, it converts a clinical relationship into a commercial one, and the client who was ashamed becomes frightened.

Error 4 — Accepting an expensive gift to avoid embarrassment. The therapist's discomfort is being paid for with a distortion of the relationship, and the client now holds something over her.

Error 5 — Billing a diagnosis the client does not meet. Fraud, and a corruption of the record that outlives the claim.

Error 6 — Subsidising the practice with the therapist's own unpaid labour, silently. It works for a year, then produces resentment, a sudden fee increase, or a withdrawal from the work that looks to the client like abandonment.

12.9 Culture, faith and the meaning of money

The gift that cannot be refused. In many of the households this book serves, a gift is the language in which a relationship is acknowledged, and its refusal is a statement that the relationship is not accepted. A therapist who declines everything on principle will be described as "proud" and will be told, by the family, that she has "refused their hand". The workable practice — clear in the therapist's own mind and sometimes stated aloud — is that tokens are accepted and assets are not, and that the distinction is explained in the family's terms: "a bowl of fruit is between friends; a plot of land would make me something else."

Payment in kind. Where cash is scarce and goods are not — fabric, food, a service, labour — the family may offer payment in kind without embarrassment. The rules of 12.3a apply, and the cultural reality argues for a narrower rather than a wider permission: the value is usually uncertain, the arrangement is usually permanent once established, and the therapist's judgement about the client's work contaminates the treatment.

The man who pays. In many households the husband pays for his wife's therapy and will want to know what it is for, which raises the Week 3 problem in a financial form. The practitioner's policy: the fee is agreed with the person who pays, the content is not discussed with anyone, and the arrangement is stated at intake — "your husband will be told that you are attending and what it costs, and nothing else, unless you ask me to tell him more."

The client who cannot pay and the therapist who is a member of the same community. The therapist's own obligations — to a church, a mosque, a family, an association — may include expectations of free help, and refusing them can be costly. The professional answer is a stated policy, applied to everyone: "I have two reduced places and both are taken; I can refer you, or you can wait for a place to come free." Said as a policy rather than as a judgement about the person, it is usually accepted.

Wealth, class and the fee. A therapist from a different class position from her client will misjudge what is affordable in both directions. The correct method is to ask, explicitly, and to believe the answer: "what would be manageable for you?" A client who says two thousand naira means two thousand naira, not a figure that the therapist thinks is realistic.

The religious objection to charging. Some clients, and some religious traditions, regard payment for counsel as improper — "you cannot sell the word of God". The therapist's answer is not a lecture on professional economics but an explanation of what the fee pays for, in one sentence: "the fee pays for the hour you have, the training behind it, and the fact that I can be here next week."

DEBATE — "Therapists should charge what the market will bear, and give pro bono time as they choose." Argue for: viability is an ethical precondition, the market allocates scarce skill, and discretionary generosity is genuine generosity. Argue against: the market allocates to those who can pay, discretionary charity produces inconsistency and resentment, and a profession that prices by the market has abandoned the poorer half of its obligation. Then set out a fee policy you could defend to both a client who cannot pay and a colleague who thinks you are under-charging.

12.10 Evidence note

What is known. Research on psychotherapy fees is limited but consistent on two points: fee arrangements affect the therapeutic relationship, and therapists' discomfort with discussing fees leads to avoidance which clients experience as distance. Studies of gift-giving in therapy find that gifts are common, that clinicians report uncertainty about them, and that gifts are frequently meaningful communications about the relationship rather than attempts to influence. In medical and psychotherapy ethics, financial conflicts of interest are among the most consistently documented sources of harm, largely through subtle changes in clinical decisions rather than overt fraud.

What is weaker. There is very little research on sliding scales, on barter, or on the outcomes of non-payment policies. The guidance in this week is derived from ethics and from practice rather than from controlled study.

What is not supported. That charging a higher fee produces better outcomes. That accepting a gift always damages the relationship — modest, culturally appropriate gifts are usually experienced as affirming. And that a therapist's financial interest does not affect her clinical judgement; the research on conflicts of interest suggests it reliably does, while the clinician reliably believes it does not.

The matrix cell. Development is probabilistic and non-linear; the same input produces different outcomes depending on timing and context. The same reduced fee, agreed at intake and agreed in month nine after three unpaid sessions, is a different arrangement with a different meaning.

12.10a The audit of your own practice

Once a year, five questions, written down, kept with the supervision record.

  1. What is my fee, and what does it pay for? If the answer cannot be given in one sentence, the fee is inherited rather than set.
  2. How many reduced places do I hold, and how were they allocated? If the answer is "whoever asked", the scale is a favour.
  3. What did I accept last year, and what did I refuse, and would I defend each decision to a colleague? The question is not whether the decisions were right but whether they were decisions.
  4. How much did I leave unbilled, and what did that do to my attitude to those clients? The honest answer is frequently "I began to avoid them", which is the clinical cost of unbilled work.
  5. What did I do for money last year that I would not want a client to know about? A consultancy, an endorsement, a referral arrangement, a discounted package sold to a distressed family. One question, once a year, and it is the one that keeps a practice honest.

What to do with the answers. Take them to supervision, not to a marketing plan. The audit's purpose is not growth; it is the discovery of the places where financial pressure and clinical judgement have begun to converge.

12.11 Key terms

Frame — the agreed arrangements of time, place, frequency, duration and payment that constitute the treatment.

Sliding scale — a published schedule of reduced fees with stated criteria and review.

Pro bono — treatment provided without fee, budgeted as part of the practice.

Barter — exchange of goods or services for treatment, permitted only in narrow documented conditions.

Conflict of interest — a financial or personal interest that could influence clinical judgement.

Upcoding — describing a service or diagnosis inaccurately to obtain higher reimbursement; fraud.

Testimonial — a client's public statement about a therapist; restricted or prohibited in most codes.

12.12 Self-test

  1. Why is the fee clinical material rather than an administrative detail?
  2. Give the six statements of the intake fee conversation.
  3. State the four-step sequence for non-payment, with the sentence for each stage.
  4. Name the four categories of gift and the default for each.
  5. Give the five steps of the gift procedure.
  6. What are the conditions under which barter may be permissible, and the specific hazard?
  7. Name five business pressures that produce unethical practice.
  8. In the Nwankwo case, what was the third option for the goat, and why was it better than accepting or refusing?

Answers. (1) Because it constitutes the frame that makes the relationship professional, and because it carries the client's history of entitlement, worth, shame and dependency, which becomes treatable when it is named. (2) The fee and session length; what the fee includes; when and how it is paid; the cancellation policy; the sliding scale; and what happens if payment becomes difficult. (3) First missed payment — "I noticed the last two sessions were not paid; is something happening?"; second — the pattern named, "you have not mentioned it, and I wonder whether it is difficult to talk about"; third — the limit, with four options; fourth — the decision, recorded. (4) Cultural token — accept and record; transitional — usually accept, discuss the meaning; expensive — decline; with strings — decline and name the string. (5) Notice; ask what it means; decide on the category; say the decision once, warmly; record it. (6) Client initiates, value agreed in writing, time-limited, supervised and recorded; the hazard is that the client's service becomes clinical material and a second arena in which she can fail. (7) Package discounts; productivity targets; upcoding; referral payments; testimonials and advertising claims; products sold to clients; the consultancy overlap; unbilled work. (8) Accept it and ask the family to sell it and put the money towards the fee — preserving the meaning of the gift, avoiding an asset transfer, and recognising the family's own form of payment.

12.12a Three sentences to keep in the drawer

"Tell me early." Said at intake and repeated at every review, it is the sentence that prevents the silent ending.

"What would it mean to you if I accepted this?" The gift question that converts an object into a conversation.

"We will write it down and look at it again on [date]." The sentence that turns generosity into a professional arrangement, and the one that protects both parties from the drift into gratitude and resentment.

12.13 Reflective task

Write two hundred and fifty words on your own money history: what you were taught about asking to be paid, what you were taught about accepting gifts, and which of those lessons is most likely to shape your practice. Then name one rule you will write down before you see your first paying client.

12.14 Further reading

  • Any current text on the business of private practice in your jurisdiction — read for the arithmetic, not the marketing.
  • Pope, K. and Vasquez, M. on gifts, fees and barter in psychotherapy — the standard treatment of the ethical questions.
  • The professional code sections on fees, gifts, barter and advertising, read in the original wording.
  • The literature on conflicts of interest in medicine — the evidence that financial interest shapes judgement while clinicians believe it does not.
  • Your indemnity provider's guidance on fees, debt recovery and court attendance charges.

Next: Week 13 — Ethics of research, assessment and diagnosis.

Week 13 — Ethics of research, assessment and diagnosis

Learning outcomes. By the end of this week the student can (1) obtain valid consent for research with families, including assent from children and the problem of group consent; (2) write about a client without identifying her, and state the limits of disguise; (3) describe the consequences of a diagnosis and decide what she can defend; (4) select and interpret an assessment instrument with attention to its validity for the population in front of her; and (5) explain the ethical difference between the clinician-researcher and the researcher who happens to be a clinician.

Three domains, one theme

Research, assessment and diagnosis are usually taught in three separate weeks and they share a single ethical structure: each of them gives the therapist power to define a person. The researcher defines what will be known about a family and who will read it. The assessor defines what will be counted as a problem and how it will be scored. The diagnostician defines what the person is, in a category that will follow her into an insurance form, a school file, a custody hearing and her own understanding of herself.

That power is not a reason to avoid the three activities; a profession that does not research, assess and diagnose is not a profession. It is the reason each of them requires a specific discipline.

DomainThe dutyThe characteristic failure
ResearchVoluntary, informed, revocable consent; protection of the vulnerable; honest reportingThe family "agrees" because the therapist asked; children's assent treated as unnecessary; the negative result not published
AssessmentCompetence to administer; validity for the population; feedback to the clientAn instrument normed on another population, interpreted as though it measured the same thing
DiagnosisDefensible, useful, communicated honestly; benefits weighed against stigmaA diagnosis given for the insurer, the school or the court rather than for the client

13.1 The lecture in ninety minutes

MinutesContentTeaching form
0–8The single theme: power to defineThree examples, students identify who was defined
8–22Five concepts: consent in families, the diagnostic act, cultural validity, the assessed client, the writing therapistLecture
22–35Consent with families: the father problem, the child's assent, withdrawal by one memberTwo ethics applications, compared
35–50Writing about clients: consent, disguise, the small-community problemStudents disguise a real case in twenty minutes; group tries to identify it
50–62Diagnosis: consequences and defensibilityFour diagnoses, students argue each is or is not defensible
62–75Instruments: validity, qualification, feedbackThree instruments presented; students ask the five questions
75–85The clinician-researcher: recruitment, dual role, authorshipCase of a thesis that went wrong
85–90The Amaka caseStudents decide whether the study proceeds

13.2 Five concepts that organise the week

Concept 1 — Consent in a family is not one person's signature. A father can consent to his own participation and cannot consent to his wife's or his children's. A child's assent is required in addition to a parent's consent where the child is old enough to understand, and the child's refusal should be respected even where the law does not require it. A mother who consents under pressure from her husband has not consented. The research consent process in family work is therefore slower, more documented and more individual than in individual research.

Concept 2 — Diagnosis is an act with consequences, and the consequences are distributed. The same diagnosis may give one person access to treatment, mark another as unsuitable for a job, settle a custody question, and change how a family talks about a member for the rest of his life. Diagnosing is not wrong; diagnosing without weighing the consequences is.

Concept 3 — An instrument measures what it was normed to measure, in the population it was normed on. A questionnaire developed and validated on one population, translated and used on another, may measure something different or nothing at all. The ethical problem is not the use of a tool; it is acting on a score whose meaning in this population is unknown.

Concept 4 — The assessment has a client, and it is not always the person being assessed. In family work the assessor must state whose question the assessment answers. A child assessed at a school's request, a couple assessed for a court, a father assessed for an insurer: each has a different client, a different consent requirement and a different limit on what can be reported back.

Concept 5 — The therapist who writes is still a therapist. Case reports, theses and teaching material are written by people who know their subjects personally. The obligations are consent, disguise, proportionality (writing only what the purpose requires), and — in small communities — a willingness not to write at all.

13.2a Research consent with families: six problems and their solutions

Problem 1 — the father signs for everyone. Solution: separate consent forms for each adult; an assent form for each child old enough to read one; a note of who was present when each was signed; and no recruitment in a session where one member is dominant.

Problem 2 — the therapist recruits her own clients. Solution: recruitment by a third party who is not the treating clinician, or a clear statement in the invitation that declining will not affect treatment, made by someone other than the therapist. Where the therapist must recruit, the invitation is written, the client is given a week, and the therapist does not ask again.

Problem 3 — one member wants to withdraw. Solution: a stated policy at the outset. In most family studies the unit is the family, and the withdrawal of one member means the family's data are withdrawn; where the design permits partial participation, that is explained in advance. The member who withdraws must not be told by the researcher that they have "ruined" the study.

Problem 4 — children's assent. Solution: an age-appropriate explanation, a written or verbal assent, and the child's right to stop at any point without anyone being told why. Children are frequently the most reliable participants on this question and the least often asked.

Problem 5 — confidentiality inside the research team. Solution: stated at consent: who will see the data, whether transcripts will be shared, how identifiers will be removed, and where data will be stored. A family consenting to research has not consented to being discussed by a research team in a seminar.

Problem 6 — the participant in crisis. Solution: a written procedure for what happens if a participant discloses risk during data collection: the interview stops, the participant is supported, the clinical pathway is followed, and the researcher's duty is stated in the consent form. A researcher who is also a therapist must know which role she is in at the moment of disclosure, and the participant should have been told in advance.

The document that results. A consent form of two pages for adults, one page for children, an information sheet in plain language, and a procedure for the crisis. Four documents, and none of them is optional.

13.2b Writing about clients: consent, disguise and the limits of disguise

Consent to publish. Where a case is written up, the client's consent is obtained in writing, and it is consent to the specific text, not to "use my case". The client reads the text, or hears it read, and may require changes. Where the client is a child, the parent's consent and the child's assent; where the client has died, the family's consent and additional care, because the dead cannot be embarrassed and the living can.

Disguise, and the four things it cannot protect. Changing the name, the occupation, the town and the ages is standard and insufficient in three situations. Small communities: a therapist in a town of thirty thousand is writing about someone her colleagues will recognise from the presenting problem. Rare presentations: a family with a specific configuration — four children, a father in the merchant navy, a mother with a particular diagnosis — is identifiable no matter how the details are changed. Public events: a case that was in the newspapers is not disguisable. Digital traces: a case that has been discussed online may be found by searching the details.

The proportionality rule. Write only what the teaching purpose requires. A case report does not need the family's immigration status, their religion, their income or the detail of the abuse in order to teach the decision. The most common ethical failure in student case reports is excess detail, which is usually written because the detail is interesting rather than because it is necessary.

The composite case. Where disguise is impossible, a composite — several cases combined into one, with a statement that it is a composite — is acceptable in teaching and unacceptable where the composite is presented as a single case with its own factual integrity. The statement must be explicit: "this case is a composite, constructed for teaching, and does not describe any single family."

The student's thesis, and the supervisor's role. The supervisor is responsible for the ethics application and for reading the case material before submission. A thesis containing a recognisable account of a real family is a failure of supervision before it is a failure of the student.

13.2c The assessed client: whose question does the assessment answer?

An assessment is always conducted for someone. Naming that person determines the consent, the feedback and the limits, and the failure to name them is the commonest ethical error in assessment work.

The four possible clients of an assessment.

Who askedConsent requiredWhat may be fed back
The person being assessedHer ownEverything, in a form she can use
The family, about one memberThe member's, plus the family's for the referral questionThe formulation, not the raw material
A school or employerThe person's written consent, and only for the agreed purposeWhat the purpose requires, and nothing more
A courtThe person's consent where possible, or the court's orderThe report as instructed, with its limits stated

The distortion that follows from not naming the client. The therapist drifts towards the interests of whoever commissioned the work. A child assessed because the school wants a diagnosis is assessed in a way that finds one; a father assessed because his wife's lawyer requested it is assessed in a way that finds what the lawyer is looking for, or — in the therapist's attempt to be fair — in a way that under-reports what is there. Naming the client is the discipline that prevents the drift, and stating it in the report is what allows a reader to judge the work.

The feedback conversation, which is part of the assessment. Three questions: what was measured, what the result means, and what it does not mean. The client who is told a score without the third element will carry a number as though it were a verdict. "Your score is in the range that suggests moderate depression. That tells us the severity is significant enough to treat. It does not tell us the cause, and it does not tell us how long it will last."

The assessment that should not be done. Where the question belongs to a third party and answering it would harm the person assessed — a family who wants an assessment to prove that a daughter is "not really ill", an employer who wants an assessment of an employee's loyalty — the therapist's answer is that the question is not one she can answer, and the reason is stated.

13.3 Diagnosis: what it does, and when it is defensible

What a diagnosis does, in four directions.

  1. To the client: it names the experience, which can relieve ("there is a name for this"), and it can imprison ("I am a borderline"). The same act produces both, and the way it is communicated determines which.
  2. To the family: it reorganises the household's explanation. A diagnosis of a child's condition may relieve the mother of blame or transfer it to her; a diagnosis of a father's depression may legitimise his withdrawal or confirm the family's view that he is weak.
  3. To the system: it opens the door to treatment, insurance, school support and legal protection — and it closes others. A diagnosis on a record follows a person into employment, custody and immigration decisions.
  4. To the therapist: it organises the treatment and, if it is wrong, organises it in the wrong direction for years. The diagnostic act is a commitment, not a label.

The four questions before making a diagnosis.

  1. Can I defend it? Is there enough evidence, gathered with the right method, to support it if challenged by a colleague, a regulator or a court?
  2. Is it mine to make? Does it lie within my competence and my licence? A family therapist may recognise depression and may not be qualified to diagnose a psychotic disorder (Week 7).
  3. What will it do for this client? Access to treatment, an explanation, a school's support — or stigma, a record, a family's certainty that the problem is located in one person?
  4. What will it do to this family's story? Diagnosing the identified patient in a family system confirms the family's belief that the problem is in that person, which is frequently the belief that has been preventing change.

The relational alternative, and its limit. In family work the formulation is frequently relational rather than individual: "this family's difficulty is in how it has organised itself around a loss", rather than "this mother is depressed". The relational formulation is often more useful and is not a substitute for a diagnosis where a diagnosis is required for care. The competent practice uses both: a diagnosis where the system requires one, and a formulation that locates the problem in the relationships where it actually lives.

The diagnosis given for a third party. "I need a diagnosis for the insurance." "The school will not act without a diagnosis." "My lawyer says a diagnosis will help in court." Each is a pressure to write a category the client may not meet, for a purpose that is not clinical. The defensible position: a diagnosis is given if it is accurate; where a letter is needed for a purpose, the letter states what is observed and what is needed, without inventing a category. Where the third party will not accept that, the therapist declines and says why.

Communication of a diagnosis, in four sentences. What it is, in plain language; what it is not; what it means for the treatment; and what it does not change about the person. "This is a name for a pattern of low mood that has lasted more than two months and is affecting your sleep, your appetite and your work. It is not a description of who you are, and it is not permanent. It tells me what treatment is likely to help. It does not change anything about what you are able to do."

13.3a Assessment instruments: six questions before you use one

1. Was it validated for this population? Not translated — validated. A translation with no psychometric work is a new instrument with unknown properties, and reporting its scores as though they meant what they mean in the original population is a methodological and an ethical error.

2. Was it normed on people like this client? Age, education, language, urban or rural, clinical or community sample. A cut-off derived from a clinical sample will classify a community sample as abnormal.

3. Am I qualified to administer and interpret it? Some instruments require specific training; all require competence in interpretation. Administering an instrument one cannot interpret is worse than not using one.

4. What will I do with the result? If the answer is "nothing that I would not do otherwise", the assessment is being done for the therapist's reassurance or for a file. If the answer is "it will change the treatment", the result must be fed back to the client.

5. How will I give the result back? The client is entitled to know what was measured, what the score means, what its limits are, and what happens next. Feedback is part of the assessment, not a courtesy.

6. Where does the raw data go, and for how long? Test data are clinical records: stored, retained and disclosed under the same rules (Week 11). Protocols are not kept in a drawer indefinitely, and they are not shown to anyone outside the treatment without consent.

The family-specific problem. Many instruments are designed for individuals and used on families by summing scores, which produces a number that means nothing. Family assessment requires either an instrument designed for family-level constructs (cohesion, adaptability, communication) or an explicit statement that the scores describe individuals within the family and not the family as a unit.

The one instrument most family therapists do use. The outcome measure, completed at each session or at intervals, which is also an intervention: it gives the client a voice in the treatment and gives the therapist feedback on deterioration. Its ethical requirements are the same as any other — validity for the population, explanation of the purpose, and a stated response when the score indicates deterioration.

13.3b The diagnosis of a child, and the family that receives it

The most consequential diagnostic act in family work, because it lands on a person who did not ask for it, in a system that will reorganise around it.

The four questions, applied to a child. Can I defend it? With a child, the evidence must come from more than one setting — home and school — and from more than one informant, because a child's behaviour in a clinic is a sample of one context. Is it mine to make? Many child diagnoses require specialist assessment; the family therapist's task is recognition and referral (Week 7). What will it do for the child? Access to support in school, a treatment pathway, an explanation for the parents — or a label that follows him through school records. What will it do to the family's story? A diagnosis of a child's condition can relieve the parents of blame, which is sometimes a kindness and sometimes a way of not examining a household in which the child is frightened.

The conversation with the parents. Five elements: what the assessment found; what it did not find; what the diagnosis means and does not mean; what the parents can do that will help; and who else will be told, and what they will be told. The fifth element is the one most often omitted and the one the parents most need, because the diagnosis will travel — to the school, the church, the extended family — and the parents will be the ones managing its journey.

The conversation with the child. Age-appropriate, brief, and about function rather than category. A nine-year-old does not need the word; he needs to know that "your brain works in a way that makes sitting still hard, and there are things that help, and it is not because you are bad." A fifteen-year-old may want the word, the explanation of what it means, and the assurance that it is not the whole of him.

The label that becomes the family's explanation. "He is like that because of his condition" is a sentence that relieves and closes at the same time. The therapist's work, over months, is to hold both truths: the condition is real and the household's patterns still matter. That is the family therapist's particular contribution to a diagnostic case, and it is the reason a diagnosis alone is never a formulation.

13.3c The diagnosis as a family event

When a diagnosis enters a household it does three things at once, and the therapist should anticipate all three.

It assigns a location to the problem. The difficulty now lives in one person. That is frequently the family's preference and frequently the reason change is difficult: if the problem is in her, the rest of the household has nothing to change. The family therapist's contribution is to accept the diagnosis and to keep the household in the formulation: "the depression is real and it is in her. And a household in which nobody speaks before noon is part of what is keeping it going."

It redistributes responsibility. A diagnosis can relieve a mother of blame ("it is an illness, not my parenting") and can transfer it to her ("it is genetic, from your side"). Both movements happen in the same session and both are heard by the person diagnosed.

It changes the family's language. Members begin to use the diagnosis as an explanation, a weapon and a prediction: "you cannot help it", "it is your condition", "you will always be like this". The therapist listens for the third use, because it is the one that forecloses change, and names it: "when you say she will always be like this, what does that do to her?"

What the therapist does with all three. Assess the diagnosis' accuracy; communicate it in the four-sentence form; then run one session, early, on what the family now believes about it. That session is frequently worth more than six months of work on the symptoms, because it determines whether the household will support the treatment or organise itself around the label.

13.4 The clinician-researcher: the thesis, the client and the dual role

The MFT 770 thesis requirement means that every student on this programme will conduct research, frequently while seeing clients, and very often on material that arises from their own practice. The dual role is the week's most practical problem.

The four ways the dual role distorts research.

Recruitment bias: the clients who agree are the ones who like the therapist, and they know the therapist's preferences. Consent under influence: the client who is asked by the person she depends on cannot truly refuse. Interpretation bias: the therapist-researcher sees her own clinical decisions confirmed by the data. Role confusion at the moment of disclosure: the participant says something that activates a duty, and the researcher must decide which hat she is wearing, in the middle of an interview.

The four controls.

  1. Recruitment by a third party wherever possible, and where not, a written invitation delivered by someone else and a stated cooling-off period.
  2. A written statement of what the researcher will do if the participant discloses risk, agreed with the ethics committee and stated in the information sheet.
  3. Analysis by someone other than the interviewer, or at least a second coder who has no clinical relationship with the participants.
  4. Supervision of the dual role, in which the student discusses the specific ethical events of the study as they happen, and a supervisor who reads the transcripts.

The ethics application, in five sections. Purpose, participants, consent, data, risks. The section that is most often thin is risks — not risks to participants' bodies, which are usually minimal in this kind of research, but risks to their relationships and reputations: a couple who both participate may disagree about what has been said; a transcript stored on a laptop may be read by a partner; a quotation may be recognised by the family. Each of these is a risk the application must name and mitigate.

Authorship and honesty. The student's thesis is the student's; the supervisor's contribution is acknowledged according to the convention of the institution. Data are not fabricated, not selectively reported, and not discarded because they do not support the hypothesis. The negative finding is a finding and is reported. A student who has learned to write up an unexpected result honestly has learned the whole of research ethics.

13.4a The seven-step research ethics check

A compressed procedure for the student designing the MFT 770 study, to be run before writing the application.

  1. Whose question is it? The researcher's, the supervisor's, or a community's. If it is the researcher's alone, the study should say what it gives back.
  2. Who is vulnerable here, and in what way? Not "vulnerable populations" in the abstract, but the specific people in this sample: the woman whose husband controls the household, the child who is asked about his parents, the couple in acute crisis.
  3. How will consent actually be obtained, in what language, by whom? If the answer involves the treating therapist, the design has a problem.
  4. What are the relational risks? What could be said in an interview that would be repeated at home, and what would happen then?
  5. What is the plan if a participant discloses risk? Written, with names and numbers, and rehearsed.
  6. What will be published, and who could be identified by it? Including the third parties who never consented.
  7. What does this community get? A returned report, a training session, a copy of the thesis in the local library, a co-author from the setting. If the answer is "nothing", the study is extractive and the ethics committee may still approve it, which does not make it right.

The two questions that predict trouble. Question 3, because the dual role lives there, and question 6, because it is the one the student has not thought about and the one the supervisor will be asked about later.

13.5 Case: Amaka's study, and the participant who was recognisable

The student. Amaka, a candidate on this programme, is conducting her MFT 770 study on "the experience of forgiveness in couples after infidelity". She has recruited eight couples: three from her own caseload, five through a church announcement.

The design. Semi-structured interviews of ninety minutes with each partner separately, audio recorded, verbatim transcripts, thematic analysis, with the church's permission to announce the study.

The three problems that emerged.

Problem one, at recruitment. One of her own clients, Mr Osagie, offered to participate "because you have done so much for us". Amaka's first instinct was to accept, and the ethics application had specified that clients in current treatment would not be recruited. She declined, and the client experienced it as rejection; the next session was spent on that, which was the right clinical decision and a cost of the design.

Problem two, at interview. During Mrs Osagie's interview (she participated through the church route, not through Amaka), she described her husband's affair in a form of words identical to the account Amaka had heard in the couple session, and added a detail — the other woman's workplace — that Amaka already knew from the clinical file. Amaka stopped the recording and said, "I want to remind you that this is research, and that what you say here goes into the study and not into our sessions. Also, I already know some of this from our work, and I need you to know that I am not going to use anything in the study that I did not hear here, today, in this interview." She recorded that statement in the note and in the study log.

Problem three, at analysis. In the draft chapter, a quotation read: "She worked in the same bank as him. I used to see her at the Christmas party." With the ages, the city and the church, the couple would be identifiable to anyone in their congregation who read the thesis, and the thesis would be in the university library.

What Amaka did.

  1. She removed the detail and replaced it with "she worked at his office", which preserved the meaning (proximity) and removed the identifier.
  2. She changed the town in every extract, not only in the demographic table.
  3. She wrote to both partners with the three quotations she intended to use, and asked whether they were content. Mr Osagie asked for one to be removed. It was removed, and Amaka noted the change.
  4. She added a paragraph to the methodology describing the measures taken and their limits: "no disguise can guarantee anonymity in a small community; the participants were told this, and the extracts used were approved by them."

The supervisor's question, which produced the case. "If the other woman read this thesis, what would she know?" Amaka had not considered her — the third party, who has not consented to anything.

What the case teaches.

  1. The dual role has a specific failure point, which is recruitment. The application's prohibition was right and its cost — a difficult session — was the price of doing it properly.
  2. The moment of overlap between the clinical and the research roles must be handled out loud. Amaka's statement in the interview was not required by any code; it was the honest management of a boundary that the participant could not have known was there.
  3. Consent to be studied is not consent to be recognised. Sending the quotations to the participants is unusual and is the highest standard available.
  4. The third party is the person everyone forgets. The affair partner, the child, the grandparent in the transcript: none of them consented to appear in a thesis, and all of them may be identifiable.

13.6 Transcript: the consent conversation for a case report

A therapist asks a former client for permission to write up the treatment for a teaching chapter. Minutes eight to twenty-two.

T1 Therapist: I am writing a chapter for students about the kind of work we did, and I would like to use your case. (pause) Before you answer, I am going to tell you exactly what that would involve, and I want you to know that you can say no and it will change nothing between us. — The request, followed immediately by the two things that make it consent rather than pressure: the information and the right of refusal. T2 Client: What would you write? T3 Therapist: The decisions I made and why, and something of what you were dealing with, so that students can learn from it. Not your name, not your town, not your job as it is, not the names of anyone in your family. — The scope, stated positively and negatively, before the document is shown. T4 Client: But you would show it to people. T5 Therapist: It would be published, yes. (pause) That is the part I want you to think about properly, not answer now. (pause) Anyone could read it, including people who know you. — The risk named plainly, including the one the therapist would rather not mention: the possibility of recognition. T6 Client: Would they know it was me? T7 Therapist: They might. (pause) I will change the details, and I cannot promise that. Your church, your children's ages, the thing that brought you in — those together are quite specific, and I would rather tell you that than pretend the changes are a guarantee. — The honest answer to the question that decides the case. A therapist who says "no one will know" has obtained a consent that is not informed. T8 Client: (pause) If I said no, would you be upset? T9 Therapist: I would be disappointed and I would not be upset with you, and I would not mention it again. (pause) That is the difference between asking and pressuring. — The distinction stated because the client cannot know it otherwise, and because the therapist's disappointment is precisely what she must not transmit. T10 Client: Can I read it first? T11 Therapist: Yes. I will send you the text before it goes anywhere, and you can strike anything you want, and I will tell the editor that you have seen it. — The offer that converts the consent from consent-to-use into consent-to-publish-this-text, which is the standard the codes describe and which few therapists actually meet. T12 Client: (pause) And after — could I say later that I have changed my mind? T13 Therapist: Before it is published, yes. After, no — it will be out. And if you asked me before publication to stop, I would stop. — The limits of revocability, stated precisely. "I would stop" is a promise the therapist can keep and the client can rely on. T14 Client: (long pause) All right. Send it to me.

What the transcript shows. Six of the seven therapist turns give the client something that makes refusal possible: the scope, the risk, the honest answer about recognition, the assurance about disappointment, the right to read the text, and the right to withdraw before publication. Consent obtained without those six things is a signature, not a consent.

What happened afterwards. The client struck one sentence — a detail about her mother — and asked that the town be changed to a different region. Both were done. The published chapter carries a footnote: "details have been changed and the client has read and approved this account."

13.7 Laboratory: the ethics application and the disguised case

Part A — the application (forty minutes). In groups, write the five sections of an ethics application for this study: "the experience of disclosure of HIV status within couples, recruited through a clinic in [state]". Each group must include: the consent documents; the recruitment route; the plan for a disclosure of risk during an interview; the data storage and destruction plan; and the specific relational risks to participants.

Part B — the disguise exercise (twenty-five minutes). Each student writes 250 words about a case, disguised, and reads it aloud. The group then tries to identify the family from the details given. The exercise is uncomfortable and instructive: almost every first draft is identifiable.

Marks (20).

Criterion0–12–34–5
ConsentOne formSeparate formsSeparate forms for adults and children, with assent and withdrawal stated
Risk planNoneRisk mentionedDisclosure procedure specific, with roles named
DisguiseNot attemptedNames changedMultiple details changed, third parties protected, limits acknowledged
DataNot addressedStorage mentionedStorage, access, retention and destruction specified
HonestyOverclaims anonymitySome cautionAnonymous where possible, and the limits stated to participants

The observation for the debrief. No group's first draft will protect the third party — the affair partner, the grandmother, the sibling who appears in one sentence. That omission is the lesson.

13.7a Four research designs and the ethics of each

The design determines which ethical problems will arise, and students choosing a design are also choosing their ethical work.

1. The interview study. Risk: the disclosure of distressing material with no clinical container; the repetition of harm in a research interview with a survivor. Mitigation: the interviewer's training, a stated procedure if distress occurs, a debrief, and the contact details of a service.

2. The survey. Risk: consent that is a click; items that are intrusive in a household where the questionnaire may be seen; the impossibility of withdrawal once data are pooled. Mitigation: plain language, the option to skip items, a statement about who may see the completed form, and a clear withdrawal window.

3. The outcome study using clinical data. Risk: using records for a purpose the client did not consent to; anonymisation that fails in a small clinic; the therapist's interest in a positive result. Mitigation: a specific consent to the use of data, an independent analyst, a pre-specified analysis, and a commitment to report the negative result.

4. The action research or service evaluation. Risk: the conflation of evaluation with research; staff or clients who cannot refuse because the evaluator is their manager or therapist. Mitigation: independent facilitation, anonymised data collection, and a written agreement about what will be reported to whom.

The design choice in a small programme. Most MFT 770 studies will be interview or survey designs with small samples. The ethical burden in a small sample is not statistical; it is the impossibility of true anonymity and the closeness of the researcher to the participants. Both are manageable with honesty, and neither is manageable with a standard consent form.

13.8 Six errors

Error 1 — Recruiting your own clients because they are available. The consent is contaminated, the sample is biased, and the client's refusal becomes a clinical event. The prohibition exists for a reason and the reason is not administrative.

Error 2 — Treating a parent's signature as family consent. Each adult consents for herself; each child assents for himself; and the consent is documented per person.

Error 3 — Promising anonymity that the design cannot deliver. A promise of anonymity to a family in a small community, from a study that will be in a university library, is a false statement made in writing.

Error 4 — Diagnosing what the payer will reimburse. It is fraud, and it is also a clinical decision made by a third party who will never meet the client.

Error 5 — Using an instrument because it is available. Validity for the population is not optional, and acting on a score whose meaning is unknown is acting on nothing.

Error 6 — Writing about clients without telling them, because the writing is "for teaching". Teaching is a purpose, not an exemption; the consent is required, and the requirement is stated in most codes in terms that students frequently have not read.

13.9 Culture, faith and the ethics of knowledge

Who researches whom. A substantial literature on research in African settings documents the problem of extractive research: studies designed elsewhere, conducted on local populations, published in journals the participants will never read, with no benefit to the community studied. The ethical response is not to stop researching; it is to build the study so that the community gains something — findings returned to the clinic or the church that hosted the study, local co-researchers, a dissemination event in the participants' language.

Community consent. In settings where a community leader, a church or a family elder is the gatekeeper, formal consent processes frequently require the gatekeeper's permission in addition to individual consent and never instead of it. A study in which the pastor agrees and the women do not is a study conducted on people who did not agree.

Instruments from elsewhere. Most widely used instruments were developed in North America or Europe. Their use in this region raises the questions of translation, conceptual equivalence (does the construct exist in the same form in this language?), and norming. A depression scale that asks about "feeling sad" may miss a presentation organised around bodily complaints, which is a well-documented pattern in many settings. The ethical requirement is honesty about the limits, not the abandonment of measurement.

Diagnosis across cultures. The cultural formulation in DSM-5 and the ICD guidance on cultural concepts of distress exist because the same experience is described differently across settings. The practitioner's discipline: describe the experience in the client's terms first, then consider whether a category fits, and record both.

Faith and the diagnosis. A family may hold that the problem is spiritual, and a diagnosis may be heard as a denial of that. The practitioner's position — Week 7's — is additive rather than competitive: the diagnosis and the family's explanation can coexist, and the therapist's task is to work with both without requiring the family to give up its account.

DEBATE — "Research on families should require the consent of every adult member, and any member's refusal should end the study for that family." Argue for: the family is a unit and the data are relational, one member's refusal will distort what the others say, and the family's privacy is shared. Argue against: an individual's participation is her own decision, a controlling husband could veto his wife's participation, and the rule would silence precisely the people whose voices are least often heard. Then state the rule you would put in your own ethics application.

13.9a Writing the thesis: five sentences the examiner will look for

Not a style guide; a list of the sentences that demonstrate the student has understood the ethics of her own study.

  1. "Participants were recruited by [a third party / letter] and were told that declining would not affect their care." The sentence that shows the dual role was considered.
  2. "Each adult gave written consent; each child gave written or verbal assent, recorded in the file." The sentence that shows consent was individual.
  3. "The sample was drawn from a single clinic and anonymity cannot be guaranteed; participants were told this." The sentence that shows honesty about limits.
  4. "Where a participant disclosed risk during the interview, the procedure agreed with the ethics committee was followed: [what happened]." The sentence that shows the protocol was not decorative.
  5. "The findings did not support the expected relationship between [x] and [y]." The sentence that shows the student reported what she found.

The missing sixth, which distinguishes a good thesis from a competent one. A short paragraph on what the study gave back: the report returned to the clinic, the session run for the staff, the plain-language summary for the participants. Students who write this paragraph have understood that research is a relationship and not a transaction.

13.10 Evidence note

What is known. The evidence on research ethics is largely in the form of guidance and documented failures rather than trials: consent processes that participants do not understand, therapeutic misconception (participants believing that research is treatment), and the systematic under-representation of children's own assent. In assessment, the cross-cultural validity literature consistently shows that instruments do not travel without psychometric work, and that translated tools can produce artefactual differences in prevalence. In diagnosis, research on labelling documents effects on self-concept, on family attributions and on clinicians' subsequent information-gathering — the diagnostic label shapes what is noticed next.

What is weaker. There is little empirical work on how therapists actually obtain consent to publish, and case reports are almost never audited for identifiability. The evidence on the harms of diagnostic labelling in family therapy specifically is thin.

What is not supported. That anonymity is achieved by changing names. That a diagnosis is harmless because it is confidential — the client's own family, school and employer frequently learn it. And that a validated instrument used outside its validation population measures the same construct.

The matrix cell. Development is probabilistic and non-linear; the same input produces different outcomes depending on timing and context. The same diagnosis given at the start of a treatment, as a working formulation, and given at the end, as a verdict on a person, has two different effects on a family.

13.11 Key terms

Assent — a child's agreement to participate, required in addition to a parent's consent.

Therapeutic misconception — the participant's belief that research activity is treatment.

Cultural validity — the extent to which an instrument measures the same construct in a different population.

Diagnostic act — the decision to assign a category, with its consequences for access, stigma and identity.

Composite case — a teaching case constructed from several cases, and labelled as such.

Extractive research — research that takes data from a community without returning benefit.

13.11a The five records a research-minded clinician keeps

A clinician who intends to write, teach or research will need material, and the material must be collected lawfully from the beginning.

  1. A consent-to-contact register, separate from the clinical file, recording who has agreed to be approached about research or teaching and when. It is the single document that makes later recruitment lawful.
  2. A teaching-consent note in the file, with the specific scope: "agreed to the use of anonymised material from this treatment for teaching and publication, subject to seeing the text; consent recorded [date]".
  3. A research log, for any study: recruitment, withdrawals, the date of each consent, and every ethical event (a disclosure of risk, a request to withdraw, an adverse reaction) with the action taken.
  4. A data map, one page: what data exist, where they are stored, who can access them, and when they will be destroyed.
  5. A publication file, containing the approved text, the participant's written approval, and the correspondence with the journal. It is the evidence that the consent was to that text and not to a general idea.

Why the fifth record is the one students omit. Because consent is usually obtained at the beginning, when the text does not yet exist, and the version published may differ from the version described. The file closes that gap and takes ten minutes.

13.12 Self-test

  1. Why is a father's signature not sufficient consent for a family study?
  2. Give three things disguise cannot protect, and the rule that follows.
  3. What are the four questions before making a diagnosis?
  4. State the six questions before using an assessment instrument.
  5. What are the four ways the clinician-researcher's dual role distorts research, and the four controls?
  6. What is therapeutic misconception, and how is it prevented?
  7. In the Amaka case, what did she do at the moment of overlap in the interview, and why?
  8. What is a composite case, and when may it be used?

Answers. (1) Because consent is individual: the father consents for himself, the mother for herself, and each child assents; a mother consenting under pressure has not consented. (2) Small communities, rare presentations, public events (and digital traces); the rule is that consent is obtained to the specific text, the limits are stated, and where disguise cannot protect, the case is not published. (3) Can I defend it; is it mine to make; what will it do for the client; what will it do to the family's story. (4) Validated for this population; normed on similar people; am I qualified; what will I do with the result; how will I give it back; where do the data go. (5) Recruitment bias, consent under influence, interpretation bias, role confusion at disclosure; controls are third-party recruitment, a written risk procedure, independent analysis or second coding, and supervision of the dual role. (6) The participant's belief that research is treatment; prevented by explicit information, separate consent, and the researcher's refusal to blur the roles. (7) She stopped the recording and stated aloud that the interview was research, that nothing from the clinical file would enter the study, and that she would use only what she heard in the interview. (8) A case constructed from several cases for teaching, labelled explicitly, and not presented as a single factual case.

13.12a Three questions the ethics committee will ask

Not the ones students prepare for.

"What will you do if the participant becomes distressed?" The answer must be a procedure, not a sentiment. Who stops the interview, what is said, who is called, and what the participant is offered afterwards.

"How will you know whether they understood the consent?" The answer is a method — a teach-back question, a second conversation, a written summary in the participant's language — not a signature.

"What happens to the data if you leave the programme?" The answer belongs to the institution, not the student: custody, storage and destruction specified in the application and followed at the end.

13.13 Reflective task

Write two hundred words about a family you have already met — in placement, in your own practice, or in this book's cases. Then answer: what would you have to change before you could publish it, and would what remained still teach anything? If the answer is no, you have found the reason that most good clinical writing is never written. If the answer is yes, write the limits into the text: what you changed, why, and what the reader should therefore not assume — because a reader who is told the limits can use the case, and a reader who is not will generalise it into a rule, and rules made from disguised cases have a way of being applied to real families who do not resemble the one described.

13.14 Further reading

  • The Declaration of Helsinki and the CIOMS guidelines — short, and the source of most national codes.
  • Your institution's research ethics guidance, read before writing the application rather than after the first rejection.
  • The DSM-5 cultural formulation and the ICD guidance on cultural concepts of distress.
  • Any current text on cross-cultural assessment and test adaptation — the psychometric requirements, not only the translations.
  • The literature on research ethics in African settings, particularly on community consent and benefit sharing.

Next: Week 14 — Integration: the decision model and your stance.

Week 14 — Integration: the decision model and your stance

Learning outcomes. By the end of this week the student can (1) apply the seven-step decision model to an unfamiliar dilemma under time pressure and defend each step; (2) identify the three ways the model is misused; (3) write a stance paper that names what she will never do, what she will always consult about, and where her own values are most likely to distort her judgement; and (4) present a case to an examiner in a way that shows reasoning rather than recall.

What integration means at the end of fourteen weeks

The course began with the question of what kind of professional a person becomes when she takes on the codes, and it ends with the same question, now answerable. Everything between — consent, confidentiality, secrets, boundaries, competence, risk, law, records, money, research — is a set of particular decisions that a stance has to hold together. A therapist who knows all fourteen weeks and has no stance will make each decision freshly, under pressure, according to how she feels that afternoon. A therapist with a stance has already decided the large things, which is what leaves her free to attend to the client in front of her.

The course's claim, in one sentence. Ethical practice is not the avoidance of wrongdoing; it is the maintenance of a structure — consent, competence, supervision, records, consultation — that makes good decisions possible when the therapist is tired, frightened, fond of the client, or alone.

14.1 The lecture in ninety minutes

MinutesContentTeaching form
0–10What integration is; the stance and why it is the assessmentTwo therapists, same dilemma, different stances
10–30The seven steps, each with its failure modeLecture with a running case
30–45The model applied end to end to a hard caseWhole-group application, one step at a time
45–55The three misuses of the modelThree transcripts of reasoning, students identify the misuse
55–70The stance paper: structure and worked extractsTwo anonymous stance papers, compared and marked
70–85The viva: what examiners ask and what they are listening forDemonstration viva with a volunteer
85–90The closing question: what will you never do?Written, collected, returned at the end of the programme

14.2 The seven steps, with the failure mode of each

Step 1 — Identify the dilemma. Not the situation: the dilemma. A situation is a mess; a dilemma is a choice between two goods or two harms. The statement to produce: "either I do X, and Y is lost, or I do Z, and W is lost." Failure mode: naming a situation as a dilemma when one option is obviously right — which is a way of avoiding the decision by making it look hard.

Step 2 — Check the codes and the law. Which clauses apply; which statute; what the jurisdiction requires. Failure mode: quoting a code from memory, or from another country. The check is documentary, and it takes ten minutes with the code in front of you.

Step 3 — Consult. A supervisor, a colleague, a lawyer, a specialist. Failure mode: consulting the colleague most likely to agree. The test of a consultation is that it changed something — a fact, an option, or the therapist's confidence in the wrong answer.

Step 4 — Generate at least three options, including the uncomfortable one. Three is the minimum because two options produce a binary in which one side is usually self-serving. The uncomfortable option is the one the therapist has not allowed herself to consider: ending the therapy, reporting, telling the client the truth, refusing the referral, transferring the case. Failure mode: the "three options" that are three versions of the preferred one.

Step 5 — Weigh the consequences for every affected party. The client, the partner, the children, the third party, the therapist, the profession, the future client who will be affected by the precedent. Written as a table, because a table exposes the party that has been left out. Failure mode: weighing only the client in the room, who is usually the most articulate and always the most present.

Step 6 — Decide, act, document. The decision is made, communicated to the client, and recorded with the reasoning, the consultation, the alternatives considered and the review date. Failure mode: documenting the decision without the reasoning, which is the note that reads as an assertion and cannot be defended.

Step 7 — Evaluate afterwards. What happened; what would be done differently; what the case has taught about the practice's structure. Failure mode: skipping step 7 because the case is over. This is the step that converts experience into competence, and it is the step that therapists in busy practices never reach.

14.2a The model applied, end to end

The case below is a composite, constructed to require every part of the course. It is the case used for the practical examination.

The situation. Ngozi A., 41, a nurse, has been in treatment for eleven weeks for depression following her husband's stroke. Her husband, Emeka, 52, is a retired teacher with significant disability from the stroke, cared for at home. Their daughter, Ada, 16, is in the household. In session eleven, Ngozi discloses that her brother-in-law has been sexually touching Ada for some months, that Ada told her in confidence, that Ngozi has not told anyone else, and that she has been "handling it by keeping him away from the house". She says she cannot manage her husband's care alone, that the brother-in-law is her husband's younger brother and the family's only financial support, and that if this becomes public "they will say I destroyed the family." She is the therapist's neighbour's sister and the family attend the therapist's church.

Step 1 — the dilemma. "Either I report the disclosure, and this woman loses the support that keeps her household functioning and possibly her marriage, or I do not, and a sixteen-year-old remains at risk and the therapist becomes a party to the concealment." Stated as a dilemma, both losses are real. That is the point of step 1.

Step 2 — the codes and the law. The child protection duty is engaged: a child under eighteen has disclosed sexual abuse. The statute in the jurisdiction requires a report on reasonable suspicion, and here there is a disclosure. The code requires the therapist to report and to explain to the client what will be reported. The data protection framework permits the disclosure of the minimum necessary. The report does not require the mother's consent and does not require proof.

Step 3 — consultation. The supervisor, the child protection lead at the local authority, and — because of the dual relationship — the therapist's own supervisor's supervisor, or an ethics consultation. Read what happened in consulation: the child protection officer confirmed the threshold was met and gave the route and the name; the supervisor identified the dual relationship as a separate problem requiring a decision.

Step 4 — the options, including the uncomfortable one.

  1. Report to the child protection unit today, tell Ngozi what will be reported, continue the treatment.
  2. Report, and transfer the case because of the dual relationship and the threat to the alliance.
  3. Support Ngozi to report herself, with a deadline of forty-eight hours, and report if she does not.
  4. Do not report, and work with Ngozi to protect Ada within the family.
  5. Report, and end the treatment.

Option 4 is unlawful and is stated in order to be rejected in writing, which is what a good record does. Options 1, 2, 3 and 5 are all lawful; they differ in what they protect. Option 3 is attractive and is wrong here: forty-eight hours of delay for a child in a household where the abuser has access.

Step 5 — consequences for every affected party.

PartyOption 1Option 2Option 5
AdaProtection; investigation; possible family disruptionSameSame
NgoziTold first; supported; may lose the therapistTold; loses the therapist at the moment of crisisTold; loses the therapist and the treatment
EmekaLoses his brother's support; may learn of the abuse in a way he cannot controlSameSame
The brother-in-lawInvestigatedSameSame
The therapistManages dual relationship and the allianceRemoves the dual relationship, abandons the clientEnds the case
The practiceA precedent for the dual-relationship questionCleaner—

Step 6 — the decision and the record. Report today; tell Ngozi in the session, in the words of Week 9; continue the treatment for the present, with the dual relationship managed and declared to the supervisor; review the decision to continue at the next supervision; record the seven steps in the file with the consultation names and times.

Step 7 — afterwards. At the review, three questions. Did the report protect Ada? Did the treatment survive? And the structural question: what in this practice allowed a therapist to be treating a family from her own church without a policy that would have raised the question at intake?

What the case demonstrates. The model does not make the decision comfortable, and it does not remove the losses. It makes the decision defensible, which is all a decision-making model can do. The therapist who reports is not choosing the child over the mother; she is choosing to comply with a duty that exists because adults in these situations frequently cannot protect the child themselves — including the mother, who is not a villain in this case and is not able to do what the law requires of the professional.

The teaching point students resist. The mother is not the problem. A model that turns her into the problem makes the case easier and the work worse. The step-5 table exists to keep her in the weighing.

14.2b The three misuses of the model

Misuse 1 — using it to justify a decision already made. The therapist reaches the conclusion in the first thirty seconds — usually the one that protects the relationship — and then walks the seven steps backwards. The signature is a step 4 that contains three versions of the same option, and a step 5 table in which the therapist's convenience does not appear because it has not been named.

Misuse 2 — skipping step 4. Two options, both bad, and the therapist chooses the less bad and calls it a decision. The uncomfortable third option is frequently the one that requires a conversation the therapist is avoiding — telling the client the truth, ending the treatment, refusing the fee, admitting the error.

Misuse 3 — treating the model as a substitute for a stance. A therapist with no position on the large questions will run the seven steps for every decision and produce technically defensible answers that do not add up to a practice. The model is a procedure for the difficult case; the stance is what handles the ordinary ones, which are most of them.

The test of a well-used model. After the decision, the therapist can state the option she rejected and why, the party she most regretted, and the part of the decision she found hardest. A therapist who reports no difficulty has probably not done step 5.

14.2c The six questions that find the dilemma

Before the seven steps, a filter. Six questions, used at intake and at every review, that identify the cases in which an ethical question is present before it announces itself.

  1. Who is not in this room, and would they be affected by what happens here? The absent party is the commonest source of ethical error in family work: the affair partner's spouse, the child who is not brought, the husband who declined, the employer who is paying.
  2. What would I be uncomfortable reading aloud in a hearing? The question that locates the weak point without requiring a theory of ethics.
  3. Am I the only person who knows this? An isolated fact is a structural risk (Weeks 3 and 4).
  4. What have I been asked to do, by whom, in whose interest? The referral question, the report, the character letter, the "just a quick word with the school".
  5. What am I avoiding asking? The question the therapist has noticed and postponed. It is almost always the one that will become the dilemma.
  6. Is my practice built to catch this? Policy, supervision, screening, records. If the answer is no, the dilemma is not a case problem; it is a practice problem that has produced a case.

Why the filter comes first. The seven steps are expensive in time and attention, and they are used on perhaps a dozen cases a year. The six questions take two minutes and are used on every case, which is where most of the protection lies.

14.3 The stance paper

The assessment for this week, and the document that outlasts the course.

What it is. Five hundred words in three sections, written in the first person, dated, and revisited at the end of every year of practice.

Section 1 — what I will never do. Six to ten statements, specific enough to be checkable. Not "I will always act ethically", but: "I will never see a client's partner without the client's knowledge"; "I will never accept a gift of money"; "I will never write an opinion about a parent I have not assessed"; "I will never continue a treatment in which I am the only person who knows something that would change it."

Section 2 — what I will always consult about. The list is shorter and it is the more important one, because the things on it are the ones the therapist will be tempted to handle alone. Typical entries: any case involving a child and a disclosure; any sexual boundary question; any decision to continue after a serious error; any case in which I notice that I am the only person the client trusts; any request I am inclined to grant as a favour.

Section 3 — where my own values are most likely to distort my judgement. The honest section, and the one that makes the other two credible. Examples of what a real entry looks like:

I was raised in a household where a woman who left her husband was a failure, and I notice that I work harder with couples who are separating than with those who are not. I will consult whenever I find myself arguing for the marriage. I am uncomfortable with displays of anger, and I will notice when I am softening a client's anger on her behalf. I am frightened of being thought incompetent, and I will notice when a referral feels like a humiliation rather than a clinical decision. Money was never discussed in my family, and I will use the practice's policy rather than my own judgement at the moment the conversation becomes difficult.

Why the third section is examined. Because a therapist who cannot name her own distortions will not notice them in the room, and because the examined stance paper is the only place in the programme where the student is required to be specific about herself rather than about clients.
