How it differs from a values statement. A values statement says what the therapist believes. A stance paper says what the therapist will do, refuse, consult about and watch for. The first is a position; the second is an instrument.

The revision. Once a year, in writing: which of the statements in section 1 did I break; which of the entries in section 3 was accurate; and what new entry belongs there. The revision is the record of professional development that no CPD log records.

14.3a The worked stance paper, in part

An extract from a student's paper, included because the specificity is instructive and because students consistently write something more abstract on the first attempt.

What I will never do. I will never treat a member of my own family, or a close friend, or the child of either, and I will say so at the point of referral rather than after the first session. I will never conduct a session without knowing whose client system I am working with. I will never let a couple's file contain a secret that either partner can demand to read. I will never give a diagnosis I have not written the reasoning for. I will never sign a letter I would not read aloud in court. What I will always consult about. Any disclosure of abuse. Any case in which I want to break a rule because the client's situation is exceptional — my exceptions are the least reliable part of my judgement. Any case in which I have begun to dislike the client. Any case in which I have become the only person the client talks to. Any fee arrangement I have made in the room rather than from the policy. Where my values distort me. I grew up in a home where conflict was forbidden, and I have discovered in role-play that I interrupt anger. I will ask my supervisor, every month, whether I have done it. I was raised in a church where divorce was a failure, and I have already noticed myself looking for reasons the marriage should survive in a case where the wife has not asked me to. My father was a violent man, and I am alert to the risk that I will see violence where there is only frustration, which is the opposite error and equally harmful. I am uncomfortable charging people who remind me of my mother, and I will use the sliding scale rather than decide in the room.

What makes this a good paper. Every statement is checkable, most are testable in a role-play, and the third section contains at least one entry that the student would rather not have written. That last feature is the marker of authenticity: a stance paper with no uncomfortable entry has been written for the examiner.

14.4 The viva: what is being examined

The form. A case presented by the candidate, usually one from her own practice or placement, followed by questions. Twenty to thirty minutes. The examiner has read the case summary; the candidate has the file.

What the examiner is listening for. Five things, in order of importance.

  1. Whether the candidate knows whose client it is. The first question is almost always about the client system, and it is asked because candidates who cannot answer it are not safe to practise.
  2. Whether the candidate can name the ethical question without prompting. The blank look followed by "there wasn't really an ethical issue" is the commonest failure, and it is a failure of recognition rather than of knowledge.
  3. Whether the reasoning is visible. Steps taken, alternatives considered, consultation sought. The candidate who says "I decided to report" scores less than the one who says "I considered three options, and here is why I rejected two".
  4. Whether the candidate can distinguish what she knows from what she believes. "I formed the impression" is acceptable; "I knew" is not, unless it was known.
  5. Whether the candidate's stance is present. Not a speech about values — the ordinary consistency of a person who has decided the large things.

The case to bring. One in which the candidate made a decision she can defend, and one thing she would do differently. The case in which everything went well teaches the examiner nothing and the candidate less. The candidate who brings a case she got partly wrong, and can say what she learned, is marked higher than the candidate who brings a success.

The three questions that are asked in some form every time.

  • "Who was your client?"
  • "What was the ethical question, and when did you first notice it?"
  • "What did you not do, and why?"

Preparing. Write a one-page summary: the family, the presenting problem, the decision point, the options, the consultation, the outcome, the reflection. Read the file. Know the dates. Rehearse the answers aloud to a colleague, because the first time these questions are answered should not be under examination.

The ethics of discussing cases in a viva. Consent from the client is not usually required for an examination conducted under confidentiality, and the candidate should nonetheless disguise the case and should say so: "the details have been changed." Examining boards treat the material as confidential; the candidate must treat it the same way outside the room.

14.4a Five answers that score badly, and their replacements

Poor answerWhy it failsReplacement
"I felt it was the right thing to do."No reasoning"I weighed three options; the deciding factor was X; here is what I recorded."
"The code says I had to."No judgement"The code required a report; the judgement was in what I told the client and when."
"I did not want to damage the relationship."The therapist's comfort as the criterion"I considered the alliance, and I judged that the duty outweighed it; here is how I protected the relationship in the telling."
"It was a difficult case."Says nothing"It was difficult because two duties conflicted; the conflict was between X and Y."
"I would do the same again."No learning"I would do the same on the report, and differently on the timing — I would have raised it in session eight."

The pattern in all five. The poor answer describes a state; the good answer describes a decision. The examiner is not asking whether the candidate is a good person. She is asking whether the candidate can show her work.

14.4b The four sentences of the professional apology

Every therapist will make an error that harms a client. What happens next is a test of the whole course.

The four sentences. "I made a mistake and I want to tell you what it was." (Specific, owned, no passive voice.) "I am sorry, and I am not asking you to reassure me." (The apology without the request for absolution, which is what makes it usable.) "Here is what I have done about it." (The correction: the supervision, the referral, the record, the change.) "Here is what I will do next, and what you can decide." (The return of agency to the client, including the right to complain.)

The apology that fails. "I am sorry if you feel..." — an apology for the client's feeling. "Mistakes happen." — a generalisation that removes the therapist. "But you have to understand the position I was in." — an explanation that asks the client to carry the therapist. And an apology made so that the client will comfort the therapist, which is a request and not an apology.

What the codes require, and what they do not. The codes require honesty, the correction of the record where appropriate, and the client's right to complain. They do not require self-flagellation, and a therapist who collapses will make the client responsible for her.

The follow-up. The mistake is recorded, presented in supervision, and — where it reveals a structural problem — changes something in the practice. The client is told what changed. That last sentence is the one that converts an error into a demonstration of how a professional relationship handles failure.

14.5 The final supervision

The last supervision of the training year, in which the candidate presents her integration case. What should happen in it, and what the supervisor is looking for.

What the candidate brings. The case; the decision; the record of the decision; the stance paper; and one thing about her own practice that the training has changed.

What the supervisor asks.

  • "What would you do differently if you met this family again tomorrow?"
  • "Which part of your stance paper did this case test?"
  • "What did you do that you did not tell me about at the time, and why?"
  • "Who will you consult about this kind of case when I am no longer your supervisor?"

The last question is the one that matters. The purpose of supervision during training is not to make the candidate dependent on a supervisor; it is to build the habit of consultation into a professional life that will have no one appointed to provide it. A candidate who can answer the fourth question precisely — a named person, a peer group, a paid consultation, a specialist — is ready to qualify.

What the supervisor writes. A short assessment of the candidate's readiness that names the strengths and the specific development need going forward. Not a character reference, and not a summary of the case: a statement about what this clinician can be trusted with and what she should not yet do alone.

14.5a The case summary the candidate brings

One page, six headings, written the week before the viva. A worked example, drawn from a completed training case.

The family. The Oyelarans: Tunde, 46, a mechanic; Kemi, 43, a trader; children Segun, 15, and Bisi, 9. Referred by the school following Segun's attendance. The presenting problem. Segun's school refusal, which the parents attributed to "bad friends" and the school to "the home". The first session produced three different accounts and no agreement about the problem. The decision point. Session four: Kemi disclosed, alone, that Tunde had been drinking heavily since losing his workshop, that there had been two episodes in which he had pushed her, and that Segun had witnessed the second. She asked me not to tell him that she had told me, and not to raise it in the family sessions, because "it will make it worse". The options I considered. (1) Continue the family work and hold the disclosure, working individually with Kemi. (2) End the family work and see both adults individually. (3) Name the violence in the family session without attributing the disclosure. (4) Refer the family to a specialist domestic violence service and end the treatment. What I did and why. I did not continue the family sessions as though nothing had been said — option 1 would have made me a participant in the concealment, and Segun's witnessing made the children's exposure a child protection question. I did not confront Tunde with the disclosure (that would have exposed Kemi). I screened for lethality (no strangulation, no threats to kill, no weapon; two pushes; escalation with the drinking); I made a child protection referral regarding the children's exposure, told Kemi what I would say before I said it, and arranged individual work with her and a separate referral for Tunde to a substance use service. I did not end the treatment. I recorded the decision, the consultation (supervisor, [date]) and the review date. What I would do differently. I would have screened both partners individually in sessions one and two, as the practice protocol requires, rather than in session four when Kemi volunteered it. The protocol existed; I followed it late. And I would have asked Kemi directly whether she had told anyone else, because her sister had known for three months and was the person who had been keeping Segun overnight. What the case taught me about my practice. I avoid asking about violence in the first session because I am afraid of what I will then have to do. That is a stance problem, not a knowledge problem, and it is now in my stance paper.

Why this summary works. It states the dilemma without dramatising it, shows the rejected options with reasons, records the consultation, names the mistake without being destroyed by it, and connects the error to the therapist's own pattern rather than to the difficulty of the case.

14.6 Transcript: twelve minutes of viva

A candidate answering the three inevitable questions. Annotated.

Q1 Examiner: Who was your client? A1 Candidate: The parents were the referrers and the school was the referrer of record. I treated the couple as the client system and I saw each of them individually under the policy I had stated in session one — which, as I will say, I did late. Segun was a client for three sessions, with his parents' consent and his own assent, and I was clear with all of them that what he told me would be shared with them only if he was at risk. — The answer names the system, the consent, the policy and the exception, in one turn. The examiner now knows most of what she needs. Q2 Examiner: When did you first notice there was an ethical question? A2 Candidate: In session four, when Kemi told me. (pause) And honestly, I should have noticed in session one, when the three accounts of the problem did not fit and nobody in the family would look at anybody else. — The honest answer with the retrospective recognition, which is what the examiner is testing: not whether the candidate noticed early, but whether she can tell the difference between noticing and understanding. Q3 Examiner: What was the question? A3 Candidate: Whether to keep the disclosure, which would have let the family work continue and would have made me part of the concealment, or to act on it, which meant the risk that Kemi would lose the only support she had. (pause) And underneath that, whether the children's exposure was a child protection matter, which it was. — Two duties named, and the second question identified as the one that decided the case. Q4 Examiner: Why did you not confront the husband? A4 Candidate: Because Kemi would have paid for it. (pause) The evidence is that conjoint confrontation in coercive situations escalates afterwards, and I had no way of predicting what he would do in the house that night. — The answer that shows the candidate has read the material and has applied it to a specific person's safety, rather than reciting a rule. Q5 Examiner: Did you consider referring the whole family out? A5 Candidate: I did, as option four. I rejected it because Kemi had disclosed to me and would not have disclosed to a stranger, and because a referral at that point would have been a way for me to avoid the decision rather than a service to her. (pause) I would refer now, if the violence escalated or if Tunde began to attend and I found myself holding both accounts. — The rejected option, the reason, and the condition under which the decision would change. This is the shape of a good answer. Q6 Examiner: What would you do differently? A6 Candidate: I would screen in sessions one and two rather than four. And I would not have assumed that because Kemi had not told me, nobody knew. Her sister knew. The sister had been keeping Segun overnight for three months, and I had not asked who else was involved. — A specific, small, real error, offered without self-punishment. Q7 Examiner: What does that tell you about your practice? A7 Candidate: That I delay asking about violence because I am afraid of the answer, and that the delay is itself a clinical decision with consequences. I have written it into my stance paper, and I have changed my intake so that the screening questions are asked in the first session of every couple. — The answer that connects an error to a change in structure, which is what makes a training case worth examining.

What the transcript shows. The candidate never defends, never performs regret, and never claims more than she knows. Her answers are short, specific, and each one contains a decision rather than a feeling. The examiner's follow-up questions get shorter as the viva proceeds, which is what happens when the answers are sufficient.

14.7 Laboratory: the mock viva and the stance review

Part A — the mock viva (sixty minutes). Students in threes: candidate, examiner, observer. Two rounds of twenty minutes, with ten minutes of feedback each. The candidate brings a one-page case summary; the examiner uses the seven questions from 14.4 and 14.6; the observer marks against the rubric below.

Criterion0–12–34–5
The client systemUnclearNamedNamed, with consent and policy, and the exceptions stated
RecognitionDid not identify the questionIdentified lateIdentified, with an honest account of when and why
ReasoningConclusion onlyReasoning partly visibleOptions, consultation, decision and record all visible
The mistakeDenied or catastrophicAdmittedNamed specifically, with the change it produced
StanceGeneric valuesSome personal materialSpecific, checkable, connected to this case

Part B — the stance review (thirty minutes). In pairs, students exchange stance papers and apply three tests. Is each statement in section 1 checkable? Convert any that are not. Is section 2 specific enough that a supervisor could hold the student to it? Does section 3 contain at least one entry the student would rather not have written? Papers that pass all three are read aloud to the group, and the group identifies which sentence would be hardest to keep.

The question to end on. "Which statement in your paper will be hardest to keep in your first year of practice, and what will make it hard?" The answers — money, a client's distress, the fear of losing the work, a supervisor who disagrees — are the actual curriculum of the first year.

14.8 Six errors of integration

Error 1 — Treating the model as a decision-maker. The seven steps produce a record of reasoning; they do not choose. The therapist who expects the model to make the decision will run it twice and still be undecided.

Error 2 — The stance paper as an essay about virtue. Written in the language of values, unverifiable, and forgotten by the second month. The paper is a set of commitments, and its test is whether it constrains behaviour.

Error 3 — Learning 700 pages and practising none of it. The student who can recite the codes and has not built a supervision arrangement, a records policy or a fee policy has learned the subject and not the practice.

Error 4 — The belief that a good therapist does not need structure. Structure is what allows the therapist to be fully present: the policy holds the decisions that would otherwise be made in the moment, under pressure, by a tired person who likes the client.

Error 5 — Recognising the ethical question only in retrospect. The commonest failure in the viva and in practice. The remedy is a checklist embedded in the ordinary workflow — screening questions in every first session, a consultation prompt in every complex case, a review date in every decision — because a habit fires where recognition does not.

Error 6 — Practising alone. Not a moral failure and a structural one: the therapist with no supervisor, no peer group and no consultation is the therapist in whom errors go uncorrected for years. The course's final recommendation is a relationship, not a rule.

14.8a The practice that is ready: a checklist of twelve

The course has argued throughout that ethical practice is structural. This is the structure, as a checklist that a graduate can work through in a fortnight and a supervisor can audit in an hour.

Documents. A consent form naming the limits and the client system; a confidentiality and secrets policy; a fee and cancellation policy; a records and retention policy; a disclosure protocol for requests from third parties; a complaints procedure; a list of verified referral contacts.

Relationships. A named supervisor with a written contract; a peer consultation group of at least three; an indemnity arrangement with access to legal advice; a professional body membership in good standing.

Habits. Screening questions in every first session of a couple or family; a supervision slot in the diary that is not moved; a note written within the day; a review date on every significant decision; one annual reading of the code and one annual audit of the practice's money.

The test. For each of the twelve, answer: does this exist in writing, and could a colleague find it without me? A practice in which the answer is yes for eight of the twelve is a practice that will survive its first serious case. A practice in which the answer is no for most of them is not unethical; it is unprotected, and the difference will be discovered by a client.

The first fortnight after qualifying. Build the documents, contract the supervisor, join the group, buy the indemnity. Not because a regulator requires it, but because each of the twelve is a decision the therapist will otherwise make alone, in the moment, with a client waiting.

14.9 Culture, faith and the professional stance

The stance is not culturally neutral, and it does not need to be. The professional obligations described in this book — individual consent, confidentiality against the family, the child's separate interests, the refusal to give legal advice — are the product of a particular history. They are nonetheless the terms on which this profession operates, and a therapist who joins the profession accepts them. The question the student actually faces is not whether the obligations are culturally local but how to hold them inside a communal world without becoming either a foreigner or a hypocrite.

Three ways of holding it.

  1. Translate rather than announce. Every obligation in this book can be explained in the family's own terms: why an individual session exists, why a record is kept, why a gift of land cannot be accepted, why a report must be made. The therapist who translates is obeying the same rule in the family's language.
  2. Keep the form and the substance separate. The therapist can eat the food, greet the elder, attend the funeral and say the prayers she says — and still not discuss the client's marriage with the aunt. The forms are the family's; the substance is the profession's.
  3. Be explicit about the limits of your own position. "I know that in your family the elders would decide this. In my work I am required to let the person decide for herself, and I want to tell you why." Naming the difference is more respectful than either imposing or pretending.

Conscience, and the cases where the therapist's own values will not yield. A therapist may hold a religious conviction about divorce, abortion or same-sex relationships. The professional position is that the conviction is hers and the decision is the client's, and where the therapist cannot provide the service without imposing the conviction, the answer is referral and not persuasion. The two sentences: "I am not the right therapist for this, and I want to tell you that it is about me and not about you", and "here is a name."

Where the law and the client's interest diverge. They do, occasionally: a reporting duty that will harm the child it was designed to protect; a court order that will disclose a client's history to a violent ex-partner; a statutory requirement that the therapist believes is wrong. The professional response is to comply, to say so, to record the objection, and to work for change through the professional body. Civil disobedience is a legitimate act for a citizen and is not available to a clinician in the room, because the person who pays for it is the client.

The closing claim. A stance is not a barrier between the therapist and the family. It is what allows the therapist to be present: the person who has decided what she will not do does not have to decide it again in the session, and the attention that would have gone into that decision goes to the client.

DEBATE — "A therapist should never impose her values on a client, and a therapist who refers a client because of her own convictions has failed her duty of care." Argue for: the client came for treatment, not judgement, and referral is a rejection that falls hardest on the most vulnerable. Argue against: a therapist who cannot work without imposing is more dangerous in the room than out of it, honesty about limits is a form of respect, and referral with a warm handover is the lesser harm. Then state where your own line is, and what you would say to a client on the other side of it.

14.9a Nothing here is a substitute for the code

One final note on authority. This book has cited principles, statutes and codes, and it is not itself any of them. Where this book and a code disagree, the code governs; where a code and a statute disagree, the statute governs; where a statute and a court order disagree, the order governs. The book's purpose has been to teach the reasoning that makes those authorities usable in a room with a family in it, and a reader who treats it as a rulebook has misread it in the direction of safety, which is at least the right direction.

14.10 Evidence note

What is known. Ethics education research finds that knowledge of codes does not reliably change behaviour, while structured decision supports, checklists and consultation habits do. The literature on clinical judgement documents the gap between self-assessed and observed competence, and the value of feedback and routine outcome measurement. Research on moral distress and moral injury in therapists shows that unaddressed ethical conflict is a major contributor to burnout and to leaving the profession, and that supervision and peer consultation are protective. Studies of decision-making under pressure consistently find that prior commitment — a written policy, a rehearsed sentence — outperforms in-the-moment reasoning.

What is weaker. There is very little evidence on the specific instrument of the stance paper, and none on the seven-step model as a measurable intervention. The claim that they improve practice is derived from the broader literature on implementation and habit rather than from trials of these tools.

What is not supported. That ethical behaviour follows from ethical knowledge. That a therapist's good intentions are protective in the absence of structure. And that experience alone produces ethical judgement — experience without feedback produces confidence, which is a different thing.

The matrix cell. Development is probabilistic and non-linear; the same input produces different outcomes depending on timing and context. The same therapist, with the same knowledge, in a practice with supervision and a policy and in a practice without them, is two different clinicians.

14.11 Key terms

Dilemma — a choice between two goods or two harms, as distinct from a difficult situation.

Stance — the set of prior commitments a therapist holds about what she will never do, always consult about, and watch for in herself.

Consultation — seeking the view of another professional on a specific question; distinct from supervision in its form and often in its authority.

Moral distress — the experience of knowing the right action and being prevented from taking it.

Moral injury — the lasting psychological harm that follows acting against one's moral convictions in a professional role.

Implementation intention — a pre-decided response to a specified situation; the mechanism by which policies survive pressure.

Stance paper — the written, dated, annually revised statement of a therapist's commitments and vulnerabilities.

14.11a The one-page summary the course actually asks for

If the fourteen weeks were compressed into a page, it would say six things.

1. Name the client system before you do anything else. Most ethical errors in family work are errors about who the client is.

2. State the frame before it is tested. Consent, confidentiality, its limits, the fee, the cancellation policy, the records. Stated at the start, they are agreements; stated when needed, they are changes of rule.

3. Do not carry what you cannot carry. A secret, a risk, a doubt about competence, an attraction, a debt: each is a signal to consult, and the signal is not a personal failing.

4. Know the four duties that override the frame. Risk to life, risk to a child, statutory reporting, and a court order. Learn them for your jurisdiction, in writing, and review them annually.

5. Write as though it will be read aloud. Because some of it will be.

6. Build the structure that decides the ordinary cases, so that your attention is available for the client in front of you. The structure is not the opposite of relationship; it is what makes relationship possible.

14.12 Self-test

  1. State the seven steps, with the failure mode of each.
  2. In the Ngozi A. composite, why was option 3 (the forty-eight-hour deadline) rejected?
  3. What are the three misuses of the decision model, and how is each detected?
  4. Give the three sections of the stance paper and an example of a good entry in each.
  5. What are the five things a viva examiner is listening for?
  6. What are the three questions asked in some form every time, and why?
  7. Give three ways of holding professional obligations inside a communal culture.
  8. Why is civil disobedience not available to the clinician in the consulting room?

Answers. (1) Identify the dilemma — naming a situation instead; check codes and law — quoting from memory; consult — consulting the colleague who will agree; generate three options including the uncomfortable one — three versions of the preferred option; weigh consequences for every party — weighing only the client in the room; decide, act, document — documenting the conclusion without the reasoning; evaluate — skipping it. (2) Because it delayed protection for a child in a household where the abuser had access; the deadline was attractive because it preserved the alliance and it subordinated the child's safety to the therapist's discomfort. (3) Justifying a decision already made — detected by a step 4 containing three versions of one option; skipping step 4 — detected by a binary choice with no uncomfortable option; substituting the model for a stance — detected by a therapist who runs the procedure for every decision and cannot say what she would never do. (4) What I will never do — "I will never write an opinion about a parent I have not assessed"; what I will always consult about — "any case in which I have become the only person the client talks to"; where my values distort me — "I interrupt anger, and I will ask my supervisor monthly whether I have done it". (5) Whose client it is; whether the question can be named without prompting; whether the reasoning is visible; whether knowledge is distinguished from belief; whether a stance is present. (6) "Who was your client?", "what was the ethical question and when did you first notice it?", "what did you not do, and why?" — because they test the three capacities that cannot be faked: defining the system, recognising the question, and reasoning about the rejected option. (7) Translate rather than announce; keep the form and the substance separate; be explicit about the limits of your own position. (8) Because the person who bears the consequence of the therapist's protest is the client, who did not consent to be the occasion of it.

14.13 Reflective task, and the close of the course

Write the five hundred words of the stance paper. Date it. Keep it somewhere you will find it in a year, and when you find it, write the revision: which statements held, which did not, and what belongs in section three now.

Then write one paragraph for yourself alone, not for an examiner, answering this question: what kind of therapist do you intend to be in the situation that frightens you most? The answer will be tested within the first eighteen months. The purpose of fourteen weeks of ethics has not been to make you cautious; it has been to give you a structure you can stand inside when the situation arrives, so that the therapist who is there — tired, fond of the client, unsure — is still recognisably you.

14.14 Further reading

  • Beauchamp, T. and Childress, J. on the four principles — the framework used throughout this book, read in the original for its argument rather than its list.
  • Kitchener, R. on ethical principles in counselling — the account that underlies the codes' structure.
  • Doherty, W. on moral conflicts in family therapy — the best short treatment of the therapist's own values.
  • Pope, K. and Vasquez, M. on ethics in psychotherapy and counselling — the standard reference, revised periodically.
  • Your professional code, read in full, once a year, with a pencil.
  • The literature on moral distress and burnout in therapists — read for the evidence that this subject is a protective factor and not an ornament.

Glossary

Autonomy — the client's right to self-determination. Beneficence / non-maleficence — the duty to benefit and the duty not to harm. Coercive control — a pattern of domination, isolation and threat, distinct from situational violence. Confidentiality — the duty not to disclose information learned in a professional relationship. Dual relationship — a second significant relationship with a client or someone close to them. Duty to warn / protect — a legal obligation, in some jurisdictions, to act on a serious threat. Informed consent — voluntary agreement based on adequate information and capacity. Mandated reporting — a legal duty to report suspected abuse or neglect. Privilege — the client's legal right to prevent disclosure in court proceedings. Subpoena — a legal order requiring documents or testimony. Welfare principle — the rule that a child's best interests govern decisions about them.

Self-assessment

Multiple choice

1. In couple therapy, the most defensible approach to individual secrets is: (a) keep everything private (b) declare a policy in advance and apply it (c) tell the partner immediately (d) refuse individual sessions entirely

2. Asking a client directly about suicide: (a) increases risk (b) does not increase risk and is required practice (c) is only for psychiatrists (d) should be avoided in family sessions

3. Couple therapy with a couple in which there is coercive control is: (a) recommended (b) contraindicated until safety is established (c) harmless (d) the only legal option

4. The four principles of biomedical ethics are: (a) autonomy, beneficence, non-maleficence, justice (b) consent, capacity, confidentiality, competence (c) duty, utility, virtue, care (d) respect, honesty, integrity, courage

5. A treating therapist asked to conduct a custody evaluation for their own client should: (a) accept (b) accept if paid (c) decline because of role conflict (d) accept with supervision

6. The welfare principle means decisions about children are governed by: (a) parental preference (b) the child's best interests (c) the court's convenience (d) financial capacity

7. The strongest single predictor of a suicide attempt is: (a) diagnosis (b) previous attempt (c) gender (d) family income

8. A small cultural gift after a bereavement should usually be: (a) refused (b) accepted, discussed and documented (c) accepted silently (d) reported to the police

9. Screening for intimate partner violence should be done: (a) with the couple present (b) with each partner separately (c) by questionnaire only (d) only if asked

10. A defensible ethical decision is one that: (a) everyone agrees with (b) can be explained and documented (c) produces the best outcome (d) follows the client's preference

Answer key

1 b · 2 b · 3 b · 4 a · 5 c · 6 b · 7 b · 8 b · 9 b · 10 b

Short answer

  1. State a confidentiality policy for couple therapy in six sentences a family could understand.
  2. List the ten elements of a defensible suicide risk assessment.
  3. Distinguish situational couple violence from coercive control, and state the treatment implication.
  4. What should you do when you receive a subpoena? Five steps.
  5. Why is "I know it is unethical but it was the right thing to do" an inadequate defence?

Essay (choose one, 2,000 words)

  1. "The most difficult ethical problems in family therapy are not conflicts between right and wrong but between two rights." Discuss with three cases.
  2. A father discloses that his 14-year-old daughter is self-harming and asks you not to tell her mother. Analyse the ethical, legal and clinical dimensions and set out your plan.
  3. Critically examine the claim that the professional codes are culturally biased in favour of Western individualism.
